# The United Nations and Global Governance - Live Learning Session

> **What this session teaches:** It teaches why a second world organisation was built within a generation of the first one failing, what its founders deliberately chose not to fix, and what the institution has actually done in the eight decades since. It begins with the design problem, because every strong answer on this topic turns on a single distinction - between an organisation that failed and an organisation that was never given the power to succeed. It teaches the wartime construction of the Charter completely: the Dumbarton Oaks proposals of 1944 between the USSR, the USA, China and Britain, the San Francisco conference of 25 April to 26 June 1945 at which fifty states drafted and signed the text, the entry into force on 24 October 1945 after ratification by the five great powers and a majority of the other signatories, and the bargain that sits underneath the whole structure - that the great powers would be inside the organisation, as the United States had not been inside the League, and that the price of their presence would be a veto over action against them. It teaches the three founding aims in the words the sources use: to preserve peace and eliminate war; to remove the causes of conflict by encouraging economic, social, educational, scientific and cultural progress, especially in under-developed countries; and to safeguard the rights of all individual human beings and the rights of peoples and nations. It teaches membership as a live political story rather than a list - the Council recommendation and the two-thirds Assembly vote, the American veto that kept the People's Republic of China out for twenty years and the 1971 reversal, the forty-three new members between 1963 and 1968, the 159 of 1985, the 183 of 1993, East Timor as the 191st member in 2002, Montenegro in 2006 and South Sudan as the 193rd in July 2011. It teaches each principal organ through its real powers rather than its job description: the General Assembly with one vote per state, the simple and two-thirds majorities, the budget and the apportionment of contributions, and the exact difference between a recommendation and a decision; the Security Council with its original eleven members, its enlargement to fifteen in 1965, the requirement of nine affirmative votes including the permanent members, the unwritten practice that abstention does not count as a veto, and the chapter structure that separates pacific settlement from enforcement; the Economic and Social Council with its regional commissions, its functional commissions and the coordination of around thirty agencies; the Trusteeship Council from the eleven trust territories to its suspension of operation on 1 November 1994; the International Court of Justice with fifteen judges of different nationalities elected for nine-year terms, the frontier case between Holland and Belgium, the fishing-limits disagreement between Britain and Norway, and the Nicaragua merits judgment of 1986 that the United States simply refused to accept; and the Secretariat, its office staff, the five-year term of a Secretary-General who is not drawn from a major power, and the eight holders of the post recorded in the core account. It teaches collective security and peacekeeping as two different things that are constantly confused: Korea in 1950, made possible only by the absence of the Soviet delegates who had been boycotting the Council since January of that year, and the Uniting for Peace resolution that followed it; Suez in 1956, where a vetoed Council resolution was answered by an Assembly vote of sixty-four to five and a force of five thousand troops from ten countries; Hungary in the same year and Czechoslovakia in 1968, where the same procedures produced nothing at all; the Congo between 1960 and 1964, the most complex operation to that date, which restored some order and brought the organisation close to bankruptcy; and the long containments in Palestine, Kashmir, Cyprus and Lebanon, where ceasefires were obtained and settlements were not, set against the one territorial dispute the organisation helped bring to a close in West New Guinea. It teaches the transformation of the Assembly by decolonisation, the Third World majority of the 1970s, the politicisation charge that followed it, the UNESCO crisis of 1983, the American withdrawal of 1985 and the American return of 2003, and the parallel construction of a Global South bargaining position through the Group of 77, the United Nations Conference on Trade and Development and the demand for a new international economic order. It teaches the non-military work at the depth the subject deserves, because that is where most of the organisation's effort and most of its results are: the Universal Declaration of Human Rights of 1948 and the rights it enumerates, the Declaration of the Rights of the Child of 1959, the reporting system that many states ignore and the naming-and-shaming that is all the system leaves; the International Labour Organization and its Nobel Peace Prize of 1969; the World Health Organization from the Egyptian cholera outbreak of 1947 to the certification of smallpox eradication in 1979 and its declaration by the World Health Assembly in 1980, the drug-resistant malaria of the 1970s and the global AIDS fund of June 2001; the Food and Agriculture Organization and its higher-yielding maize and rice varieties; UNESCO, its literacy work, the International Hydrological Decade and the Florence floods of 1968; UNICEF from its founding in 1946 to the child health revolution of 1983; the Relief and Works Agency of 1950; and the humanitarian coordination office that grew out of the department established in 1991 and took its present form in 1998. It teaches the financial machinery and the financial crisis together, because the two cannot be separated: assessed contributions based on wealth, peacekeeping shares, the ninety-eight members in arrears in 1986, the American withholding and the reform that followed in 1987, and the Secretary-General's warning of August 1993. It teaches the post-Cold-War decade honestly - the impressive reversal of the Iraqi occupation of Kuwait, the settlements in Cambodia and Mozambique, the humiliations in Somalia and Bosnia, and the absence from Rwanda - and it teaches the Iraq crisis of 2003 as the sharpest test of the organisation's legal authority, with the Secretary-General's own account of the fork in the road. It teaches reform as three different arguments that candidates habitually merge: what the Charter itself would have to change, what practice could change without amendment, and what is administrative housekeeping. It closes on the verdict, the answer architecture and the controls that keep a confident answer from becoming a wrong one. Two boundaries are held throughout: the Cold War crises belong to the Cold War owner and are used here only as institutional tests, and India's current negotiating positions on Council reform belong to the Indian foreign-policy and international-relations owners and are not asserted here.

## How to read this session

Work through the lessons in order. The order is not the order of the chapter that supplies most of the evidence, and the difference is deliberate. A chapter describes the structure first, then the peacekeeping record, then the other work, then a verdict. That arrangement produces the standard failing script: an organ list followed by a mission list followed by an opinion. Here the design problem comes first, because an answer that has not decided what the institution was built to do cannot judge whether it did it. The Charter settlement follows, then purposes and membership, because the legal text is the standard against which every later performance is measured. The organs then come in the order in which their powers actually matter: the Assembly, whose votes are almost never binding; the Council, whose votes are; and much later the Court and the Secretariat, whose powers are easy to overstate and easy to miss. Collective security and peacekeeping are separated by two full lessons, because merging them is the most common factual error in the topic. Failure comes immediately after success, in the same decade and often in the same month, because the Suez and Hungary pairing is the cleanest proof of the success condition that this whole session is built around. The Assembly's transformation by decolonisation comes before the non-military work, because the change in who votes explains the change in what the organisation was asked to do. Money comes after mandate, because the funding crisis is a consequence of the widening mandate and not an independent misfortune. Reform comes last among the substantive lessons, because reform arguments are only worth anything once the structure, the record and the money are all in view.

Inside a lesson, take the visual first, then the plain explanation, then the exact evidence, then the objection and the reply, and only then the examination use.

Four labels run through this session, and separating them is the single habit that most improves an answer on this topic. Three of them carry a symbol; the fourth is announced in words.

| Label | Meaning | Use in an answer |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Fact** | Directly supported by a verified source - the two canonical owners held for this topic, the single core world-history account read for this session, the cross-topic owners consulted for boundaries, the verified question ledgers, or a checked item from the one verification pass recorded in the final ledger | Assert it. Dates, articles, procedures, institutions, named individuals and recorded outcomes can be stated flatly |
| ⚠️ **Analytical inference** | Interpretation, weighting, comparison or synthesis built on those facts | Argue it, signal it, and expect a counter-position |
| 📰 **Current linkage** | A dated live-source item, used once in this session and explicitly limited there | Use it as one dated sentence, never as the substance of a history answer |
| **Reform advocacy** | A proposal about what the institution *should* become, announced as such in the text | Attribute it to whoever proposes it; never write it as a description of what exists |

That fourth label has no symbol because it must be visible in the sentence itself. ⚠️ An answer that slides from "the Council has five permanent members" to "the Council should have more" without a marker in between has stopped describing and started campaigning, and an examiner reading at speed will treat the whole paragraph as opinion.

Seven disciplines are specific to this topic. Each exists because this is a part of the syllabus where fluency is common and precision is rare.

**The four-register discipline.** ⚠️ Every claim in this topic belongs to one of four registers, and mixing them is what turns a good script into an average one. The first register is **Charter law**: what the founding treaty says, which is fixed, checkable and usually short. The second is **recorded practice**: what the organisation has actually done, which frequently diverges from the text and sometimes contradicts it. ✅ The clearest example in the whole topic is the abstention rule - the Charter requires the concurring votes of the permanent members, and in practice it has gradually been accepted that abstention by a permanent member does not count as a veto, but this has never been written into the Charter. The third register is **analytical inference**, which is where the marks are earned. The fourth is **reform advocacy**, which is where the marks are lost when it is smuggled in as fact. ⚠️ A script that writes "the Charter provides for a permanent United Nations army" has confused register one with register four, and has made a statement that is simply false.

**The category discipline.** ✅ The organisation is not a single body but a system of legally distinct entities, and examiners test the distinctions rather than the names. A **principal organ** is created by the Charter itself. A **specialized agency** is a separate international organisation with its own membership and constitution, brought into relationship with the United Nations - the World Health Organization, the Food and Agriculture Organization, UNESCO, the International Labour Organization, the International Monetary Fund and the World Bank are in this category. A **fund** or **programme** is created by a resolution of the General Assembly and reports to it - UNICEF is the standard example. ✅ UNRWA is a General Assembly-created agency with its own distinct mandate. ✅ The International Court of Justice is a principal organ; the **International Criminal Court is not**, and it is not a seventh organ of the United Nations, whatever a general textbook list may suggest. ✅ The World Trade Organization belongs to the wider multilateral system but is **not** a United Nations specialized agency. ⚠️ Flattening these into one undifferentiated list is the most reliably penalised error in the structure question.

**The mechanism discipline.** ⚠️ Six operations are routinely called "United Nations intervention" and they are six different things. **Pacific settlement** is mediation, inquiry and good offices with no force at all. **Observation** is unarmed or lightly armed monitoring of a line that both sides have accepted. **Consent-based peacekeeping** interposes a force between parties who have agreed to its presence. **Robust peacekeeping** adds tactical force with Council authorisation while retaining host consent. **Authorised enforcement** is the Council licensing member states to use force against a party that has not consented, and it is conducted by national armies under national command. **Unilateral action** is a state using force without that licence at all. ✅ Korea in 1950 and the Gulf operation of 1991 belong to the fifth category and not the third; ✅ the Suez force of 1956 belongs to the third; ✅ Iraq in 2003 belongs to the sixth. ⚠️ Getting this wrong makes the entire performance assessment incoherent, because the six categories have completely different success conditions.

**The date-and-status discipline.** ✅ The structural facts used in this session are historically stable: six principal organs under the Charter; a fifteen-member Council with five permanent members; the veto; the absence of a standing force; the suspension of the Trusteeship Council; the founding dates and the founding aims. ❌ This session does **not** assert the current state of Council reform negotiations, any current membership-expansion outcome, any current budget or arrears figure, or any current Council composition, because none of those is stable and a history answer does not need them. ⚠️ Where a live institutional development is genuinely relevant, it appears once, dated, in Lesson 17, and it is limited there in the text itself.

**The agency discipline.** ⚠️ "The United Nations failed in Hungary" is a sentence that hides its own subject. The organisation has no army, no revenue of its own and no power to act against a permanent member of the Council; when enforcement is blocked, the accurate sentence names the blocking state. ✅ The core account is explicit about this in case after case: the Russians vetoed, the Russians refused to co-operate, the USSR ignored the organisation, the United States and Britain were determined to attack without authorisation and the organisation could do nothing about it. ⚠️ But the reverse error is equally damaging. In administration, norm-setting, standard-setting, humanitarian coordination and the operation of the specialized agencies, the organisation does act with a will of its own, and treating it as a mere mirror of its members cannot explain why smallpox was eliminated or why a refugee agency built schools and hospitals for two generations. ⚠️ The working rule for this session: **on enforcement, name the state; on service delivery and norms, name the institution.**

**The quantity discipline.** Every figure in this session comes from a permitted source with the measure that source states, and no figure is totalled, averaged, projected or updated. ✅ The permitted set is finite and is used repeatedly: the original eleven Council members with five permanent and six elected, and the enlargement of the non-permanent seats to ten in 1965; the requirement of nine affirmative votes out of fifteen; the five representatives a state may send to the Assembly and its single vote; the six official languages; the twenty-four hours of the Uniting for Peace procedure; the sixty-four votes to five at the Suez session and the force of five thousand troops from ten countries; the sixteen countries whose troops served in Korea and the 38th parallel; the Congo force of over twenty thousand at its largest; the three thousand troops stationed in Cyprus and the Greek share of about eighty per cent of the island's population; the interim force in Lebanon at about seven thousand, then around eight thousand, then some three thousand in 2002, then twelve thousand after 2006; the thirty-point Universal Declaration of 1948; the ECOSOC membership of twenty-seven recorded in the core account and the fifty-four members recorded by the organisation today, a divergence handled openly in Lesson 13; the five regional commissions and the roughly thirty agencies; the more than ninety per cent of annual expenditure devoted to ECOSOC activities by 1980; the fifteen judges of the Court elected for nine-year terms with five retiring every third year; the Secretariat's staff of over fifty thousand as recorded in the core account; the well over thirty peacekeeping operations between 1990 and 2003 and the peak of over eighty thousand troops from seventy-seven countries in the mid-1990s; the thirty-seven thousand troops recorded as arriving in Somalia in December 1992; the ninety-eight members in arrears in 1986 and the American withholding of more than one hundred million dollars; the American, European Community and Japanese shares of about thirty, about thirty-five and eleven per cent that together made three-quarters of the expenses; the ten billion dollars a year targeted by the global AIDS fund of June 2001; the standing brigade-size forces of five thousand troops recommended in 2000; the forty-three members who joined between 1963 and 1968, the 159 of 1985, the 183 of 1993, the 191st member in 2002 and the 193rd in July 2011; the around 190 nations described as attending the world assembly and the around eighty-five thousand peacekeepers recorded as in action in 2012; and the fifty states at San Francisco. ✅ Every other quantity used anywhere in this session - the smaller counts, proportions and dated figures that appear inside a single sentence of evidence - is likewise taken directly from a permitted source with the measure that source states. ❌ No figure is invented, adjusted, totalled or extrapolated. ❌ No current figure for the budget, arrears, mission count, troop contributions, staff numbers or membership of any organ is asserted anywhere in this session.

**The sensitive-material discipline.** ⚠️ Three episodes in this topic involve mass killing: Cambodia under the Khmer Rouge, Bosnia in 1995 and Rwanda in 1994. Each is taught because the institutional question cannot be understood without it, and each is taught through dates, mandates, decisions and named responsibility rather than through description. ✅ Where the core account states a figure - it records that Serb forces murdered around eight thousand Muslim men at Srebrenica after the Council had designated the town a safe area, and that the Khmer Rouge regime slaughtered about a third of the population - the figure is reported as that account's statement and is not adjusted, rounded or supplemented. ⚠️ Responsibility is attributed to governments, armed forces, named commanders, party formations and the specific organs that acted or failed to act, never to nations, peoples or religions.

**The boundary discipline.** Five neighbouring topics own material this session touches constantly, and the handovers are stated in the lessons rather than hidden. The League of Nations record belongs to the interwar international-relations owner; this session uses the League only as the design brief against which the Charter was written. The Second World War belongs to the war owner; this session begins where the wartime conferences turn from strategy to institutional design. The Cold War narratives around Korea, Suez, Hungary, Cuba and détente belong to the Cold War owner; this session uses those crises only as institutional tests, and it does not re-derive their diplomacy. Mass decolonisation belongs to the decolonisation owner; this session takes only the change it produced in the composition and behaviour of the Assembly. The focused post-1991 order belongs to its own owner. ⚠️ And India's live positions on Council reform belong to the Indian foreign-policy and international-relations owners; this session teaches the structural argument that makes reform a serious historical question and stops there.

## Roadmap

| Lesson | Dependency-led question | Stage |
|---:|---|---|
| 1 | What was this institution built to fix - and what were its founders careful not to fix? | Foundation |
| 2 | From Dumbarton Oaks to San Francisco: how was the Charter actually settled, and what was traded for what? | Foundation |
| 3 | Purposes, principles and membership: what does the Charter promise, and what does it deliberately withhold? | Core |
| 4 | The General Assembly: if almost nothing it passes is binding, why does it matter at all? | Core |
| 5 | The Security Council: composition, the veto, and what makes a decision binding | Core |
| 6 | Korea 1950: the one time collective security worked, and the resolution that tried to make it repeatable | Core |
| 7 | Suez 1956 and the invention of peacekeeping: a tool the Charter never mentions | Core |
| 8 | Hungary and Czechoslovakia: the same procedures, the opposite result, and the success condition | Core |
| 9 | West New Guinea, Palestine, Kashmir, Cyprus, Lebanon, Iran-Iraq: settlement once, containment thereafter | Core |
| 10 | The Congo, trusteeship and Namibia: complexity, cost, and the first crisis of solvency and authority | Core |
| 11 | The Assembly transformed: decolonisation, the Third World majority and the politicisation charge | Advanced |
| 12 | The other United Nations: rights, labour, health, food, education, children, refugees and relief | Core |
| 13 | ECOSOC, the financial agencies and the money: coordination, overlap and the arrears crisis | Advanced |
| 14 | The Court and the Secretariat: law without enforcement, administration with a voice | Advanced |
| 15 | After the Cold War: Kuwait, Cambodia, Mozambique, Somalia, Bosnia and the absence from Rwanda | Core |
| 16 | Iraq 2003: authorisation, unilateralism and the fork in the road | Advanced |
| 17 | Reform: Charter amendment, practice, housekeeping - and where India's claim actually sits | Advanced |
| 18 | The verdict and the answer architecture: converting eight decades into marks | Advanced |

⚠️ The eighteen lessons are derived from the dependency audit, not from any source's section order, and they fall into seven groups that account for all eighteen: **three** build the legal and institutional foundation (Lessons 1-3); **two** anatomise the pair of political organs, the Assembly and the Council (Lessons 4-5); **five** follow the Cold War security record from Korea to the Congo (Lessons 6-10); **three** cover the transformed Assembly, the non-military system and the money (Lessons 11-13); **one** treats the judicial and administrative organs (Lesson 14); **one** covers the post-Cold-War decade (Lesson 15); and the last **three** move from the legitimacy crisis to reform and then to marks (Lessons 16-18). ⚠️ 3 + 2 + 5 + 3 + 1 + 1 + 3 = 18.

## Lesson 1 - The design problem: what the institution was built to fix, and what its founders were careful not to fix

Progress: 1/18 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the League as a design brief; the three founding aims; the two layers this topic must be prepared in; what was improved and what was deliberately retained; the difference between an institution failing and its members failing; the boundary map that keeps a Topic 16 answer from becoming a Topic 11, 14, 15 or 18 answer

### Visual: a design brief, and what the designers did and did not accept

```text
   THE BRIEF, WRITTEN BY A FAILURE
   ================================
   The League of Nations had proved INCAPABLE OF RESTRAINING
   AGGRESSIVE DICTATORS. The great powers now tried to ELIMINATE
   SOME - not all - of the weaknesses that had handicapped it.

   DEFECT IN THE LEAGUE          ACCEPTED AND FIXED?        RESULT IN 1945
   --------------------------    ---------------------      -----------------------
   USA never joined              YES - fixed                Both USA and USSR were
                                                            founder-members; a far
                                                            wider world organisation
   Tied to the Versailles order  YES - fixed                Founded on its own
                                                            Charter aims, not on a
                                                            peace treaty
   Unanimity paralysis in the    PARTLY - fixed             Simple and two-thirds
   Assembly                                                 majorities replaced the
                                                            unanimity rule
   Narrow social remit           YES - fixed, and widely    Economic, social,
                                                            educational, scientific
                                                            and cultural progress
                                                            written into the aims;
                                                            individual human rights
                                                            added
   ------------------------------------------------------------------------
   NO LEAGUE ARMY                NO - RETAINED              Still no permanent army
                                                            of its own
   GREAT-POWER BLOCKING POWER    NO - RETAINED AND          The VETO: any one
                                 FORMALISED                 permanent member can
                                                            prevent any action
   ========================================================================
   THE TWO ITEMS THAT WERE NOT FIXED ARE NOT OVERSIGHTS.
   THEY ARE THE PRICE PAID FOR FIXING THE FIRST ITEM ON THE LIST.
```

*⚠️ Read the table downwards and one pattern appears immediately. Four defects were repaired and two were kept. The two that were kept are exactly the two that would have required the great powers to accept restraint on themselves - and the founders had just watched an organisation die because the most powerful state of the age had refused to join it at all. The design logic of 1945 is therefore a trade: universality and great-power presence were bought with great-power immunity. Almost every argument in this topic, including the reform argument eighty years later, is an argument about whether that trade was worth making and whether it can now be renegotiated.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Imagine being asked in 1944 to design a world organisation to keep the peace, knowing that the last attempt had just collapsed into the largest war in history. You would have the failure report in front of you, and it would tell you two contradictory things.

It would tell you, first, that the old organisation was too weak. It had no force of its own, its members would not commit themselves to fight aggressors, and its decision rules let a single objection stop everything. It would tell you, second, that the old organisation was also too demanding. It had asked states to accept obligations they were not willing to accept, and the most powerful state in the world had responded by staying outside altogether.

Those two findings pull in opposite directions. Make the new organisation stronger and the great powers may refuse to join. Make it weaker and it will fail exactly as its predecessor did.

⚠️ What the designers of 1945 did was to refuse the choice. They made the organisation much stronger in the areas where the great powers had no reason to object - economic work, social work, health, education, refugees, human rights, administration, and the sheer breadth of membership - and they left it exactly as weak as before in the one area where the great powers would have had to accept restraint on themselves. That is the whole design, and it explains both halves of the record. ⚠️ The institution has been extraordinarily effective at the things nobody powerful wanted to stop, and almost completely ineffective at the things a permanent member of the Council wanted to do.

### 2. The founding aims, exactly as the sources state them

✅ **Fact:** the organisation officially came into existence in October 1945 after the Second World War. ✅ It was formed to replace the League of Nations, which had proved incapable of restraining aggressive dictators like Hitler and Mussolini. ✅ In setting it up, the great powers tried to eliminate some of the weaknesses which had handicapped the League.

✅ **Fact:** the Charter was drawn up at San Francisco in 1945, and was based on proposals made at an earlier meeting between the USSR, the USA, China and Britain, held at Dumbarton Oaks in the United States in 1944.

✅ **Fact:** the three aims are stated in the sources as follows:

- ✅ to preserve peace and eliminate war;
- ✅ to remove the causes of conflict by encouraging economic, social, educational, scientific and cultural progress throughout the world, especially in under-developed countries;
- ✅ to safeguard the rights of all individual human beings, and the rights of peoples and nations.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice how much of the mandate is not about war. Only the first aim concerns peace directly. The second is a theory of causation - that conflict has economic, social and educational roots and can be reduced by working on them - and the third is a commitment that the League never made at all. ⚠️ Two of the three founding aims, in other words, describe work that has nothing to do with armies, and a script that judges the institution solely on the first aim has judged it against a third of its own constitution.

⚠️ A second feature of the aims repays attention. The second aim contains the phrase "especially in under-developed countries". That clause was written in 1945, when most of the world's population lived under colonial rule and was not represented at San Francisco at all. ⚠️ It became, two decades later, one of the levers by which the newly independent majority reshaped what the organisation actually spent its money on - a story taken up in Lesson 11.

### 3. The two layers this topic must be prepared in

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the only topic in the world-history sequence that sits squarely across two General Studies papers, and preparing it in one layer is the commonest structural mistake candidates make with it.

| Layer | What it is | Which paper it serves | What a script must supply |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Historical origin and record** | The post-1945 settlement, the Charter's construction, the peacekeeping and enforcement record from Korea to Iraq, the transformation by decolonisation | General Studies Paper I, under the world wars and their effects | Dated evidence, named cases, causal explanation and a qualified verdict |
| **Structure and mandate** | The organs, their powers, the legal categories of the system, the coordination machinery and the reform question | General Studies Paper II, under important international institutions, agencies and fora - their structure and mandate | Accurate categories, precise powers, the difference between recommendation and decision, and status discipline |

⚠️ The two layers must not be blended into a single undifferentiated essay. An answer in the first layer that consists of an organ chart has answered a Paper II question in a Paper I slot. An answer in the second layer that narrates Suez and Hungary at length has done the reverse. ⚠️ The correct habit is to prepare both completely and then let the directive of the question decide which one leads and which one supplies one supporting paragraph.

### 4. What changed, what did not, and why the distinction carries marks

✅ **Fact:** the sources set out the improvements over the League precisely.

- ✅ The new organisation spends much more time and resources on economic and social matters, and its scope is much wider than the League's. ✅ All the specialized agencies, with the single exception of the International Labour Organization founded in 1919, were set up in 1945 or later.
- ✅ It is committed to safeguarding individual human rights, which the League did not get involved in.
- ✅ Changes in the procedures of the Assembly and the Council - especially the Uniting for Peace resolution - and the increased power and prestige of the Secretary-General have enabled it, on occasion, to take more decisive action than the League ever achieved.
- ✅ It has a much wider membership and is therefore more of a genuine world organisation than the League, with all the extra prestige that entails. ✅ Both the USA and the USSR were founder-members, whereas the USA never joined the League.

✅ **Fact:** the sources are equally precise about what survived.

- ✅ Any one of the five permanent members of the Council can use its power of veto to prevent decisive action being taken.
- ✅ Like the League, it has no permanent army of its own and has to use forces belonging to its member states.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the two surviving weaknesses are not of the same kind, and an answer that treats them as a pair of complaints has missed the more interesting one. The absence of a standing force is a *capability* gap: the institution cannot act on its own even when everyone agrees it should. The veto is a *permission* gap: the institution may not act even when it could. ⚠️ Capability gaps can be closed by money and administration; permission gaps can only be closed by constitutional change that the holders of the permission must themselves approve. ⚠️ That asymmetry is the reason eighty years of reform effort has produced a great deal of administrative change and almost no change to the Council.

### 5. Institution or members? The question that decides the tone of every answer

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** when a candidate writes "the United Nations failed to stop the invasion of Hungary", the sentence is doing something subtle and usually unintentional. It is treating the organisation as an agent that chose not to act.

✅ **Fact:** the record shows what actually happened. ✅ The Hungarian government appealed; the Russians vetoed a Council resolution calling for a withdrawal of their forces; the Assembly passed the same resolution and set up a committee to investigate; the Russians refused to co-operate with the committee, and no progress could be made.

⚠️ Every step in that sequence was taken. The appeal was heard, the Council met, the Assembly voted, an investigative committee was constituted. What did not happen was compliance. ⚠️ The accurate sentence is therefore not "the organisation failed" but "the organisation had no instrument that could compel a permanent member of the Council, and the state concerned knew it."

⚠️ But the discipline cuts both ways, and the second half is what separates a balanced answer from a cynical one. Consider a different sentence: "the United Nations drove smallpox to extinction, the last naturally occurring case being recorded in 1977." Here the institutional subject is entirely appropriate. ✅ A specialized agency with its own constitution, budget, technical staff and worldwide field presence ran a campaign over decades that no single state could have run and that no state had a strong incentive to block. ⚠️ Treating the organisation as nothing but a mirror of great-power will cannot explain that outcome, and a script that adopts the cynical register throughout will be unable to write the second half of a balanced answer at all.

⚠️ The working rule for this session, stated once here and applied everywhere: **on enforcement, name the state that blocked or acted; on norms, standards, administration and service delivery, name the institution.**

### 6. The boundary map

⚠️ This topic is surrounded by neighbours whose material is constantly within reach, and the most common way to lose marks here is to write a competent answer to the wrong question.

| Neighbour | What it owns | What this session takes from it |
|---|---|---|
| **The interwar international-relations topic** | The League's aims, organs, achievements and the full account of why it failed - Versailles linkage, American absence, late German and Soviet entry, the Conference of Ambassadors, unanimity, Manchuria, Abyssinia | Only the defect list, used as a design brief. This session does not re-narrate the League's collapse |
| **The Second World War topic** | The war itself, its conduct, its turning points and its consequences | Only the point at which wartime conferences turned from strategy to institutional design |
| **The Cold War topic** | The origins, ideology, alliances and crises of the superpower conflict, including Korea, Suez, Cuba, Vietnam and détente as diplomatic episodes | Only the institutional behaviour visible inside those crises - who voted, who vetoed, what was mandated and what followed |
| **The decolonisation topic** | Why empires ended, the independence movements and the post-colonial state | Only the resulting change in membership and in the Assembly's voting behaviour |
| **The post-1991 topic** | The focused transition after the Soviet collapse | Only the changed peacekeeping environment of the 1990s |
| **Indian foreign policy and international relations** | India's current diplomatic positions, its Council campaign and live negotiating alignments | Nothing. This session teaches the structural argument and hands over |

⚠️ One test settles most boundary doubts. Ask: *is the institution the subject of this sentence, or merely its venue?* If the Council is where a Cold War dispute happened, the sentence belongs to the Cold War owner. If the Council's procedure, composition or authority is what the sentence is about, it belongs here.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** the framing of this lesson is too generous. It presents the veto as a deliberate trade rather than as a defect, which is exactly how the permanent members themselves describe it. On this view the organisation was not designed to keep the peace at all; it was designed to legitimise a great-power directorate, and calling that a "design logic" dresses up a power grab as institutional engineering.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection is serious and part of it must be conceded. ✅ The Council's permanent membership was fixed by the distribution of power in 1945, and the sources record that the veto has repeatedly prevented action that the majority of members wanted - on Hungary, on Czechoslovakia, and in the long American blocking of Chinese membership. ⚠️ So the directorate reading describes something real.

⚠️ What the objection cannot explain is the rest of the record. If the institution were merely a legitimating device for the powerful, three things would be inexplicable. ✅ First, the Assembly developed a procedure - Uniting for Peace - explicitly designed to route around a Council veto, and used it many times despite Russian protests. ✅ Second, the powerful have repeatedly found themselves on the losing side: Britain and France were condemned by an Assembly vote of sixty-four to five over Suez and withdrew; the western nations found by the 1970s that they could no longer get their resolutions through the Assembly; and the United States withdrew from UNESCO in 1985 precisely because it could not control it. ✅ Third, more than ninety per cent of annual expenditure by 1980 went to economic and social activities, which is not what a security directorate spends its money on.

⚠️ The defensible formulation is therefore neither celebratory nor dismissive: **the institution was built as a great-power bargain and has been repeatedly used against the intentions of the powers that built it - which is why both the cynical and the idealistic readings can each quote real evidence, and why neither survives contact with the whole record.**

### 8. Examination use

⚠️ This lesson supplies the opening move for every question in the topic, and the whole answer for questions that ask about design.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ How far did the United Nations overcome the weaknesses of the League of Nations?
> ⚠️ "The United Nations was designed to reflect power, not to restrain it." Examine.
> ⚠️ Discuss the historical circumstances in which the United Nations was created and assess how far its structure reflected them.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first framing, the weak script lists League failures and then lists improvements. The upper-band script does something different in its first three sentences: it states that four defects were repaired and two retained, states that the two retained are the price of repairing the first, and then uses the rest of the answer to show that this single trade explains the shape of the record. ⚠️ That structure converts a comparison into an argument, and it can be set up in about forty words.

### Traps

- ⚠️ Writing that the new organisation "corrected the mistakes of the League". ✅ The sources say the great powers tried to eliminate *some* of the weaknesses, and name two that remained.
- ⚠️ Dating the establishment loosely. ✅ The Charter was drawn up at San Francisco in 1945 on the basis of the Dumbarton Oaks proposals of 1944, and the organisation officially came into existence in October 1945.
- ⚠️ Attributing the Dumbarton Oaks proposals to the wrong participants. ✅ The meeting was between the USSR, the USA, China and Britain.
- ⚠️ Reducing the founding aims to peacekeeping. ✅ Two of the three aims concern economic, social, educational, scientific and cultural progress and the rights of individuals, peoples and nations.
- ⚠️ Treating the absence of a standing force and the veto as the same complaint. ⚠️ One is a capability gap, the other a permission gap, and only the first can be closed administratively.
- ⚠️ Writing "the United Nations failed" where the evidence names a state that blocked. ⚠️ Name the blocking state on enforcement questions.
- ⚠️ Importing the League's full failure narrative. ⚠️ That belongs to the interwar owner; only the defect list is needed here.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Came into existence officially in **October 1945**, after the Second World War, to replace a League that had proved incapable of restraining aggressive dictators.
2. ✅ Charter drawn up at **San Francisco in 1945**, based on proposals from **Dumbarton Oaks, 1944**, a meeting of the **USSR, the USA, China and Britain**.
3. ✅ Three aims: preserve peace and eliminate war; remove causes of conflict through economic, social, educational, scientific and cultural progress, especially in under-developed countries; safeguard the rights of individuals, peoples and nations.
4. ✅ Four League defects repaired: American absence, treaty linkage, unanimity paralysis, narrow social remit.
5. ✅ Two retained: **no permanent army of its own**, and the **veto** of any one permanent member.
6. ✅ All specialized agencies except the ILO, founded in 1919, were set up in 1945 or later.
7. ✅ Both the USA and the USSR were founder-members; the USA never joined the League.
8. ⚠️ Design logic: universality and great-power presence were purchased with great-power immunity.
9. ⚠️ Capability gap versus permission gap - the distinction that explains why reform has been administrative rather than constitutional.
10. ⚠️ Two-layer preparation: historical origin and record for Paper I; structure and mandate for Paper II.
11. ⚠️ Agency rule: on enforcement name the state, on norms and service delivery name the institution.
12. ⚠️ Boundary test: is the institution the subject of the sentence, or only its venue?

### Concept check

**Question:** The founders of 1945 had a full list of the League's defects in front of them and repaired only some. Identify the two they did not repair, explain why those two were left in place, and state what this implies about which kinds of reform have since been possible.

**Model answer:** The two retained defects were the absence of a permanent force of the organisation's own and the power of any one of the five permanent members of the Council to veto a decision. They were retained because the single largest failure of the League was the absence of the United States, and the founders' overriding objective was to have every great power inside the new organisation rather than outside it. A body with its own army and no great-power veto would have been a body capable of coercing its most powerful members, and those members would not have joined it - so universality was purchased with immunity. The implication for reform is that the two surviving defects are of different types. The missing force is a capability gap, which can in principle be narrowed by money, standby arrangements and administrative planning, which is why proposals of that kind have repeatedly been made and partly implemented. The veto is a permission gap, and closing it would require amending the Charter with the consent of precisely those states whose permission it protects. That is why eighty years of reform effort has produced extensive administrative and operational change and no change at all to the composition or voting rule of the Council.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the veto as a drafting error that the founders failed to notice. It was a condition of the organisation's existence, not an oversight - the sources record both superpowers as founder-members, which was the whole point, and the same sources record the veto as an unrepaired weakness. An answer that calls it a mistake cannot explain why the states that hold it also created and funded everything else in the system.

## Lesson 2 - From Dumbarton Oaks to San Francisco: how the Charter was settled, and what was traded for what

Progress: 2/18 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the wartime origins of the design; the Dumbarton Oaks proposals of 1944 and the four participants; the San Francisco conference of 25 April to 26 June 1945 and the fifty states present; signature on 26 June 1945 and entry into force on 24 October 1945; the fifty-one original members; the ratification condition; the Charter as a treaty and what that implies; the three amendments; the annexed Statute of the Court

### Visual: the construction sequence, with the decision taken at each stage

```text
   1944  DUMBARTON OAKS (Washington, USA)
         Participants: USSR · USA · CHINA · BRITAIN
         Output: the PROPOSALS on which the Charter was based
         Settled: that there would be an Assembly, a Council with
                  permanent members, a Secretariat, a Court and an
                  economic and social body
         NOT settled: the exact voting rule in the Council
                                |
                                v
   1945  THE VOTING FORMULA agreed among the great powers before
         the drafting conference met
         Settled: permanent members would each hold a blocking vote
                                |
                                v
   25 APRIL - 26 JUNE 1945   SAN FRANCISCO
         United Nations Conference on International Organization
         FIFTY countries attend, draft and then SIGN the Charter
         Signature date: 26 JUNE 1945
                                |
                                v
   RATIFICATION CONDITION
         Entry into force required ratification by CHINA, FRANCE,
         the SOVIET UNION, the UNITED KINGDOM and the UNITED STATES
         AND by a majority of the other signatories
                                |
                                v
   24 OCTOBER 1945   THE CHARTER COMES INTO FORCE
         The organisation officially begins
         ORIGINAL MEMBERS: 51
         (the fifty at San Francisco, plus Poland, which signed later)
                                |
                                v
   AMENDED ONLY IN THE 1960s AND EARLY 1970s
     Arts 23, 27, 61  adopted 1963, in force 1965
     Art 109          adopted 1965, in force 1968
     Art 61 again     adopted 1971, in force 1973
   EVERY AMENDMENT ENLARGED AN ORGAN OR RAISED A VOTE THRESHOLD.
   NONE TOUCHED THE PERMANENT MEMBERS OR THE VETO.
   The STATUTE OF THE INTERNATIONAL COURT OF JUSTICE is ANNEXED
   to the Charter and forms an INTEGRAL PART of it
```

*⚠️ The sequence is worth learning as a sequence rather than as four dates, because each stage settled something the previous stage had left open. The 1944 meeting settled the architecture; the great-power formula settled the voting; the 1945 conference settled the text; and ratification settled the timing. ⚠️ Notice where the fifty states at San Francisco entered the process: after the architecture and the voting rule had already been agreed among four powers. That sequencing is the strongest single piece of evidence for the claim that the organisation was built around a great-power bargain, and it is far more persuasive in an answer than any adjective.*

### 1. Why the design work happened during the war and not after it

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the most important structural fact about the Charter is one that candidates rarely state - it was written while the war was still being fought, by the states that were winning it, and it entered into force six weeks after the fighting stopped.

⚠️ That timing has three consequences that run through the entire topic.

⚠️ First, the design was made by a coalition that was still cooperating. The wartime alliance held long enough to produce an institution; it did not hold long enough to operate one as intended. ✅ The sources record the result plainly: despite the careful framing of the Charter, the organisation was unable to solve many of the problems of international relations, particularly those caused by the Cold War. ⚠️ The machinery was built for a world in which the great powers agreed, and it was delivered into a world in which two of them did not.

⚠️ Second, the design was made before the membership existed. Most of Asia and almost all of Africa were under colonial rule in 1945 and were not represented. ⚠️ The organisation that exists today therefore operates under a constitution written by fifty states for a membership that reached 193 - and Lesson 11 shows what that mismatch produced.

⚠️ Third, the design was made in the memory of a specific failure and not of failure in general. The founders were solving the League's problem, not every possible problem. ⚠️ That is why the Charter is meticulous about aggression between states, which was the 1930s problem, and comparatively silent about civil war within states, which became the dominant problem after 1990 - a gap taken up in Lesson 15.

### 2. Dumbarton Oaks, 1944: the architecture

✅ **Fact:** the Charter was based on proposals made at a meeting between the **USSR, the USA, China and Britain**, held at **Dumbarton Oaks** in the United States in **1944**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the participant list is itself examinable, and for a reason. Four powers met; France was not among them, because in 1944 its government had only recently been restored and its status was unsettled; yet France appears as a permanent member of the Council in the finished text. ⚠️ The gap between the four who designed and the five who received permanent seats is a small but precise illustration of how the settlement worked: permanent membership was allocated by an assessment of future importance made by the states doing the allocating.

⚠️ What Dumbarton Oaks settled was the shape: a general assembly of all members, a council with permanent members carrying the primary responsibility for security, a secretariat, a court, and a body for economic and social work. ⚠️ What it did not settle was the voting rule inside the council, which was the single most contentious question and which was agreed separately among the great powers before the drafting conference opened. ⚠️ A candidate who remembers only that the Charter was "framed at San Francisco" has lost the more interesting fact - that the hardest question had already been closed before the conference met.

### 3. San Francisco, 1945: the drafting and the signature

✅ **Fact:** representatives of **fifty countries** gathered at the United Nations Conference on International Organization in San Francisco from **25 April to 26 June 1945**, and over those two months drafted and then signed the Charter.

✅ **Fact:** the Charter was **signed on 26 June 1945** at the conclusion of that conference, and **came into force on 24 October 1945**.

✅ **Fact:** it came into existence on that date after its Charter had been ratified by **China, France, the Soviet Union, the United Kingdom and the United States** and by a **majority of the other signatories**.

✅ **Fact:** the organisation's own record of the period states that the founders hoped the new body would prevent another world war like the one they had just lived through.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the ratification condition deserves a moment. It required the five permanent members *individually*, and then merely a majority of everyone else. ⚠️ That is the veto appearing before the organisation even existed - written into the conditions of its own birth. ⚠️ An answer that wants to argue that the veto is structural rather than incidental can make the point with this single fact and then move on.

⚠️ There is also a useful correction of a very common error buried in these dates. The signature and the entry into force are six weeks and four months apart respectively, and they are different events. ❌ The organisation was not founded on 26 June 1945; the text was signed then. ✅ It officially came into existence in October 1945, and the precise date on which the Charter entered into force is 24 October 1945.

### 4. The original membership, and why fifty at the conference became fifty-one members

✅ **Fact:** fifty countries were represented at San Francisco. ✅ The organisation had **fifty-one original members**, Poland having signed the Charter after the conference although it was not represented there.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the discrepancy between fifty and fifty-one is a favourite of close-option objective questions, and the correct handling is to state both numbers with their different referents: fifty *at the conference*, fifty-one *original members*. ⚠️ A script that gives one number without the referent is exposed to whichever framing the examiner chose.

⚠️ It is worth pausing on how small fifty-one is. ✅ The League never had more than about fifty members and was criticised as unrepresentative; ✅ the new organisation began at essentially the same size and reached 193. ⚠️ The claim that it is "a far wider world organisation than the League" is therefore a claim about what it *became*, not about what it was in 1945, and the difference was produced almost entirely by decolonisation.

### 5. The Charter as a treaty: what that status actually does

✅ **Fact:** the Charter is the founding document of the organisation and is considered an international treaty; it is therefore an instrument of international law, and member states are bound by it.

✅ **Fact:** it codifies major principles of international relations, from the sovereign equality of states to the prohibition of the use of force in international relations.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the treaty status has one consequence that carries most of the analytical weight in this topic. A treaty binds the parties that have accepted it, and it can only be changed by a procedure the parties have accepted. ⚠️ The organisation therefore cannot reform itself in the way a national government can amend an administrative rule. ⚠️ Every genuinely constitutional change - composition of the Council, the voting rule, the status of an organ - requires the amendment procedure, and the amendment procedure requires ratification by the permanent members. Lesson 17 develops the consequence; the point to fix here is that it follows from the Charter's legal nature and not from anyone's obstinacy.

✅ **Fact:** the amendment record is short and it is worth stating precisely, because the summary version and the detailed version of it do not quite agree.

| Amendment | Adopted | In force | What it did |
|---|---|---|---|
| ✅ Articles 23, 27 and 61 | 17 December 1963 | 31 August 1965 | Enlarged the Security Council from eleven to fifteen; raised the affirmative-vote requirement from seven to nine; enlarged ECOSOC from eighteen to twenty-seven |
| ✅ Article 109 | 20 December 1965 | 12 June 1968 | Raised from seven to nine the number of Council votes needed to fix a Charter review conference |
| ✅ Article 61, again | 20 December 1971 | 24 September 1973 | Enlarged ECOSOC from twenty-seven to fifty-four |

⚠️ **Discrepancy, stated rather than smoothed.** ✅ The organisation's own summary description says the Charter has been amended three times, in 1963, 1965 and 1973, while the explanatory note attached to the Charter text records four adopted amendments with entry-into-force dates of 1965, 1968 and 1973. ⚠️ The two statements can be reconciled - the summary appears to count adoption years and to treat the two ECOSOC enlargements as one line of change - but this session does not assert the reconciliation. ⚠️ The safe formulation for an answer is: **the Charter has been amended only a handful of times, in the 1960s and early 1970s, and every amendment enlarged an organ or adjusted a vote threshold.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that is an extremely low amendment rate for the constitution of a body whose membership almost quadrupled, and the content of the amendments matters more than the count. ⚠️ Every one of them concerned the *size* of an organ or the arithmetic of a threshold. ❌ Not one redistributed power: no amendment has ever touched the identity of the permanent members or their blocking vote. ⚠️ The Charter, in other words, has proved amendable when the change did not touch the bargain and unamendable when it did. ⚠️ That single sentence is worth more in a reform answer than a page of proposals.

✅ **Fact:** the amendment procedure itself explains why. ✅ Under Article 108, an amendment comes into force when it has been adopted by a two-thirds vote of the General Assembly **and** ratified by two-thirds of the members **including all the permanent members of the Security Council**. ✅ Article 109 applies the same permanent-member ratification requirement to any alteration recommended by a general review conference.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the precise legal reason the veto cannot be removed by outvoting its holders. ⚠️ A candidate who writes that "reform requires a two-thirds majority in the General Assembly" has stated a third of the rule and has missed the operative clause.

✅ **Fact:** the Statute of the International Court of Justice is **annexed to the Charter and forms an integral part of it**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the technical reason the Court is a principal organ and the International Criminal Court is not. The judicial organ's constituting instrument is part of the Charter itself; the criminal court's constituting instrument is a separate treaty with its own parties. ⚠️ Candidates who have learned the distinction as a list often cannot say *why* it holds; this is why.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** spending a lesson on drafting history is antiquarian. Examiners ask about structure, performance and reform, not about who met where in 1944. The dates are worth two marks at most.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection is right about the dates and wrong about the lesson. ⚠️ Three of the most heavily weighted arguments in the topic are *derived* from this history and cannot be made without it.

⚠️ First, the claim that the veto is structural rather than accidental rests on the ratification condition and on the fact that the voting formula was settled among the great powers before the fifty-state conference convened. ⚠️ Second, the claim that the organisation's composition of authority has fallen out of step with its composition of membership rests on the contrast between fifty states in 1945 and 193 today. ⚠️ Third, the claim that constitutional reform is blocked while administrative reform is easy rests on the treaty status and on the record of three amendments, all of them about the size of organs.

⚠️ So the defensible formulation is: **the drafting history is not background to the argument; it is the evidence for it.** ⚠️ A script that can deploy the 1944-to-1945 sequence in two sentences has purchased three analytical claims at the price of one.

### 7. Examination use

⚠️ This lesson supplies the dated spine for any origin question and the evidentiary base for the structural argument in Lesson 17.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Trace the circumstances in which the Charter of the United Nations was framed, and examine how far its provisions reflected the distribution of power in 1945.
> ⚠️ "The United Nations was a wartime creation and continues to bear the marks of its origins." Comment.
> ⚠️ Explain the process by which the United Nations came into existence.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the second framing, the low-band script narrates the conferences. The upper-band script identifies three specific marks of origin and evidences each: a Council whose permanent membership was fixed by an assessment made in 1944-45; a Charter meticulous about inter-state aggression and comparatively silent on internal conflict, because the 1930s were the problem being solved; and an amendment rule that has permitted three enlargements and no redistribution. ⚠️ Each mark takes one sentence of evidence, so the whole argument fits comfortably inside a 150-word answer.

### Traps

- ❌ Writing that the organisation was founded on 26 June 1945. ✅ The Charter was signed on that date; it came into force on **24 October 1945**, and the organisation officially came into existence in October 1945.
- ❌ Naming France among the Dumbarton Oaks participants. ✅ The four were the **USSR, the USA, China and Britain**.
- ❌ Giving fifty-one as the number of states at San Francisco. ✅ **Fifty** countries were represented; there were **fifty-one original members**.
- ❌ Saying the Charter has never been amended. ✅ It has been amended a handful of times in the 1960s and early 1970s - Council eleven to fifteen, ECOSOC eighteen to twenty-seven and then to fifty-four, and vote thresholds from seven to nine - and never in a way that redistributed power.
- ❌ Stating that Charter amendment needs only a two-thirds Assembly majority. ✅ **Article 108** also requires ratification by two-thirds of members **including all the permanent members of the Security Council**.
- ❌ Treating the Statute of the Court as a separate treaty like the Rome Statute. ✅ It is **annexed to the Charter and forms an integral part of it**.
- ⚠️ Describing San Francisco as the moment the voting rule was decided. ⚠️ The great-power formula was settled before the conference; San Francisco drafted and signed the text.
- ⚠️ Calling the ratification condition a formality. ⚠️ It required the five permanent members individually and only a majority of the rest.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **1944 - Dumbarton Oaks**, United States: USSR, USA, China and Britain produce the proposals on which the Charter was based.
2. ✅ The great-power voting formula was settled separately, before the drafting conference met.
3. ✅ **25 April to 26 June 1945 - San Francisco**, the United Nations Conference on International Organization; **fifty countries** drafted and signed.
4. ✅ Charter **signed 26 June 1945**; **in force 24 October 1945**; organisation officially in existence from October 1945.
5. ✅ Entry into force required ratification by **China, France, the Soviet Union, the United Kingdom and the United States**, plus a **majority of other signatories**.
6. ✅ **Fifty-one original members** - the fifty at the conference plus Poland, which signed later.
7. ✅ The Charter is an **international treaty**; members are bound by it; it codifies sovereign equality and the prohibition of the use of force.
8. ✅ **Amendments:** Articles 23, 27 and 61 adopted 1963 and in force 1965 (Council eleven to fifteen; votes seven to nine; ECOSOC eighteen to twenty-seven); Article 109 adopted 1965, in force 1968; Article 61 again adopted 1971, in force 1973 (ECOSOC twenty-seven to fifty-four). ⚠️ Summary descriptions saying "three times, in 1963, 1965 and 1973" count adoption years - the divergence is recorded, not reconciled. ✅ **Article 108** requires a two-thirds Assembly vote **and** ratification by two-thirds of members **including all five permanent members** - the legal reason the veto cannot be outvoted.
9. ✅ The **Statute of the International Court of Justice is annexed** to the Charter and forms an integral part of it - the technical reason the Court is a principal organ.
10. ⚠️ Three marks of origin: permanent membership fixed by a 1944-45 assessment; a text built for inter-state aggression; an amendment rule that permits enlargement but not redistribution.
11. ⚠️ Fifty states wrote a constitution now operated by 193 - the mismatch that powers every reform argument.
12. ⚠️ The machinery was built for great-power agreement and delivered into great-power conflict; the sources attribute the early failures to the Cold War specifically.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using only the construction sequence of 1944-45, build the strongest available case that the veto is a structural feature of the organisation rather than an incidental defect - and then state the one thing this case does not establish.

**Model answer:** Three facts from the construction sequence carry the case. First, the architecture was settled at Dumbarton Oaks in 1944 by four powers - the USSR, the USA, China and Britain - and the fifty states that drafted the text at San Francisco between 25 April and 26 June 1945 entered a process whose hardest question, the Council voting rule, had already been closed among the great powers. Second, the Charter's own entry into force was made conditional on individual ratification by China, France, the Soviet Union, the United Kingdom and the United States, together with a mere majority of all other signatories - so the asymmetry between five states and everyone else is written into the conditions of the organisation's birth and not merely into one of its articles. Third, the Charter is a treaty, so it can only be altered by a procedure requiring the consent of those same five, and the record of amendment - Council eleven to fifteen, ECOSOC eighteen to twenty-seven and then to fifty-four, vote thresholds seven to nine, and nothing at all touching the identity of the permanent members or their blocking vote - shows the practical effect. What this case does not establish is that the veto was a good arrangement. It establishes only that it was a condition of the organisation existing at all, which is a claim about necessity and not about merit - and an answer that wants to criticise the veto must therefore argue that the bargain has outlived its justification rather than that it was never a bargain.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating San Francisco as the place where the Charter's contents were decided. The conference drafted, refined and signed a text whose essential architecture came from Dumbarton Oaks in 1944 and whose voting rule had been agreed among the great powers beforehand. An answer that presents fifty states negotiating the security structure from scratch produces a far weaker account of why that structure favours five of them.
