### Visual: a chain of accidents, and the rule written to replace the accident

```text
   JANUARY 1950
   The USSR begins BOYCOTTING Security Council meetings
   Reason: the failure to allow communist China to join
   (see Lesson 3 - the American veto of the PRC application)
                    |
                    v
   JUNE 1950   SOUTH KOREA IS INVADED BY COMMUNIST NORTH KOREA
                    |
                    +--> the US government had ALREADY DECIDED to
                    |    intervene with force THE DAY BEFORE the
                    |    Security Council decision was taken
                    v
   THE SECURITY COUNCIL IMMEDIATELY PASSES A RESOLUTION
     - condemning North Korea
     - CALLING ON MEMBER STATES TO SEND HELP TO THE SOUTH
   Possible ONLY because the Russian delegates were ABSENT and
   could not veto it
                    |
                    v
   The Russian delegates RETURN SMARTLY - TOO LATE
                    |
                    v
   TROOPS OF SIXTEEN COUNTRIES repel the invasion
   The frontier along the 38TH PARALLEL is preserved
   Commander-in-Chief: GENERAL MacARTHUR (American)
   Vast majority of troops: AMERICAN
                    |
          +---------+---------+
          v                   v
   RESULT 1                RESULT 2
   'UNITING FOR PEACE'     Bitter Russian attack on
   General Assembly         Secretary-General TRYGVE LIE
   resolution 377 A (V),    for his role in the crisis;
   3 November 1950          his position becomes impossible;
     If the Council is      he retires early and is replaced
     vetoed, the Assembly   by DAG HAMMARSKJOLD
     may MEET WITHIN 24
     HOURS and decide what
     action to take, EVEN
     MILITARY INTERVENTION
     Decision needs a
     TWO-THIRDS MAJORITY
     NOT added to the Charter
     USSR always maintained a Council veto should take
     precedence; the Assembly acted this way MANY TIMES anyway
   ================================================================
   THE LESSON THE ORGANISATION DREW: never depend on an empty chair.
   ================================================================
```

*⚠️ The diagram is drawn as a chain because that is what it is. A membership dispute produced a boycott; the boycott produced an empty chair; the empty chair produced the only full collective-security action of the Cold War; and the action produced a procedural innovation designed to make the outcome reproducible without the accident. ⚠️ An answer that narrates Korea as a war has missed all four links. An answer that narrates it as a chain has explained why collective security worked once and then did not work again for forty years.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

The Charter promised collective security: if a state commits aggression, the organisation acts against it. Lesson 5 explained why that promise is conditional on the permanent members agreeing. The obvious question follows - has the promise ever actually been kept?

✅ The answer given by the core account is precise and it is almost a single case. ✅ Korea was **the only occasion on which decisive action was taken in a crisis directly involving the interests of one of the superpowers**.

⚠️ That sentence should be read twice. It does not say Korea was the only success; the peacekeeping record has many. ⚠️ It says Korea was the only time the organisation acted decisively where a superpower's interests were *directly* engaged. ⚠️ Everywhere else, the pattern established in Lesson 8 holds: where the great powers collide, the organisation is a spectator.

⚠️ And the reason Korea was the exception is almost embarrassing. ⚠️ It happened because one of the two superpowers was not in the room.

### 2. The invasion and the response, exactly

✅ **Fact:** when South Korea was invaded by communist North Korea in **June 1950**, the Security Council **immediately passed a resolution condemning North Korea, and called on member states to send help to the South**.

✅ **Fact:** this was possible **only because of the temporary absence of the Russian delegates**, who would have vetoed the resolution if they had not been **boycotting Security Council meetings since January of that year in protest at the failure to allow communist China to join** the organisation.

✅ **Fact:** although the Russian delegates **returned smartly, it was too late for them to prevent action going ahead**.

✅ **Fact:** **troops of sixteen countries** were able to repel the invasion and **preserve the frontier between the two Koreas along the 38th parallel**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the causal chain from Lesson 3 to this moment is one of the most satisfying in the syllabus and is worth stating explicitly in an answer. ⚠️ The United States vetoed the People's Republic of China's membership application in 1950. The Soviet Union protested by boycotting the Council. The boycott left the Soviet seat empty in June. The empty seat allowed a resolution that the Soviet Union would certainly have vetoed. ⚠️ So an American veto exercised over membership produced, within months, the one enforcement action of the Cold War - against the interests of the state whose protest had made it possible. ⚠️ Institutions are full of such loops, and examiners reward candidates who can trace one.

### 3. Whose operation was it?

✅ **Fact:** though this was claimed by the West as a great success for the organisation, it was **in fact very much an American operation** - the **vast majority of troops and the Commander-in-Chief, General MacArthur, were American**, and the **US government had already decided to intervene with force the day before the Security Council decision was taken**.

✅ **Fact:** **only the absence of the Russians enabled the USA to turn it into a United Nations operation.** ✅ This was **a situation not likely to be repeated**, since the USSR would take good care to be present at all future Council sessions.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the sequencing point - the American decision preceding the Council's - is the single most important evidential detail in this lesson, and it supports two opposite arguments, which is why it is so useful.

⚠️ Used critically, it shows that the Council did not *cause* the intervention; it *authorised* an intervention that was going to happen anyway, and thereby converted a national policy into a collective one. ⚠️ On this reading the organisation supplied legitimacy rather than decision, which is exactly the charge made against it by critics of great-power dominance.

⚠️ Used constructively, it shows something the same critics tend to overlook: that a great power *wanted* the authorisation. ⚠️ The United States could have acted alone and chose to act through the Council when the opportunity presented itself. ⚠️ That tells us the authorisation was worth something - legally, diplomatically and in terms of the contributions of fifteen other states. ⚠️ An organisation whose approval the strongest state in the world troubles to obtain is not a nullity.

⚠️ The balanced formulation: **Korea shows the organisation at its most and least impressive simultaneously - capable of mobilising sixteen national contingents behind a legal determination, and capable of doing so only when a superpower's policy and an accident of procedure happened to coincide.**

### 4. Enforcement is not peacekeeping: the distinction fixed here

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the point at which the mechanism discipline from the session opening must be applied rigorously, because Korea is the case most often misdescribed.

| | **Korea 1950 - authorised enforcement** | **Suez 1956 - consent-based peacekeeping** |
|---|---|---|
| Legal basis | ✅ A Council resolution condemning the aggressor and calling on members to send help | ✅ An Assembly decision after a Council resolution had been vetoed |
| Consent of the parties | ⚠️ Irrelevant - North Korea was the target | ✅ Essential - the force entered on the basis that the belligerents would withdraw |
| Purpose | ⚠️ To defeat an aggressor and restore a frontier | ⚠️ To interpose between parties and supervise a settlement |
| Command | ✅ American Commander-in-Chief, MacArthur | ⚠️ A force assembled by the organisation from ten contributing countries |
| Composition | ✅ Troops of sixteen countries, vast majority American | ✅ Five thousand troops from ten different countries |
| Repeatable? | ✅ Explicitly **not** - it depended on Soviet absence | ⚠️ Yes - and it became the model for the next sixty years |

⚠️ Two errors follow from confusing them, and both are fatal to an answer. ⚠️ The first is factual: describing Korea as a blue-helmet operation. ⚠️ The second is analytical: judging the organisation's peacekeeping record by Korea's standard, and concluding that it has "failed" whenever it did not defeat somebody. ⚠️ Consent-based peacekeeping is not trying to defeat anyone; measuring it against enforcement is like judging a referee by whether he scores.

### 5. Uniting for Peace: an attempt to make the exception into a rule

✅ **Fact:** the Korean War had important results for the future of the organisation. ✅ One was the passing of the **Uniting for Peace resolution**, which would permit a Security Council veto to be bypassed by a General Assembly vote.

✅ **Fact:** in order to secure some action in case of a veto by one of the permanent members, the Assembly - **at the time of the Korean War in 1950** - introduced the resolution; it stated that **if the Council's proposals were vetoed, the Assembly could meet within twenty-four hours and decide what action to take, even military intervention if necessary**. ✅ In such cases a decision by the Assembly would only need a **two-thirds majority**.

✅ **Fact:** the resolution is General Assembly resolution **377 A (V)**, adopted on **3 November 1950**.

✅ **Fact:** this new rule **was not added to the Charter**, and the **USSR, which used the veto more often than any other member, always maintained that a Security Council veto should take precedence over a General Assembly decision**. ✅ **Nevertheless, the Assembly acted in this way many times, ignoring Russian protests.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Soviet objection was not merely obstructive; it had a real textual basis, and an answer that states it accurately will look much better informed than one that dismisses it.

⚠️ The basis is Article 12, met in Lesson 4: while the Council is exercising its Charter functions in respect of a dispute, the Assembly **shall not make any recommendation** with regard to that dispute unless the Council so requests. ⚠️ Add Article 24's conferral of *primary* responsibility on the Council, and the Soviet position becomes arguable - the Charter assigns security to one organ and expressly restrains the other while the first is seised of the matter.

⚠️ The counter-argument, equally textual, is that a vetoed resolution means the Council has **failed to exercise** its function, and that the Assembly's general power of discussion and recommendation under Articles 10, 11 and 14 revives. ⚠️ And crucially, what the Assembly produces even under Uniting for Peace remains a **recommendation**; it cannot manufacture the binding quality of an Article 25 decision out of a procedural device.

⚠️ That last point resolves the whole dispute in the way an examiner will reward. ⚠️ **Uniting for Peace does not transfer the Council's power to the Assembly; it transfers the Council's agenda.** ⚠️ The Assembly can meet, debate, condemn and recommend action including military action, and states remain legally free to decline. ⚠️ What it produces is political pressure with an unmistakable numerical form - which, as Lesson 7 shows at Suez, was quite enough against states that needed legitimacy, and, as Lesson 8 shows in Hungary, quite useless against a state that did not.

### 6. The cost to the Secretariat, and the first lesson about the Secretary-General

✅ **Fact:** another result of the Korean War was **the launching of a bitter attack by the Russians on Secretary-General Trygve Lie for what they considered to be his biased role in the crisis**. ✅ His position soon became impossible and **he eventually agreed to retire early, to be replaced by Dag Hammarskjold**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the first appearance of a pattern that recurs throughout the topic and is developed fully in Lesson 14. ⚠️ The Secretary-General is required by the Charter to be an international official who neither seeks nor receives instructions from any government, and is simultaneously appointed by the Assembly **on the recommendation of the Security Council** - which means each permanent member holds a veto over the appointment and, effectively, over any renewal.

⚠️ The structural consequence is severe: an office designed for independence is filled by a procedure that rewards acceptability to the five. ⚠️ Lie's fate in 1950 is the earliest demonstration; Lesson 14 supplies later ones running in the opposite political direction. ⚠️ The pattern is not partisan - it is procedural, and it has operated against holders of the office who displeased the East and against holders who displeased the West.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** treating Korea as a triumph of collective security is untenable. A resolution passed because one party was absent is procedurally exploitative; a war commanded by an American general with mostly American troops, decided upon in Washington a day before the Council met, is an American war with an international flag. The honest conclusion is that collective security has never operated at all.

⚠️ **Reply:** most of this must be conceded, and conceding it is what makes the remaining defence credible. ✅ The absence was the enabling condition; ✅ the operation was very much an American one; ✅ the decision to intervene preceded the Council's; ✅ and the situation was recognised at once as one not likely to be repeated.

⚠️ Three things nonetheless survive the concession. ⚠️ First, sixteen countries sent troops. ⚠️ Whatever the origin of the decision, fifteen states other than the United States accepted a legal determination as a reason to commit forces, which is a real instance of collective action and not a fiction. ⚠️ Second, the outcome matched the mandate: the invasion was repelled and the frontier along the 38th parallel was preserved. ⚠️ Collective security promises restoration of the status quo against aggression, and that is what occurred. ⚠️ Third, the organisation's own response to the accident was to try to institutionalise it, within months, through Uniting for Peace - which is the behaviour of an institution taking its mandate seriously, not of a fig leaf.

⚠️ The defensible formulation: **Korea was not collective security functioning as designed; it was collective security functioning by accident - and the most instructive thing about it is that the organisation immediately tried to build a rule that would remove the need for the accident, and only partly succeeded.**

### 8. Examination use

⚠️ This lesson is the anchor case for every question about collective security, the veto and the Assembly's security role.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "The United Nations has succeeded in resolving conflict only when a superpower has supported it." How far do you agree?
> ⚠️ Examine the significance of the Korean War for the development of the United Nations.
> ⚠️ Assess the Uniting for Peace resolution as a device for overcoming the Security Council veto.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first framing, Korea is the strongest evidence *for* the proposition and must be conceded early rather than argued around; the answer is then won in the second half, by showing the categories of success that did not depend on superpower support - consent-based peacekeeping between smaller states, ceasefire supervision, and the entire non-military record of Lesson 12. ⚠️ For the third framing, the decisive analytical move is the one made in section 5: the resolution transfers the agenda, not the power, because an Assembly recommendation remains a recommendation.

### Traps

- ❌ Calling the Korean operation a peacekeeping mission. ✅ It was **authorised enforcement**: a resolution condemning the aggressor and calling on members to send help, executed by national forces under an American commander.
- ❌ Saying the USSR was outvoted. ✅ The USSR was **absent**, boycotting since January 1950 over the exclusion of communist China, and returned too late.
- ❌ Attributing the boycott to the Korean issue. ✅ The boycott began in **January 1950**, months before the invasion, and its cause was the Chinese membership question.
- ❌ Omitting that the American decision preceded the Council's. ✅ The US government had decided to intervene with force **the day before** the Council decision.
- ❌ Getting the contributor count wrong. ✅ **Sixteen countries** sent troops; the frontier preserved was the **38th parallel**.
- ❌ Saying Uniting for Peace amended the Charter. ✅ It was **not added to the Charter**; it is **General Assembly resolution 377 A (V) of 3 November 1950**.
- ❌ Treating Uniting for Peace as giving the Assembly binding power. ⚠️ Assembly output remains a **recommendation**, even under this procedure.
- ⚠️ Ignoring the Soviet legal objection. ✅ The USSR always maintained a Council veto should take precedence, and Articles 12 and 24 give that position a textual basis.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Korea was **the only occasion** on which decisive action was taken in a crisis **directly involving the interests of one of the superpowers**.
2. ✅ **June 1950:** South Korea invaded by communist North Korea; the Council **immediately** passed a resolution condemning the North and calling on members to send help to the South.
3. ✅ Possible only because the Russian delegates were **absent**, boycotting since **January 1950** over the failure to admit communist China; they returned smartly but too late.
4. ✅ **Sixteen countries** sent troops; the invasion was repelled and the frontier along the **38th parallel** preserved.
5. ✅ Very much an **American operation**: vast majority of troops and Commander-in-Chief **General MacArthur**; the US decision to intervene came **a day before** the Council's.
6. ✅ Recognised at the time as **not likely to be repeated**, since the USSR would ensure its presence at future sessions.
7. ✅ **Uniting for Peace - General Assembly resolution 377 A (V), 3 November 1950:** if Council proposals are vetoed, the Assembly may meet **within twenty-four hours** and decide what action to take, **even military intervention**, by a **two-thirds majority**.
8. ✅ **Not added to the Charter.** The USSR always maintained a Council veto should take precedence; the Assembly acted this way **many times** regardless.
9. ⚠️ The Soviet objection had a textual basis in **Article 12** and **Article 24**; the counter is that a vetoed Council has failed to exercise its function.
10. ⚠️ The resolution transfers **the agenda, not the power** - Assembly output remains a recommendation.
11. ✅ Second result: a bitter Russian attack on **Trygve Lie**, whose position became impossible; he retired early and was replaced by **Dag Hammarskjold**.
12. ⚠️ The Secretary-General is appointed by the Assembly **on the Council's recommendation** - independence by mandate, acceptability by procedure.
13. ⚠️ The chain to remember: American veto on Chinese membership -> Soviet boycott -> empty seat -> enforcement resolution -> Uniting for Peace.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Korean action of 1950 both proves and disproves the claim that the United Nations is capable of collective security, and state precisely what the organisation did in response to the feature of the case that made it exceptional.

**Model answer:** It proves the claim in the sense that the machinery worked exactly as designed. An act of aggression occurred; the Council immediately determined against the aggressor and called on member states to send help; sixteen countries contributed troops; and the mandate was fulfilled, with the invasion repelled and the frontier along the 38th parallel preserved. That is collective security in operation, and it is the only case in the Cold War in which decisive action was taken where a superpower's interests were directly engaged. It disproves the claim in the sense that none of this was reproducible. The resolution passed only because the Russian delegates were absent, having boycotted the Council since January 1950 in protest at the exclusion of communist China, and they returned too late to stop it; the operation was in substance American, with the vast majority of the troops and General MacArthur as Commander-in-Chief; and the United States had decided to intervene with force the day before the Council acted. The enabling condition was therefore an accident of procedure that the Soviet Union would obviously never repeat. What the organisation did in response was to attempt to institutionalise the outcome without the accident: the General Assembly adopted the Uniting for Peace resolution, 377 A (V) of 3 November 1950, providing that if Council proposals were vetoed the Assembly could meet within twenty-four hours and decide what action to take, including military intervention, by a two-thirds majority. It was never added to the Charter, the USSR always insisted that a Council veto should take precedence, and the Assembly used the procedure many times regardless.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that Uniting for Peace solved the veto problem. It changed which organ could put the question, not what the answer could compel. An Assembly decision under the procedure remains a recommendation, so it works against states that are sensitive to isolation - as at Suez - and fails entirely against a state prepared to absorb condemnation, as Hungary demonstrated in the very same month.

## Lesson 7 - Suez 1956 and the invention of peacekeeping: a tool the Charter never mentions

Progress: 7/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the nationalisation of the canal and the three-power action; the vetoed Council resolution and the Assembly vote of sixty-four to five; the force of five thousand from ten countries and the withdrawal of the aggressors; Hammarskjold's role and the qualification about superpower pressure; the earlier observer missions of 1948 and 1949; peacekeeping as a practice between Chapter VI and Chapter VII; the three principles of consent, impartiality and non-use of force; robust mandates and where the line runs

### Visual: the sequence at Suez, and the invention it produced

```text
   NASSER NATIONALIZES THE SUEZ CANAL
   Many of its shares owned by the British and French
            |
            +-----------------------------------------+
            v                                         v
   BRITAIN AND FRANCE protest strongly          ISRAEL invades Egypt
   and send troops 'to protect their            FROM THE EAST
   interests'                                        |
            +-----------------------------------------+
                              |
            THE REAL AIM OF ALL THREE: to bring down President Nasser
                              |
                              v
   SECURITY COUNCIL resolution condemning force
            >>> VETOED BY BRITAIN AND FRANCE <<<
                              |
                              v
   GENERAL ASSEMBLY condemns the invasions and calls for withdrawal
            >>> 64 VOTES TO 5 <<<
                              |
                              v
   'In view of the WEIGHT OF OPINION AGAINST THEM, the aggressors
    agreed to withdraw' - PROVIDED the organisation
      (a) ensured a reasonable settlement over the canal, and
      (b) kept the Arabs and Israelis from slaughtering each other
                              |
                              v
   A UN FORCE OF 5,000, made up of troops from TEN DIFFERENT
   COUNTRIES, MOVES IN
   The British, French and Israelis GO HOME
                              |
                              v
   PRESTIGE of the organisation and of DAG HAMMARSKJOLD
   'greatly enhanced'
   QUALIFICATION: American and Russian pressure was ALSO important
                  in bringing about the ceasefire

   ================ WHAT HAD JUST BEEN INVENTED ================
   Not enforcement (nobody was defeated).
   Not mere mediation (soldiers were deployed).
   A THIRD THING, nowhere named in the Charter:
      an impartial international force, admitted BY CONSENT,
      interposed between parties, using force only in self-defence.
   =============================================================
```

*⚠️ Trace the causal arrows and notice what is doing the work. No enforcement decision was taken; the Council's attempt was vetoed by two of its own permanent members. What produced the withdrawal was a recorded number - sixty-four to five - and the condition attached to the withdrawal produced the force. ⚠️ Peacekeeping was not designed and then applied; it was improvised to satisfy a condition that withdrawing aggressors themselves demanded, and it then became the organisation's principal security instrument for the next sixty years.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

The Council is blocked. The aggressors are two permanent members and their partner, so Chapter VII is legally unavailable - the very states that would have to be restrained hold the vote. Mediation alone will achieve nothing, because Egypt cannot be expected to accept the withdrawal of invading armies on a promise. And there is no army of the organisation's own to interpose.

⚠️ What is left? Only two things: a vote, and an offer.

⚠️ The vote establishes, publicly and numerically, that the invading states stand almost alone. The offer supplies them with a way to stop that does not look like capitulation - the organisation will guarantee that the situation they claim to be worried about is managed after they leave.

⚠️ Peacekeeping is the institutional form of that offer. **It is not a way of defeating an aggressor; it is a way of making withdrawal tolerable.** ⚠️ Once that is understood, the whole later record becomes legible - including the failures, which almost always occur where there is nothing to make tolerable because at least one party does not want to stop.

### 2. The crisis, exactly

✅ **Fact:** when President Nasser of Egypt suddenly **nationalized the Suez Canal**, many of whose shares were owned by the British and French, both these powers **protested strongly and sent troops "to protect their interests"**.

✅ **Fact:** at the same time **the Israelis invaded Egypt from the east**; the **real aim of all three states was to bring down President Nasser**.

✅ **Fact:** a **Security Council resolution condemning force was vetoed by Britain and France**, whereupon the **General Assembly, by a majority of sixty-four votes to five, condemned the invasions and called for a withdrawal of troops**.

✅ **Fact:** **in view of the weight of opinion against them, the aggressors agreed to withdraw**, provided the organisation **ensured a reasonable settlement over the canal and kept the Arabs and Israelis from slaughtering each other**.

✅ **Fact:** a **force of five thousand, made up of troops from ten different countries, moved in, while the British, French and Israelis went home**.

✅ **Fact:** the **prestige of the organisation and of Dag Hammarskjold, who handled the operation with considerable skill, was greatly enhanced** - though **American and Russian pressure was also important in bringing about a ceasefire**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that last clause is the qualification that separates a careful answer from a celebratory one, and it should never be omitted. ⚠️ The organisation was not operating in a vacuum; both superpowers wanted the invasion stopped, for entirely different reasons, and their pressure ran in the same direction as the Assembly vote. ⚠️ Suez therefore confirms rather than contradicts the success condition developed in the next lesson: **the organisation succeeded here because the great powers whose opposition mattered were not the aggressors, and the aggressors were great powers of a declining kind who still needed international legitimacy.**

### 3. Why the veto did not prevent the outcome

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the most instructive procedural sequence in the entire topic, and it is the place where the Assembly's real power becomes visible.

⚠️ Britain and France used the veto and got exactly what a veto delivers: no Council action. ⚠️ What they could not prevent was the Assembly meeting and voting, and the vote of sixty-four to five turned a legal immunity into a political catastrophe. ⚠️ The precise mechanism is worth stating: a veto stops a decision, and a recorded majority creates an audience. ⚠️ Against a state that is indifferent to audiences, the veto wins outright; against a state that depends on allies, credit, empire and domestic opinion, the audience wins.

⚠️ A further point that repays attention. ⚠️ The Assembly's action did not, and could not, make the withdrawal legally compulsory. ⚠️ The withdrawal happened because the invading governments made a calculation, and the terms of that calculation are recorded: they agreed to withdraw **provided** the organisation would guarantee two things. ⚠️ So the Assembly produced not a command but a negotiation, and the peacekeeping force was the consideration in the bargain.

⚠️ This is why the sequence matters more than the outcome. ⚠️ Anyone can remember that Suez was a success. ⚠️ What earns marks is being able to say **how** a body with no binding power and a blocked Council nevertheless reversed an invasion by two permanent members - and the answer is: by converting a vote into a negotiating position and a negotiating position into a deployment.

### 4. What peacekeeping actually is, and where it sits in the Charter

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the word "peacekeeping" does not appear in the Charter. ⚠️ The activity was improvised, and the standard description of where it sits - between the pacific settlement of Chapter VI and the enforcement of Chapter VII, and therefore often called "Chapter Six and a Half", a coinage usually attributed to Hammarskjold himself - is a description of practice and not a legal category. ⚠️ Treat the phrase as a teaching aid, attribute it when you use it, and do not present it as a provision.

✅ **Fact:** the organisation's own statement of the principles that continue to set its peacekeeping operations apart names three, which are inter-related and mutually reinforcing:

| Principle | What it means in operation | What breaks it |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Consent of the parties** | Operations are deployed with the consent of the main parties, which requires their commitment to a political process; that acceptance gives the organisation the political and physical freedom of action to carry out its mandate | ✅ In the absence of consent the operation risks becoming a party to the conflict and being drawn towards enforcement. ✅ Consent by the main parties does not guarantee consent at local level, especially where parties are internally divided, have weak command and control, or where armed groups and spoilers are present |
| ✅ **Impartiality** | Peacekeepers must be impartial in their dealings with the parties, **but not neutral in the execution of the mandate**; like a good referee, an operation penalises infractions rather than condoning violations of the peace process or of international norms | ✅ Failure to apply impartiality rigorously - often from fear of misinterpretation or retaliation - undermines credibility and legitimacy and can lead a party to withdraw consent |
| ✅ **Non-use of force except in self-defence and defence of the mandate** | Operations are **not an enforcement tool**, but may use force at the tactical level with Council authorisation in self-defence and in defence of the mandate; force is a last resort, calibrated, proportionate and within the principle of minimum necessary force | ✅ In volatile situations the Council has given "robust" mandates authorising the use of all necessary means to deter disruption of a political process, protect civilians under imminent threat and assist national authorities in maintaining law and order |

✅ **Fact:** robust peacekeeping must not be confused with peace enforcement. ✅ Robust peacekeeping involves the use of force **at the tactical level**, with Council authorisation **and the consent of the host nation or main parties**. ✅ Peace enforcement **does not require the consent of the main parties** and may involve military force at the strategic or international level - force that is normally prohibited to member states under **Article 2(4)** unless authorised by the Council.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the second principle is the one candidates most often state wrongly, and the correction is a genuine discriminator. ⚠️ Impartiality is not neutrality. ⚠️ A neutral force treats a violation and a compliance as equivalent; an impartial force treats both parties equally *and penalises violations by either*. ⚠️ The referee analogy in the organisation's own formulation captures it exactly, and a candidate who writes "peacekeepers must be neutral" has stated a principle the organisation explicitly disclaims.

### 5. What came before Suez, and why Suez is still the turning point

✅ **Fact:** peacekeeping did not begin in 1956. ✅ The first operation, the United Nations Truce Supervision Organization, was established in **1948** to monitor the armistice arrangements in the Middle East; ✅ the United Nations Military Observer Group in India and Pakistan began operations in **January 1949** to observe the ceasefire in Kashmir.

✅ **Fact:** the force deployed after Suez - the **United Nations Emergency Force**, created in **November 1956** by the Assembly meeting in emergency special session - was the **first armed peacekeeping force**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the distinction between the two generations is examinable and easy to state. ⚠️ The 1948 and 1949 missions were **observer** operations: small groups of unarmed or lightly armed military observers, watching a line and reporting violations. ⚠️ The 1956 force was an **interposition** operation: an armed contingent of five thousand from ten countries, physically placed between the parties, whose presence rather than whose firepower produced the effect.

⚠️ That shift explains why Suez is treated as the birth of peacekeeping even though it was not the first mission. ⚠️ Observation requires only a ceasefire; interposition requires a force, a mandate, contributing states, command arrangements, financing and a political sponsor - in other words, an institution. ⚠️ Hammarskjold built that institution in days, which is why the record notes his skill specifically.

⚠️ One further feature is worth noticing and is often missed. ⚠️ The force was created by the **Assembly**, not the Council, through the emergency procedure established after Korea. ⚠️ So the two innovations of Lessons 6 and 7 are causally linked: the resolution devised to bypass a Soviet veto in 1950 was first used at full strength in 1956 to bypass a British and French veto. ⚠️ A procedure built against one bloc was first deployed against the other - which is, incidentally, the strongest available reply to the claim that the organisation's machinery merely serves western interests.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** Suez is a misleading advertisement for the organisation. The invasion collapsed because the United States refused to support its own allies and because the Soviet Union threatened them; the Assembly vote and the peacekeeping force were decorations on a decision taken in Washington and Moscow. Presenting this as the organisation's finest hour inverts cause and effect.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection is partly correct and the correct part is recorded in the sources themselves. ✅ American and Russian pressure **was** also important in bringing about a ceasefire. ⚠️ Any answer that claims the organisation stopped the invasion on its own is overstating the evidence.

⚠️ But three things remain that superpower pressure cannot explain. ⚠️ First, the *terms* of the withdrawal. The aggressors did not simply leave; they left on the condition that the organisation would ensure a settlement over the canal and keep the parties apart. Superpower pressure can compel a withdrawal; it cannot supply the face-saving instrument that makes withdrawal acceptable, and it certainly cannot supply a multinational force. ⚠️ Second, the *force itself*. Five thousand troops from ten countries were assembled, transported, mandated and deployed by an institution, not by a superpower. Neither Washington nor Moscow could have placed such a force in Sinai without making the situation worse, precisely because neither was impartial. ⚠️ Third, the *precedent*. The superpower pressure of 1956 produced nothing durable; the improvised force produced an instrument still in use six decades later.

⚠️ The defensible formulation: **at Suez the superpowers supplied the coercion and the organisation supplied the exit - and because only the organisation could supply the exit, the claim that it was decorative fails even though the claim that it acted alone fails too.**

### 7. Examination use

⚠️ This lesson is the anchor for every peacekeeping question and the essential counterpart to Korea in every question on the veto.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Assess the United Nations as a peacekeeping organisation with suitable examples.
> ⚠️ "Peacekeeping is an invention of practice rather than a provision of the Charter." Discuss.
> ⚠️ Examine the circumstances in which the General Assembly has been able to act where the Security Council could not.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first framing, the low-band script lists missions. ⚠️ The upper-band script opens by defining peacekeeping through its three principles - consent, impartiality that is not neutrality, and non-use of force except in self-defence and defence of the mandate - and then uses cases to show what happens when each principle is present or absent. ⚠️ That structure converts a list into a diagnostic, and it is the same structure that makes the failures of Lesson 15 explicable rather than merely embarrassing.

### Traps

- ❌ Saying the Security Council authorised the Suez force. ✅ The Council resolution **was vetoed by Britain and France**; the Assembly condemned the invasions **sixty-four to five** and the force followed.
- ❌ Omitting Israel from the account. ✅ The Israelis **invaded Egypt from the east** at the same time, and the real aim of all three states was to bring down Nasser.
- ❌ Getting the force wrong. ✅ **Five thousand troops from ten different countries.**
- ❌ Presenting the withdrawal as unconditional. ✅ The aggressors agreed to withdraw **provided** the organisation ensured a reasonable settlement over the canal and kept the parties apart.
- ❌ Omitting the superpower qualification. ✅ **American and Russian pressure was also important** in bringing about the ceasefire.
- ❌ Writing that peacekeepers must be neutral. ✅ They must be **impartial but not neutral in the execution of the mandate**.
- ❌ Calling robust peacekeeping a form of enforcement. ✅ Robust peacekeeping keeps host consent and uses force at the **tactical** level; enforcement requires no consent and operates at the strategic level.
- ⚠️ Saying peacekeeping began in 1956. ✅ Observer missions date from **1948** and **1949**; 1956 produced the **first armed** peacekeeping force.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Nasser nationalized the canal, many of whose shares were British and French-owned; Britain and France protested and sent troops; **Israel invaded from the east**; the real aim of all three was to bring down Nasser.
2. ✅ The Council resolution condemning force was **vetoed by Britain and France**.
3. ✅ The Assembly condemned the invasions and called for withdrawal by **sixty-four votes to five**.
4. ✅ The aggressors withdrew **in view of the weight of opinion against them**, on condition that the organisation ensure a canal settlement and keep the parties apart.
5. ✅ A force of **five thousand from ten different countries** moved in; the British, French and Israelis went home.
6. ✅ Prestige of the organisation and of **Dag Hammarskjold** greatly enhanced; but **American and Russian pressure was also important**.
7. ✅ Earlier missions: **UNTSO, 1948** (Middle East truce supervision) and **UNMOGIP, from January 1949** (Kashmir) - observation, not interposition.
8. ✅ **UNEF, November 1956** - the **first armed peacekeeping force**, created by the Assembly in emergency special session under the Uniting for Peace procedure.
9. ✅ Three principles: **consent of the parties; impartiality but not neutrality; non-use of force except in self-defence and defence of the mandate**.
10. ✅ Consent by main parties does not guarantee local consent where parties are divided, command and control is weak, or spoilers are present.
11. ✅ **Robust peacekeeping** = tactical force with Council authorisation **and host consent**; **peace enforcement** = no consent required, strategic-level force, otherwise prohibited by **Article 2(4)** unless Council-authorised.
12. ⚠️ Peacekeeping is nowhere in the Charter; "Chapter Six and a Half" is a description of practice, usually attributed to Hammarskjold.
13. ⚠️ The mechanism at Suez: a veto stops a decision, a recorded majority creates an audience, and the force was the consideration that made withdrawal tolerable.
14. ⚠️ A procedure built in 1950 against a Soviet veto was first used at full strength in 1956 against a British and French one.

### Concept check

**Question:** The Security Council was blocked at Suez by two of its own permanent members, yet the invasion was reversed. Explain the mechanism by which this happened, and state what the case establishes about the nature of peacekeeping.

**Model answer:** The mechanism had three stages and none of them involved compulsion. First, the Council resolution condemning force was vetoed by Britain and France, which closed the enforcement route entirely, since the states that would have had to be restrained held the blocking vote. Second, the General Assembly condemned the invasions and called for withdrawal by sixty-four votes to five - a recorded, public, numerically overwhelming statement that could not be vetoed because Assembly procedure knows no veto. The invading governments then withdrew in view of the weight of opinion against them, which is a political calculation and not a legal obligation, and it worked because these were states that depended on allies, credit and standing. Third, the withdrawal was conditional: the aggressors agreed to leave provided the organisation ensured a reasonable settlement over the canal and kept the Arabs and Israelis from slaughtering each other, and a force of five thousand troops from ten different countries moved in to discharge exactly that undertaking. The case establishes that peacekeeping is not a method of defeating an aggressor but a method of making withdrawal tolerable: it is an instrument of consent, deployed as part of a bargain, and it works where the parties want a way to stop. The qualification the sources insist on must accompany the account - American and Russian pressure was also important in bringing about the ceasefire.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding from Suez that a determined General Assembly can substitute for the Security Council. The Assembly supplied pressure, and pressure works only on states that are sensitive to it. In the very same month the identical procedure was applied to the Soviet intervention in Hungary and produced nothing at all, which is the paired case taught next.

## Lesson 8 - Hungary and Czechoslovakia: the same procedures, the opposite result, and the success condition

Progress: 8/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Hungarian rising and the appeal to the organisation; the vetoed Council resolution, the Assembly resolution and the investigative committee; Soviet refusal to co-operate; Czechoslovakia in 1968 as a repeat performance with a claimed invitation; the explicit contrast with Suez; the general success condition and its two limbs; Afghanistan 1980 as a later confirmation; why the condition is a better organising device than a list of successes and failures

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's section on the Hungarian Rising as showing the organisation at its most ineffective: Soviet troops entering Hungary to crush the revolt, the Hungarian government's appeal, the Russian veto of a Council resolution calling for withdrawal, the Assembly passing the same resolution and setting up a committee of investigation, the Russian refusal to co-operate and the absence of progress, and the striking contrast with Suez; the Czechoslovak crisis of 1968 as almost a repeat performance, with the Russian veto and the claim that the Czech government had asked for intervention, denied by the Czechs; and the general judgement that the organisation has often been just as ineffective as the League where the interests of a great power seemed threatened and that power decided to ignore or defy it, together with the record that the USSR was able to ignore demands for withdrawal from Hungary in 1956 and Afghanistan in 1980

### Visual: two crises, one month, identical machinery, opposite outcomes

```text
                    SUEZ, 1956                    HUNGARY, 1956
                    (Lesson 7)                    (this lesson)
   ---------------------------------------------------------------------
   TRIGGER          Anglo-French-Israeli          Hungarians try to exert
                    action against Egypt          independence from
                                                  Russian control
   ---------------------------------------------------------------------
   FORCE USED BY    Britain, France, Israel       Soviet troops enter to
                                                  crush the revolt
   ---------------------------------------------------------------------
   APPEAL           Brought to the Council        Hungarian government
                                                  appeals
   ---------------------------------------------------------------------
   COUNCIL          Resolution condemning         Resolution calling for
                    force VETOED by               withdrawal VETOED by
                    BRITAIN and FRANCE            the RUSSIANS
   ---------------------------------------------------------------------
   ASSEMBLY         Condemned the invasions       Passed THE SAME
                    and called for withdrawal     resolution, and set up
                    64 TO 5                       a COMMITTEE to
                                                  investigate
   ---------------------------------------------------------------------
   RESPONSE OF      'In view of the weight of     The Russians REFUSED TO
   THE STATE        opinion against them, the     CO-OPERATE with the
   USING FORCE      aggressors AGREED TO          committee, and NO
                    WITHDRAW'                     PROGRESS COULD BE MADE
   ---------------------------------------------------------------------
   OUTCOME          Withdrawal + peacekeeping     Nothing
                    force of 5,000 from 10
                    countries
   ---------------------------------------------------------------------
   THE RECORDED CONTRAST:
   'there, Britain and France were willing to bow to international
    pressure; the Russians simply ignored the UN, and nothing could
    be done'

   TWELVE YEARS LATER - CZECHOSLOVAKIA, 1968
   'almost a repeat performance of the Hungarian rising'
     Russian and other Warsaw Pact troops sent in to enforce obedience
     Council motion condemning the action VETOED by the Russians
     Russian claim: the Czech government HAD ASKED for intervention
     The Czechs DENIED this
     'there was nothing the UN could do in view of the USSR's refusal
      to co-operate'
```

*⚠️ This is the most valuable comparison in the topic, and its value comes from what is held constant. The machinery is identical in both columns: appeal, Council, veto, Assembly, resolution. The legal position is identical. The month is the same. Only one variable changes - who was using force - and the outcomes are total success and total failure. ⚠️ When a comparison isolates a single variable like this, it is not an illustration; it is evidence, and an answer that presents it as evidence is making an argument rather than telling two stories.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Lesson 7 ended with an organisation that had just reversed an invasion by two permanent members of its own Council. It would be reasonable to conclude that the machinery works - that a blocked Council can be routed around, that the Assembly can generate decisive pressure, and that peacekeeping supplies the instrument.

⚠️ In the same month, the same machinery was applied to a Soviet intervention and produced nothing whatever.

⚠️ The question this lesson answers is therefore not "why does the organisation sometimes fail?" - a question too vague to have an answer. ⚠️ It is: **given identical procedures and identical law, what variable determines the outcome?** ⚠️ And because the two cases are simultaneous, every other explanation is eliminated. It cannot be the state of the Cold War, the personality of the Secretary-General, the mood of the membership or the competence of the Secretariat, because all of those were the same in both cases.

### 2. Hungary, 1956, exactly

✅ **Fact:** the Hungarian Rising **took place at the same time as the Suez Crisis, and showed the organisation at its most ineffective**.

✅ **Fact:** when the Hungarians **tried to exert their independence from Russian control, Soviet troops entered Hungary to crush the revolt**.

✅ **Fact:** the **Hungarian government appealed** to the organisation, but the **Russians vetoed a Security Council resolution calling for a withdrawal of their forces**.

✅ **Fact:** the **General Assembly passed the same resolution and set up a committee to investigate the problem; but the Russians refused to co-operate with the committee and no progress could be made**.

✅ **Fact:** **the contrast with Suez was striking: there, Britain and France were willing to bow to international pressure; the Russians simply ignored the organisation, and nothing could be done.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice how much the organisation did. It received the appeal from the legitimate government. It convened the Council. When the Council was blocked, the Assembly passed the identical text. It established an investigative machinery. ⚠️ Every procedural step available under the Charter and under the Uniting for Peace innovation was taken, in the correct order, within days.

⚠️ That is why "the United Nations did nothing about Hungary" is a false sentence, and why the agency discipline set out at the start of this session matters here more than anywhere else. ⚠️ The organisation did everything it could do. What it could not do was make a permanent member of the Council comply, and the reason it could not is not an administrative failing but Article 27 - which is to say, the founders' deliberate choice examined in Lesson 1.

### 3. Czechoslovakia, 1968: the same case with one addition

✅ **Fact:** the Czechoslovak crisis of 1968 was **almost a repeat performance of the Hungarian rising twelve years earlier**.

✅ **Fact:** when the Czechs showed what Moscow considered to be **too much independence, Russian and other Warsaw Pact troops were sent in to enforce obedience to the USSR**.

✅ **Fact:** the **Security Council tried to pass a motion condemning this action, but the Russians vetoed it, claiming that the Czech government had asked for their intervention**. ✅ **Although the Czechs denied this, there was nothing the organisation could do in view of the USSR's refusal to co-operate.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the addition in 1968 is the **claimed invitation**, and it is worth a paragraph because it introduces a legal device that has been used ever since and is central to several later controversies.

⚠️ A state that intervenes by invitation is not violating Article 2(4) at all, because consent removes the element of coercion against territorial integrity or political independence. ⚠️ So the Soviet claim was not a crude evasion; it was an attempt to place the action outside the prohibition rather than inside an exception. ⚠️ The Czech denial is what made the claim contestable - and the organisation had no mechanism to determine which account was true, because any fact-finding would have required the cooperation that was being withheld.

⚠️ This exposes a structural gap that a good answer can name precisely: **the Charter provides no impartial procedure for determining contested facts when the state in control of the territory is also the state refusing access.** ⚠️ The Court can decide law between consenting parties; the Council can determine a threat to the peace if nine members including all five permanent members agree. ⚠️ Neither is available when the contested fact is whether a permanent member was invited.

### 4. The success condition, stated as a rule

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the general condition governing the whole security record can now be stated, and it has two limbs which must both be given.

✅ **Fact:** the core account states the pattern twice. ✅ The organisation has been more successful than the League in its peacekeeping efforts, **especially in crises which did not directly involve the interests of the great powers**, such as the Congo and West New Guinea. ✅ It has often been **just as ineffective as the League in situations where the interests of one of the great powers seemed to be threatened, and where the great power decided to ignore or defy it** - the Hungarian rising of 1956 and the Czech crisis of 1968 being the named examples.

⚠️ The condition, stated as a rule for use in answers:

> ⚠️ **The organisation acts effectively when (a) the interests of the great powers are not directly engaged, or (b) the great powers whose interests are engaged choose to support or accept the action. It fails when a great power's interests are directly engaged and that power decides to ignore or defy it.**

⚠️ Both limbs are needed. ⚠️ Limb (a) alone would predict failure in Korea and in the Gulf, where great-power interests were very much engaged and the organisation nevertheless acted. ⚠️ Limb (b) accounts for those cases: the engaged great power supported the action. ⚠️ A candidate who offers only limb (a) has a rule with two glaring counter-examples; a candidate who offers both has a rule that fits every case in this session.

⚠️ There is also a sub-condition visible in the Suez case which sharpens the rule and is worth having in reserve. ⚠️ Britain and France were great powers whose interests were directly engaged, and they complied. ⚠️ The reason is that they were great powers of a kind that still required international legitimacy - allies, credit, empire and domestic consent. ⚠️ So the operative variable is not raw power but **sensitivity to isolation**, and the rule can be refined accordingly: the organisation restrains a powerful state to the extent that the state values what the organisation can withdraw.

### 5. A later confirmation, and the limits of the confirmation

✅ **Fact:** the pattern recurred. ✅ Because the organisation lacks a permanent army, it is difficult to prevail upon powerful states to accept its decisions if they choose to put self-interest first; ✅ the USSR was able to ignore demands for the withdrawal of Russian troops from **Hungary in 1956** and from **Afghanistan in 1980**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Afghanistan case is useful in an answer precisely because it is a quarter of a century later. ⚠️ It shows the condition is not an artefact of the 1950s, of a particular Secretary-General or of a small membership. ⚠️ By 1980 the membership was well over 150, the Assembly had a permanent Third World majority, and the procedure was long established - and the outcome was identical.

⚠️ The limit of the confirmation should also be stated, because an examiner will look for it. ⚠️ The pattern is about **enforcement against a great power**. ⚠️ It says nothing about the organisation's record in ceasefire supervision, in transitions, in decolonisation, or in the entire non-military field of Lesson 12. ⚠️ A candidate who extrapolates from Hungary and Afghanistan to "the United Nations is ineffective" has generalised from the one category in which no international institution has ever been effective, and has ignored the categories in which this one has been extraordinarily so.

### 6. Comparison: three ways a case can fail

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** "failure" is used far too loosely in answers on this topic, and distinguishing its types is one of the quickest ways to look precise.

| Type of failure | Mechanism | Cases in this session |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ **Blocked** | A permanent member prevents any decision; the organisation is legally unable to act | ✅ Hungary 1956; ✅ Czechoslovakia 1968 |
| ⚠️ **Defied** | A decision or recommendation exists and the target simply does not comply, and no member is willing to enforce it | ✅ Afghanistan 1980; ✅ the Namibian case before 1990; ✅ the Nicaragua merits judgment of 1986 |
| ⚠️ **Overmatched** | Action is authorised and undertaken, but the mandate, the force or the political backing is inadequate to the situation | ✅ Somalia; ✅ Bosnia - both in Lesson 15 |
| ⚠️ **Bypassed** | The organisation is not blocked or defied but simply circumvented; action is taken without seeking its authority | ✅ Iraq 2003 - Lesson 16 |

⚠️ The four types demand four different remedies, which is why the distinction matters beyond terminology. ⚠️ Blocking can only be addressed by changing the voting rule - the hardest reform of all. ⚠️ Defiance can be addressed by increasing the costs of non-compliance. ⚠️ Being overmatched can be addressed by mandates, resources and command arrangements, and is therefore the one category where reform proposals have actually produced results. ⚠️ Being bypassed can only be addressed politically, by making the authorisation worth having.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** the success condition is a tautology dressed as an explanation. To say that the organisation succeeds when great powers permit it and fails when they do not is simply to restate the veto rule. It has no predictive content and explains nothing that Article 27 does not already explain.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection would be fatal if the condition were only about the Council, and it is not.

⚠️ Two of its applications lie entirely outside the voting rule. ⚠️ First, the Suez case: the veto **was** exercised, by Britain and France, and the invasion was nevertheless reversed. ⚠️ Article 27 predicts failure; the condition, with its sensitivity-to-isolation refinement, predicts success, and success is what happened. ⚠️ Second, the failures of Lesson 15: in Somalia and Bosnia there was no veto at all. ✅ The Council authorised action; the organisation deployed thirty-seven thousand troops to Somalia and designated safe areas in Bosnia; and both ended in withdrawal and humiliation. ⚠️ Article 27 explains neither case. ⚠️ The condition, in the form "great powers whose interests are engaged choose to support or accept the action", explains both - because the European Community and the United States were reluctant to get involved in Bosnia, and the American withdrawal from Somalia preceded the general one.

⚠️ So the condition is not a restatement of the veto rule but a broader claim about political backing, of which the veto is one instrument. ⚠️ The defensible formulation: **the veto is the legal form of great-power consent; the success condition is about the substance, of which the legal form is only the most visible expression - which is why the condition continues to operate in cases where no veto was cast at all.**

### 8. Examination use

⚠️ This lesson supplies the organising thesis for every evaluative question in the topic.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Under what conditions has the United Nations succeeded or failed in maintaining peace? Illustrate with examples.
> ⚠️ "The United Nations has been as ineffective as the League of Nations whenever a great power was involved." Critically examine.
> ⚠️ Compare the response of the United Nations to the Suez Crisis and to the Hungarian Rising of 1956.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the third framing, the entire answer is available in the paired table of this lesson, and the decisive move is to hold the machinery constant and isolate the variable. ⚠️ For the first framing, state the two-limbed condition in the opening sentences and then use each subsequent paragraph as a test of it, including at least one case that appears to contradict it - Suez is the best - so that the condition is seen to survive a hostile case rather than merely being illustrated by friendly ones.

### Traps

- ❌ Writing that the organisation did nothing about Hungary. ✅ The appeal was received, the Council met, the resolution was vetoed, **the Assembly passed the same resolution** and **set up a committee to investigate**; the Russians refused to co-operate.
- ❌ Confusing the two Eastern European cases. ✅ **Hungary 1956** - Soviet troops crush a rising; **Czechoslovakia 1968** - Russian and other **Warsaw Pact** troops enforce obedience.
- ❌ Omitting the claimed invitation in 1968. ✅ The Russians vetoed **claiming the Czech government had asked for their intervention**; the Czechs denied it.
- ❌ Treating Suez and Hungary as unrelated episodes. ✅ They were simultaneous, and the core account draws the contrast explicitly.
- ❌ Stating the success condition with only one limb. ⚠️ Both are needed: great-power interests not directly engaged, **or** engaged great powers choosing to support or accept the action.
- ❌ Using "failure" as a single category. ⚠️ Distinguish **blocked**, **defied**, **overmatched** and **bypassed**.
- ⚠️ Generalising from Hungary to the whole record. ⚠️ The condition governs enforcement against a great power and says nothing about supervision, transitions or the non-military record.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Hungary 1956 took place **at the same time as Suez** and showed the organisation **at its most ineffective**.
2. ✅ Hungarians tried to exert independence from Russian control; **Soviet troops entered to crush the revolt**; the Hungarian government appealed.
3. ✅ The **Russians vetoed** a Council resolution calling for withdrawal; the **Assembly passed the same resolution** and **set up a committee to investigate**; the Russians refused to co-operate and **no progress could be made**.
4. ✅ The recorded contrast: **Britain and France were willing to bow to international pressure; the Russians simply ignored the organisation.**
5. ✅ Czechoslovakia 1968 - **almost a repeat performance**: **Russian and other Warsaw Pact troops** sent in; Council motion **vetoed**; Russian claim of an **invitation**, **denied by the Czechs**; nothing could be done.
6. ⚠️ The invitation device places an intervention **outside Article 2(4)** rather than inside an exception - and the Charter supplies no impartial mechanism to settle the contested fact.
7. ✅ The general judgement: success **especially in crises not directly involving great-power interests**; ineffectiveness where a great power's interests seemed threatened **and that power decided to ignore or defy** the organisation.
8. ⚠️ **The success condition, two limbs:** great-power interests not directly engaged, **or** the engaged great powers choose to support or accept the action.
9. ⚠️ **Refinement:** the operative variable is sensitivity to isolation - the organisation restrains a state to the extent that the state values what it can withdraw.
10. ✅ Later confirmation: the USSR ignored withdrawal demands over **Hungary 1956** and **Afghanistan 1980**; the absence of a permanent army makes it hard to prevail on powerful states that put self-interest first.
11. ⚠️ **Four types of failure:** blocked (Hungary, Czechoslovakia); defied (Afghanistan, Namibia before 1990, the Nicaragua merits judgment); overmatched (Somalia, Bosnia); bypassed (Iraq 2003).
12. ⚠️ Each type demands a different remedy - which is why only the "overmatched" category has produced real reform.
13. ⚠️ The condition is not a restatement of the veto rule: it explains Suez, where a veto was cast and failed, and Somalia and Bosnia, where none was cast at all.

### Concept check

**Question:** Two crises in the same month, handled under identical procedures, produced opposite results. Using this pair, state the condition that governs the organisation's security record, show why a one-limbed version of the condition is inadequate, and identify the refinement that the Suez case forces.

**Model answer:** In October and November 1956 the same machinery operated twice. At Suez a Council resolution was vetoed by Britain and France, the Assembly condemned the invasions sixty-four to five, and the aggressors withdrew in view of the weight of opinion against them, with a force of five thousand from ten countries moving in. Over Hungary a Council resolution calling for Soviet withdrawal was vetoed by the Russians, the Assembly passed the identical text and appointed a committee of investigation, the Russians refused to co-operate and no progress could be made. Since the law, the procedures and the moment were constant, the only variable was the identity of the state using force. The condition that follows has two limbs: the organisation acts effectively where great-power interests are not directly engaged, or where the great powers whose interests are engaged choose to support or accept the action; it fails where a great power's interests are engaged and that power decides to ignore or defy it. A one-limbed version stating only the first is inadequate because Korea in 1950 and the Gulf operation in 1991 both engaged great-power interests directly and both succeeded, so the version without the second limb has two immediate counter-examples. The Suez case forces a further refinement, because Britain and France were great powers whose interests were engaged and who nevertheless complied. The reason is that they still required international legitimacy, so the operative variable is not raw power but sensitivity to isolation: the organisation restrains a powerful state to the degree that the state values what the organisation is able to withdraw.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the Hungarian outcome as evidence that the organisation is powerless in general. The condition it establishes concerns enforcement against a great power, which is the single category in which no international institution has ever succeeded. It is silent about ceasefire supervision, supervised transfers of territory, decolonisation and the entire humanitarian and developmental record - and an answer that extrapolates from this case to the whole institution has generalised from the hardest possible case.

## Lesson 9 - Settlement once, containment thereafter: West New Guinea, Palestine, Kashmir, Cyprus, Lebanon and the Iran-Iraq War

Progress: 9/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: West New Guinea as the case where a settlement was actually reached; Palestine 1947 and the most controversial decision the organisation has taken; Kashmir from the 1948 ceasefire to the clashes of 1999; Cyprus from 1964 to the Annan Plan of 2004; Lebanon and UNIFIL from 1978 to 2011; the Iran-Iraq ceasefire and the role of exhaustion; the difference between managing a crisis and resolving a conflict; why a long deployment is not by itself a failure

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's case sequence: West New Guinea, with the 1946 independence arrangement for the Dutch East Indies, the fighting of 1961, U Thant's appeal, the 1962 agreement transferring the territory to Indonesia and the transfer organised and policed by a UN force; Palestine 1947, the investigation, the decision to divide Palestine and set up Israel, Arab rejection, the wars of 1948-9, 1967 and 1973, the ceasefire and supervisory work and the refugee agency; Kashmir, the 1948 ceasefire, sixteen years of policing the line, the 1965 war and the violent clashes of 1999; Cyprus, which the account calls a British colony since 1878, independence in 1960, civil war in 1963 between a Greek population of about eighty per cent and the Turks, the peacekeeping force of March 1964 and three thousand troops, the 1974 Greek Cypriot attempt at union with Greece, the Turkish seizure of the north and the expulsions, and the Annan Plan of 2004 accepted by the Turks and rejected by the Greeks; Lebanon, the civil war the account dates 1975-87, the Israeli invasion of March 1978, the interim force of about seven thousand from June 1978, Hezbollah from the early 1990s, the attack of April 1996 and the occupation until 1999, the increase to around eight thousand, the reduction to some three thousand in 2002, the July 2006 ambush and Israeli response, the mid-August ceasefire and the increase to twelve thousand, and the renewed incidents of 2011 around the village the extraction renders "Gharjah"; and the Iran-Iraq War of 1980-8, where the organisation at last negotiated a ceasefire after years of attempting to mediate, helped by the exhaustion of both sides
