### Visual: six disputes, sorted by what the organisation was actually able to deliver

```text
   DELIVERED A SETTLEMENT
   +------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | WEST NEW GUINEA (WEST IRIAN)                                      |
   | 1946 UN helps arrange independence from Holland for the Dutch     |
   |      East Indies -> INDONESIA; West New Guinea left unresolved,   |
   |      claimed by both                                              |
   | 1961 FIGHTING BREAKS OUT                                          |
   | ---- U THANT appeals to both sides to REOPEN NEGOTIATIONS         |
   | 1962 AGREED: the territory should become part of Indonesia        |
   | ---- THE TRANSFER WAS ORGANIZED AND POLICED BY A UN FORCE         |
   | VERDICT: 'a vital role in getting negotiations off the ground,    |
   |          though it did not itself make the decision'              |
   +------------------------------------------------------------------+

   DELIVERED A CEASEFIRE, NOT A SETTLEMENT
   +------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | PALESTINE   1947 investigation -> DECISION TO DIVIDE PALESTINE    |
   |             and set up ISRAEL. 'One of the most controversial     |
   |             decisions', not accepted by the majority of Arabs.    |
   |             Could NOT prevent wars in 1948-9, 1967, 1973.         |
   |             DID useful work: ceasefires, supervisory forces,      |
   |             UNRWA caring for the Arab refugees                    |
   | KASHMIR     1948 ceasefire negotiated. Indians in the south,      |
   |             Pakistanis in the north; UN policed the line for      |
   |             SIXTEEN YEARS. 1965 invasion -> short war -> UN       |
   |             intervenes successfully, hostilities cease.           |
   |             1999 violent clashes. 'Little prospect of the UN or   |
   |             any other agency finding a permanent solution'        |
   | CYPRUS      Under BRITISH ADMINISTRATION from 1878, annexed 1914, |
   |             CROWN COLONY 1925; independence 1960;                 |
   |             1963 civil war (Greeks ~80% of population v Turks);   |
   |             MARCH 1964 peacekeeping force; 3,000 troops needed    |
   |             PERMANENTLY; 1974 Greek Cypriot attempt at union      |
   |             with Greece -> Turkish Cypriots + invading Turkish    |
   |             troops seize the north and expel Greeks living there; |
   |             UN condemned but could not remove the Turks; achieved |
   |             a ceasefire and still polices the frontier;           |
   |             ANNAN PLAN 2004: accepted by the Turks, REJECTED by   |
   |             the Greeks                                            |
   | LEBANON     CIVIL WAR 1975-1990 + a southern frontier dispute;    |
   |             March 1978 Israeli invasion; June 1978 Israel agrees  |
   |             to withdraw PROVIDED the UN polices the frontier ->   |
   |             UNIFIL, about 7,000, supervises the withdrawal;       |
   |             early 1990s Hezbollah; April 1996 major Israeli       |
   |             attack, occupation until 1999; UNIFIL raised to       |
   |             around 8,000; 2002 reduced to some 3,000; July 2006   |
   |             ambush -> blockade, bombing, rocket fire -> ceasefire |
   |             mid-August, UNIFIL raised to 12,000; 2011 incidents   |
   |             around GHAJAR                                         |
   +------------------------------------------------------------------+

   DELIVERED AN END TO A WAR, WITH HELP FROM EXHAUSTION
   +------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | IRAN-IRAQ WAR 1980-8   After YEARS of attempting to mediate, the  |
   | UN at last negotiated a ceasefire - 'admittedly they were helped  |
   | by the fact that both sides were close to exhaustion'             |
   +------------------------------------------------------------------+
```

*⚠️ The three boxes are three different products, and confusing them is the commonest analytical error in peacekeeping answers. The first box contains a dispute that ended. The second contains disputes that were contained for decades without ending. The third contains a war that ended partly because the belligerents had run out of capacity. ⚠️ A script that calls the second box "failure" has assumed that containment is worthless; a script that calls it "success" has ignored that none of those disputes was settled. The accurate description is that the organisation converted a series of wars into a series of supervised stalemates - and whether that is success depends entirely on what the alternative would have been.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Suppose two parties are fighting, neither can win outright, and neither will accept the other's terms. An outside body arrives and separates them. The shooting stops. Thirty years later the shooting has still not restarted, and the underlying dispute is exactly where it was.

⚠️ Has the outside body succeeded or failed?

⚠️ The answer depends on what it was asked to do, and this is precisely where most answers on this topic go wrong. ⚠️ If the mandate was to resolve the dispute, it has failed. ⚠️ If the mandate was to stop people being killed while a political process is attempted by others, it has succeeded, and the fact that the political process never happened is a failure of the parties and their patrons rather than of the force standing between them.

⚠️ The organisation's peacekeeping mandates have, with very few exceptions, been of the second kind. ⚠️ Peacekeeping was invented at Suez, as Lesson 7 showed, as the instrument that made withdrawal tolerable. ⚠️ An instrument designed to make stopping possible cannot be blamed for not producing agreement, because agreement was never within its gift.

⚠️ The useful vocabulary is therefore: **crisis management** versus **conflict resolution**. ⚠️ The organisation's record at the first is strong. Its record at the second is thin, and it is thin for a structural reason - resolution requires the parties to change their objectives, and no force, however large or however long deployed, can do that for them.

### 2. West New Guinea: the case where the dispute actually ended

✅ **Fact:** in **1946** the organisation **helped to arrange independence from Holland for the Dutch East Indies, which became Indonesia**. ✅ However, **no agreement was reached about the future of West New Guinea (West Irian), which was claimed by both countries**.

✅ **Fact:** in **1961 fighting broke out**; after **U Thant had appealed to both sides to reopen negotiations**, it was agreed in **1962** that **the territory should become part of Indonesia**. ✅ **The transfer was organized and policed by a UN force.**

✅ **Fact:** the recorded verdict is exact and carefully limited: in this case the organisation **played a vital role in getting negotiations off the ground, though it did not itself make the decision about West Irian's future**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that final clause is what makes this case useful rather than merely pleasant. ⚠️ The organisation did not impose an outcome; it reopened a negotiation, and then supplied the one thing the parties could not supply for themselves - an impartial body capable of organising and policing a transfer of territory. ⚠️ Handing over a province is an act neither side can perform without the other suspecting a trick, and a third party with no interest in the province is therefore not a luxury but a precondition.

⚠️ So the general lesson from West New Guinea is not "the organisation can settle disputes". ⚠️ It is: **where the parties are willing to settle, the organisation can supply the execution machinery that makes settlement physically possible.** ⚠️ Test that against the failures in the next sections and it holds every time - in each of them, the missing element is the willingness, not the machinery.

### 3. Palestine: the most controversial decision, and its long consequence

✅ **Fact:** the dispute between Jews and Arabs in Palestine was **brought before the organisation in 1947**. ✅ After an investigation, it **decided to divide Palestine, setting up the Jewish state of Israel**.

✅ **Fact:** this was **one of the most controversial decisions** the organisation has taken, and **it was not accepted by the majority of Arabs**.

✅ **Fact:** the organisation was **unable to prevent a series of wars between Israel and various Arab states - 1948-9, 1967 and 1973** - though it **did useful work arranging ceasefires and providing supervisory forces**, while the **Relief and Works Agency cared for the Arab refugees**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Palestine occupies a unique place in this topic because it is the one case where the organisation did not merely respond to a dispute but **created the legal framework of one**. ⚠️ Everywhere else the parties and the territory were given and the organisation arrived afterwards; here the partition decision defined the states whose subsequent conflict it then spent seventy years managing.

⚠️ Three consequences follow and each is examinable.

⚠️ First, on impartiality. A body that has made the constitutive decision cannot easily be seen as impartial by the party that rejected it. ⚠️ The peacekeeping principle of consent becomes correspondingly harder to satisfy, because consent is being sought by an institution that one side regards as the author of its grievance.

⚠️ Second, on institutional longevity. The refugee agency established to care for those displaced became one of the organisation's largest and longest-running operations - Lesson 12 records that as the camps proved permanent it began building schools, hospitals, houses and training centres. ⚠️ An emergency relief measure became, by default, a system of public administration for a stateless population, because the political settlement that would have ended the need never arrived.

⚠️ Third, on the limits of the record. ✅ The organisation could not prevent three named wars. ⚠️ But the same sentence records ceasefires arranged and supervisory forces provided in each. ⚠️ The honest formulation is that it failed at prevention and succeeded, repeatedly, at termination - which is the crisis-management-versus-resolution distinction in its clearest form.

### 4. Kashmir: sixteen years of policing a line

✅ **Fact:** in Kashmir the organisation found itself in a situation **similar to the one in Cyprus**. ✅ After 1947 this large province, lying between India and Pakistan, was **claimed by both states**.

✅ **Fact:** **already in 1948 the organisation had negotiated a ceasefire after fighting broke out**. ✅ At that point **the Indians were occupying the southern part of Kashmir, the Pakistanis the northern part, and for the next sixteen years the organisation policed the ceasefire line between the two zones**.

✅ **Fact:** when **Pakistani troops invaded the Indian zone in 1965, a short war developed, but once again the organisation successfully intervened and hostilities ceased**.

✅ **Fact:** **the original dispute still remained, however, and in 1999 there were violent clashes as Pakistanis again unsuccessfully invaded the Indian zone.** ✅ **There seemed little prospect of the organisation or any other agency finding a permanent solution.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three features of the recorded account should be carried into an answer with care.

⚠️ First, note what the organisation is recorded as doing and not doing. It negotiated a ceasefire, policed a line and intervened successfully to end hostilities in 1965. ⚠️ It is not recorded as adjudicating the claim, determining sovereignty or imposing terms. ⚠️ A candidate writing on this case for an Indian examination should be scrupulous about that distinction: the evidence here concerns the organisation's institutional behaviour, and the substantive dispute, India's legal position and the subsequent bilateral framework belong to the Indian foreign-policy and international-relations owners, not to a world-history session.

⚠️ Second, the phrase about **any other agency** is doing work. The recorded pessimism is not about this organisation specifically; it extends to any external body. ⚠️ That is a general claim about the limits of third-party conflict resolution where both parties' core claims are territorial and non-divisible, and it is the fairest possible framing for an answer.

⚠️ Third, the sixteen-year figure. ⚠️ Long deployments are routinely cited as evidence of failure. ⚠️ The opposite inference is at least as defensible: a line that required policing for sixteen years was a line that would not have held without it, and the recorded fact that fighting resumed in 1965 and again in 1999 shows what the underlying pressure was.

### 5. Cyprus: a permanent garrison and a rejected plan

✅ **Fact:** Cyprus **has kept the organisation busy since 1964**. ✅ The island passed **under British administration in 1878** under the Cyprus Convention, was **annexed in 1914**, became a **Crown colony in 1925**, and was **granted independence in 1960**.

⚠️ **Precision note.** ⚠️ The core account compresses this as "a British colony since 1878". ⚠️ That is the shorthand of a general history rather than the constitutional sequence, and the difference matters in an objective question, because 1878 is the date of *administration* and 1925 the date of *colonial status*.

✅ **Fact:** in **1963 civil war broke out between the Greeks, who made up about eighty per cent of the population, and the Turks**. ✅ A **peacekeeping force arrived in March 1964**; an **uneasy peace was restored, but it needed three thousand troops permanently stationed in Cyprus** to prevent Greeks and Turks tearing each other apart.

✅ **Fact:** in **1974 the Greek Cypriots tried to unite the island with Greece**. ✅ This prompted the **Turkish Cypriots, helped by invading Turkish army troops, to seize the north of the island for their own territory**, and they went on to **expel all Greeks who were unfortunate enough to be living in that area**.

✅ **Fact:** the organisation **condemned the invasion but was unable to remove the Turks**. ✅ Its forces **did at least achieve a ceasefire and are still policing the frontier between Greeks and Turks**. ✅ However, it has **still not been successful in finding an acceptable constitution or any other compromise**. ✅ The most recent attempt recorded, the **Annan Plan of 2004, was accepted by the Turks but rejected by the Greeks**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Cyprus supplies the single most precise illustration in the topic of what peacekeeping can and cannot do, because the same force is recorded doing both at once.

⚠️ What it could do: restore an uneasy peace in 1964 and hold it; achieve a ceasefire in 1974; police a frontier for decades. ⚠️ What it could not do: prevent the 1974 attempt at union, prevent the Turkish seizure of the north, reverse that seizure, prevent the expulsions, or produce a constitution.

⚠️ The dividing line between the two lists is exact and worth stating as a rule: **a peacekeeping force can hold a line that both sides have accepted, and it cannot prevent, reverse or punish a determined military action by a state that has decided to take one.** ⚠️ That is not a defect of the Cypriot mission; it is the definition of the instrument, which uses force only in self-defence and defence of its mandate.

⚠️ The Annan Plan adds a second lesson that is easy to miss. ⚠️ The plan existed, it was negotiated, and it was accepted by one community and rejected by the other. ⚠️ So the obstacle in 2004 was not the absence of a proposal, nor the absence of an intermediary, nor the absence of a force on the ground - all three were present. ⚠️ The obstacle was a referendum result. ⚠️ Any answer arguing that the organisation should "do more" to resolve frozen conflicts has to explain what more it could have done when the machinery produced a complete plan and one party declined it.

### 6. Lebanon: a mandate that kept changing while the force stayed

✅ **Fact:** while **civil war was raging in the Lebanon** matters were further complicated by a **frontier dispute in the south** between Lebanese Christians, aided by the Israelis, and Palestinians.

⚠️ **Date correction.** ✅ The Lebanese civil war ran from **1975 to 1990**, the Taif Agreement of 1989 supplying the settlement that closed it. ⚠️ The core account gives the span as 1975-87, which understates it by three years; the corrected span is used throughout this session and the divergence is recorded in the source ledger.

✅ **Fact:** in **March 1978 the Israelis invaded South Lebanon** in order to destroy Palestinian guerrilla bases from which attacks were being made on northern Israel. ✅ In **June 1978 the Israelis agreed to withdraw, provided the organisation assumed responsibility for policing the frontier area**. ✅ The **United Nations Interim Force in Lebanon (UNIFIL), consisting of about seven thousand troops**, was sent to South Lebanon.

✅ **Fact:** it **supervised the Israeli withdrawal and had some success in maintaining relative peace in the area; but it was a constant struggle against frontier violations, assassinations, terrorism and the seizing of hostages**.

✅ **Fact:** during the **early 1990s** a new enemy began to harass Israel from bases in South Lebanon - the Muslim Shi'ite group **Hezbollah**, which the Israeli government said was backed by Iran and Syria. ✅ In retaliation the Israelis **launched a major attack on South Lebanon in April 1996 and occupied most of the region until 1999**. ✅ Once again UNIFIL **helped to supervise an Israeli withdrawal and the force was increased to around eight thousand**. ✅ In **2002**, as the region seemed calmer than for many years, UNIFIL was **reduced to some three thousand**. ✅ It **worked hard to strengthen the Lebanese army, providing training and equipment**, and eventually the two forces were able to work together to maintain stability, though a permanent solution still seemed far off.

✅ **Fact:** in **July 2006 Hezbollah ambushed an Israeli patrol**; eight Israeli soldiers were killed and two taken prisoner. ✅ The Israelis responded immediately, demanding the return of the captured soldiers, blockading Lebanon from the sea, bombing Beirut and destroying Hezbollah's headquarters; ✅ Hezbollah retaliated by firing rockets into Israel at a rate of over a hundred a day. ✅ It was **mid-August before the organisation succeeded in arranging a ceasefire**. ✅ **UNIFIL was increased to twelve thousand** and there was relative calm for the next four years. ✅ **Early in 2011 violent incidents began again**, with the Israelis still refusing to move out of a small area around the village of **Ghajar**, north of the withdrawal line agreed in 2006; there were exchanges of fire between the Lebanese army and the Israeli Defence Force, terrorist attacks on UNIFIL itself, and the firing of rockets into Israel.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Lebanese case is the best available demonstration of a phenomenon the earlier cases only hint at - **mandate drift**. ⚠️ Follow what the force was actually for at each stage.

| Stage | What the force was for | Force level |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ 1978 | Supervise an Israeli withdrawal, as the condition on which Israel agreed to withdraw | ✅ About seven thousand |
| ✅ 1978-96 | Maintain relative peace against frontier violations, assassinations, terrorism and hostage-taking | ✅ About seven thousand |
| ✅ 1996-99 | Present during an Israeli occupation it could not prevent; then supervise a second withdrawal | ✅ Raised to around eight thousand |
| ✅ 1999-2006 | Train and equip the Lebanese army so that a national force could hold the area | ✅ Reduced to some three thousand in 2002 |
| ✅ 2006 onward | Hold a line after a month-long war it could not prevent, alongside a partially rebuilt Lebanese army | ✅ Raised to twelve thousand |

⚠️ Notice two things. ⚠️ First, the force size tracks the failure of the political process, not the success of the mission: it rises after each war and falls in periods of calm. ⚠️ Second, the most genuinely strategic phase - training and equipping the Lebanese army so that the state itself could hold its own territory - is also the phase with the smallest force. ⚠️ That combination is the strongest argument available for the proposition that **capacity-building, not interposition, is the only exit from an open-ended peacekeeping commitment**, and it can be evidenced entirely from this single case.

### 7. Iran-Iraq: mediation that finally worked, and the honest qualification

✅ **Fact:** the organisation **was successful in bringing an end to the long-drawn-out war between Iran and Iraq (1980-8)**. ✅ After **years of attempting to mediate**, it **at last negotiated a ceasefire**, **though admittedly they were helped by the fact that both sides were close to exhaustion**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this case is often used in answers as an unqualified success, and the qualification recorded in the source is the whole interest of it.

⚠️ Two readings are available and a strong answer states both. ⚠️ The deflationary reading: the war ended because the belligerents could no longer fight it, and the mediation supplied a form of words for a conclusion that material exhaustion had already determined. ⚠️ The constructive reading: exhaustion produces a willingness to stop, not a mechanism for stopping, and two states that have spent eight years destroying each other cannot open direct negotiations without one of them appearing to capitulate. ⚠️ An intermediary that has been present and persistent for years is precisely the instrument that converts exhaustion into a ceasefire.

⚠️ The second reading is strengthened by the phrase "after years of attempting to mediate". ⚠️ The organisation was not summoned at the end; it was there throughout, failing, and its failure during the years when both sides still expected to win is what made it available at the moment when neither did.

⚠️ The general principle, stated for use elsewhere in the session: **mediation has no power to change a party's objectives, and it has considerable power to supply a route once objectives have changed.** ⚠️ This explains why the same organisation appears ineffective for years and then effective in a matter of weeks, and it removes the temptation to attribute the difference to better diplomacy.

### 8. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** describing decades of unresolved disputes as "containment" is a euphemism. In Cyprus a force has stood on a line since 1964; in Kashmir since 1948; in Lebanon since 1978. Permanent deployment is not a form of success, it is the institutionalisation of failure, and it consumes resources that might have produced settlements elsewhere.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection has real force and two parts of it should be conceded. ⚠️ Open-ended deployments do consume resources indefinitely, and the record contains a candid admission that no acceptable constitution or compromise has been found in Cyprus and little prospect of a permanent solution in Kashmir.

⚠️ But the objection contains a hidden assumption that does not survive examination - that the alternative to a long deployment is a settlement. ⚠️ The evidence points to a different alternative. ✅ In Cyprus, three thousand troops were needed **permanently** to prevent the communities tearing each other apart; that is an assessment of what would happen in their absence. ✅ In Kashmir, fighting resumed in 1965 and again in 1999, on a line the organisation had policed for sixteen years. ✅ In Lebanon, each reduction of the force was followed by renewed conflict and each conflict by an increase.

⚠️ The comparison that the objection needs is therefore not "deployment versus settlement" but "deployment versus the resumption of fighting", and on that comparison the deployments look considerably better. ⚠️ And the Annan Plan case settles the question of whether more effort would have produced settlement: a complete plan was produced, and one community voted it down.

⚠️ The defensible formulation: **an open-ended deployment records the failure of the parties to settle, not the failure of the organisation to try - and a body whose instrument is consent cannot manufacture consent it has not been given.**

### 9. Examination use

⚠️ This lesson supplies the case bank for every peacekeeping question and the vocabulary that makes the bank usable.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "The United Nations has managed conflicts better than it has resolved them." Examine with examples.
> ⚠️ Assess the role of the United Nations in the disputes of the post-1945 Middle East and South Asia.
> ⚠️ Examine the conditions under which United Nations peacekeeping operations have achieved durable results.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first framing, the structure is given: state the distinction between crisis management and conflict resolution in the opening sentence, give West New Guinea as the case that reached settlement and explain **why** it did - the parties were willing and the organisation supplied the execution machinery - then give two containment cases, then close on the Annan Plan as proof that the obstacle is consent rather than effort. ⚠️ Three cases used analytically will always outscore six cases narrated.

### Traps

- ❌ Saying the organisation created Israel by force. ✅ After an investigation it **decided to divide Palestine**, setting up the Jewish state of Israel; the decision was **not accepted by the majority of Arabs** and it could not prevent the subsequent wars.
- ❌ Claiming the organisation solved the Kashmir dispute. ✅ It negotiated the **1948 ceasefire**, policed the line for **sixteen years**, intervened successfully in **1965**, and the original dispute remained, with violent clashes in **1999**.
- ❌ Dating the Cyprus force wrongly. ✅ Civil war **1963**; peacekeeping force **March 1964**; **three thousand** troops permanently stationed; Turkish seizure of the north **1974**; **Annan Plan 2004**, accepted by the Turks and rejected by the Greeks.
- ❌ Calling Cyprus a British colony from 1878. ✅ **Administration** from **1878**; **annexation 1914**; **Crown colony 1925**; independence **1960**.
- ❌ Ending the Lebanese civil war in 1987. ✅ It ran **1975-1990**, closed by the Taif Agreement of 1989; the core account's 1975-87 understates it.
- ❌ Reproducing the extraction's spelling of the Blue Line village. ✅ The village is **Ghajar**.
- ❌ Treating UNIFIL as a single unchanging deployment. ✅ About seven thousand in 1978; around eight thousand after 1996; some three thousand in 2002; **twelve thousand** after 2006.
- ❌ Presenting the Iran-Iraq ceasefire as pure diplomatic achievement. ✅ The account records that the mediators **were helped by the fact that both sides were close to exhaustion**.
- ❌ Forgetting West New Guinea. ✅ It is the recorded case where the organisation played **a vital role in getting negotiations off the ground**, though it **did not itself make the decision**, and where a UN force **organized and policed the transfer**.
- ⚠️ Using a long deployment as automatic evidence of failure. ⚠️ Compare it with the resumption of fighting, which is the actual alternative the evidence records.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **West New Guinea:** 1946 independence for the Dutch East Indies as Indonesia, West Irian left disputed; **1961** fighting; **U Thant** appeals; **1962** agreement that the territory become part of Indonesia; **transfer organized and policed by a UN force**. Verdict: vital in starting negotiations, **did not itself make the decision**.
2. ✅ **Palestine 1947:** after investigation, the organisation **decided to divide Palestine** and set up Israel - **one of its most controversial decisions**, rejected by the majority of Arabs.
3. ✅ Could **not prevent** the wars of **1948-9, 1967 and 1973**; **did** arrange ceasefires and provide supervisory forces; **UNRWA** cared for the Arab refugees.
4. ✅ **Kashmir:** claimed by both states after 1947; **1948 ceasefire negotiated**; line policed for **sixteen years**; **1965** Pakistani invasion and short war, ended by successful intervention; **1999** violent clashes; **little prospect of the organisation or any other agency finding a permanent solution**.
5. ✅ **Cyprus:** under **British administration from 1878**, annexed **1914**, **Crown colony 1925**; independence **1960**; civil war **1963** (Greeks about **eighty per cent** of the population); force from **March 1964**; **three thousand** troops permanently required.
6. ✅ **1974:** Greek Cypriot attempt at union with Greece; Turkish Cypriots with invading Turkish troops seize the north and expel Greeks living there; the organisation condemned the invasion, **could not remove the Turks**, achieved a ceasefire and still polices the frontier.
7. ✅ **Annan Plan 2004:** accepted by the Turks, **rejected by the Greeks** - a complete plan defeated by consent, not by absence of effort.
8. ✅ **Lebanon:** civil war **1975-1990**; Israeli invasion **March 1978**; **June 1978** Israel agrees to withdraw **provided** the organisation polices the frontier; **UNIFIL about seven thousand**.
9. ✅ Hezbollah from the early 1990s; Israeli attack **April 1996** and occupation until **1999**; force raised to about **eight thousand**; reduced to some **three thousand** in **2002**; UNIFIL trains and equips the **Lebanese army**.
10. ✅ **July 2006** Hezbollah ambush, Israeli blockade and bombing, rockets at over a hundred a day; ceasefire **mid-August**; UNIFIL raised to **twelve thousand**; renewed incidents in **2011** around **Ghajar**.
11. ✅ **Iran-Iraq 1980-8:** ceasefire negotiated after years of attempted mediation, **helped by both sides being close to exhaustion**.
12. ⚠️ **Crisis management** (strong record) versus **conflict resolution** (thin record) - the distinction that organises every answer on this material.
13. ⚠️ **The rule from Cyprus:** a peacekeeping force can hold a line both sides accept and cannot prevent, reverse or punish a determined military action by a state that has decided to take one.
14. ⚠️ **The rule from Lebanon:** force levels track the failure of the political process; capacity-building, not interposition, is the only exit from an open-ended commitment.
15. ⚠️ **The rule from Iran-Iraq:** mediation cannot change objectives and can supply a route once objectives have changed.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using West New Guinea and Cyprus, explain why the organisation was able to end one territorial dispute and not the other, and state the general rule about what peacekeeping can deliver.

**Model answer:** In West New Guinea the dispute ended because the parties became willing to settle and the organisation supplied what they could not supply themselves. Fighting broke out in 1961 over a territory left unresolved when the Dutch East Indies became Indonesia in 1946; U Thant appealed to both sides to reopen negotiations; agreement was reached in 1962 that the territory should become part of Indonesia; and the transfer was then organised and policed by a UN force. The recorded verdict is precise - the organisation played a vital role in getting negotiations off the ground but did not itself make the decision - so its contribution was procedural and executive, not adjudicative. A handover of territory is something neither claimant can perform without the other suspecting bad faith, and an impartial body is therefore a precondition rather than a courtesy. In Cyprus the willingness was absent at every stage. Civil war broke out in 1963 between a Greek population of about eighty per cent and the Turks; a peacekeeping force arrived in March 1964 and three thousand troops were permanently required; in 1974 a Greek Cypriot attempt at union with Greece prompted Turkish Cypriots, helped by invading Turkish troops, to seize the north and expel the Greeks living there. The organisation condemned the invasion, could not remove the Turks, achieved a ceasefire and still polices the frontier, and the Annan Plan of 2004 was accepted by the Turks and rejected by the Greeks - which shows that the missing element was consent and not a proposal. The general rule is that peacekeeping can hold a line that both sides have accepted and can execute a settlement that the parties have already reached; it cannot prevent, reverse or punish a determined military action, and it cannot manufacture the agreement it exists to implement.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the length of a deployment as the measure of failure. The relevant comparison is not between a long deployment and a settlement, but between a long deployment and the resumption of fighting - and the record supplies the comparison directly, since three thousand troops were judged permanently necessary in Cyprus to keep the communities apart, fighting resumed in Kashmir in 1965 and 1999 on a policed line, and every reduction of the force in Lebanon was followed by renewed conflict.

## Lesson 10 - The Congo, trusteeship and Namibia: complexity, cost, and the first crisis of solvency and authority

Progress: 10/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Congo operation of 1960-64 as the most complex to that date; the force of over twenty thousand and the Congo Fund; the refusal of the USSR, France and Belgium to pay and the approach to bankruptcy; the death of Hammarskjold; Namibia, South African defiance, the Court's ruling of 1971 and the release of 1990; the Trusteeship Council's record; what the two cases together establish about authority without enforcement and about who pays for what

Book context: queried - the core account's section on the civil war in the Congo, described as the most complex operation to date except for Korea: the dissolution into chaos immediately after independence, the force numbering over twenty thousand at its largest which managed to restore some sort of precarious order, the special Congo Fund for recovery and development, the financial cost that brought the organisation close to bankruptcy when the USSR, France and Belgium refused to pay their contributions because they disapproved of the way the situation had been handled, and the death of Dag Hammarskjold, which the account places "in a plane crash in the Congo"; and its section on the Trusteeship Council, which replaced the League's Mandates Commission, did its job well so that by 1970 most mandates had gained independence, and the Namibian exception - South African refusal, repeated condemnation, the 1971 International Court of Justice ruling that the occupation was a breach of international law requiring immediate withdrawal, South African defiance, and the release of Namibia in 1990 under the pressure of black African nationalism and world opinion

### Visual: one operation, four simultaneous problems

```text

      +---------+-----------+-----------+------------------+
      v         v           v           v                  v
   MILITARY   POLITICAL   FINANCIAL   INSTITUTIONAL     HUMAN
   A force    Restoring   The cost    Three states -    Dag
   of OVER    'some sort  brought     the USSR,         HAMMARSKJOLD
   20,000     of          the UN      FRANCE and        killed in a
   at its     PRECARIOUS  CLOSE TO    BELGIUM -         PLANE CRASH on
   largest    ORDER'      BANKRUPTCY  REFUSED TO PAY    18 SEPT 1961
                          A special   their             near NDOLA,
                          CONGO FUND  contributions     NORTHERN
                          set up for  because they      RHODESIA
                          recovery    DISAPPROVED of    (now ZAMBIA)
                          and         how the           - NOT in the
                          development situation had     Congo itself
                                      been handled

   ================== WHAT THE CASE PROVED ==================
   1  The organisation CAN mount a large, complex operation.
   2  It CANNOT compel the members to pay for one.
   3  Non-payment is a VETO EXERCISED AFTER THE DECISION -
      a second blocking power, available to any large contributor,
      requiring no seat on the Council at all.
   ==========================================================

   NAMIBIA - AUTHORITY WITHOUT ENFORCEMENT
   Trusteeship Council replaces the League's MANDATES COMMISSION
   (created 1919 for territories taken from Germany and Turkey)
     -> 'did its job well': BY 1970 MOST MANDATES HAD GAINED
        INDEPENDENCE
     -> EXCEPT NAMIBIA. South Africa, ruled by a government
        representing the WHITE MINORITY, refused independence to a
        state on its own frontier that would be ruled by a
        BLACK AFRICAN MAJORITY government
   1971  INTERNATIONAL COURT OF JUSTICE rules the occupation A BREACH
         OF INTERNATIONAL LAW; South Africa MUST WITHDRAW IMMEDIATELY
   ----> SOUTH AFRICA IGNORED THE UN
   1990  Pressure of BLACK AFRICAN NATIONALISM and WORLD OPINION
         forces South Africa to release its grip
   ==========================================================
   NINETEEN YEARS between the ruling and the outcome.
   The law was clear throughout. What changed was the POLITICS.
   ==========================================================
```

*⚠️ The two halves of this visual describe the same limitation from opposite ends. In the Congo the organisation had the authority and ran out of money; over Namibia it had the law and had no means of applying it. ⚠️ Both cases are usually filed under "weaknesses of the UN", which is true but useless. What they actually establish is more precise: that authority in this system is one input among several, and that the others - money, force and political will - are supplied by members who can withhold them individually, after the decision has been taken, without breaking any rule.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Lessons 6 to 9 have all concerned the same obstacle in different forms: a decision could not be taken, or could not be enforced against a state that refused to comply.

⚠️ This lesson introduces a different and less obvious obstacle. ⚠️ Suppose the decision **is** taken. Suppose it is lawful, widely supported and actually implemented, with a large force in the field doing exactly what it was mandated to do. ⚠️ What can still go wrong?

⚠️ Two things, and the Congo demonstrates both. ⚠️ The operation can cost more than the members are prepared to pay, and states that disapprove of how it is being conducted can decline to pay their share without leaving the organisation, defying a legal obligation in any visible way or casting a single vote.

⚠️ That is a second blocking power, and it is in some ways more troubling than the veto, because it is exercised **after** a lawful decision, it is available to any large contributor rather than only to the permanent five, and it operates silently. ⚠️ The Congo is where the organisation discovered it.

### 2. The Congo operation, exactly

✅ **Fact:** here the organisation **mounted its most complex operation to date, except for Korea**.

✅ **Fact:** when the **Congo - known as Zaire since 1971 - dissolved into chaos immediately after gaining independence**, a force **numbering over twenty thousand at its largest managed to restore some sort of precarious order**.

✅ **Fact:** a **special Congo Fund was set up to help with the recovery and development of the ravaged country**.

✅ **Fact:** but the **financial cost was so high that the organisation was brought close to bankruptcy**, **especially when the USSR, France and Belgium refused to pay their contributions towards the cost of the operations, because they disapproved of the way the situation had been handled**.

✅ **Fact:** the operation also **cost the life of Dag Hammarskjold**, who died in an air crash on **18 September 1961 near Ndola, in Northern Rhodesia - now Zambia** - while travelling to negotiate over the Congo.

⚠️ **Location correction.** ⚠️ The core account places the crash "in the Congo". ⚠️ It occurred outside Congolese territory, near Ndola in what was then Northern Rhodesia, and the distinction is not pedantry: the Secretary-General died on a diplomatic journey to meet the Katangese leadership across an international frontier, which is part of why the circumstances of the crash have been investigated repeatedly ever since. ⚠️ The corrected location is used throughout this session and the divergence is recorded in the source ledger.

✅ **Fact:** the Congo is also one of the two cases the account names when stating that the organisation has been more successful than the League in peacekeeping **especially in crises which did not directly involve the interests of the great powers** - the civil war in the Congo between 1960 and 1964, and the dispute between the Netherlands and Indonesia over West New Guinea.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** there is an apparent contradiction in those last two facts that is worth resolving explicitly, because it is exactly the kind of tension an examiner probes.

⚠️ The Congo is classified as a case not directly involving great-power interests, and yet three states - one of them a superpower, two of them permanent members of the Council - cared enough about its conduct to withhold funds. ⚠️ The resolution is that the classification concerns the **stakes** and the withholding concerns the **conduct**. ⚠️ None of the three had a vital interest in the outcome of the Congolese civil war; all three objected to how the operation was run. ⚠️ So the case establishes something the Hungary-Suez pair could not: **great powers can damage an operation they have no vital interest in, simply by disapproving of it, and the damage they do is financial rather than legal.**

### 3. Why the Congo was different from everything before it

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the operation marks a genuine break in the history of the instrument, and setting it against the Suez model shows why.

| | ✅ **Suez, 1956** | ✅ **Congo, 1960-64** |
|---|---|---|
| Type of conflict | ⚠️ Inter-state: identifiable armies with identifiable governments | ⚠️ **Internal collapse**: a state dissolving into chaos immediately after independence |
| Who consents? | ⚠️ Governments that had agreed to withdraw | ⚠️ Unclear - in a collapsing state, who speaks for the territory? |
| What is the line to be held? | ⚠️ A frontier between belligerents | ⚠️ There is no line; disorder is distributed |
| Objective | ⚠️ Interposition and supervision of withdrawal | ⚠️ Restoring order and, through the Fund, recovery and development |
| Size | ✅ Five thousand from ten countries | ✅ **Over twenty thousand** at its largest |
| Outcome | ✅ Withdrawal achieved; prestige greatly enhanced | ✅ **Some sort of precarious order** restored; near-bankruptcy; the Secretary-General killed |

⚠️ Every structural difficulty that would overwhelm the missions of the 1990s is already present in this column. ⚠️ Consent is ambiguous because the consenting authority is contested. ⚠️ Impartiality is hard to maintain because restoring order means acting against whoever is disturbing it. ⚠️ The mandate includes state-building - recovery and development - which no interposition force is equipped to deliver. ⚠️ And the cost of doing all this at scale is more than the membership will bear.

⚠️ The analytical payoff is substantial: **the Congo was not an anomaly of the 1960s but the prototype of the post-Cold-War mission**, and an answer that makes this link will explain Somalia and Bosnia in Lesson 15 as the repetition of a known problem rather than as a new surprise.

### 4. The financial mechanism, and why it is a second veto

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** it is worth being exact about how withholding functions, because the precision is what distinguishes a good answer here.

⚠️ Under the arrangements examined in Lesson 4 and Lesson 13, expenses are borne by members as apportioned by the Assembly, and members additionally pay a proportion of the cost of each peacekeeping operation. ⚠️ A state that objects to an operation therefore has two moments of influence: the decision, where it may have a vote and possibly a veto, and the payment, where it has neither obligation it can be forced to honour nor any procedure to answer to.

⚠️ The Charter does provide a sanction - Article 19's loss of the Assembly vote after two full years of arrears. ⚠️ But notice its limits. ⚠️ It is triggered only by a two-year threshold, it removes a vote rather than recovering money, and it is subject to the Assembly's discretion where failure to pay is due to conditions beyond the member's control. ⚠️ Against a state that is making a political point rather than pleading poverty, the sanction is weak and its application is itself a political question.

⚠️ Hence the formulation: **withholding is a veto exercised after the vote.** ⚠️ It is available to any state whose share is large enough to matter, it requires no seat on the Council, it cannot be overruled, and it attacks the operation rather than the decision - which means the organisation is left holding a lawful mandate it cannot fund.

### 5. Namibia: law without a bailiff

✅ **Fact:** the Trusteeship Council **replaced the League of Nations Mandates Commission**, which had come into existence in **1919** to keep an eye on the territories taken away from Germany and Turkey at the end of the First World War. ✅ Some of these areas, known as mandated territories, had been handed over to the victorious powers, whose job was to **govern the territories and prepare them for independence**.

✅ **Fact:** the Trusteeship Council **did its job well and by 1970 most of the mandates had gained their independence**.

✅ **Fact:** **Namibia remained a problem, since South Africa refused to give the area independence.** ✅ South Africa, **ruled by a government representing the white minority of the population, was unwilling to give independence to a state right on its own frontier that would be ruled by a government representing its black African majority**.

✅ **Fact:** the organisation **repeatedly condemned South Africa for its attitude**; in **1971 the International Court of Justice ruled that South Africa's occupation of Namibia was a breach of international law and that South Africa must withdraw immediately**. ✅ **South Africa ignored the organisation**, but as the other states of Africa gradually gained independence under black governments, it became more difficult for South Africa to maintain both its position in Namibia and its own white minority rule. ✅ **At last in 1990 the pressure of black African nationalism and world opinion forced South Africa to release its grip on Namibia.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the nineteen-year interval between the ruling and the outcome is the substance of this case, and it can be read in two directions. Both belong in an answer.

⚠️ Read as failure: the highest judicial organ of the international system found an occupation unlawful and ordered immediate withdrawal, and the occupation continued for nineteen more years. ⚠️ No enforcement followed. ✅ Lesson 14 records the general position - although the Council has in theory the power to take appropriate measures to enforce the Court's decisions, it has never done so.

⚠️ Read as vindication: the ruling did not become irrelevant during those nineteen years; it became the legal basis of a campaign. ✅ The recorded cause of the eventual release is **the pressure of black African nationalism and world opinion** - and world opinion in 1990 could point to a judicial determination rather than merely to a preference. ⚠️ The decolonisation of the rest of Africa, examined in Lesson 11, changed the political arithmetic; the ruling supplied the legal content that the changed arithmetic could act upon.

⚠️ The general proposition, which transfers to several other cases in this session: **a legal determination the system cannot enforce is not thereby worthless; it is a stored asset that becomes effective when the political conditions change.** ⚠️ The candidate's task is to say so without implying that law and enforcement are the same thing.

### 6. What the Trusteeship record actually shows

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Trusteeship Council is routinely dismissed in answers as a defunct organ, which misses the more interesting judgement: it is the only principal organ that **completed its mandate**.

✅ **Fact:** it did its job well, and by 1970 most of the mandates had gained independence. ✅ It provided international supervision for eleven trust territories placed under the administration of seven member states, and ensured that adequate steps were taken to prepare them for self-government and independence. ✅ By 1994 all trust territories had attained self-government or independence, and the Council **suspended operation on 1 November 1994**; by a resolution adopted on 25 May 1994 it amended its rules of procedure to drop the obligation to meet annually and agreed to meet as occasion required.

⚠️ Two observations follow. ⚠️ First, on how to describe it: the accurate word is **suspended**, not abolished. It remains a principal organ under the Charter, because removing it would require an amendment, and it retains the capacity to meet as occasion requires. ⚠️ A candidate who writes that the Trusteeship Council "was abolished" has made a small error with a visible cause - forgetting that the Charter is a treaty.

⚠️ Second, on what it proves. ⚠️ An organ that worked itself out of existence is the strongest single piece of evidence against the claim that international institutions exist only to perpetuate themselves. ⚠️ It is also a precise rebuttal to the general charge of ineffectiveness: eleven territories, seven administering powers, and a completed transition to self-government or independence in every case.

⚠️ The qualification that keeps the claim honest: ⚠️ the trusteeship system covered only the territories inherited from the mandates arrangement, which was a small fraction of the colonial world. ⚠️ The mass of colonial territories were never trust territories at all, and their independence came through the process examined in the next lesson. ⚠️ So the Trusteeship Council supervised a completed transition in a limited domain; it did not decolonise Asia and Africa.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** the Congo is presented here as a demonstration of a financial mechanism, which is far too kind. The operation was widely judged to have been mishandled - that is precisely why three states refused to pay - and an operation that restores only "some sort of precarious order" while bankrupting its sponsor and killing its Secretary-General is a failure by any ordinary standard.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection is well grounded, and two elements must be conceded outright. ✅ The three states withheld funds **because they disapproved of the way the situation had been handled**, which is evidence that serious criticism existed at the time and came from governments with access to the facts. ✅ And the recorded outcome is deliberately hedged - some sort of precarious order.

⚠️ Two considerations nonetheless qualify the verdict. ⚠️ First, the standard being applied. The Congo had dissolved into chaos immediately after independence. ⚠️ The comparison for "some sort of precarious order" is not an orderly state but the continuation of that chaos, and an operation of over twenty thousand personnel that halts a collapse is not obviously a failure merely because the order it produces is fragile. ⚠️ Second, the objection does not engage the institutional finding, which is independent of whether the operation was well run. ⚠️ Even if every criticism of the conduct were correct, the fact would remain that **disapproval of conduct was expressed through non-payment rather than through any procedure**, and that the organisation had no answer to it. ⚠️ A well-run operation would have been just as vulnerable to a state that disapproved for other reasons - and Lesson 13 shows exactly that happening in the 1980s over matters of ideology and management rather than of peacekeeping.

⚠️ The defensible formulation: **the Congo's conduct is debatable and its institutional lesson is not - an organisation that must ask its members to fund each operation separately has given every substantial contributor a second veto that no rule constrains.**

### 8. Examination use

⚠️ This lesson supplies the financial dimension that most answers on this topic omit entirely, and the Namibian case for any question on the Court or on the limits of international law.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "The United Nations has never lacked authority; it has lacked resources." Examine.
> ⚠️ Assess the significance of the Congo operation for the development of United Nations peacekeeping.
> ⚠️ Examine the role of the United Nations in the ending of colonial rule, with reference to the trusteeship system.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first framing, the Congo and Namibia together give a complete answer with a twist: the Congo shows authority without resources, Namibia shows authority without enforcement, and the twist is that the second is not the same as the first - money can be supplied by a willing member, while enforcement against a determined state cannot be supplied at all. ⚠️ For the third framing, be precise about scope: the trusteeship system covered eleven territories under seven administering powers and completed its work; the mass of decolonisation happened outside it, and that is the subject of the next lesson.

### Traps

- ❌ Calling the Congo the largest operation ever. ✅ It was the **most complex operation to date, except for Korea**, with a force of **over twenty thousand at its largest**.
- ❌ Saying the organisation restored order in the Congo. ✅ It restored **some sort of precarious order**; the hedge is in the source and should be preserved.
- ❌ Naming the wrong states as withholders. ✅ The **USSR, France and Belgium** refused to pay, **because they disapproved of the way the situation had been handled**.
- ❌ Forgetting the Congo Fund. ✅ A **special Congo Fund** was set up for the recovery and development of the ravaged country.
- ❌ Placing Hammarskjold's death in the Congo. ✅ He died in an air crash on **18 September 1961 near Ndola, Northern Rhodesia - now Zambia**, while travelling to negotiate over the Congo; the core account's "in the Congo" is inaccurate.
- ❌ Saying the Trusteeship Council was abolished. ✅ It **suspended operation on 1 November 1994**; it remains a principal organ and may meet as occasion requires.
- ❌ Dating the Namibian ruling loosely. ✅ The **International Court of Justice ruled in 1971** that the occupation was a breach of international law requiring immediate withdrawal; **South Africa ignored it**; release came in **1990**.
- ⚠️ Crediting the trusteeship system with decolonisation generally. ✅ It supervised **eleven trust territories** under **seven** administering states; the mass of colonies were never trust territories.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The Congo, **1960-64**, was the **most complex operation to date except for Korea**; the country **dissolved into chaos immediately after independence**.
2. ✅ A force of **over twenty thousand at its largest** restored **some sort of precarious order**; a special **Congo Fund** supported recovery and development.
3. ✅ The **cost brought the organisation close to bankruptcy**, especially when the **USSR, France and Belgium refused to pay** because they **disapproved of the way the situation had been handled**.
4. ✅ **Dag Hammarskjold died in an air crash on 18 September 1961 near Ndola, Northern Rhodesia - now Zambia** - not in the Congo, as the core account states.
5. ✅ The Congo and West New Guinea are the two cases named as successes **in crises not directly involving great-power interests**.
6. ⚠️ Resolution of the apparent tension: the classification concerns the **stakes**, the withholding concerned the **conduct**.
7. ⚠️ **Withholding is a veto exercised after the vote** - available to any substantial contributor, needing no Council seat, unanswerable by any procedure, and attacking the operation rather than the decision.
8. ⚠️ **Article 19** is a weak answer to it: a two-year threshold, loss of vote rather than recovery of money, and a discretionary exception.
9. ⚠️ The Congo is the **prototype of the post-Cold-War mission**: ambiguous consent, no line to hold, order-restoration instead of interposition, state-building in the mandate, and costs beyond what members will bear.
10. ✅ The Trusteeship Council **replaced the League's Mandates Commission of 1919**; it **did its job well** and by **1970 most mandates had gained independence**.
11. ✅ It supervised **eleven trust territories** administered by **seven** member states; by 1994 all had attained self-government or independence; it **suspended operation on 1 November 1994**.
12. ✅ **Namibia:** South Africa, governed by a white-minority government, refused independence to a territory on its frontier that would have a black African majority government.
13. ✅ **1971:** the International Court of Justice ruled the occupation a **breach of international law** requiring **immediate withdrawal**; **South Africa ignored the organisation**; **1990:** black African nationalism and world opinion forced release.
14. ⚠️ **The stored-asset principle:** an unenforceable legal determination is not worthless; it becomes effective when the political conditions change - nineteen years, in this case.
15. ⚠️ The Trusteeship Council is the only principal organ to have **completed its mandate** - the strongest evidence against the claim that institutions exist only to perpetuate themselves.

### Concept check

**Question:** The Congo operation was lawfully mandated, large and substantially implemented, yet it nearly bankrupted the organisation. Explain the mechanism that produced this outcome, and state why it constitutes a distinct limitation from the veto.

**Model answer:** The mechanism was selective non-payment. Members bear the organisation's expenses as apportioned by the General Assembly and additionally contribute a proportion of the cost of each peacekeeping operation, so an operation's finances depend on continuing payments rather than on the original decision. In the Congo the cost was so high that the organisation was brought close to bankruptcy, especially when the USSR, France and Belgium refused to pay their contributions because they disapproved of the way the situation had been handled. Three states therefore imposed a severe constraint on an operation whose legality none of them had successfully challenged, and they did so without voting, without breaching any procedure that could be invoked against them, and without leaving the organisation. This is distinct from the veto in four respects. It operates after the decision rather than before it, so it can defeat a mandate that has already been lawfully adopted. It is available to any member whose assessed share is large enough to matter, not only to the five permanent members. It cannot be overruled, whereas a veto at least leaves the Uniting for Peace route open to the Assembly. And the only sanction against it, the loss of the Assembly vote under Article 19 after arrears equalling two full years' contributions, removes a vote rather than recovering the money and is subject to a discretionary exception, which makes it almost useless against a state acting on principle rather than from inability to pay.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that because three permanent or major states objected, the Congo must have been a case where great-power interests were directly engaged. The record classifies it among the crises that did **not** directly involve great-power interests, and the objection concerned how the operation was handled rather than what was at stake - which is precisely why the case is important, since it shows that a great power can cripple an operation it has no vital interest in simply by disliking its conduct.

## Lesson 11 - The Assembly transformed: decolonisation, the Third World majority and the politicisation charge

Progress: 11/18 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: how self-determination in the Charter became an instrument against the states that wrote it; the Declaration of 1960 and the Assembly's role in decolonisation; the arrival of the Third World majority by 1970 and what it did to voting; the western reaction and the meaning of the word "political"; the UNESCO crisis of 1983 and the withdrawals of 1985 and 1986; the construction of a Global South bargaining position through the Group of 77, UNCTAD and the demand for a new economic order; what the episode establishes about representation and legitimacy

Book context: queried - the core account's section on the increasing membership from the 1970s: the clear Third World majority by 1970, the ability of that bloc alone to be certain of having its resolutions passed, the growing difficulty for both western and communist blocs in getting resolutions through the Assembly, the western criticism of the Third World bloc for being too political and the account's gloss that by this they meant acting in a way the West disapproved of, the UNESCO resolutions of 1974 condemning colonialism and imperialism, the defeat of the 1979 western motion condemning terrorism by the Arab states and their supporters, the crisis at the UNESCO General Congress of 1983 with accusations of inefficiency, waste and unacceptable political aims, the proposal for internal licensing of foreign journalists and the American objection, the announced American withdrawal from 1 January 1985, the British and Singaporean withdrawals in 1986 and the returns of Britain in 1997 and of the USA in a year the account gives as 2002; together with the membership series and the account of UNCTAD, first meeting in 1964 and soon becoming a permanent body to encourage industry in the Third World and pressurise rich countries into buying Third World products

### Visual: a majority changes hands, and the meaning of "political" changes with it
