*⚠️ The left column is a constitutional story and the right column is a political one, and the arrow between them is the whole point of this lesson. A treaty written in 1945 by imperial powers contained a principle - self-determination - and an obligation - the sacred trust of promoting self-government. Twenty years later the beneficiaries of those clauses were a voting majority in the organ that interprets them. ⚠️ Nothing illegal or even surprising happened. The instrument worked as written, and the states that wrote it discovered they did not like the result.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

An institution designed by a small group of powerful states admits new members on equal terms. The new members eventually outnumber the founders. What happens?

⚠️ In most international organisations, nothing much, because the founders retain a weighted vote or a blocking mechanism. ⚠️ In the General Assembly, something did happen, because the Assembly has no weighted vote and no veto. ⚠️ One state, one vote, applied to a membership that quadrupled, produced a body in which the original architects could be outvoted routinely.

⚠️ The reaction was immediate and it took a revealing form: **a complaint that the Assembly had become "political"**. ⚠️ That complaint is worth pausing on, because it is analytically empty and politically loaded. Every vote in a political organ is political. ⚠️ The core account puts the point with unusual bluntness and the phrasing should be quoted in any answer on this subject: the western nations criticised the Third World bloc for being too political, and **by this, they meant acting in a way the West disapproved of**.

⚠️ The interesting question is therefore not whether the Assembly became political - it always was - but **what changed when the majority changed**, and what that reveals about how legitimacy is assessed in international institutions. ⚠️ The answer, developed below, is that legitimacy claims track outcomes: bodies whose decisions suit us are representative, and bodies whose decisions do not are politicised.

### 2. The textual lever: how the Charter armed its own critics

✅ **Fact:** Article 1, Purpose 2 commits the organisation to developing friendly relations among nations **based on respect for the principle of equal rights and self-determination of peoples**.

✅ **Fact:** Chapter XI of the Charter contains a **Declaration Regarding Non-Self-Governing Territories**. ✅ Under Article 73, members which have or assume responsibilities for the administration of territories whose peoples have not yet attained a full measure of self-government **recognize the principle that the interests of the inhabitants of these territories are paramount**, and **accept as a sacred trust the obligation to promote to the utmost** the well-being of those inhabitants - including their political, economic, social and educational advancement, their just treatment and protection against abuses, and the development of self-government with due account taken of the political aspirations of the peoples.

✅ **Fact:** in **1960** the General Assembly adopted the **Declaration on the Granting of Independence to Colonial Countries and Peoples**, resolution **1514 (XV)**, on **14 December 1960**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three observations make this into an argument rather than a list.

⚠️ First, Article 73 applies to **all** non-self-governing territories, not merely to the trust territories supervised by the Trusteeship Council. ⚠️ Lesson 10 established that the trusteeship system covered eleven territories under seven administering powers; Chapter XI covered the rest of the colonial world, and it did so with an obligation - a sacred trust - rather than a supervisory mechanism. ⚠️ The asymmetry is important: the trust territories had a supervisory organ and the remainder had a principle, and the principle turned out to be the more powerful of the two once the votes existed to invoke it.

⚠️ Second, the colonial powers signed both clauses in 1945 without expecting them to operate quickly, because in 1945 the membership that would invoke them did not exist. ⚠️ The clauses were, in effect, promissory notes issued to a creditor who had not yet been born.

⚠️ Third, the 1960 Declaration was an Assembly resolution - a recommendation, in the strict sense of Lesson 4, binding nobody. ⚠️ And yet the two decades that followed produced the largest transfer of sovereignty in recorded history. ⚠️ That is the clearest evidence in the whole session for the proposition advanced in Lesson 4: **Assembly output is costly rather than binding, and a cost applied continuously to a state that needs legitimacy is a powerful instrument.**

### 3. The majority arrives, and what it did to voting

✅ **Fact:** between **1963 and 1968 no fewer than forty-three new members joined**, mainly the emerging states of Africa and Asia.

✅ **Fact:** the increasing membership during the 1970s **brought new problems**. ✅ **By 1970 members from the Third World - Africa and Asia - were in a clear majority.**

✅ **Fact:** as these nations began to **work more and more together**, it meant that **only they could be certain of having their resolutions passed**, and it became **increasingly difficult for both western and communist blocs to get their resolutions through the General Assembly**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the phrase "both western and communist blocs" is essential and is almost always dropped in candidate answers, where the episode is presented as a straightforward confrontation between the West and the Third World.

⚠️ The record says otherwise, and the correction improves the analysis considerably. ⚠️ A bloc that can block both Cold War alignments is not a client of either. ⚠️ That is exactly what non-alignment meant institutionally: not neutrality of sentiment but a voting capacity that neither superpower could command. ⚠️ And it explains a puzzle about the period - that criticism of the Assembly in the 1970s and 1980s came from both sides of the Cold War divide, each irritated for its own reasons.

⚠️ A second inference concerns what a majority can and cannot achieve. ⚠️ The new majority could pass resolutions, set agendas, shape the budget allocations examined in Lesson 13 and direct the programmes of the specialized agencies. ⚠️ It could not amend the Charter, because Article 108 requires the permanent members' ratification; it could not alter the Council; and it could not enforce anything. ⚠️ So the transformation was real in the domain of norms, programmes and money, and nil in the domain of security - which is precisely the asymmetry that keeps the reform question alive.

### 4. Two test votes, and the UNESCO crisis

✅ **Fact:** in **1974 UNESCO passed resolutions condemning "colonialism" and "imperialism"**.

✅ **Fact:** in **1979, when the western bloc introduced a General Assembly motion condemning terrorism, it was defeated by the Arab states and their supporters**.

✅ **Fact:** **friction reached crisis point in 1983 at the UNESCO General Congress.** ✅ Many western nations, including the United States, **accused UNESCO of being inefficient and wasteful and of having unacceptable political aims**.

✅ **Fact:** what **brought matters to a head was a proposal by some communist states for the internal licensing of foreign journalists**. ✅ According to the United States, **this would lead to a situation in which member states could exercise an effective censorship of each other's media organizations**.

✅ **Fact:** consequently the **Americans announced that they would withdraw from UNESCO on 1 January 1985**, since it had become **"hostile to the basic institutions of a free society, especially a free market and a free press"**. ✅ **Britain and Singapore withdrew in 1986 for similar reasons.** ✅ **Britain rejoined in 1997.** ✅ The United States **announced its return in September 2002 and rejoined in 2003**.

⚠️ **Date correction.** ⚠️ The core account records the American return simply as "2002". ⚠️ That is the year of the *announcement*; the return itself took effect in **2003**, at the General Conference. ⚠️ The two-stage sequence is the accurate one and is used throughout this session; the divergence is recorded in the source ledger.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this episode is much richer than the summary "the West left UNESCO over politicisation", and unpacking it is where the marks are.

⚠️ First, notice that the accusations were of **three distinct kinds** and that they are usually merged: inefficiency, waste and unacceptable political aims. ⚠️ The first two are managerial charges that could in principle be verified and remedied; the third is a disagreement about direction. ⚠️ Combining them is an effective political technique, because a managerial complaint lends objectivity to a political one - and an answer that separates them is doing the analysis the episode requires.

⚠️ Second, notice **who made the proposal that triggered the crisis**. ✅ The licensing proposal came from **some communist states**, not from the Third World majority. ⚠️ So the flashpoint was a Cold War disagreement about press freedom occurring inside an agency whose general direction the West already disliked. ⚠️ The episode is therefore a convergence of two separate conflicts, and presenting it as a simple North-South confrontation misstates it.

⚠️ Third, notice that the American objection was **substantive and not merely procedural**. ⚠️ Licensing of foreign journalists by host states is a mechanism with obvious censorship potential, and the stated ground - that it would allow states to censor one another's media - is a serious objection on its face. ⚠️ A balanced answer concedes this rather than treating the withdrawal as pure pique.

⚠️ Fourth, and most usefully, notice the **returns**. ✅ Britain rejoined in 1997, and the United States announced its return in September 2002 and rejoined in 2003. ⚠️ Withdrawal from a specialized agency is therefore reversible and was in fact reversed, which tells us something about the underlying calculation: the cost of absence eventually exceeded the cost of disagreement. ⚠️ That is the strongest available evidence that these bodies deliver something their critics value, since states that had denounced an agency as hostile to their basic institutions returned to it within a generation.

### 5. From majority to programme: the Global South builds a bargaining position

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a voting majority is a capacity, not a policy. ⚠️ What made the new majority consequential was that it built institutions of its own inside the system, through which a common position could be formed before votes were taken.

✅ **Fact:** the **United Nations Conference on Trade and Development first met in 1964 and soon became a permanent body**. ✅ Its role is to **encourage the development of industry in the Third World and to pressurize rich countries into buying Third World products**.

✅ **Fact:** the **Group of 77** was established on **15 June 1964**, at the first session of that Conference, and originally had seventy-seven members.

✅ **Fact:** in **1974** the General Assembly adopted the **Declaration on the Establishment of a New International Economic Order**, resolution **3201 (S-VI)**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the sequence is tighter than it looks and is worth stating as a causal chain. ⚠️ Decolonisation produced members; members produced a majority; the majority created a permanent forum devoted to its economic interests; the forum produced a caucus; and the caucus produced a programme. ⚠️ Each step took roughly a decade, and by 1974 the organisation contained a structured bloc with a permanent secretariat-backed agenda.

⚠️ Note also what the Conference's stated purpose implies. ✅ It exists to encourage Third World industry **and to pressurise rich countries into buying Third World products**. ⚠️ That is not a technical mandate; it is an explicitly redistributive one, and it was created by the organisation's own machinery at the request of its own majority. ⚠️ Lesson 13 records the consequence that most embarrassed the system: that this body and the trade-liberalisation machinery **even seem to be working against each other**, since one seeks to eliminate anything restricting trade while the other seeks preferential treatment for Third World products.

⚠️ For an Indian examination, one boundary must be observed here. ⚠️ The role of particular states in building the non-aligned and Group of 77 positions, their diplomatic strategies and their contemporary successors belong to the international-relations owner. ⚠️ What belongs here is the institutional fact: **the organisation supplied the arena, the voting rule and the permanent machinery through which a coalition of newly independent states converted numerical strength into an economic programme** - which is a claim about the institution, not about any member's foreign policy.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** the account here is too sympathetic to the new majority. A majority that can pass any resolution it likes and enforce none of them produces exactly what critics described: an arena for propaganda speeches. Resolutions condemning colonialism and imperialism in 1974 changed nothing; a programme for a new international economic order was never implemented; and the defeat of a motion condemning terrorism in 1979 is not evidence of independence but of a bloc protecting its own.

⚠️ **Reply:** each of the three examples is correctly stated and the conclusion still does not follow.

⚠️ On the 1974 resolutions: the claim that they changed nothing has to be set against the fact that they helped produce a withdrawal by the world's most powerful state from the agency that passed them, at the cost of that agency's budget and of the state's own influence within it. ⚠️ A resolution that has that effect is not a speech.

⚠️ On the economic programme: it is true that the demands were not met. ⚠️ But an unmet demand that is formally adopted by the representative organ of the international system becomes a permanent reference point in every subsequent negotiation, and the machinery created alongside it - a permanent conference body and a standing caucus - long outlived the specific programme.

⚠️ On the 1979 terrorism motion, the objection is at its strongest and the honest reply concedes ground. ⚠️ A bloc that can defeat a motion condemning terrorism is exercising exactly the kind of protective voting that its own critique of great-power behaviour condemns. ⚠️ The general lesson is uncomfortable and should be stated: **no configuration of voting power has produced disinterested outcomes, and the case for majority representation is not that majorities are wiser but that unrepresentative bodies cannot claim legitimacy.**

⚠️ The defensible formulation: **the transformed Assembly was neither the voice of humanity nor a propaganda arena; it was a genuinely representative body behaving as representative bodies behave - which is precisely why the complaint against it had to be dressed as a complaint about efficiency.**

### 7. Examination use

⚠️ This lesson serves questions on decolonisation, on representation and legitimacy, and on the Global South - and it supplies the historical foundation for the reform argument in Lesson 17.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Examine the role of the United Nations in the process of decolonisation after 1945.
> ⚠️ "The expansion of United Nations membership changed the organisation more than any reform has." Discuss.
> ⚠️ Assess the significance of the Third World majority in the General Assembly for the working of the United Nations.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first framing, resist narrating independence movements - that material belongs to the decolonisation owner and an examiner will notice the drift. ⚠️ The institutional answer is: the Charter supplied the principle in Article 1(2) and the obligation in Article 73; the trusteeship system supervised a completed transition in eleven territories; the 1960 Declaration supplied the general demand; and the resulting members then transformed the organ that had issued it. ⚠️ That is a complete answer in four moves, each institutional, and it leaves room for the qualification that the Assembly could change norms and programmes but not the Council.

### Traps

- ❌ Saying the Third World majority arrived in the 1950s. ✅ **Forty-three** joined between **1963 and 1968**, and members from the Third World were in a **clear majority by 1970**.
- ❌ Presenting the bloc as an anti-western formation only. ✅ It became **increasingly difficult for both western and communist blocs** to get resolutions through.
- ❌ Attributing the journalist-licensing proposal to the Third World bloc. ✅ It came from **some communist states**.
- ❌ Giving the wrong withdrawal dates. ✅ The **USA announced withdrawal from UNESCO on 1 January 1985**; **Britain and Singapore withdrew in 1986**; **Britain rejoined in 1997**; the **USA announced its return in September 2002 and rejoined in 2003**.
- ❌ Merging the three western charges against UNESCO. ✅ They were **inefficiency, waste and unacceptable political aims** - two managerial, one directional.
- ❌ Treating "political" as a neutral description. ✅ The account states that by "too political" the western nations **meant acting in a way the West disapproved of**.
- ❌ Confusing the trusteeship system with decolonisation generally. ✅ **Chapter XI and Article 73** cover all non-self-governing territories; trusteeship covered eleven.
- ⚠️ Importing independence-movement narratives. ⚠️ Those belong to the decolonisation owner; this lesson owns the institutional consequences.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Article 1(2):** friendly relations based on **equal rights and self-determination of peoples**. ✅ **Article 73:** administering powers accept **as a sacred trust** the obligation to promote self-government in non-self-governing territories.
2. ✅ **GA resolution 1514 (XV), 14 December 1960** - the Declaration on the Granting of Independence to Colonial Countries and Peoples.
3. ⚠️ The clauses were promissory notes issued in 1945 to a membership that did not yet exist.
4. ✅ **Forty-three new members, 1963-68**, mainly emerging African and Asian states; **clear Third World majority by 1970**.
5. ✅ Only that bloc could be certain of passing its resolutions; **both western and communist blocs** found it increasingly difficult to get theirs through.
6. ✅ **1974:** UNESCO resolutions condemning **colonialism** and **imperialism**. ✅ **1979:** a western motion condemning **terrorism** defeated by the Arab states and their supporters.
7. ✅ **1983 UNESCO General Congress crisis:** charges of **inefficiency, waste and unacceptable political aims**; trigger was a proposal by **some communist states** for the **internal licensing of foreign journalists**; the American objection was that this would permit states to censor one another's media.
8. ✅ **USA withdrew from 1 January 1985**, calling the agency hostile to the basic institutions of a free society, especially a free market and a free press; **Britain and Singapore withdrew in 1986**; **Britain rejoined 1997**; the **USA announced its return in September 2002 and rejoined in 2003**.
9. ⚠️ The returns are the strongest evidence that the agencies deliver something their critics value.
10. ✅ **UNCTAD:** first met **1964**, soon a permanent body; role is to encourage Third World industry and **pressurise rich countries into buying Third World products**.
11. ✅ **Group of 77** founded **15 June 1964** at UNCTAD's first session, originally seventy-seven members. ✅ **1974:** Declaration on the Establishment of a **New International Economic Order**, GA resolution **3201 (S-VI)**.
12. ⚠️ The causal chain: decolonisation -> members -> majority -> permanent forum -> caucus -> programme, at roughly a decade a step.
13. ⚠️ The transformation was real in norms, programmes and money and **nil in security**, because Article 108 puts the Charter beyond a simple majority.
14. ⚠️ The legitimacy lesson: complaints of "politicisation" track outcomes rather than procedures, and the case for representation is not that majorities are wiser but that unrepresentative bodies cannot claim legitimacy.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain how provisions written into the Charter in 1945 by colonial powers became the instruments of their own retreat, and state precisely what the transformed General Assembly could and could not change.

**Model answer:** Two provisions did the work. Article 1, Purpose 2 commits the organisation to developing friendly relations among nations based on respect for the principle of equal rights and self-determination of peoples. Article 73, in the Declaration Regarding Non-Self-Governing Territories, requires members administering territories whose peoples have not attained a full measure of self-government to recognise that the interests of the inhabitants are paramount and to accept as a sacred trust the obligation to promote their political advancement and the development of self-government. Both were signed by states that then governed most of Asia and Africa, at a moment when the peoples covered by them were unrepresented, so the obligations were undertaken towards a constituency that did not yet sit in the organisation. Once decolonisation began, the beneficiaries became members: forty-three joined between 1963 and 1968, and by 1970 Third World members were in a clear majority. The General Assembly had already adopted, in resolution 1514 (XV) of 14 December 1960, the Declaration on the Granting of Independence to Colonial Countries and Peoples. What the transformed Assembly could change was substantial: it could pass resolutions at will, since only that bloc could be certain of carrying them and both western and communist blocs found it increasingly hard to carry theirs; it could reshape the direction of the specialized agencies, as the UNESCO resolutions of 1974 and the crisis of 1983 showed; and it could build permanent machinery for its own economic programme through UNCTAD from 1964, the Group of 77 from June 1964 and the new international economic order declaration of 1974. What it could not change was the Charter itself, because Article 108 requires ratification by all five permanent members, and therefore it could not alter the composition of the Security Council, the veto or the enforcement system.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the western complaint about "politicisation" as a description of a procedural fault. The record is explicit that by calling the Third World bloc too political, the western nations meant that it was acting in a way the West disapproved of. The Assembly's procedures did not change at all in this period; only the majority did, which is why the complaint is evidence about perceptions of legitimacy rather than about the working of the organ.

## Lesson 12 - The other United Nations: rights, labour, health, food, education, children, refugees and relief

Progress: 12/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: why the non-military record is the larger half of the institution; the Human Rights Commission, the Universal Declaration and the Rights of the Child; the reporting system and the naming-and-shaming instrument; the International Labour Organization and its principles; the World Health Organization from cholera to smallpox to AIDS; the Food and Agriculture Organization; UNESCO's educational and scientific work; UNICEF from 1946 to the child health revolution; the Relief and Works Agency; the humanitarian coordination office and the tsunami response; what the record proves and what it does not

*⚠️ Count the boxes. The security record that occupies nine-tenths of most candidate answers occupies, on the organisation's own accounting, a minority of its work - and Lesson 13 supplies the figure that proves it, since more than ninety per cent of annual expenditure by 1980 went to economic and social activities. ⚠️ This is the single most effective way to rescue an answer that would otherwise be a list of peacekeeping missions, and it is also the honest description of what the institution is.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Every question on this topic invites a trap, and the trap is a tempting one. The security record is dramatic, it is full of named crises, and it supports strong verdicts. So candidates write about it, reach a balanced conclusion about the veto, and stop.

⚠️ The difficulty is that they have then written about a minority of the institution. ✅ The organisation's own account says so directly: although the peacekeeping and mediation role most often gets into the headlines, **the majority of its work is concerned with its less spectacular aims** of safeguarding human rights and encouraging economic, social, educational and cultural progress.

⚠️ There is also an analytical reason to take this material seriously rather than appending it as a paragraph of goodwill. ⚠️ The non-military record is the **control case** for every claim made about the security record. ⚠️ If the organisation were merely a mirror of great-power will, its performance ought to be uniformly poor. It is not. ⚠️ The difference between a body that cannot restrain a superpower and a body that eliminated smallpox needs an explanation, and the explanation is the one given in Lesson 1's agency discipline: **where action does not require coercing a powerful state, the institution acts with real capacity of its own.**

⚠️ So this lesson is not a list of agencies. It is the evidence that makes a balanced verdict possible.

### 2. Human rights: a declaration, a reporting system and an instrument of last resort

✅ **Fact:** the Human Rights Commission **works under the supervision of ECOSOC and tries to ensure that all governments treat their people in a civilized way**.

✅ **Fact:** a **thirty-point Universal Declaration of Human Rights was adopted by the General Assembly in 1948**; this means that **every person, no matter what country he or she lives in, should have certain basic rights**, the most important of which are the rights to:

- ✅ a standard of living high enough to keep the person and the family in good health;
- ✅ be free from slavery, racial discrimination, arrest and imprisonment without trial, and torture;
- ✅ have a fair trial in public and to be presumed innocent until proved guilty;
- ✅ move about freely in one's country and be able to leave the country;
- ✅ get married, have children, work, own property and vote in elections;
- ✅ have opinions and express them freely.

✅ **Fact:** the Declaration was adopted by General Assembly resolution **217 A (III)** on **10 December 1948**.

✅ **Fact:** later the Commission, **concerned about the plight of children in many countries, produced a Declaration of the Rights of the Child in 1959**, foremost among whose rights are **adequate food and medical care; free education; adequate opportunity for relaxation and play, to guard against excessive child labour; and protection from racial, religious and any other type of discrimination**.

✅ **Fact:** **all member governments are expected to produce a report every three years on the state of human rights in their country.** ✅ **However, the problem is that many states do not produce the reports and they ignore the terms of the Declarations.** ✅ **When this happens, all the organisation can do is publicize countries where the most flagrant violations of human rights take place, and hope that pressure of world opinion will influence the governments concerned.**

✅ **Fact:** it **campaigned against apartheid in South Africa** and against **General Pinochet's brutal treatment of political prisoners in Chile**. ✅ **Mary Robinson**, a former president of the Irish Republic and Commissioner for Human Rights **from 1997 until 2002**, **worked hard to raise world awareness of the problems by naming and shaming guilty states**. ✅ **Unfortunately she made some powerful enemies by her outspoken criticism of their human rights records - among them Russia, China and the USA, all permanent members of the Security Council.** ✅ **Secretary-General Annan was pleased with her work and wanted her to serve another term**; however, **she was replaced by Sergio Vieira de Mello, and it was widely reported that her second term had been blocked by the USA.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Robinson episode is the single most useful piece of evidence in this lesson and deserves to be used deliberately rather than mentioned.

⚠️ Consider what it demonstrates. ⚠️ First, that the human-rights machinery is capable of real effect, or it would not have provoked opposition. ⚠️ Second, that the opposition it provoked came from **all three of the permanent members named**, which cut across the Cold War alignment and across every ideological division in the system. ⚠️ Third, that the method of retaliation was personnel rather than argument: the office was not abolished, the reports were not refuted, and the Commissioner was simply not reappointed.

⚠️ That third point connects directly to Lesson 6 and forward to Lesson 14. ⚠️ The Korean crisis ended a Secretary-General's tenure through Soviet pressure; a human-rights commissionership ended, it was widely reported, through American pressure. ⚠️ **The vulnerability is structural and non-partisan: senior international officials serve at the sufferance of states that hold appointment vetoes, and the consequence is felt precisely by the officials who use their offices most vigorously.**

### 3. Labour standards: principles, prizes and the compliance problem

✅ **Fact:** the **International Labour Organization operates from its headquarters in Geneva** and works on the principles that: ✅ every person is entitled to a job; ✅ there should be equal opportunities for everybody to get jobs, **irrespective of race, sex or religion**; ✅ there should be **minimum standards of decent working conditions**; ✅ **workers should have the right to organize themselves into unions and other associations in order to negotiate for better conditions and pay** - collective bargaining; and ✅ there should be **full social security provision for all workers**, such as unemployment, health and maternity benefits.

✅ **Fact:** the ILO **does excellent work providing help for countries trying to improve working conditions, and it was awarded the Nobel Peace Prize in 1969**. ✅ It **sends experts out to demonstrate new equipment and techniques, sets up training centres in developing countries, and runs the International Centre for Advanced Technology and Vocational Training in Turin**, which provides high-level training for people from all over the Third World.

✅ **Fact:** again, though, the ILO - **like the Human Rights Commission** - **is always faced with the problem of what to do when governments ignore the rules**. ✅ **Many governments, including those of communist countries and of Latin American countries such as Chile, Argentina and Mexico, would not allow workers to organize trade unions.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the ILO is also the answer to a question examiners like to ask about institutional continuity. ✅ Lesson 1 recorded that all the specialized agencies **except the ILO, founded in 1919**, were set up in 1945 or later. ⚠️ So the one agency older than the organisation itself is the labour agency, created with the League and surviving it - which is evidence that functional international cooperation can outlive the political architecture it was attached to. ⚠️ That is a genuinely useful observation for a comparison question, because it shows the League's failure was specific to collective security rather than general to international organisation.

### 4. Health: the clearest case of institutional capacity

✅ **Fact:** the World Health Organization **is one of the organisation's most successful agencies**. ✅ It aims to bring the world to a point where **all its peoples are not just free of disease, but are "at a high level of health"**.

✅ **Fact:** one of its **first jobs was to tackle a cholera epidemic in Egypt in 1947** which threatened to spread through Africa and the Middle East; **quick action by a UN team soon brought the epidemic under control and it was eliminated in a few weeks**. ✅ It now **keeps a permanent cholera vaccine bank** in case of further outbreaks, and **wages a continual battle against other diseases such as malaria, tuberculosis and leprosy**. ✅ It **provides money to train doctors, nurses and other health workers for developing countries, keeps governments informed about new drugs and provides free contraceptive pills for women in Third World countries**.

✅ **Fact:** its most striking achievement was the eradication of **smallpox**. ✅ The **last known naturally occurring case was recorded in 1977**; ✅ a **Global Commission certified the world free of smallpox in December 1979**; and ✅ the **Thirty-third World Health Assembly, on 8 May 1980, solemnly declared that the world and its peoples had won freedom from smallpox**, in resolution WHA33.3.

⚠️ **Date correction.** ⚠️ The core account says the agency "eliminated smallpox in the 1980s". ⚠️ The eradication campaign closed in the 1970s - the last natural case in 1977 - and only the *certification* and the *declaration* fall in 1979 and 1980. ⚠️ An answer that dates the achievement to the 1980s has attributed to that decade work that was completed in the previous one, and the precise three-stage sequence is what a close-option question tests.

✅ **Fact:** at the same time the agency seemed well on the way towards eliminating malaria, **but during the 1970s a new strain of malaria appeared which had developed a resistance to anti-malaria drugs**, and research into new anti-malaria drugs became a priority. ✅ In **March 2000 it was reported that the problem of tuberculosis was growing worse - killing two million people every year**.

✅ **Fact:** the **most serious world health problem in recent years has been the AIDS epidemic**. ✅ The organisation **has done excellent work collecting evidence and statistics, producing reports and putting pressure on pharmaceutical companies to reduce prices of drugs to treat the condition**. ✅ In **June 2001 the global AIDS fund was set up, which aimed to raise $10 billion a year to fight the disease**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the health record contains the strongest single argument available against the sceptical reading of the whole institution, and it is worth setting out as an argument rather than as praise.

⚠️ The eradication of smallpox required simultaneous action in dozens of states with different political systems, many of them hostile to one another, over decades, with surveillance, supply chains, trained personnel and shared data. ⚠️ No state could have organised it; no alliance would have financed it for a disease that had ceased to be a threat to its own population; and no commercial actor had an incentive to complete it. ⚠️ It is, in the strict sense, an achievement only an impartial universal body could have delivered.

⚠️ Equally instructive is the drug-resistant malaria case, because it shows the limit. ⚠️ The agency was well on the way to eliminating malaria when biology changed the problem. ⚠️ That is a failure of neither politics nor administration, and it is a useful reminder that some limits on the institution have nothing to do with the veto at all.

⚠️ The AIDS material shows a third mode of operation: **collecting evidence, producing reports and putting pressure on pharmaceutical companies**. ⚠️ Here the agency is acting neither as a service provider nor as a norm-setter but as a countervailing power against private actors, which is a role the Charter never contemplated and which the organisation acquired by necessity.

### 5. Food, education, children and refugees

✅ **Fact:** the **Food and Agriculture Organization aims to raise living standards by encouraging improvements in agricultural production**. ✅ It was **responsible for introducing new varieties of maize and rice which have a higher yield and are less susceptible to disease**. ✅ Its experts **show people in poor countries how to increase food production by the use of fertilizers, new techniques and new machinery**, and **cash is provided to fund new projects**. ✅ Its **main problem is having to deal with emergencies caused by drought, floods, civil war and other disasters**, when food supplies must be rushed into a country as quickly as possible. ✅ The judgement recorded is that it **has done an excellent job, and there is no doubt that many more people would have died from starvation and malnutrition without its work**.

✅ **Fact:** **UNESCO**, operating from its **headquarters in Paris**, **does its best to encourage the spread of literacy**; it also **fosters international co-operation between scientists, scholars and artists in all fields, working on the theory that the best way to avoid war is by educating people's minds in the pursuit of peace**. ✅ Much of its time and resources are spent **setting up schools and teacher-training colleges in under-developed countries**. ✅ Examples of one-off projects include the **International Hydrological Decade (1965-75)**, during which it helped finance research into world water resources, and its part in **repairing and restoring damaged art treasures and historic buildings after the 1968 floods in Florence**.

✅ **Fact:** **UNICEF was founded originally in 1946 to help children left homeless by the Second World War.** ✅ It dealt with this problem so efficiently that it was decided to make it a **permanent agency and the word "emergency" was dropped from its title in 1953**. ✅ Its new function was to **help improve the health and living standards of children all over the world, especially in poorer countries**, and it **works closely with the WHO, setting up health centres, training health workers and running health education and sanitation schemes**. ✅ In **1983 it launched its "child health revolution" campaign**, designed to reduce the child death rate **by simple methods such as encouraging breastfeeding, which is more hygienic than bottle-feeding, and immunizing babies against common diseases such as measles, diphtheria, polio and tetanus**.

✅ **Fact:** the **Relief and Works Agency was set up in 1950 to deal with the problem of Arab refugees from Palestine** who were forced to leave their homes when Palestine was divided to form the new state of Israel. ✅ It **did a remarkable job providing basic food, clothing, shelter and medical supplies**. ✅ **Later, as it became clear that the refugee camps were going to be permanent, it began to build schools, hospitals, houses and training centres to enable refugees to get jobs and make the camps self-supporting.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three institutional patterns run through these four bodies and naming them converts a description into analysis.

⚠️ **Pattern one - the emergency that becomes permanent.** UNICEF was created for the children of one war and became a permanent agency for children everywhere; the refugee agency was created for a displacement expected to be temporary and became a system of schools, hospitals and housing. ⚠️ In both cases the institution outlived its founding emergency because the underlying condition did not end. ⚠️ This is the institutional signature of political failure elsewhere: **a permanent humanitarian agency is usually the shadow cast by an unresolved political question.**

⚠️ **Pattern two - technical transfer as the dominant method.** The food agency's higher-yielding varieties, the labour agency's Turin training centre, the health agency's training of doctors and nurses, the education agency's teacher-training colleges: the characteristic activity across the system is not relief but the transfer of technique. ⚠️ That is a slower and less visible business than emergency aid and it accounts for most of the record.

⚠️ **Pattern three - the doctrine behind the education agency.** ✅ UNESCO works on the theory that **the best way to avoid war is by educating people's minds in the pursuit of peace**. ⚠️ That is a causal claim about the origins of conflict, and it is the same claim that appears in the organisation's second founding aim - removing the causes of conflict through economic, social, educational, scientific and cultural progress. ⚠️ The agencies are therefore not a charitable annexe to the security system; they are the **preventive half of the same theory of peace**, and an answer that presents them that way has understood the Charter's design rather than merely listing its bodies.

### 6. Humanitarian coordination and the tsunami

✅ **Fact:** the Office for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs **began life as the Department of Humanitarian Affairs, set up in 1991** to enable the organisation to respond more effectively to natural disasters and **"complex emergencies" - the phrase for human disasters caused by wars and other political events**. ✅ Its **functions were expanded in 1998 to include the co-ordination of responses to all humanitarian disasters and projects for human development**, and it **assumed its present title** at that time. ✅ It had a **staff of some 860 members, some based in New York, some in Geneva and some working in the field**.

✅ **Fact:** much valuable relief work was done in **a whole series of crisis situations caused by earthquakes, hurricanes and floods**, with help mainly needed **in poor countries with less developed infrastructures and high population densities**. ✅ Statistics suggested that **in 2003 alone, some 200 million victims of natural disasters and 45 million victims of "complex emergencies" received aid**, either supplied directly or organised by the organisation. ✅ **However, a recurring criticism was that it lacked the power and the resources to operate as effectively as it might.**

✅ **Fact:** the **greatest challenge came at the beginning of 2005 in what became known as the tsunami disaster**. ✅ On **Boxing Day 2004, two huge earthquakes occurred in the Indian Ocean**, triggering a series of massive tidal waves. ✅ **No effective warning system existed**, and within hours the waves were battering the shores of many countries around the Indian Ocean, **including Indonesia, India, the Maldive Islands, Sri Lanka, Thailand, Malaysia and even Somalia on the east coast of Africa**. ✅ **At least 150,000 people were killed and thousands more were missing**; worst affected were **Indonesia, Sri Lanka and Thailand**, where in some coastal areas entire towns and villages had been destroyed.

✅ **Fact:** the response **from around the world was heartening**: ordinary people gave unstintingly, foreign governments promised enormous amounts of cash, **eleven states sent troops, ships and aircraft**, and **over 400 non-government agencies and charities** - including Christian Aid, the Red Cross, Red Crescent, the Salvation Army, Oxfam and Medecins sans Frontieres - **got involved within a few days**. ✅ An Oxfam spokesman said the organisation **was doing as good a job as anybody could reasonably expect in the horrific circumstances**, and that they were grateful for the **plain-speaking leadership of Mr Jan Egeland, the Emergency Relief Coordinator, and of Secretary-General Kofi Annan**. ✅ But **a long-term operation lay ahead: after saving tens of thousands from death by starvation and disease, the next step was to rebuild communities and restore infrastructures.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the coordination office is the clearest illustration of a role that is easy to undervalue because it produces nothing visible. ⚠️ Its function is not to deliver aid but to prevent a very large number of independent actors - eleven states with military assets, over 400 agencies and charities, and the governments of half a dozen affected countries - from duplicating, obstructing and misallocating one another's efforts. ⚠️ A modest staff, distributed across two headquarters and the field, exists to make other people's resources usable.

⚠️ And the recorded criticism attaches to exactly that design: **it lacked the power and the resources to operate as effectively as it might.** ⚠️ A coordinating body with no authority over the bodies it coordinates depends entirely on their willingness to be coordinated, which is the humanitarian analogue of the security problem examined throughout this session.

⚠️ One further point belongs to an Indian answer and is properly stated here rather than elsewhere. ✅ India appears in this record as an affected state in 2004-05. ⚠️ India's wider role in the system - as a contributor of personnel to peacekeeping, as a member of the Global South coalitions of Lesson 11 and as a claimant in the reform debate of Lesson 17 - is handled where it belongs, and live Indian institutional positions remain with the Indian foreign-policy and international-relations owners.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** the non-military record is being used here to rescue a poor security record, and the rescue is illegitimate. Health campaigns and literacy programmes are worthy, but the organisation was established to prevent war. Judging it by the number of children immunised is like judging a fire brigade by the quality of its canteen.

⚠️ **Reply:** the analogy is sharp and it fails on the organisation's own constitutive documents, which is the decisive answer.

✅ The founding aims are three, not one. ✅ The second is to **remove the causes of conflict by encouraging economic, social, educational, scientific and cultural progress throughout the world, especially in under-developed countries**, and the third is to **safeguard the rights of all individual human beings, and the rights of peoples and nations**. ⚠️ Two of the three founding aims therefore describe precisely the work this lesson covers. ⚠️ A fire brigade's constitution does not mention its canteen; this organisation's constitution devotes two of its three aims to what the objection calls the canteen.

⚠️ Two further points reinforce the reply. ⚠️ First, the expenditure evidence in Lesson 13: more than ninety per cent of annual expenditure by 1980 went to economic and social activities. ⚠️ An institution that spends nine-tenths of its money on something is not doing that thing incidentally. ⚠️ Second, the theory behind the aims: the education agency's stated premise is that the best way to avoid war is by educating people's minds in the pursuit of peace, which makes the social work a **preventive security strategy** rather than an alternative to one.

⚠️ The concession that keeps this honest: ⚠️ the preventive theory is a hypothesis, not a demonstrated causal law, and it is impossible to show how many wars literacy and public health have prevented. ⚠️ So the objection is right that the social record cannot be counted as security success; it is wrong that the social record is extraneous to the mandate.

⚠️ The defensible formulation: **the organisation should be judged against all three of its founding aims, and the honest verdict is uneven by design - weak where it must coerce the powerful, strong where it must coordinate the willing.**

### 8. Examination use

⚠️ This lesson supplies the balance material without which no evaluative answer on this topic can reach the upper band.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "The greatest strength of the United Nations lies outside the battlefield." Discuss.
> ⚠️ "There can be little doubt that the social, economic and humanitarian work of the United Nations has been far more successful and valuable than its peacekeeping role." Assess the validity of this verdict.
> ⚠️ Examine the role of the specialized agencies of the United Nations in promoting human development.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the second framing, note that the quotation invites agreement and that unqualified agreement scores poorly. ⚠️ The upper-band structure is: accept the empirical claim with the expenditure evidence and two or three concrete achievements; then refuse the implied comparison, because the two arms face different problems - one must coordinate willing actors and the other must coerce unwilling great powers; then close by observing that the social work is not an alternative to the security mandate but the preventive half of the same theory, stated in the second founding aim. ⚠️ That structure engages the verdict rather than merely voting on it.

### Traps

- ❌ Calling every body in this lesson a specialized agency. ✅ **WHO, FAO, UNESCO and the ILO** are specialized agencies; **UNICEF** is a fund; **UNRWA** is a General Assembly-created agency; **OCHA** is part of the Secretariat's machinery; the **Human Rights Commission** worked under ECOSOC.
- ❌ Dating the Universal Declaration wrongly. ✅ **Thirty points, adopted by the General Assembly in 1948** - resolution **217 A (III), 10 December 1948**. ✅ The **Rights of the Child** Declaration is **1959**.
- ❌ Saying states must report annually. ✅ Governments are expected to report **every three years**; many do not report at all.
- ❌ Omitting the Robinson episode. ✅ Commissioner **1997-2002**, made enemies of **Russia, China and the USA**, and was replaced despite the Secretary-General wanting her reappointed; it was **widely reported** that the USA blocked a second term.
- ❌ Getting the ILO's date wrong. ✅ Founded **1919**, the only specialized agency older than the organisation; **Nobel Peace Prize 1969**; training centre at **Turin**.
- ❌ Saying WHO eradicated malaria. ✅ It eradicated **smallpox**; a **drug-resistant malaria strain appeared in the 1970s** and research into new drugs became a priority.
- ❌ Dating smallpox eradication to the 1980s. ✅ **Last natural case 1977**; **certified December 1979**; **declared by the World Health Assembly on 8 May 1980** in resolution WHA33.3.
- ❌ Misdating UNICEF. ✅ Founded **1946**; the word **"emergency" dropped in 1953**; the **child health revolution** campaign is **1983**.
- ❌ Confusing the humanitarian office's dates. ✅ **Department of Humanitarian Affairs 1991**; functions expanded and present title assumed in **1998**; staff of **some 860**.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The **majority of the organisation's work** concerns human rights and economic, social, educational and cultural progress, not peacekeeping.
2. ✅ **Human Rights Commission** under **ECOSOC**; **thirty-point Universal Declaration, 1948**, resolution **217 A (III), 10 December 1948**; **Declaration of the Rights of the Child, 1959**.
3. ✅ Reports expected **every three years**; many states do not report and ignore the terms; the only remaining instrument is **publicity and world opinion**.
4. ✅ Campaigns against **apartheid** in South Africa and **Pinochet's** treatment of political prisoners in Chile.
5. ✅ **Mary Robinson**, Commissioner **1997-2002**, named and shamed; made enemies of **Russia, China and the USA**; replaced by **Sergio Vieira de Mello** despite the Secretary-General's wish, with the block **widely reported** to be American.
6. ✅ **ILO**, Geneva, **founded 1919** - the only specialized agency older than the organisation; five principles including the right to organise and bargain collectively; **Nobel Peace Prize 1969**; **Turin** training centre; obstructed where governments forbid trade unions.
7. ✅ **WHO:** Egyptian cholera **1947** contained in weeks; permanent **cholera vaccine bank**; **smallpox - last natural case 1977, certified December 1979, declared by the World Health Assembly on 8 May 1980 (WHA33.3)**; **drug-resistant malaria** in the **1970s**; TB worsening as reported in **March 2000**; AIDS work through evidence, reports and pressure on drug prices; **global AIDS fund, June 2001, aiming at $10 billion a year**.
8. ✅ **FAO:** higher-yielding, disease-resistant **maize and rice**; fertilizers, techniques, machinery and project funding; emergency food supply its main problem.
9. ✅ **UNESCO**, Paris: literacy; scientific and scholarly co-operation; schools and teacher-training colleges; **International Hydrological Decade 1965-75**; restoration after the **Florence floods of 1968**.
10. ✅ **UNICEF:** founded **1946** for children left homeless by the war; **"emergency" dropped in 1953**; works with WHO; **1983 child health revolution** - breastfeeding and immunization against measles, diphtheria, polio and tetanus.
11. ✅ **UNRWA:** set up **1950** for Arab refugees from Palestine; food, clothing, shelter and medical supplies; then **schools, hospitals, houses and training centres** as the camps proved permanent.
12. ✅ **OCHA:** **Department of Humanitarian Affairs, 1991**; expanded and renamed **1998**; staff of **some 860** across New York, Geneva and the field; **2003** aid to some **200 million** disaster victims and **45 million** victims of complex emergencies; criticised for lacking power and resources.
13. ✅ **Tsunami:** **Boxing Day 2004**, two Indian Ocean earthquakes, **no effective warning system**; at least **150,000 killed**; **eleven states** sent troops, ships and aircraft and **over 400** agencies were involved within days; **Jan Egeland** as Emergency Relief Coordinator.
14. ⚠️ Three institutional patterns: the **emergency that becomes permanent**; **technical transfer** as the dominant method; and the agencies as the **preventive half of the Charter's theory of peace**.
15. ⚠️ The structural vulnerability of senior officials: Robinson's non-renewal in 2002 and Lie's early retirement in the 1950s show the same appointment mechanism operating from opposite political directions.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate argues that the organisation's social and humanitarian work is irrelevant to assessing whether it has fulfilled its purpose. Refute this using the organisation's own founding documents and two pieces of evidence, and state the one concession the refutation must make.

**Model answer:** The argument fails at the level of the mandate. The organisation has three founding aims, not one: to preserve peace and eliminate war; to remove the causes of conflict by encouraging economic, social, educational, scientific and cultural progress throughout the world, especially in under-developed countries; and to safeguard the rights of all individual human beings and the rights of peoples and nations. Two of the three describe exactly the work in question, so social and humanitarian activity is constitutive of the purpose rather than incidental to it. The first supporting evidence is expenditure: by 1980 more than ninety per cent of annual expenditure was devoted to economic and social activities, which is not how an institution allocates resources to a peripheral function. The second is capacity: the eradication of smallpox, whose last naturally occurring case was recorded in 1977 and whose eradication was certified in December 1979 and declared by the World Health Assembly on 8 May 1980, required simultaneous, sustained action across states with hostile relations and different political systems, which no single state could have organised and no alliance would have financed, and it was accompanied by a whole record of similar work - the containment of the Egyptian cholera epidemic of 1947 within weeks, higher-yielding maize and rice varieties, teacher-training colleges, and the child health revolution campaign of 1983. There is also a doctrinal link: the education agency works on the premise that the best way to avoid war is by educating people's minds in the pursuit of peace, which makes this activity the preventive half of the same theory of peace rather than an alternative to it. The concession the refutation must make is that the preventive theory is a hypothesis and not a demonstrated law - it is impossible to show how many wars public health and literacy have prevented - so the social record cannot be counted as security success, only as fulfilment of two separate founding aims.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating all the bodies in this record as legally identical. WHO, FAO, UNESCO and the ILO are specialized agencies with their own constitutions and memberships, brought into relationship with the organisation; UNICEF is a fund created by the General Assembly; UNRWA is a General Assembly-created agency with a distinct mandate; the humanitarian coordination office grew out of a Secretariat department established in 1991; and the Human Rights Commission operated under ECOSOC. An answer that calls all of them agencies of the United Nations has lost the category distinction that the structure question is usually designed to test.

## Lesson 13 - ECOSOC, the financial agencies and the money: coordination, overlap and the arrears crisis

Progress: 13/18 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: ECOSOC's composition and the twenty-seven-to-fifty-four discrepancy; its commissions and the coordination of around thirty agencies; more than ninety per cent of expenditure by 1980; the financial agencies and the conditionality quarrel; trade liberalisation against preference; the legal categories examiners test; the duplication problem; the funding system and the arrears crisis of the 1980s and 1990s; the development mandate from the Declaration of 1948 to the goals of 2000 and 2015
