*⚠️ Read the diagram as a single argument rather than as three panels. The coordination organ sits at the top of a system of about thirty bodies; that system consumes more than nine-tenths of the money; the money comes from assessed contributions that a handful of states supply in bulk; and those states have repeatedly used the supply as leverage. ⚠️ The dispute recorded in 1986 is therefore not a quarrel about accounting. It is a constitutional argument about whether an organisation of sovereign equals can be financed by unequal contributors without becoming accountable to them.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Lesson 12 described a very large amount of work spread across a very large number of bodies. Two questions follow immediately, and they are the questions this lesson answers.

⚠️ The first is **who holds it together?** Around thirty agencies and commissions, each with its own constitution, budget, membership, headquarters and professional culture, working on overlapping problems in the same countries, is not a system unless something coordinates it. ⚠️ The Charter's answer is ECOSOC, and the interesting question is how much coordination an organ with recommendatory powers can actually achieve.

⚠️ The second is **who pays, and what do they get for it?** An organisation with no revenue of its own, assessing contributions by majority vote of sovereign equals while depending on a handful of large contributors, has a structural instability built into its finances. ⚠️ That instability produced the crisis of the 1980s, the reform of 1987 and the warning of 1993, and it remains the most reliable predictor of what the organisation can and cannot undertake.

### 2. ECOSOC: composition, and a discrepancy that must be stated rather than smoothed

✅ **Fact:** ECOSOC is elected by the General Assembly, **with one-third retiring each year**. ✅ It **organizes projects concerned with health, education and other social and economic matters**.

✅ **Fact:** its task is so enormous that it works through **regional commissions** as well as **commissions on population problems, drugs problems, human rights and the status of women**.

⚠️ **Number and name correction.** ⚠️ The core account records **four** regional commissions - Europe, Latin America, Africa, and "Asia and the Far East". ✅ There are **five**, and one of the names it uses is obsolete:

| Commission | Region | Note |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **ECE** | Europe | ✅ Established 1947 |
| ✅ **ECLAC** | Latin America and the Caribbean | ✅ Established 1948 |
| ✅ **ECA** | Africa | ✅ Established 1958 |
| ✅ **ESCAP** | Asia and the Pacific | ✅ Created in 1947 as ECAFE, the Economic Commission for Asia and the Far East, and **renamed ESCAP in 1974** |
| ✅ **ESCWA** | Western Asia | ✅ **Established in 1973** - after the core account's four-commission description was accurate, which is precisely why it is missing from it |

⚠️ So the account's figure is not a simple mistake: it describes the position before 1973, and "Asia and the Far East" is the pre-1974 name. ⚠️ The corrected number and names are used throughout this session and the divergence is recorded in the source ledger. ❌ Do not carry "four regional commissions" into an answer.

✅ **Fact:** it also **co-ordinates the work of an astonishing array of other commissions and specialized agencies, around thirty in all**, among the best known being the **ILO, WHO, FAO, UNESCO, UNICEF and UNRWA**.

✅ **Fact:** its scope **expanded in such a remarkable way that by 1980 more than ninety per cent of the organisation's annual expenditure was devoted to ECOSOC activities**.

⚠️ **Discrepancy, stated rather than smoothed.** ✅ The core account gives ECOSOC **twenty-seven members**. ✅ The Charter as amended, and the organisation's current description of its own main bodies, give **fifty-four members elected by the General Assembly for overlapping three-year terms**.

✅ **Fact:** the explanatory note attached to the Charter resolves how the numbers changed. ✅ An amendment to Article 61 adopted in 1963 and in force on **31 August 1965** enlarged ECOSOC **from eighteen to twenty-seven**; a further amendment adopted in 1971 and in force on **24 September 1973** increased it **from twenty-seven to fifty-four**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the figure of twenty-seven is therefore **not an error; it is out of date**, and it corresponds exactly to the position between 1965 and 1973. ⚠️ The correct handling in an answer is to use **fifty-four** as the current membership and to be aware that older accounts give twenty-seven or eighteen for the periods when those figures were accurate. ⚠️ This session does not reconcile the two by choosing one silently, because the discrepancy is itself instructive: it shows that a general history's structural description is a snapshot of its own moment, and that structure questions require the current constitutional position rather than a textbook's.

⚠️ There is also a substantive point buried in the enlargement history. ⚠️ ECOSOC tripled in size between 1965 and 1973, which is the same period in which the membership transformation of Lesson 11 was completing. ⚠️ The economic and social organ was therefore enlarged twice, rapidly, to accommodate the new majority, while the Security Council was enlarged once and never touched again. ⚠️ **The system expanded representation exactly where representation carried no enforcement power.**

### 3. What coordination actually means here

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the word "coordination" conceals the central difficulty, and stating the difficulty precisely is worth a mark.

✅ The Charter's mechanism is set out in Articles 62 to 64. ✅ ECOSOC may make or initiate studies and reports and make recommendations to the Assembly, to members and to the specialized agencies; ✅ it may enter into **agreements with the agencies defining the terms on which they are brought into relationship with the organisation**, subject to Assembly approval; ✅ it may **co-ordinate their activities through consultation with and recommendations to those agencies**; and ✅ it may **obtain regular reports** from them.

⚠️ Read that list and notice what is absent. ⚠️ There is no power to instruct, no power to allocate an agency's budget, no power to merge agencies and no power to resolve a conflict of mandates. ⚠️ Coordination here means **relationship agreements, consultation, recommendation and reporting** - which is to say, influence rather than authority.

⚠️ This explains the duplication problem in the next section far better than any complaint about bureaucracy. ⚠️ The agencies are separate international organisations with their own member states and their own governing bodies. ⚠️ A coordinating council composed of a subset of those same states, exercising recommendatory power, cannot compel two agencies with overlapping mandates to stop overlapping, because each agency answers to its own membership. ⚠️ **The overlap is a consequence of the system's legal architecture, not of administrative laziness.**

### 4. The duplication problem, and the contradiction at its sharpest

✅ **Fact:** there **is a waste of effort and resources among the agencies**. ✅ Some of them **sometimes seem to duplicate each other's work**; critics claim that **the WHO and the FAO overlap too much**. ✅ The FAO was **criticised in 1984 for spending too much on administration and not enough on improving agricultural systems**.

✅ **Fact:** **GATT and UNCTAD even seem to be working against each other: GATT tries to eliminate tariffs and anything else that restricts trade, whereas UNCTAD tries to get preferential treatment for the products of Third World countries.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the last of these is not duplication at all, and treating it as such loses the point. ⚠️ Two bodies inside the same system were pursuing **mutually inconsistent objectives**, and both were doing exactly what their own memberships had instructed them to do.

⚠️ The explanation is the one built in Lesson 11. ⚠️ The trade-liberalisation machinery of 1947 was created by and for the industrial economies of the immediate post-war period. ⚠️ The trade-and-development body of 1964 was created by and for the newly independent majority, with the explicit purpose of pressurising rich countries into buying Third World products. ⚠️ The system did not choose between the two theories of trade; it institutionalised both, at an interval of seventeen years, because the membership that generated the second had not existed when the first was built.

⚠️ That produces the most useful general statement available about the whole economic and social system: **its incoherence is a record of its own democratisation.** ⚠️ An organisation that admits new members on equal terms and lets them create institutions will end up with institutions that contradict one another, and the alternative - refusing the new majority its own machinery - would have been incoherent in a different and worse way.

### 5. The financial agencies and the conditionality quarrel

✅ **Fact:** the **International Monetary Fund is designed to foster co-operation between nations in order to encourage the growth of trade and the full development of nations' economic potential**. ✅ It **allows short-term loans to countries in financial difficulties, provided that their economic policies meet with its approval and that they are prepared to change policies if it thinks it necessary**.

✅ **Fact:** by the **mid-1970s many Third World nations were heavily in debt**, and in **1977 the Fund set up an emergency fund**. ✅ However, **there was a great deal of resentment among the poorer nations when the Board of Governors - dominated by the rich western countries, especially the USA, which provides most of the cash - began to attach conditions to the loans**. ✅ **Jamaica and Tanzania, for example, were required to change their socialist policies before loans were allowed**, and this was **seen by many as unacceptable interference in the internal affairs of member states**.

✅ **Fact:** the **International Bank for Reconstruction and Development - the World Bank - provides loans for specific development projects, such as building dams to generate electricity, and introducing new agricultural techniques and family planning campaigns**. ✅ Again, **the USA, which provides the largest share of the cash for the bank, controls its decisions**. ✅ When **Poland and Czechoslovakia applied for loans, they were both refused because they were communist states**; both **resigned from the Bank and from the Fund - Poland in 1950 and Czechoslovakia in 1954**.

✅ **Fact:** the **General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade was first signed in 1947** when member states agreed to reduce some of their tariffs in order to encourage international trade; members continued to meet **under the supervision of ECOSOC** to keep tariffs as low as possible. ✅ **In January 1995 GATT became the World Trade Organization**, whose aim was to **liberalize and monitor world trade and to resolve trade disputes**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the conditionality quarrel deserves careful handling, because it connects directly to a Charter principle taught in Lesson 3 and because it is very easy to write about it in a partisan way that costs marks.

⚠️ The claim made by the poorer states was that conditionality amounted to **unacceptable interference in the internal affairs of member states**, which is the language of Article 2(7). ⚠️ The counter-claim, implicit in the arrangement, is that a lender may attach terms to a loan, and that a state which prefers not to accept the terms need not borrow. ⚠️ Both propositions are defensible, and the disagreement is genuine rather than a matter of one side being confused.

⚠️ What an answer can do with this is identify the **structural asymmetry** that makes the disagreement intractable. ⚠️ In the Assembly, each member has one vote regardless of contribution. ⚠️ In the financial bodies, decisions are controlled by the states that provide most of the money. ⚠️ So the same governments sit in two institutions inside one system under opposite rules, and each set of rules is invoked when it suits. ⚠️ The Polish and Czechoslovak resignations of 1950 and 1954 and the Jamaican and Tanzanian conditions of the 1970s are the same structural fact producing complaints from opposite ideological directions.

⚠️ **Category discipline, stated once and for all.** ⚠️ This is the point in the session where the legal categories must be fixed, because the trade and finance bodies are where they are most often confused.

| Body | Category | Note |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **IMF** | Specialized agency | ✅ Brought into relationship with the organisation under Article 57 |
| ✅ **World Bank** | Specialized agency | ✅ Same relationship mechanism; separate membership and governance |
| ✅ **UNCTAD** | A body of the United Nations | ✅ First met 1964 and soon became a permanent body |
| ✅ **WTO** | **Not** a United Nations specialized agency | ✅ Part of the wider multilateral trade system; GATT became the WTO in January 1995 |
| ✅ **ILO, WHO, FAO, UNESCO** | Specialized agencies | ✅ ILO alone predates the organisation, founded 1919 |
| ✅ **UNICEF** | A fund | ✅ Created by the Assembly; "emergency" dropped from the title in 1953 |
| ✅ **UNRWA** | An agency created by the General Assembly | ✅ Distinct mandate, established 1950 |
| ✅ **ICJ** | Principal organ | ✅ Its Statute is annexed to the Charter |
| ✅ **ICC** | **Not** a United Nations organ | ✅ A separate treaty-based court; the Rome Statute, **adopted 17 July 1998**, entered into force on **1 July 2002** |

⚠️ The two rows marked "not" are the ones examiners reach for. ⚠️ Learn them as a pair: **the WTO is not a specialized agency and the ICC is not an organ**, and both are nevertheless central to global governance - which is exactly why the confusion arises.

### 6. The money: assessment, arrears and the constitutional quarrel of 1986

✅ **Fact:** **throughout its history the organisation has always been short of funds.** ✅ The **vast scope of its work means that it needs incredibly large sums of money**, and it is **entirely dependent on contributions from member states**.

✅ **Fact:** **each state pays a regular annual contribution based on its general wealth and ability to pay.** ✅ In addition, **members pay a proportion of the cost of each peacekeeping operation**, and they are **also expected to contribute towards the expenses of the special agencies**.

✅ **Fact:** **many member states refused to pay from time to time, either because of financial difficulties of their own, or as a mark of disapproval of policies.** ✅ **1986 was a bad year financially: no fewer than ninety-eight of its members owed money, chief among them being the USA, which withheld more than $100 million until the organisation reformed its budgeting system and curbed its extravagance.**

✅ **Fact:** **the Americans wanted the countries that gave most to have more say in how the money was spent, but most smaller members rejected this as undemocratic.** ✅ As one of Sri Lanka's delegates put it: **"in our political processes at home, the wealthy do not have more votes than the poor. We should like this to be the practice in the UN as well."**

✅ **Fact:** in **1987 changes were introduced giving the main financial contributors more control over spending, and the financial situation soon improved**.

✅ **Fact:** **however, expenses soared alarmingly in the early 1990s** as the organisation became involved in a series of new crises **in the Middle East (Gulf War), Yugoslavia and Somalia**. ✅ In **August 1993 the Secretary-General, Dr Boutros-Ghali, revealed that many states were well in arrears with their payments** and **warned that unless there was an immediate injection of cash from the world's rich states, all peacekeeping operations would be in jeopardy**.

✅ **Fact:** **yet the Americans and Europeans felt that they already paid too much** - the **USA with about thirty per cent, the European Community about thirty-five per cent and Japan eleven per cent paid three-quarters of the expenses** - and there was a feeling that **many other wealthy states could afford to contribute much more than they were doing**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the exchange recorded in 1986 is the most quotable moment in the whole topic, and it deserves to be treated as the constitutional argument it is rather than as colour.

⚠️ The American position rests on a principle of **accountability**: those who supply the resources should have proportionate control over their use, which is the ordinary rule in almost every institution that spends other people's money. ⚠️ The Sri Lankan reply rests on a principle of **equality**: in domestic politics the wealthy do not have more votes than the poor, and there is no reason the international analogue should be different. ⚠️ Neither proposition is absurd, and the two are irreconcilable.

⚠️ And notice the outcome. ✅ In 1987 **changes were introduced giving the main financial contributors more control over spending, and the financial situation soon improved.** ⚠️ The principle of equality was affirmed rhetorically and the principle of accountability prevailed operationally - which is a precise miniature of how the whole organisation works. ⚠️ **Formal equality in the Assembly, effective inequality wherever resources are decisive.**

⚠️ One further inference, which the three-quarters figure supports. ⚠️ A financing system in which three contributors supply three-quarters of the expenses is one in which each of the three holds the withholding power described in Lesson 10. ⚠️ The 1993 warning shows the consequence in its starkest form: the Secretary-General publicly announcing that all peacekeeping operations were in jeopardy, not because any decision had been vetoed, but because bills were unpaid.

### 7. From aims to goals: the development mandate over time

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the organisation's development work is frequently taught as a set of agencies, which makes it look static. ⚠️ It is better understood as a mandate that has been restated roughly once a generation, each time in the vocabulary of its period.

| Stage | Instrument | What changed |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ 1945 | The second founding aim: removing the causes of conflict through economic, social, educational, scientific and cultural progress, **especially in under-developed countries** | ⚠️ Development enters as a **means to peace**, not as an end in itself |
| ✅ 1948 | The Universal Declaration's right to **a standard of living high enough to keep a person and the family in good health** | ⚠️ Development becomes an **individual entitlement** as well as a collective programme |
| ✅ 1964-74 | UNCTAD, the Group of 77 and the demand for a **new international economic order** | ⚠️ Development becomes a claim about the **terms of the international economy** rather than about aid |
| ✅ 2000 | The **Millennium Declaration**, General Assembly resolution **55/2**, adopted at the Millennium Summit in September 2000 | ⚠️ Development becomes a set of **measurable targets with deadlines** |
| ✅ 2015 | The **2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development** and its Sustainable Development Goals, General Assembly resolution **70/1**, September 2015 | ⚠️ Targets become **universal** - applying to every state rather than only to poor ones - and are tied to the environment |

⚠️ Two observations make this table usable. ⚠️ First, the direction of travel is from **discretion to measurement**: the 1945 aim is a purpose, the 2000 and 2015 instruments are numbered targets with dates, and an organisation that publishes targets can be held to them by its own membership. ⚠️ Second, ECOSOC's role changed accordingly - the organisation now describes it as the principal body for coordination, policy review, policy dialogue and recommendations on economic, social and environmental issues, **as well as implementation of internationally agreed development goals**, and as the central platform for reflection and debate on sustainable development.

⚠️ The boundary must be observed here as elsewhere. ⚠️ The substance of the goals, India's performance against them and current development-policy debates belong to the governance, social-justice and international-relations owners. ⚠️ What belongs to this session is the institutional point: **the development mandate has been repeatedly restated by the organisation's own representative organ, and each restatement has been an attempt to convert a purpose into an obligation without acquiring any new power of enforcement.**

### 8. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** the account of the funding crisis is too even-handed. A body that is chronically short of money, whose largest contributor withholds payment to force policy change, and whose Secretary-General must publicly beg for cash to keep peacekeeping alive, is not a sovereign institution at all. It is a contractor dependent on a few clients, and its formal equality is a fiction.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection describes the dependency accurately and draws too strong a conclusion from it, for three reasons.

⚠️ First, the dependency is mutual in a way the objection ignores. ⚠️ The United States withheld funds in 1986 and rejoined the education agency in 2003, eighteen years after leaving it; Britain rejoined in 1997. ⚠️ States that treat an organisation as an optional contractor do not return to bodies they have denounced. ⚠️ The pattern shows that the contributors need the institution as well as funding it.

⚠️ Second, the fiction charge proves too much. ⚠️ If formal equality were a fiction, the 1986 dispute would not have occurred, because the contributors would simply have taken control. ⚠️ Instead they had to negotiate for two years and accept a compromise giving them **more control**, not control. ⚠️ A fiction that has to be bargained with is not a fiction.

⚠️ Third, and most importantly, the objection cannot explain the spending pattern. ⚠️ If the organisation were a contractor for its largest funders, its expenditure would track their priorities. ✅ By 1980 more than ninety per cent of annual expenditure was devoted to economic and social activities - much of it in states that contribute almost nothing - and the agencies pursuing it were, on the same contributors' own account, "too political" and in one case hostile to their basic institutions.

⚠️ The defensible formulation: **the organisation is financially dependent and politically autonomous within limits set by that dependence - which is why its contributors can shape what it does, cannot direct it, and periodically find it necessary to leave and then return.**

### 9. Examination use

⚠️ This lesson serves structure-and-mandate questions, questions on global economic governance and any question on why reform is difficult.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Examine the role of ECOSOC in coordinating the economic and social work of the United Nations system.
> ⚠️ "The United Nations has too many agencies and too little money." Critically examine.
> ⚠️ Discuss the relationship between the United Nations and the international financial and trade institutions.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the third framing, the category discipline table is the answer's spine: specify that the Fund and the Bank are specialized agencies, that the trade-and-development body is a United Nations body, and that the trade organisation formed in January 1995 is **not** a specialized agency; then show the governance asymmetry - one vote each in the Assembly, contribution-weighted control in the financial bodies - and evidence it with the conditionality and resignation cases; then close on the UNCTAD-GATT contradiction as proof that the system institutionalised two incompatible theories of trade.

### Traps

- ❌ Giving ECOSOC twenty-seven members as its current size. ✅ **Fifty-four**, elected by the Assembly for overlapping three-year terms; twenty-seven was correct between **1965 and 1973**, and eighteen before that.
- ❌ Forgetting the expenditure figure. ✅ By **1980 more than ninety per cent** of annual expenditure went to ECOSOC activities.
- ❌ Giving four regional commissions. ✅ There are **five** - **ECE** (Europe), **ECLAC** (Latin America and the Caribbean), **ECA** (Africa), **ESCAP** (Asia and the Pacific, renamed from ECAFE in **1974**) and **ESCWA** (Western Asia, **1973**). ⚠️ The core account's four, and its "Asia and the Far East", describe the position before 1973-74.
- ❌ Getting the functional commissions wrong. ✅ Those named are **population, drugs, human rights and the status of women**.
- ❌ Calling the WTO a specialized agency. ✅ It is **not**; **GATT became the WTO in January 1995**.
- ❌ Treating the UNCTAD-GATT problem as duplication. ✅ It is **contradiction**: one eliminates trade restrictions, the other seeks preference for Third World products.
- ❌ Getting the resignations wrong. ✅ **Poland resigned in 1950 and Czechoslovakia in 1954**, after loan refusals on the ground that they were communist states.
- ❌ Misquoting the 1986 crisis. ✅ **Ninety-eight** members owed money; the USA withheld **more than $100 million** until the budgeting system was reformed and extravagance curbed; **1987** changes gave the main contributors more control.
- ❌ Getting the burden shares wrong. ✅ **USA about thirty per cent, European Community about thirty-five per cent, Japan eleven per cent** - together **three-quarters** of the expenses.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ ECOSOC is elected by the Assembly with **one-third retiring each year**; it organises projects on health, education and other social and economic matters.
2. ✅ **Five regional commissions** - **ECE** Europe (1947), **ECLAC** Latin America and the Caribbean (1948), **ECA** Africa (1958), **ESCAP** Asia and the Pacific (ECAFE 1947, **renamed 1974**) and **ESCWA** Western Asia (**1973**) - plus commissions on **population, drugs, human rights and the status of women**. ⚠️ The core account's "four" predates ESCWA.
3. ✅ Coordinates **around thirty** commissions and specialized agencies; best known are **ILO, WHO, FAO, UNESCO, UNICEF and UNRWA**.
4. ✅ **By 1980, more than ninety per cent of annual expenditure** went to ECOSOC activities.
5. ⚠️ **Membership discrepancy:** the core account gives twenty-seven; the Charter as amended and current practice give **fifty-four**. Article 61 was amended from eighteen to twenty-seven in force **1965**, and from twenty-seven to fifty-four in force **1973**.
6. ⚠️ Coordination under **Articles 62-64** means relationship agreements, consultation, recommendation and reporting - **influence, not authority** - which is why overlap persists.
7. ✅ **Duplication:** WHO and FAO criticised for overlapping; FAO criticised in **1984** for spending too much on administration. ✅ **Contradiction:** GATT eliminates trade restrictions while UNCTAD seeks preference for Third World products.
8. ✅ **IMF:** short-term loans **provided policies meet its approval**; emergency fund **1977**; conditions imposed on **Jamaica and Tanzania**, seen as unacceptable interference; Board dominated by rich western countries, especially the USA.
9. ✅ **World Bank:** project loans - dams, agricultural techniques, family planning; the USA provides the largest share and **controls its decisions**; **Poland resigned 1950, Czechoslovakia 1954** after refusals.
10. ✅ **GATT** first signed **1947**, met under ECOSOC supervision, **became the WTO in January 1995**; the WTO is **not** a specialized agency.
11. ✅ Funding: an annual contribution **based on general wealth and ability to pay**, plus a share of **each peacekeeping operation**, plus contributions to the special agencies.
12. ✅ **1986:** **ninety-eight** members owing; the USA withheld **over $100 million**; it wanted the largest givers to have more say; smaller members called this **undemocratic**, with the Sri Lankan delegate's reply that at home the wealthy do not have more votes than the poor.
13. ✅ **1987:** changes gave main contributors more control and the situation improved. ✅ **Early 1990s:** expenses soared over the Gulf, Yugoslavia and Somalia. ✅ **August 1993:** Boutros-Ghali warned that without an immediate injection of cash **all peacekeeping operations would be in jeopardy**.
14. ✅ **USA about 30%, European Community about 35%, Japan 11% = three-quarters of the expenses.**
15. ⚠️ **Category pairs to memorise:** the **WTO is not a specialized agency**; the **ICC is not an organ** - the Rome Statute, **adopted 17 July 1998**, entered into force on **1 July 2002**.
16. ⚠️ The development mandate restated: founding aim (1945) -> Universal Declaration entitlement (1948) -> UNCTAD, the Group of 77 and the new economic order (1964-74) -> Millennium Declaration, resolution **55/2** (2000) -> 2030 Agenda and the Sustainable Development Goals, resolution **70/1** (2015).
17. ⚠️ The constitutional formula this lesson establishes: **formal equality in the Assembly, effective inequality wherever resources are decisive.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why overlapping and even contradictory mandates persist across the United Nations system, using ECOSOC's legal powers and one concrete example, and state what this implies about proposals to rationalise the system.

**Model answer:** The persistence follows from the system's legal architecture rather than from administrative failure. ECOSOC's coordinating powers under Articles 62 to 64 are to make studies and reports, to conclude relationship agreements with the specialized agencies subject to Assembly approval, to coordinate their activities through consultation with and recommendations to them, and to obtain regular reports. There is no power to instruct an agency, to set its budget, to merge it with another or to resolve a conflict of mandates, and the agencies are separate international organisations with their own constitutions, memberships and governing bodies. A coordinating council exercising recommendatory power over bodies that answer to their own memberships cannot compel them to stop duplicating. The sharpest example is not duplication but contradiction: GATT sought to eliminate tariffs and anything else restricting trade, while UNCTAD, which first met in 1964, seeks preferential treatment for the products of Third World countries - so the two bodies were pursuing incompatible objectives, each on the instructions of the membership that created it. The reason is chronological: the trade-liberalisation machinery dates from 1947 and the trade-and-development body from 1964, by which time decolonisation had produced a majority with different interests. The implication for rationalisation is that it cannot be achieved by better management. Consolidating mandates requires the agreement of each agency's own membership, and where two agencies express genuinely opposed interests of different groups of states, a merger would not resolve the disagreement but merely relocate it - which is why proposals of this kind recur and advance slowly.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming the core account's figure of twenty-seven ECOSOC members is simply wrong. It was accurate for the period between the amendment in force in 1965 and the amendment in force in 1973, when membership rose from twenty-seven to fifty-four. The lesson is about the nature of the source rather than about the number: a general history describes the structure of its own moment, and a structure-and-mandate answer requires the current constitutional position.

## Lesson 14 - The Court and the Secretariat: law without enforcement, administration with a voice

Progress: 14/18 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the Court's composition, election and seat; its record of successes and the phased Nicaragua case from the 1984 jurisdiction ruling to the merits judgment of 27 June 1986; Article 94 and the enforcement power never used; advisory opinions; why the Court and the criminal court are not interchangeable; the Secretariat's size and staff; appointment of the Secretary-General under Article 97 and the exclusion of the major powers; the list of holders; Article 99 and the political voice; independence under Article 100 against the politics of appointment; the Boutros-Ghali and Annan episodes

### Visual: two organs, two kinds of powerlessness, two kinds of influence

```text
   INTERNATIONAL COURT OF JUSTICE - the PRINCIPAL JUDICIAL ORGAN
   Seat: THE HAGUE (Netherlands) - the only one of the six principal
   organs not located in New York
   FIFTEEN JUDGES, ALL OF DIFFERENT NATIONALITIES
   Elected for NINE-YEAR TERMS, FIVE RETIRING EVERY THIRD YEAR,
   by the ASSEMBLY AND SECURITY COUNCIL JOINTLY
   Statute ANNEXED to the Charter (Art 92)
   All UN members are IPSO FACTO parties to the Statute (Art 93)

   WHAT IT DOES                        WHAT HAPPENS WHEN IT IS IGNORED
   Adjudicates disputes between        NICARAGUA v UNITED STATES, PHASED
   states                              (mining of Nicaragua's harbours)
   Successes include:                  ---- 10 MAY 1984: PROVISIONAL
     a frontier dispute between             MEASURES ordered
     HOLLAND and BELGIUM               ---- 26 NOV 1984: JURISDICTION AND
     a disagreement between BRITAIN         ADMISSIBILITY UPHELD
     and NORWAY over FISHING LIMITS    ---- JAN 1985: the USA WITHDRAWS
   Advisory opinions to the Assembly        FROM THE PROCEEDINGS
   and Council (Art 96) - e.g. the     ---- 27 JUNE 1986: MERITS JUDGMENT
   1971 Namibia ruling                      for NICARAGUA; REPARATION HELD
                                            DUE, its form and amount left
   *** THE COURT CAN ONLY OPERATE           for later determination
   SUCCESSFULLY WHEN BOTH PARTIES     ---- THE USA REFUSED TO ACCEPT THE
   AGREE TO ACCEPT THE VERDICT,            JUDGMENT
   WHICHEVER WAY IT GOES ***          ---- NO FURTHER ACTION WAS TAKEN

   NOTE: 1984 is the year the USA LOST THE JURISDICTIONAL ARGUMENT.
   The JUDGMENT it refused is 27 JUNE 1986. Do not merge the two.

   Art 94: a party may have recourse to the SECURITY COUNCIL, which MAY
   make recommendations or decide on measures to give effect to the
   judgment - 'IT HAS NEVER DONE SO'

   ==================================================================

   THE SECRETARIAT - the 'OFFICE STAFF'
   OVER 50,000 EMPLOYEES: minutes, translations, information,
   administration
   Headed by the SECRETARY-GENERAL, appointed for a FIVE-YEAR TERM
   by the ASSEMBLY ON THE RECOMMENDATION OF THE SECURITY COUNCIL
   'In order to ensure some degree of impartiality, HE IS NOT FROM ONE
    OF THE MAJOR POWERS'
   Art 97: CHIEF ADMINISTRATIVE OFFICER
   Art 99: MAY BRING TO THE COUNCIL'S ATTENTION ANY MATTER WHICH IN
           HIS OPINION MAY THREATEN INTERNATIONAL PEACE AND SECURITY
   Art 100: shall NOT SEEK OR RECEIVE INSTRUCTIONS from any government;
            members undertake to RESPECT the exclusively international
            character of the office and NOT TO SEEK TO INFLUENCE it

   THE HOLDERS
     Trygve Lie          NORWAY         1946-52
     Dag Hammarskjold    SWEDEN         1952-61
     U Thant             BURMA          1961-71
     Kurt Waldheim       AUSTRIA        1971-81
     Javier Perez de Cuellar  PERU      1981-91
     Boutros Boutros-Ghali    EGYPT     1991-6
     Kofi Annan          GHANA          1996-2006
     Ban Ki-moon         SOUTH KOREA    from 2006
     [Antonio Guterres   PORTUGAL       from 1 January 2017]

   ARTICLE 100 SAYS INDEPENDENT. ARTICLE 97 SAYS APPOINTED BY THE FIVE.
   THE RECORD SHOWS WHICH ONE WINS.
```

*⚠️ Put the two halves side by side and a pattern appears that neither organ shows on its own. The Court has authority and no bailiff; the Secretariat has a voice and no independence of tenure. In both cases the Charter creates an institution that looks strong on paper and then routes its effectiveness through the Security Council - enforcement of judgments under Article 94, appointment of the Secretary-General under Article 97. ⚠️ The founders did not forget to protect these organs. They deliberately made the Council the gateway, and the consequences are visible in every case below.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Two organs remain, and they are the two that candidates most often get wrong - usually by overstating the first and understating the second.

⚠️ The Court is overstated because it is a court, and courts in domestic systems give binding judgments that are enforced by the state. ⚠️ An international court has no state behind it. Its jurisdiction rests on consent; its judgments bind the parties; and if a party declines to comply there is no bailiff, only a Council that may act and has never done so.

⚠️ The Secretariat is understated because "office staff" sounds administrative. ⚠️ But the head of that staff is the only individual in the system who speaks for the organisation as a whole, who may put an item on the Council's agenda on his own authority, and whose public assessment of a crisis becomes part of the international record. ⚠️ That is political power of a real kind - and it is held by someone whose appointment and renewal each of five states can veto.

⚠️ The lesson therefore tests a single question in two forms: **what is authority worth when the power to act belongs to somebody else?**

### 2. The Court: composition, jurisdiction and record

✅ **Fact:** the International Court of Justice at **The Hague** has **fifteen judges, all of different nationalities, elected for nine-year terms - five retiring every third year - by the Assembly and the Security Council jointly**.

✅ **Fact:** it is the **principal judicial organ** of the organisation and **the only one of the six principal organs not located in New York**. ✅ It settles, in accordance with international law, **legal disputes submitted to it by states**, and gives **advisory opinions on legal questions referred to it by authorised organs and specialized agencies**.

✅ **Fact:** under Article 92 it functions in accordance with the **annexed Statute**, which forms an integral part of the Charter; under Article 93 **all members are ipso facto parties to the Statute**; under Article 96 the **Assembly or the Council may request an advisory opinion on any legal question**, and other organs and specialized agencies may do so if authorised by the Assembly.

✅ **Fact:** it **adjudicates in disputes between states**, and a **number of cases have been successfully dealt with**, including **a frontier dispute between Holland and Belgium and a disagreement between Britain and Norway over fishing limits**.

✅ **Fact:** **in other cases, however, it was not so successful.** ✅ The core account records the Nicaragua case compactly: in 1984 Nicaragua sued the United States for mining its harbours; the Court judged in favour of Nicaragua and ordered the payment of compensation; **the USA refused to accept the verdict, and no further action was taken**.

⚠️ **Phase correction - the single most examinable detail in this case.** ⚠️ That summary compresses two different years into one, and the compression conceals the most damaging fact in the whole episode. The case ran in distinct phases:

| Phase | Date | What happened |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Provisional measures | ✅ 10 May 1984 | ✅ The Court ordered interim measures while the case proceeded |
| ✅ Jurisdiction and admissibility | ✅ 26 November 1984 | ✅ The Court held that it **had jurisdiction** and that the application was admissible - against American argument |
| ✅ American withdrawal | ✅ January 1985 | ✅ Having lost the jurisdictional argument, the United States **withdrew from the proceedings** and took no part in the merits |
| ✅ Merits judgment | ✅ **27 June 1986** | ✅ The Court found for Nicaragua and held that reparation was due; ✅ the United States refused to accept the judgment and no further action followed |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** so 1984 is the year the United States *lost the argument that the Court could hear the case at all*, and **1986 is the year of the judgment it refused to accept**. ⚠️ The sequence matters because the withdrawal came *between* the two, which means the merits judgment was delivered against a party that had already left the courtroom. ⚠️ That is a sharper illustration of the Court's predicament than "a state ignored a judgment": the state first contested jurisdiction, then, having lost, denied the forum rather than the finding. ❌ Do not date the judgment to 1984.

✅ **Fact:** **although in theory the Security Council has the power to take "appropriate measures" to enforce the Court's decisions, it has never done so.** ✅ Under Article 94, each member undertakes to comply with the decision in any case to which it is a party, and if a party fails to perform, **the other party may have recourse to the Security Council, which may, if it deems necessary, make recommendations or decide upon measures to be taken to give effect to the judgment**.

✅ **Fact:** the Court **can only operate successfully when both parties to a dispute agree to accept the verdict, whichever way it should happen to go**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three features of this record deserve separate treatment.

⚠️ First, the **selection of the successful cases is itself the finding**. A frontier dispute between Holland and Belgium and a fishing-limits disagreement between Britain and Norway are, in international terms, small quarrels between friendly states with functioning legal systems and no vital interest at stake. ⚠️ That is exactly where adjudication works: where both parties would rather have a ruling than a quarrel. ⚠️ The condition is the same one identified in Lesson 8 for peacekeeping, appearing in a judicial register.

⚠️ Second, the **Nicaragua case is the Article 94 loop closing on itself**, and the point is worth making explicitly because it is a very elegant institutional trap. ⚠️ A state that loses a case may be taken to the Council. ⚠️ If the losing state is a permanent member, it can veto any measure the Council might take to enforce the judgment against it. ⚠️ So the Charter's enforcement route for judicial decisions is, as against five states, structurally unavailable - and that, rather than American obstinacy alone, is why no further action followed the merits judgment of 1986.

⚠️ Third, the **consent requirement**. Contentious jurisdiction depends on the parties accepting it, which means the Court cannot reach out to a dispute; it must be brought one. ⚠️ Combined with the enforcement gap, this produces the accurate summary: **the Court is a strong instrument for states that want to be bound and no instrument at all against a state that does not.**

⚠️ The advisory function partly escapes this limit, and Lesson 10 supplied the demonstration. ✅ The 1971 ruling that South Africa's occupation of Namibia was a breach of international law requiring immediate withdrawal was ignored for nineteen years and then formed part of the legal basis on which world opinion and African nationalism eventually prevailed. ⚠️ An advisory opinion binds nobody and can still change what the argument is about.

### 3. Why the Court and the criminal court are not interchangeable

✅ **Fact:** the idea of an international criminal court to try individuals accused of crimes against humanity **was first discussed by a League of Nations convention in 1937, but nothing came of it**. ✅ **The Cold War prevented further progress until, in 1989, it was suggested again** as a way of dealing with drug-traffickers and terrorists.

✅ **Fact:** progress towards a permanent court was **slow**, and it was **left to the Security Council to set up two special war crimes tribunals** to try individuals accused of committing atrocities **in 1994 in Rwanda and in 1995 in Bosnia**. ✅ The **most high-profile case was that of Slobodan Milosevic, the former Yugoslav president**, who was **handed over by Belgrade to the tribunal in the Netherlands** on charges of **crimes against humanity in Bosnia, Croatia and Kosovo**, and who was **the first former head of state ever to be brought before an international criminal tribunal**. ✅ He **died before a verdict was reached**.

⚠️ **Date correction.** ⚠️ The core account says the trial "opened in July 2001" and "dragged on for almost five years". ✅ July 2001 is the date of his **initial appearance** before the tribunal - **3 July 2001**, days after his transfer on 29 June - and the **trial itself commenced on 12 February 2002**. ✅ He died on 11 March 2006, so the proceedings ran a little over four years from commencement rather than almost five. ⚠️ An initial appearance is an arraignment, not a trial; the corrected dates are used here and the divergence is recorded in the source ledger.

✅ **Fact:** the **Rome Statute was adopted on 17 July 1998** at the diplomatic conference in Rome **by a vote of 120 in favour, 7 against and 21 abstentions**, creating a permanent court to deal with **war crimes, genocide and other crimes against humanity**. ✅ It was **open for signature until 31 December 2000 and had attracted 139 signatures** by that deadline. ✅ It **entered into force on 1 July 2002**, and the **court of eighteen elected judges was formally inaugurated in March 2003** at The Hague.

⚠️ **Count correction.** ❌ The core account says the Statute was "signed by 120 member states". ✅ 120 is the number of states that **voted to adopt** the text on 17 July 1998; the number of **signatures** by the closing date of 31 December 2000 was **139**. ⚠️ Adoption, signature and ratification are three separate acts, and conflating the first two is exactly the kind of error a structure question is built to catch.

✅ **Fact:** **the US government did not like the idea that some of its citizens might be tried in the court** - particularly Americans acting as peacekeepers who might face politicized prosecutions. ✅ **Although the Clinton administration had signed the 1998 agreement, President Bush insisted that the signature should be withdrawn in May 2002.** ✅ **Consequently the USA did not recognize the court and by June 2003 had signed separate agreements with thirty-seven states promising that no US personnel would be handed over for trial**, in some cases threatening to withdraw economic or military aid if a state refused to comply.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the distinction must be stated with its reasons, because a bare assertion that "the ICC is not a UN organ" is worth little.

| | ✅ **International Court of Justice** | ✅ **International Criminal Court** |
|---|---|---|
| Constituting instrument | ✅ A **Statute annexed to the Charter**, forming an integral part of it | ✅ The **Rome Statute, adopted 17 July 1998** by 120 votes to 7 with 21 abstentions, a separate treaty with **139 signatures** by 31 December 2000, in force **1 July 2002** |
| Status | ✅ A **principal organ** of the United Nations | ✅ An **independent treaty-based court**, not a United Nations organ |
| Who is a party? | ✅ **All UN members** are ipso facto parties to the Statute | ✅ Only states that ratify the Rome Statute; the USA withdrew its signature in **May 2002** |
| Who is judged? | ✅ **States** | ✅ **Individuals** |
| Composition | ✅ **Fifteen** judges, nine-year terms | ✅ **Eighteen** elected judges; inaugurated **March 2003** |
| Seat | ✅ The Hague | ✅ The Hague |

⚠️ The shared seat is why the two are confused, and the shared seat is the least important thing about them. ⚠️ A candidate who can state the constituting instrument for each has the distinction permanently.

⚠️ One further inference connects this material to the rest of the session. ⚠️ Notice that the criminal tribunals for the 1994 and 1995 atrocities were **set up by the Security Council**, which is to say under Chapter VII, because a determination that a situation threatens the peace is the doorway through which individual criminal responsibility could be reached without a treaty. ⚠️ That is the Article 2(7) proviso of Lesson 3 being used to create courts. ⚠️ And the permanent court, created instead by treaty, is precisely the alternative route that does not require Council consent - which is why a permanent member could reject it without vetoing anything.

### 4. The Secretariat: what it is and what it is for

✅ **Fact:** the Secretariat is the **"office staff"** of the organisation and consists of **over 50,000 employees**. ✅ They **look after the administrative work, preparing minutes of meetings, translations and information**.

✅ **Fact:** it comprises the Secretary-General and the international staff who carry out the day-to-day work as mandated by the Assembly and the other principal bodies. ✅ Under Article 97 the Secretary-General is **appointed by the General Assembly upon the recommendation of the Security Council** and is the **chief administrative officer** of the organisation. ✅ Under Article 98 he acts in that capacity in all meetings of the Assembly, the Council, ECOSOC and the Trusteeship Council, and **makes an annual report to the Assembly on the work of the organisation**.

✅ **Fact:** under **Article 99**, the Secretary-General **may bring to the attention of the Security Council any matter which in his opinion may threaten the maintenance of international peace and security**.

✅ **Fact:** under **Article 100**, the Secretary-General and the staff **shall not seek or receive instructions from any government or from any other authority external to the organisation**, and shall refrain from any action reflecting on their position as **international officials responsible only to the organisation**; each member undertakes to **respect the exclusively international character of their responsibilities and not to seek to influence them**.

✅ **Fact:** under Article 101 the staff are appointed by the Secretary-General under Assembly regulations, and the **paramount consideration** in employment is **the highest standards of efficiency, competence and integrity**, with **due regard to recruiting on as wide a geographical basis as possible**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Article 99 is the provision that converts an administrator into a political actor, and it is usually omitted from answers. ⚠️ Every other route to the Council's agenda runs through a state: a member brings a dispute, a party to a dispute is invited, the Assembly draws attention. ⚠️ Article 99 allows an individual official, on his own judgement, to place a matter before the organ with primary responsibility for peace and security. ⚠️ It is a small clause with a large implication: **the Charter contemplated that the international civil service would sometimes see a threat that no government wished to raise.**

⚠️ Article 100 is the necessary complement. ⚠️ An official who may act on his own judgement must be protected from instruction, which is why the article forbids seeking or receiving instructions and binds members not to seek to influence. ⚠️ Read together, Articles 99 and 100 describe a genuinely independent political office.

⚠️ And then Article 97 undoes much of it, which is the tension the rest of this lesson examines.

### 5. The office and the five: appointment as the effective control

✅ **Fact:** the Secretary-General is **appointed for a five-year term by the Assembly on the recommendation of the Security Council**. ✅ **In order to ensure some degree of impartiality, he is not from one of the major powers.** ✅ He **acts as the main spokesperson for the organisation and is always at the forefront of international affairs**.

✅ **Fact:** the holders recorded in the core account are: ✅ **Trygve Lie of Norway (1946-52); Dag Hammarskjold of Sweden (1952-61); U Thant of Burma (1961-71); Kurt Waldheim of Austria (1971-81); Javier Perez de Cuellar of Peru (1981-91); Boutros Boutros-Ghali of Egypt (1991-6); Kofi Annan of Ghana (1996-2006); and Ban Ki-moon of South Korea from 2006.** ✅ The office has since been held by **Antonio Guterres of Portugal, who took office on 1 January 2017**.

✅ **Fact:** **an important American technique in its quest to control the organisation was to secure the appointment of a sympathetic Secretary-General.** ✅ **A prime example was Kofi Annan, Secretary-General from 1996 until 2006, who wholeheartedly supported the American line on every major involvement except one - Iraq.**

✅ **Fact:** **since 1993 Annan had been in charge of all peacekeeping operations under Secretary-General Boutros-Ghali.** ✅ **Dr Boutros-Ghali had displeased Washington by refusing to send a mission into Somalia and delaying the NATO bombing of Serbia; in both cases Annan had supported the American line.** ✅ **In 1996 all the signs were that Boutros-Ghali would have his mandate extended for another five years, but President Clinton was determined to get rid of him; it was relentless pressure from the Clinton administration that got Kofi Annan chosen instead.**

✅ **Fact:** **consequently, when NATO launched its bombing attack on Yugoslavia early in 1999, instead of condemning it as a blatant violation of the UN Charter - which it most certainly was - Annan announced that it was a legitimate action.**

✅ **Fact:** **however, the attack on Iraq in 2003 was more difficult for Annan. When the joint US and British operation against Iraq was launched without a second Security Council resolution authorizing the attack, Annan was eventually forced to admit that the invasion had been illegal.** ✅ Asked in a broadcast interview whether he was bothered that the United States was becoming an unrestrainable, unilateral superpower, he replied: **"I think in the end everybody is concluding that it is best to work together with our allies."**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this material must be handled with care, because it is the most strongly worded passage in the core account and it contains both recorded fact and the account's own judgement.

⚠️ The recorded facts are the sequence: Boutros-Ghali displeased Washington over two specific decisions; the signs pointed to renewal; the Clinton administration applied pressure; Annan was chosen instead; Annan had supported the American line in both earlier disputes. ⚠️ The account's **judgement** is that the 1999 bombing was a blatant violation of the Charter which "most certainly" was one. ⚠️ That is a legal conclusion stated with a confidence the Charter text alone does not compel - as Lesson 5 showed, the relevant provisions are Chapter VIII's bar on enforcement action by regional agencies without Council authorisation and Article 2(4) - and this session reports it as the account's position rather than adopting it as established law. ⚠️ Distinguishing the two is exactly the four-register discipline in operation.

⚠️ What the sequence establishes, on any reading, is a structural point. ⚠️ **The office designed by Articles 99 and 100 to be independent is filled and renewed by a procedure in which each permanent member holds a veto, and the record contains at least three occasions on which that procedure produced a consequence:** ✅ Trygve Lie's early retirement after Soviet attacks over Korea; ✅ Boutros-Ghali's non-renewal under American pressure; ✅ and, in the parallel human-rights case of Lesson 12, Mary Robinson's replacement despite the Secretary-General's wish that she continue.

⚠️ The three episodes run in different political directions - one Soviet, two American - which is what makes the finding structural rather than partisan. ⚠️ An answer that presents it as evidence of one state's domination has misread it; an answer that presents it as the predictable consequence of Article 97 has understood the institution.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** if the Court cannot enforce and the Secretary-General cannot be independent, both organs are decorative. The Charter's provisions about judicial settlement and an impartial international civil service are aspirations that the Council's gateway powers cancel in practice.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection treats "cannot compel" as equivalent to "achieves nothing", and three lines of evidence refute the equivalence.

⚠️ First, the Court's ordinary work. ✅ A number of cases have been successfully dealt with, including the frontier dispute between Holland and Belgium and the fishing-limits disagreement between Britain and Norway. ⚠️ These are not trivial: a frontier and a fishing zone are precisely the kinds of dispute that have historically produced wars, and their removal from the political arena into a judicial one is a real service. ⚠️ The Court works for the large majority of disputes where both parties prefer a ruling to a quarrel, and that majority is invisible precisely because those cases do not become crises.

⚠️ Second, the stored-asset effect established in Lesson 10. ⚠️ The Namibian advisory opinion was unenforceable and became the legal foundation of a successful campaign nineteen years later. ⚠️ Judicial determinations survive the political conditions that make them unenforceable.

⚠️ Third, the Secretary-General's record contradicts the claim of total capture. ⚠️ On the account's own evidence, the holder alleged to have been installed by American pressure **was eventually forced to admit that the Iraq invasion had been illegal** and warned publicly that unilateral action would bring the organisation to a fork in the road. ⚠️ An office that can be filled by pressure and still produce that statement is constrained, not owned.

⚠️ The defensible formulation: **both organs are strong within the domain of consent and weak at its boundary - and since most international interaction lies inside that domain rather than at its boundary, judging them only by the boundary cases systematically understates them.**

### 7. Examination use

⚠️ This lesson serves structure questions, questions on international law, and any question on the role of the Secretary-General.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Examine the role and limitations of the International Court of Justice in the settlement of international disputes.
> ⚠️ "The Secretary-General is the servant of the Security Council rather than of the United Nations." Critically examine.
> ⚠️ Distinguish between the International Court of Justice and the International Criminal Court, and assess the significance of each for global governance.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the third framing, lead with the constituting instruments rather than with the functions, because that single distinction generates all the others - annexed Statute and principal organ against separate treaty and independent court; all members automatically parties against ratification only; states judged against individuals judged. ⚠️ For the second framing, the strongest structure is to accept the charge as a description of the appointment mechanism under Article 97, then set it against Articles 99 and 100, then use the Iraq admission as the case where the mechanism failed to produce compliance.

### Traps

- ❌ Saying the Court has compulsory jurisdiction over all disputes. ✅ It **can only operate successfully when both parties agree to accept the verdict, whichever way it goes**.
- ❌ Getting the composition wrong. ✅ **Fifteen judges, all of different nationalities, nine-year terms, five retiring every third year**, elected by the **Assembly and Council jointly**.
- ❌ Saying nobody has ever defied the Court. ✅ The Court upheld its **jurisdiction on 26 November 1984**, the United States **withdrew from the proceedings in January 1985**, and the **merits judgment of 27 June 1986** found for **Nicaragua** over the **mining of its harbours**; the **USA refused to accept it and no further action was taken**.
- ❌ Dating the Nicaragua judgment to 1984. ✅ 1984 is the provisional-measures and jurisdiction year; **the judgment is 27 June 1986**.
- ❌ Claiming the Council enforces judgments. ✅ **Article 94** permits recourse to the Council, which may make recommendations or decide on measures - and **it has never done so**.
- ❌ Calling the criminal court a principal organ. ✅ It is a **separate treaty-based court**; the **Rome Statute adopted 17 July 1998** by **120 votes to 7 with 21 abstentions**, **139 signatures** by 31 December 2000, in force **1 July 2002**; the court of **eighteen judges** was inaugurated in **March 2003**.
- ❌ Saying the Rome Statute was "signed by 120 states". ✅ 120 is the **adoption vote**; the signature count by the 31 December 2000 deadline was **139**.
- ❌ Saying the USA never signed the Rome Statute. ✅ The **Clinton administration signed** in 1998; **President Bush withdrew the signature in May 2002**, and by **June 2003** the USA had agreements with **thirty-seven states** against surrendering its personnel.
- ❌ Saying the Secretary-General is elected by the Assembly alone. ✅ **Appointed by the Assembly on the recommendation of the Security Council**, for a **five-year term**, and **not from one of the major powers**.
- ❌ Omitting **Article 99**. ✅ It allows the Secretary-General to bring to the Council's attention **any matter which in his opinion may threaten international peace and security**.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **ICJ:** The Hague; **fifteen judges of different nationalities**; **nine-year terms, five retiring every third year**; elected by the **Assembly and Council jointly**; the only principal organ not in New York.
2. ✅ **Article 92:** the Statute is annexed to the Charter and forms an integral part of it. ✅ **Article 93:** all members are ipso facto parties. ✅ **Article 96:** advisory opinions to the Assembly, the Council and authorised organs and agencies.
3. ✅ Successes include a **frontier dispute between Holland and Belgium** and a **fishing-limits disagreement between Britain and Norway**.
4. ✅ **Nicaragua, phased:** provisional measures **10 May 1984**; **jurisdiction and admissibility upheld 26 November 1984**; **US withdrawal from the proceedings January 1985**; **merits judgment 27 June 1986** finding for Nicaragua over the mining of its harbours, with reparation due; **the USA refused to accept it and no further action was taken**.
5. ✅ **Article 94:** recourse to the Council, which **may** make recommendations or decide on measures to give effect to a judgment - **it has never done so**.
6. ⚠️ The Article 94 loop: a losing permanent member can veto the enforcement of a judgment against itself.
7. ✅ The Court **can only operate successfully when both parties agree to accept the verdict** - so it is a strong instrument for states that wish to be bound and none at all against a state that does not.
8. ✅ **ICC:** idea first discussed at a League convention in **1937**; revived **1989**; Council-created tribunals for **Rwanda 1994** and **Bosnia 1995**; **Milosevic** transferred by Belgrade **29 June 2001**, **initial appearance 3 July 2001**, **trial commenced 12 February 2002**, first former head of state before an international criminal tribunal, **died 11 March 2006** before a verdict.
9. ✅ **Rome Statute adopted 17 July 1998** by **120 votes to 7 with 21 abstentions**; **139 signatures** by **31 December 2000**; in force **1 July 2002**; court of **eighteen judges** inaugurated **March 2003** at The Hague; Clinton signed, **Bush withdrew the signature in May 2002**; by **June 2003**, agreements with **thirty-seven states** against surrender of US personnel.
10. ⚠️ The tribunals were created by the **Council under its peace-and-security powers**; the permanent court was created **by treaty** - which is why a permanent member could reject it without casting a veto.
11. ✅ **Secretariat:** the office staff, **over 50,000 employees**; minutes, translations, information and administration.
12. ✅ **Article 97:** the Secretary-General is **appointed by the Assembly on the Council's recommendation**, **five-year term**, **not from a major power**, chief administrative officer. ✅ **Article 98:** annual report to the Assembly.
13. ✅ **Article 99:** may bring to the Council any matter which in his opinion may threaten peace and security. ✅ **Article 100:** shall not seek or receive instructions; members undertake not to seek to influence.
14. ✅ **Holders:** Lie (Norway, 1946-52); Hammarskjold (Sweden, 1952-61); U Thant (Burma, 1961-71); Waldheim (Austria, 1971-81); Perez de Cuellar (Peru, 1981-91); Boutros-Ghali (Egypt, 1991-6); Annan (Ghana, 1996-2006); Ban Ki-moon (South Korea, from 2006); Guterres (Portugal, from 1 January 2017).
15. ✅ **1996:** Boutros-Ghali had displeased Washington by refusing a Somalia mission and delaying the NATO bombing of Serbia; renewal looked likely; **Clinton administration pressure secured Annan instead**.
16. ✅ **1999:** Annan called the NATO bombing of Yugoslavia legitimate - the account judges it a blatant Charter violation, and this session reports that as the account's position. ✅ **2003:** Annan was eventually forced to admit the Iraq invasion had been illegal.
17. ⚠️ Three appointment episodes - Lie, Boutros-Ghali, Robinson - running in different political directions, which makes the finding **structural rather than partisan**.

### Concept check

**Question:** The Charter makes the International Court of Justice a principal organ and declares the Secretary-General independent of all governments. Explain why both provisions are less effective than they appear, identify the common mechanism, and state what each organ nevertheless achieves.

**Model answer:** Both provisions route the organ's effectiveness through the Security Council, and that is the common mechanism. For the Court, Article 94 provides that a party may comply voluntarily and that where it does not, the other party may have recourse to the Council, which may make recommendations or decide upon measures to give effect to the judgment. The Council has never done so, and the reason is structural rather than accidental: if the non-complying state is a permanent member it can veto any such measure, which is precisely what left the merits judgment of 27 June 1986 in favour of Nicaragua over the mining of its harbours without consequence after the United States, having lost the jurisdictional ruling of November 1984, withdrew from the proceedings in January 1985 and then refused to accept the judgment. For the Secretary-General, Articles 99 and 100 create a genuinely political and genuinely independent office - he may bring any matter he judges a threat to peace and security before the Council, and he may neither seek nor receive instructions - but Article 97 provides that he is appointed by the General Assembly upon the recommendation of the Security Council, so each permanent member holds a veto over appointment and renewal. The record shows the mechanism operating from opposite political directions: Trygve Lie retired early after Soviet attacks over Korea; Boutros-Ghali, whose renewal looked likely, was replaced after relentless Clinton administration pressure, having displeased Washington by refusing a Somalia mission and delaying the NATO bombing of Serbia. What each organ nevertheless achieves is substantial within the domain of consent. The Court has settled real disputes between states willing to be bound, including a frontier dispute between Holland and Belgium and a fishing-limits disagreement between Britain and Norway, and its advisory opinions survive the political conditions that make them unenforceable - the 1971 Namibia ruling was ignored for nineteen years and then supplied the legal content of the campaign that succeeded in 1990. The Secretary-General retains a public voice that even a contested appointment does not silence, as the eventual admission that the Iraq invasion had been illegal demonstrates.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the appointment record as evidence that one state controls the office. The same mechanism produced a Soviet-driven departure in the 1950s, an American-driven replacement in 1996 and, in the parallel human-rights case, the replacement of a Commissioner who had made enemies of Russia, China and the United States alike. The finding is about Article 97, not about any particular member.

## Lesson 15 - After the Cold War: Kuwait, Cambodia, Mozambique, Somalia, Bosnia and the absence from Rwanda

Progress: 15/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: why the end of the Cold War multiplied rather than reduced demand; over thirty operations between 1990 and 2003 and the mid-1990s peak; the Gulf operation of 1991 and the oil objection; Cambodia and Mozambique as completed transitions; Somalia as an impossible mandate; Bosnia, the safe areas and the failure of will; the absence from Rwanda; the recorded rule about when military intervention has most chance of success; what changed in the nature of conflict and what the Charter never anticipated
