*⚠️ The four boxes are the same decade and the same institution. What separates them is not competence but the relationship between the mandate and the political will behind it. Where the mandate was to defeat a named aggressor and a great power wanted it done, the outcome was decisive. Where the mandate was to implement an agreement the parties had already reached, the outcome was a completed transition. Where the mandate was to disarm armed men who refused to be disarmed, or to protect towns without the troops to protect them, the outcome was withdrawal and atrocity. ⚠️ And the rule at the bottom is drawn by the source itself, which is why it can be asserted rather than argued.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

The expectation in 1990 was reasonable: if great-power deadlock had paralysed the Council for forty years, the end of that deadlock should release the organisation to do what the Charter always intended.

✅ **Fact:** the expectation was wrong. ✅ **The end of the Cold War unfortunately did not mean the end of potential conflict**: there were a number of disputes still rolling on which had originated many years earlier; the Middle East continued to be volatile; and there were more problems in south-east Asia and Africa.

✅ **Fact:** **between 1990 and 2003 the organisation undertook well over thirty peacekeeping operations**; at the peak of its involvement, in the **mid-1990s**, there were **over eighty thousand troops on active service, from seventy-seven countries**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** two things changed at once, and separating them is the analytical key to the decade.

⚠️ The first change was **permissive**: the Council could now agree, so mandates could be issued that had been impossible for forty years. ⚠️ The second change was in **the nature of the conflicts themselves**: the wars that now demanded attention were predominantly internal - collapsed states, warlord competition, ethnic civil war and genocide - rather than the inter-state aggression the Charter was built to address.

⚠️ The combination is what produced the decade's record. ⚠️ **The organisation was freed to act at precisely the moment when the problems requiring action had become the kind it was least equipped to solve.** ⚠️ An answer that states this in its opening sentences has explained both the volume and the failures in one move.

⚠️ And note that the organisation had met this problem before. ⚠️ Lesson 10 showed every structural difficulty of the 1990s already present in the Congo between 1960 and 1964: a collapsed state, ambiguous consent, no line to hold, order-restoration instead of interposition, and costs beyond what the membership would bear. ⚠️ The 1990s did not invent the problem; they made it the normal case.

### 2. The Gulf, 1991: enforcement, and the objection that follows it

✅ **Fact:** **UN action during the Gulf War of 1991 was impressive.** ✅ When **Saddam Hussein of Iraq sent his troops to invade and capture the tiny, but extremely rich, neighbouring state of Kuwait in August 1990**, the **Security Council warned him to withdraw or face the consequences**. ✅ **When he refused, a large UN force was sent to Saudi Arabia**, and **in a short and decisive campaign, Iraqi troops were driven out, suffering heavy losses, and Kuwait was liberated**.

✅ **Fact:** the legal instrument was Security Council resolution **678**, adopted on **29 November 1990**, which authorised member states co-operating with the Government of Kuwait to use **all necessary means** to uphold and implement the Council's earlier resolutions and to restore international peace and security in the area.

✅ **Fact:** **critics complained that Kuwait had received help only because the West needed her oil supplies; other small nations, which had no value to the West, had received no help when they were invaded by larger neighbours** - for example **East Timor, taken over by Indonesia in 1975**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three points must be made about this case and they pull in different directions, which is why it is such good examination material.

⚠️ First, on **mechanism**. This was **authorised enforcement**, not peacekeeping - the fifth category in the mechanism discipline of the session opening. ⚠️ The formula "all necessary means" is the operative device: it licenses member states to use force without creating a force under United Nations command. ⚠️ So the 1991 operation stands with Korea in 1950 and apart from every blue-helmet mission in this session. ⚠️ A candidate who calls it a peacekeeping success has made the same category error twice in one topic.

⚠️ Second, on **the success condition**. The Gulf case is the clearest confirmation of limb (b) of the rule established in Lesson 8. ⚠️ Great-power interests were directly and intensely engaged; the operation succeeded because the engaged great powers supported it. ⚠️ Nothing about the Council's structure changed between Hungary and Kuwait; what changed was that the permanent members wanted the same outcome.

⚠️ Third, on **the oil objection**, which must be engaged rather than either endorsed or dismissed. ⚠️ The objection is a claim about **selectivity**, and the comparison it offers - East Timor in 1975 - is well chosen, because an annexation of a small territory by a larger neighbour is the same legal wrong that was reversed in Kuwait. ⚠️ The objection is therefore not that the Kuwait action was unlawful but that the law was applied where it suited the powerful and not elsewhere.

⚠️ The reply that a strong answer makes is not a denial. ⚠️ It is that **selectivity is the predictable output of a system in which enforcement requires great-power consent, and that this is an argument about the design examined in Lesson 5 rather than about the Gulf operation specifically.** ⚠️ The alternative to selective enforcement in such a system is not universal enforcement; it is no enforcement at all. ⚠️ Whether that is a satisfactory answer is exactly the kind of judgement an examiner is looking for a candidate to make explicitly.

### 3. Cambodia and Mozambique: what the organisation can complete

✅ **Fact:** problems in Cambodia **dragged on for nearly twenty years, but eventually the organisation was able to arrange a solution**. ✅ In **1975 the Khmer Rouge, a communist guerrilla force led by Pol Pot, seized power from the right-wing government of Prince Sihanouk**. ✅ **Over the next three years Pol Pot's brutal regime slaughtered about a third of the population, until in 1978 a Vietnamese army invaded the country**, drove out the Khmer Rouge and set up a new government.

✅ **Fact:** **at first the organisation, prompted by the USA, condemned this action, although many people thought Vietnam had done the people of Cambodia a great service by getting rid of the cruel Pol Pot regime.** ✅ **But it was all part of the Cold War, which meant that any action by Vietnam, an ally of the USSR, would be condemned by the USA.**

✅ **Fact:** **the end of the Cold War enabled the organisation to organize and police a solution.** ✅ **Vietnamese forces were withdrawn in September 1989**, and after a long period of negotiations and persuasion, **elections were held in June 1993, won by Prince Sihanouk's party**. ✅ The **result was widely accepted**, though not by what was left of the Khmer Rouge, which refused to take part, and **the country gradually began to settle down**.

✅ **Fact:** **Mozambique, which gained independence from Portugal in 1975, was torn by civil war for many years.** ✅ **By 1990 the country was in ruins and both sides were exhausted.** ✅ Although a **ceasefire agreement had been signed in Rome in October 1992** at a conference **organized by the Roman Catholic Church and the Italian government, it was not holding**; there were many violations and no way that elections could be held in such an atmosphere.

✅ **Fact:** **the organisation now became fully involved, operating a programme of demobilizing and disarming the various armies, distributing humanitarian relief and preparing for elections, which took place successfully in October 1994.** ✅ **Joachim Chissano of FRELIMO was elected president and re-elected for a further term in 1999.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** these two cases are usually listed as successes and rarely analysed. ⚠️ Analysed, they yield the single most useful positive finding in the whole post-Cold-War record.

⚠️ Note the common features. ⚠️ In both cases the fighting had reached a point where the parties were ready to stop: in Cambodia foreign forces had withdrawn and negotiation was possible; in Mozambique the country was in ruins and **both sides were exhausted**. ⚠️ In both cases an agreement existed before the organisation became fully involved - a withdrawal in one, a ceasefire signed in Rome in the other. ⚠️ And in both cases the agreement was **not self-executing**: the Cambodian settlement needed elections organised and policed; the Mozambican ceasefire **was not holding** and needed armies demobilised and disarmed before an election was even possible.

⚠️ That is the profile of the tasks the organisation performs best: **implementing an agreement the parties have already made but cannot execute themselves.** ⚠️ It is exactly the profile of West New Guinea in Lesson 9, thirty years earlier, and it explains why both decades produced successes of the same shape.

⚠️ The Cambodian case carries a second, sharper lesson about the Cold War itself. ⚠️ The same organisation condemned the removal of a regime that had slaughtered about a third of the population, because the state doing the removing was aligned with the wrong superpower - and then, once the Cold War ended, organised the settlement. ⚠️ **The institution's judgement did not improve; its members' alignment changed.** ⚠️ That is the agency discipline of Lesson 1 in its most uncomfortable application, and a candidate willing to state it will be writing about the institution rather than for it.

### 4. Somalia: an impossible task, precisely described

✅ **Fact:** **Somalia disintegrated into civil war in 1991 when the dictator Siad Barre was overthrown.** ✅ A **power struggle developed between rival supporters of Generals Aidid and Ali Mohammed**; the situation was chaotic as **food supplies and communications broke down and thousands of refugees were fleeing into Kenya**.

✅ **Fact:** **the Organization of African Unity asked for help, and thirty-seven thousand troops, mainly American, arrived in December 1992 to safeguard the aid and to restore law and order by disarming the "warlords"**.

⚠️ **Command correction - the distinction the core account collapses.** ⚠️ The account calls the December 1992 deployment "UN troops". ⚠️ It was not a United Nations-commanded operation. ✅ The force that arrived in December 1992 was the **Unified Task Force, UNITAF** - a **United States-led coalition authorised by Security Council resolution 794 of 3 December 1992** to use all necessary means to establish a secure environment for relief, but operating **under American command, not under United Nations command**. ✅ The **United Nations assumed command** of the Somalia operation only in **May 1993**, when **UNOSOM II** took over.

⚠️ **Why this matters, and it matters twice.** ⚠️ First, it belongs to the mechanism discipline of this session: December 1992 is **authorised enforcement** conducted by a member state, the same legal form as Korea and the Gulf, and only the second phase is a United Nations operation in the ordinary sense. ⚠️ Second, it changes the allocation of responsibility: the failure that followed was distributed between a coalition acting under national command and a later mission under United Nations command, and an answer that writes "UN troops" for the whole period has blurred exactly the distinction that determines who decided what. ⚠️ The corrected account is used here; the divergence is recorded in the source ledger.

✅ **Fact:** **however, the warlords, especially Aidid, were not prepared to be disarmed, and UN troops began to suffer casualties.** ✅ **The Americans withdrew their troops in March 1994, and the remaining UN troops were withdrawn in March 1995, leaving the warlords to fight it out.**

✅ **Fact:** **it was a humiliating backdown; but in fact the organisation had set itself an impossible task from the beginning - to forcibly disarm two extremely powerful armies which were determined to carry on fighting each other, and to combine this with a humanitarian relief programme.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the final sentence is the most analytically generous thing in the core account, and it should be used rather than paraphrased away, because it identifies the defect precisely.

⚠️ The mandate contained **two incompatible tasks**. ⚠️ Forcible disarmament of parties who refuse to be disarmed is enforcement: it requires treating them as adversaries, and once that begins the force is a belligerent. ⚠️ Humanitarian relief requires access, which in a fragmented territory depends on the cooperation - or at least the tolerance - of the very armed men being disarmed. ⚠️ The first task destroys the conditions for the second.

⚠️ Now set this against the three principles of Lesson 7. ⚠️ **Consent**: absent, since the warlords had not agreed to be disarmed. ⚠️ **Impartiality**: unsustainable, because disarming one side's opponents is not experienced as impartial. ⚠️ **Non-use of force except in self-defence and defence of the mandate**: the mandate itself required offensive action, which converts the third principle into its opposite.

⚠️ All three principles were therefore violated by the mandate rather than by the troops, and the organisation's own doctrine predicts the outcome: in the absence of consent a peacekeeping operation risks becoming a party to the conflict and being drawn towards enforcement, away from its fundamental role. ⚠️ **Somalia is not evidence that peacekeeping fails; it is evidence that peacekeeping was used where enforcement was required, and enforcement requires a political will that nobody had.**

### 5. Bosnia and Rwanda: the failure of will, and the absence

✅ **Fact:** **a similar situation developed in Bosnia.** ✅ In the **civil war between Bosnian Muslims and Serbs, the organisation failed to send enough troops to impose law and order**. ✅ **This was partly because both the European Community and the USA were reluctant to get involved.**

✅ **Fact:** there was **further humiliation in July 1995 when they were unable to prevent Serb forces from capturing two towns - Srebrenica and Zepa - which the Security Council had designated as safe areas for Muslims**. ✅ **Helplessness was underlined when the Serbs went on to murder around eight thousand Muslim men in Srebrenica.**

✅ **Fact:** **at the same time the organisation took no action in the civil war and genocide taking place in Rwanda in 1994.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** these two cases must be handled with precision and without rhetoric, and the precision is what distinguishes a serious answer.

⚠️ Bosnia is an instance of a specific and instructive failure: **a mandate was created that generated an obligation the mandating states were unwilling to resource.** ⚠️ Designating a town a safe area is a promise of protection. ⚠️ The Council made the designation; the members did not supply the troops; the promise could not be kept; and people who had relied on the designation were murdered. ⚠️ The sequence establishes something more damaging than simple inaction: **the designation itself increased the harm, because it concentrated a vulnerable population in a place that had been publicly declared safe and was not.**

⚠️ That produces a hard institutional proposition, and an answer that states it will stand out: **a commitment the organisation cannot honour is worse than no commitment, because the organisation's declarations change the behaviour of the people who depend on them.**

⚠️ Rwanda is a different and starker category. ⚠️ The record is one sentence: no action was taken. ⚠️ There was no inadequate mandate to criticise and no mission to under-resource. ⚠️ The Somali experience of the preceding two years - casualties, an American withdrawal in March 1994 and an approaching general withdrawal - is the immediate context in which that non-decision was taken, and the chronological proximity is exact: the American withdrawal from Somalia and the Rwandan genocide belong to the same year.

⚠️ The inference available from that proximity, stated carefully as an inference: ⚠️ **the cost of the Somali failure was paid in Rwanda**, because an organisation whose leading contributor had just withdrawn from an African intervention under fire was not going to obtain troops for another one. ⚠️ This is a judgement about political sequence rather than a recorded causal claim, and it should be labelled as such in an answer.

⚠️ The responsibility discipline from the session opening applies here with full force. ⚠️ The killings at Srebrenica are attributed by the record to **Serb forces**; the Rwandan killings to the perpetrators of that genocide. ⚠️ Responsibility for the organisation's failure lies with the organs that issued unresourced mandates and with the member states that declined to supply troops - and the account names them: **both the European Community and the USA were reluctant to get involved.** ⚠️ No responsibility is attributed here to nations or peoples.

### 6. The rule the record itself produces

✅ **Fact:** **UN military interventions had most chance of success when, as in Korea in 1950-3 and the 1991 Gulf War, UN troops actively supported one side against the other.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is one of the most uncomfortable sentences in the source material and it is also one of the most useful, because it is a rule drawn by the account itself rather than an interpretation imposed on it.

⚠️ Its implication is worth drawing out. ⚠️ The activity the organisation is best at, *militarily*, is the one that looks least like impartial peacekeeping: taking a side and defeating the other. ⚠️ And the activity it is worst at is the one the post-Cold-War decade demanded most - acting inside a collapsed or contested state where there is no aggressor to defeat, no line to hold and no agreement to implement.

⚠️ Three conclusions follow, and together they organise the whole decade.

| Task | Political requirement | Record |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ **Defeat a named aggressor** | Great-power agreement and willingness to fight | ✅ Korea 1950-3; ✅ Kuwait 1991 - decisive success |
| ⚠️ **Implement an agreement the parties have made** | Parties ready to stop; resources for demobilisation and elections | ✅ Cambodia 1993; ✅ Mozambique 1994 - completed transitions |
| ⚠️ **Impose order where parties refuse to stop** | A mandate matched by force and sustained political will | ✅ Somalia; ✅ Bosnia - withdrawal and atrocity; ✅ Rwanda - no action at all |

⚠️ Notice that the third row is not a failure of peacekeeping doctrine but a mismatch between task and instrument. ⚠️ And notice that the political requirement in every row is supplied by members rather than by the institution - which returns the whole analysis to the agency discipline stated in Lesson 1.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** the account here excuses too much. Describing Somalia as "an impossible task" and Bosnia as a failure of member-state will turns every failure into somebody else's fault. The organisation accepted these mandates, deployed under them and claimed the authority; it cannot disclaim the results. And no amount of institutional analysis addresses the moral fact of Rwanda.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection is the strongest in this session and two parts of it must be accepted without qualification.

⚠️ First, the organisation did accept the mandates. ✅ The account's own phrasing is that **it had set itself an impossible task**, which places the mandate design inside the institution and not outside it. ⚠️ A body that designs a mandate it cannot execute is responsible for the design.

⚠️ Second, on Rwanda, the institutional explanation is not a defence. ⚠️ Explaining why no troops were forthcoming after Somalia accounts for the decision; it does not discharge it. ⚠️ An answer that offers the explanation as though it settled the matter has mistaken analysis for exculpation, and an examiner will notice.

⚠️ What the objection nevertheless misses is the practical question that follows. ⚠️ If the organisation is responsible for accepting unexecutable mandates, the remedy is that it should decline them - and declining means that the Council, faced with a massacre it cannot stop, says so publicly and does nothing. ⚠️ That is the Bosnian alternative, and it is not obviously better; it is merely honest earlier. ⚠️ The genuine dilemma, which a strong answer will name, is that **an organisation which only accepts mandates it can certainly fulfil will decline every hard case, and an organisation which accepts hard cases will sometimes fail them catastrophically.**

⚠️ The defensible formulation: **the post-Cold-War record is not a record of incompetence but of a standing dilemma between honest impotence and dishonest commitment - and the reform effort examined in the next two lessons is best read as an attempt to escape that choice rather than to allocate blame for it.**

### 8. Examination use

⚠️ This lesson supplies the modern case bank and the material that any question on humanitarian intervention requires.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "The United Nations has been more successful in dealing with conflicts since 1990 than it was during the Cold War." To what extent would it be true to say this?
> ⚠️ Examine the changing nature of United Nations peacekeeping after the end of the Cold War.
> ⚠️ Assess the record of the United Nations in responding to internal conflict and mass atrocity.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first framing - which invites a simple comparison and punishes one - the winning structure is to separate volume from quality: more than thirty operations between 1990 and 2003 and a mid-1990s peak of over eighty thousand troops from seventy-seven countries show a released institution; Cambodia and Mozambique show what release made possible; Somalia, Bosnia and Rwanda show that the constraint had moved from the Council's voting rule to the members' willingness to supply force. ⚠️ Conclude that the organisation became freer and not stronger, which is a precise answer to a loose question.

### Traps

- ❌ Calling the 1991 Gulf operation peacekeeping. ✅ It was **authorised enforcement**: the Council warned Saddam Hussein, a large force was sent to Saudi Arabia, and resolution **678 of 29 November 1990** authorised **all necessary means**.
- ❌ Omitting the oil objection. ✅ Critics complained that **Kuwait received help only because the West needed her oil**, citing **East Timor, taken over by Indonesia in 1975**.
- ❌ Getting the Cambodian sequence wrong. ✅ Khmer Rouge seize power **1975**; the regime **slaughtered about a third of the population**; **Vietnam invades in 1978** and is **condemned by the organisation, prompted by the USA**; **Vietnamese withdrawal September 1989**; **elections June 1993**, won by Sihanouk's party.
- ❌ Crediting the organisation with the Mozambican ceasefire. ✅ The **October 1992 Rome ceasefire** was organised by the **Roman Catholic Church and the Italian government** and **was not holding**; the organisation then demobilised and disarmed the armies and prepared the **October 1994** elections that made **Joachim Chissano** president.
- ❌ Getting the Somali figures and dates wrong. ✅ **Siad Barre overthrown 1991**; the **OAU** asked for help; **thirty-seven thousand troops, mainly American, arrived December 1992**; **Americans withdrew March 1994**; the rest **March 1995**.
- ❌ Calling the December 1992 deployment a UN-commanded force. ✅ It was **UNITAF**, a **US-led coalition authorised by resolution 794 of 3 December 1992**; **UN command came only with UNOSOM II in May 1993**.
- ❌ Saying Somalia failed because of poor execution. ✅ The account states the organisation **had set itself an impossible task from the beginning** - forcible disarmament of two powerful armies determined to keep fighting, combined with humanitarian relief.
- ❌ Attributing the Srebrenica killings loosely. ✅ **Serb forces** captured Srebrenica and Zepa in **July 1995**, towns the **Council had designated as safe areas for Muslims**, and then murdered **around eight thousand Muslim men**.
- ❌ Omitting Rwanda. ✅ **The organisation took no action in the civil war and genocide taking place in Rwanda in 1994.**

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **The end of the Cold War did not mean the end of potential conflict**; disputes rolled on, the Middle East stayed volatile and there were more problems in south-east Asia and Africa.
2. ✅ **Well over thirty operations, 1990-2003**; mid-1990s peak of **over eighty thousand troops from seventy-seven countries**.
3. ⚠️ Two changes at once: the Council could now agree, and the conflicts requiring action had become internal - so the organisation was freed exactly when the problems became the kind it was least equipped for.
4. ✅ **Gulf 1991:** Kuwait invaded **August 1990**; Council warning; a large force sent to **Saudi Arabia**; short decisive campaign; Kuwait liberated; resolution **678, 29 November 1990**, authorising **all necessary means**.
5. ✅ The **oil objection** and the **East Timor 1975** comparison - a claim about selectivity, not about legality.
6. ✅ **Cambodia:** Khmer Rouge under **Pol Pot** from **1975**; about a **third of the population** slaughtered in three years; **Vietnamese invasion 1978**, condemned by the organisation at American prompting because Vietnam was a Soviet ally; withdrawal **September 1989**; elections **June 1993** won by Sihanouk's party and widely accepted.
7. ✅ **Mozambique:** independent from Portugal **1975**; in ruins by 1990 with both sides exhausted; **Rome ceasefire October 1992**, organised by the Roman Catholic Church and the Italian government, **not holding**; the organisation demobilised and disarmed the armies, distributed relief and prepared the **October 1994** elections; **Joachim Chissano** of FRELIMO elected, re-elected **1999**.
8. ⚠️ The completed-transition profile: an agreement already made, parties ready to stop, and an agreement that is **not self-executing**.
9. ✅ **Somalia:** **Siad Barre** overthrown **1991**; Generals **Aidid** and **Ali Mohammed**; **OAU** request; **thirty-seven thousand troops, mainly American, December 1992 - UNITAF, a US-led coalition authorised by resolution 794 of 3 December 1992, not a UN-commanded force; UN command followed with UNOSOM II in May 1993**; warlords refused disarmament; casualties; **American withdrawal March 1994**, general withdrawal **March 1995**; a **humiliating backdown** on an **impossible task set from the beginning**.
10. ⚠️ Somalia against the three principles: consent absent, impartiality unsustainable, and a mandate requiring offensive action - all three breached by the mandate rather than by the troops.
11. ✅ **Bosnia:** not enough troops sent to impose law and order, **partly because both the European Community and the USA were reluctant to get involved**; **July 1995** Serb forces captured **Srebrenica and Zepa**, designated **safe areas**, and murdered **around eight thousand Muslim men** in Srebrenica.
12. ⚠️ The safe-area lesson: **a commitment the organisation cannot honour is worse than no commitment**, because its declarations change the behaviour of those who rely on them.
13. ✅ **Rwanda 1994:** the organisation **took no action** in the civil war and genocide.
14. ⚠️ The Somalia-to-Rwanda sequence is an inference about political consequence, not a recorded causal claim - label it as such.
15. ✅ **The recorded rule:** military interventions had **most chance of success when UN troops actively supported one side against the other**, as in **Korea 1950-3** and the **1991 Gulf War**.
16. ⚠️ The standing dilemma: an organisation that accepts only mandates it can certainly fulfil will decline every hard case; one that accepts hard cases will sometimes fail them catastrophically.

### Concept check

**Question:** Somalia and Mozambique were undertaken in the same period by the same institution, and produced opposite results. Identify the feature of each mandate that determined the outcome, and state the general principle the comparison establishes.

**Model answer:** In Mozambique the parties had already stopped. The country was in ruins by 1990 and both sides were exhausted; a ceasefire had been signed in Rome in October 1992 at a conference organised by the Roman Catholic Church and the Italian government, and the problem was that it was not holding and no election could be held in such an atmosphere. The mandate was therefore to execute something the parties had agreed to but could not carry out themselves - demobilising and disarming the various armies, distributing humanitarian relief and preparing elections - and the elections duly took place successfully in October 1994, with Joachim Chissano elected president and re-elected in 1999. In Somalia the parties had not stopped and had no intention of stopping. After Siad Barre's overthrow in 1991 a power struggle continued between the supporters of Generals Aidid and Ali Mohammed; thirty-seven thousand troops, mainly American, arrived in December 1992 to safeguard aid and to restore law and order by disarming the warlords; and the warlords, especially Aidid, were not prepared to be disarmed. The mandate therefore required forcible disarmament of two powerful armies determined to keep fighting, combined with a humanitarian relief programme - an impossible task set from the beginning, since disarmament by force makes the operation a belligerent and belligerency destroys the access that relief requires. The Americans withdrew in March 1994 and the remainder in March 1995, leaving the warlords to fight it out. The general principle is that the organisation can implement an agreement the parties have reached and cannot manufacture one they have not: consent is not a procedural formality attached to peacekeeping but the condition that makes the instrument work at all.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that Somalia proves peacekeeping to be ineffective. It proves that a peacekeeping instrument was used where enforcement was required. Enforcement demands the sustained political will of states prepared to fight and accept casualties, which is exactly what the American withdrawal in March 1994 shows was absent - and the account's own rule confirms the point, since it records that military interventions had most chance of success where UN troops actively supported one side against the other, as in Korea and the Gulf.

## Lesson 16 - Iraq 2003: authorisation, unilateralism and the fork in the road

Progress: 16/18 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the inspections and the invasion; the states that wanted more time and the resolution never put to a vote; the claim that earlier violations justified war; the blow to prestige; the Secretary-General's fork-in-the-road warning and Article 51; the rejections of 2001 and the sole-superpower problem; the difference between being blocked, defied and bypassed; the responsibility-to-protect answer of 2005 and what it did and did not settle

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's section on the overthrow of Saddam Hussein: the March 2003 invasion by the USA and Britain on the stated grounds of removing weapons of mass destruction and freeing the Iraqi people, the weapons inspectors who had spent months in Iraq and found nothing of any significance, the attack proceeding without Security Council authorization, the attempt to push a resolution through the Council and the wish of France, Russia, China and Germany to allow more time, the decision to go ahead unilaterally without putting the resolution to a vote when it became clear France and Russia would veto, and the claim that Saddam's violations of earlier resolutions justified war; Kofi Annan's September 2003 statement that the action had brought the organisation to a fork in the road, the position under Article 51 that all states needed Council authorization to use force beyond self-defence, and his warning about a proliferation of the unilateral and lawless use of force; and its section on the future, including the tensions from 2001 with the rejection of the 1972 Anti-Ballistic Missile Treaty, the 1997 Kyoto Protocol, the Rome Statute and Council offers of a resolution authorizing a war against terrorism, and the judgement that the USA was so disproportionately powerful that it could ignore the organisation unless the organisation delivered the outcome it wanted

### Visual: the sequence of March 2003, and the legal claim at each step

```text
   BEFORE THE INVASION
   UN WEAPONS INSPECTORS had already spent MONTHS in Iraq searching
   for weapons of mass destruction
   >>> they 'HAD FOUND NOTHING OF ANY SIGNIFICANCE' <<<
                              |
                              v
   THE USA AND BRITAIN TRY TO PUSH A RESOLUTION THROUGH THE COUNCIL
   approving military action
                              |
              FRANCE · RUSSIA · CHINA · GERMANY
              wanted to ALLOW SADDAM MORE TIME to co-operate
              with the weapons inspectors
                              |
              It became clear FRANCE AND RUSSIA were prepared to
              VETO any such resolution
                              v
   >>> THE RESOLUTION WAS NEVER PUT TO A VOTE <<<
       The USA and Britain 'RESOLVED TO GO AHEAD UNILATERALLY'
                              |
                              v
   MARCH 2003   INVASION OF IRAQ
   STATED GROUNDS: to get rid of weapons of mass destruction and to
   free the Iraqi people from the brutal regime of Saddam Hussein
   LEGAL CLAIM: that SADDAM'S VIOLATIONS OF EARLIER UN RESOLUTIONS
   were a justification for war
                              |
                              v
   'THE US AND BRITISH ACTION WAS A SERIOUS BLOW TO THE PRESTIGE OF
    THE UN'
                              |
                              v
   SEPTEMBER 2003   KOFI ANNAN, opening the annual session of the
   General Assembly:
     - their action had brought the UN to 'A FORK IN THE ROAD'
     - UNTIL THEN, all states needed the authorization of the
       Security Council to use force BEYOND THE NORMAL RIGHT OF
       SELF-DEFENCE, AS PRESCRIBED BY ARTICLE 51 OF THE CHARTER
     - if states continued to act UNILATERALLY AND PRE-EMPTIVELY
       against a perceived threat, that would present A FUNDAMENTAL
       CHALLENGE TO THE ENTIRE PRINCIPLES of world peace and
       stability on which the UN was based and which it had been
       striving to achieve, HOWEVER IMPERFECTLY, FOR THE LAST 58
       YEARS
     - this could only set precedents resulting in 'A PROLIFERATION
       OF THE UNILATERAL AND LAWLESS USE OF FORCE'

   ============ THE CATEGORY THIS CASE BELONGS TO ============
   NOT BLOCKED - no veto was cast, because no vote was held
   NOT DEFIED  - no decision existed to defy
   >>> BYPASSED - the organisation was simply gone around <<<
   ===========================================================
```

*⚠️ The most important feature of this diagram is a negative. No veto was exercised, because no resolution was put. The procedural machinery of the Charter was never engaged at the decisive moment; it was anticipated and avoided. ⚠️ That is what makes 2003 different in kind from Hungary, from Czechoslovakia, from the Nicaragua judgment and from every other case in this session - and it is why the Secretary-General described the moment as a fork in the road rather than as another failure.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Every earlier failure in this session had a procedural shape. Something was proposed and blocked; something was decided and ignored; something was mandated and under-resourced. In each case the organisation's machinery operated and produced a visible outcome, even when the outcome was nothing.

⚠️ In March 2003 the machinery did not operate. ⚠️ The states intending to act calculated that a resolution would be vetoed, declined to put it, and acted anyway.

⚠️ Why does that matter more than a veto? ⚠️ Because a veto, however frustrating, is an exercise of the Charter. ⚠️ A state that vetoes is using a power the Charter gave it, inside the institution, on the record, at a cost to its own reputation. ⚠️ A state that does not table the resolution at all is asserting that **the Charter's authorisation requirement is optional**, and if that assertion is generally accepted, the entire legal structure built in Lessons 3 and 5 - the prohibition on force in Article 2(4), the narrow exception in Article 51, the Council's monopoly on authorising everything else - collapses into a description of what powerful states choose to do.

⚠️ That is the fork in the road. ⚠️ Not "will the organisation be effective?", but **"is there still a rule?"**

### 2. The sequence, exactly

✅ **Fact:** **in March 2003 the USA and Britain launched an invasion of Iraq, on the grounds that they intended to get rid of its weapons of mass destruction and to free the Iraqi people from the brutal regime of Saddam Hussein.**

✅ **Fact:** **UN weapons inspectors had already spent months in Iraq searching for weapons of mass destruction, but had found nothing of any significance.**

✅ **Fact:** **the attack went ahead even though the UN Security Council had not given its authorization.** ✅ **The USA and Britain had tried to push a resolution through the Council approving military action, but France, Russia, China and Germany wanted to allow Saddam more time to co-operate with the weapons inspectors.**

✅ **Fact:** **when it became clear that France and Russia were prepared to veto any such resolution, the USA and Britain resolved to go ahead unilaterally, without putting the resolution to a Security Council vote.** ✅ **They claimed that Saddam's violations of earlier UN resolutions were a justification for war.**

✅ **Fact:** **the US and British action was a serious blow to the prestige of the UN.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** two details in that sequence are frequently lost and both are examinable.

⚠️ First, **Germany**. It is named among the states wanting more time, and Germany was not a permanent member. ⚠️ Its inclusion tells us the disagreement was not confined to veto-holders and was not a straightforward Cold War residue; it was a division among western states about the sufficiency of the evidence.

⚠️ Second, **the inspections finding**. ✅ The inspectors had spent months and found nothing of any significance. ⚠️ That is a fact about the state of the evidence at the time of the decision, and it is what gives the "more time" position its force. ⚠️ An answer that omits it presents the disagreement as a difference of political preference, when the record presents it as a difference about whether the factual basis for war had been established.

### 3. The legal claim, and why it is the crux

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the invading states did not claim that authorisation was unnecessary in general. ✅ They claimed that **Saddam's violations of earlier UN resolutions were a justification for war** - which is a claim that authorisation had **already been given** by earlier Council decisions and revived by non-compliance.

⚠️ That is worth taking seriously rather than dismissing, because it determines what the case actually establishes.

⚠️ If the claim is right, 2003 is a dispute about the interpretation of existing resolutions, which is an ordinary legal disagreement of a kind international institutions have all the time. ⚠️ If the claim is wrong, 2003 is the use of force without any lawful basis by two permanent members of the organ entrusted with authorising force.

⚠️ This session does not adjudicate the question, and the reason belongs to the four-register discipline: no source held here settles it, and inventing a determination would be the precise error this session is built to avoid. ⚠️ What **is** recorded is the following, and it is sufficient for an examination answer.

✅ The Council had **not given its authorization** for the attack. ✅ The resolution approving military action was **never put to a vote**. ✅ The Secretary-General stated that **until then, all states needed the authorization of the Security Council if they intended to use force beyond the normal right of self-defence, as prescribed by Article 51**. ✅ And he **was eventually forced to admit that the invasion had been illegal** - a statement recorded in Lesson 14.

⚠️ So the position an answer can safely take is: **the organisation's own chief officer stated that the action lacked authorisation and was illegal; the acting states asserted a derived authorisation from earlier resolutions; and the dispute was never resolved by any organ competent to resolve it, because no vote was taken and no case was brought.** ⚠️ That last clause is the sharpest institutional observation available on this case - **the system contains no mechanism for determining the lawfulness of a use of force when the acting states are permanent members.**

### 4. The fork in the road: what Annan actually said and what it means

✅ **Fact:** Secretary-General **Kofi Annan, speaking at the opening of the annual session of the General Assembly in September 2003**, said that the action had brought the organisation to **"a fork in the road"**.

✅ **Fact:** his reasoning was as follows. ✅ **Until then, all states needed the authorization of the Security Council if they intended to use force beyond the normal right of self-defence, as prescribed by Article 51 of the Charter.** ✅ **However, if states continued to act unilaterally and pre-emptively against a perceived threat, that would present a fundamental challenge to the entire principles of world peace and stability on which the organisation was based, and which it had been striving to achieve, however imperfectly, for the last fifty-eight years.** ✅ **This, he said, could only set precedents resulting in "a proliferation of the unilateral and lawless use of force".**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the argument has a precise structure and reproducing that structure is worth more than quoting the phrase.

⚠️ It is an argument about **precedent, not about Iraq**. ⚠️ The Secretary-General does not say the invasion will cause a catastrophe in Iraq; he says the *justification* offered for it, if accepted, becomes available to every state. ⚠️ The justification was action **unilaterally and pre-emptively against a perceived threat** - and the italicised words are doing the work. ⚠️ "Perceived" means the threat need not be demonstrated; "pre-emptively" means the armed attack required by Article 51 need not have occurred; "unilaterally" means the Council need not agree.

⚠️ Strip those three qualifications out of the Charter and what remains is the position before 1945: states use force when they judge it necessary and explain afterwards. ⚠️ **That is why the phrase is "fork in the road" and not "setback" - one branch leads back to the pre-Charter world.**

⚠️ Note also the honesty of the qualifier: the principles the organisation **had been striving to achieve, however imperfectly, for the last fifty-eight years**. ⚠️ The claim being defended is not that the rule had been observed but that it had existed. ⚠️ A rule frequently broken is still a rule; a rule openly declared optional is not. ⚠️ A candidate who reproduces that distinction is making a genuinely legal argument rather than a political complaint.

### 5. The wider pattern: the sole superpower and the four rejections

✅ **Fact:** **the really serious problem, which had been brewing ever since the end of the Cold War and the emergence of the USA as sole superpower, was about the future relationship between the organisation and the USA.**

✅ **Fact:** **tensions began to mount as soon as the Bush administration took office in 2001: within its first year the new government rejected the 1972 Anti-Ballistic Missile Treaty, the 1997 Kyoto Protocol which aimed to limit the emission of greenhouse gases, and the Rome Statute of the new International Criminal Court, as well as Security Council offers of a resolution authorizing a war against terrorism** - the last **because it prefers to conduct its own self-defence in whatever way it chooses**.

✅ **Fact:** **tensions reached a climax in March 2003**, and **the USA was so disproportionately powerful that it could ignore the organisation and act as it pleased unless the organisation delivered the outcome it wanted**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the four rejections are more instructive together than separately, because they cover four different domains and the same logic runs through all of them.

| Rejected instrument | Domain | What acceptance would have constrained |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ The 1972 Anti-Ballistic Missile Treaty | Strategic arms | ⚠️ Missile-defence deployment |
| ✅ The 1997 Kyoto Protocol | Environment | ⚠️ Domestic emissions policy |
| ✅ The Rome Statute | International criminal law | ⚠️ Exposure of nationals, particularly peacekeepers, to prosecution |
| ✅ Council offers of a resolution authorising a war against terrorism | Collective security | ⚠️ The manner and limits of its own self-defence |

⚠️ The fourth is the most revealing and the one most often skipped. ⚠️ The Council **offered** a resolution authorising action against terrorism - that is, offered the very legitimacy the organisation exists to confer - and the offer was declined **because it preferred to conduct its own self-defence in whatever way it chooses**. ⚠️ A state that declines an authorisation it could have had is telling us what it thinks authorisation is worth: not a benefit, but a constraint.

⚠️ That observation completes an argument that runs through this whole session. ⚠️ In 1950 the United States valued the Council's authorisation enough to seize the opportunity created by an empty Soviet chair. ⚠️ In 1991 it obtained authorisation and built a coalition on it. ⚠️ In 2001 it declined an offered authorisation, and in 2003 it acted without seeking one. ⚠️ **The variable across those five decades is not the Charter, which did not change, but the distribution of power - and the value a state places on collective authorisation turns out to be inversely related to its capacity to act alone.**

✅ The account's own conclusion follows directly: **the challenge for the organisation is to find a way to harness and make use of the power and influence of the USA instead of being impeded or stampeded by it.**

### 6. The answer the system attempted: the responsibility to protect

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Iraq crisis and the failures of Lesson 15 posed the same question from opposite ends. ⚠️ Bosnia and Rwanda asked: what happens when the organisation does not act and people are massacred? ⚠️ Iraq asked: what happens when states act without the organisation and claim a humanitarian purpose? ⚠️ Both questions concern the boundary between Article 2(7)'s bar on intervention and the Chapter VII doorway.

✅ **Fact:** the attempted answer came in the **2005 World Summit Outcome**, General Assembly resolution **60/1**, adopted on **16 September 2005**, whose **paragraphs 138 and 139** set out the responsibility to protect populations from **genocide, war crimes, ethnic cleansing and crimes against humanity**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** what matters for this session is the structure of the doctrine rather than its details, because the structure is an institutional response to everything in Lessons 15 and 16.

⚠️ It places the primary responsibility on each **state** for its own population, which preserves sovereignty and Article 2(7). ⚠️ It then places a responsibility on the **international community** to assist, and finally to take collective action **through the Security Council, in accordance with the Charter** and on a case-by-case basis, where national authorities manifestly fail.

⚠️ Read those three tiers against the two failures. ⚠️ Against Rwanda and Bosnia, the doctrine asserts that mass atrocity is a matter of legitimate international concern and not solely a domestic one - which answers the Article 2(7) objection. ⚠️ Against Iraq, it routes any resulting action **through the Council**, which denies that a humanitarian purpose creates a right of unilateral intervention.

⚠️ And therefore what the doctrine did **not** settle is exactly the problem this session has been tracking. ⚠️ It creates an obligation to act and locates the decision in the organ that can be blocked by any of five states. ⚠️ **It converts an absence of principle into an absence of will**, which is progress of a real but limited kind: after 2005 a Council that fails to act in the face of atrocity is failing against a standard it has itself adopted, rather than merely failing to do something nobody had required.

⚠️ The boundary must be observed. ⚠️ The subsequent operational history of the doctrine, its invocations, its controversies and current debates about it belong to the international-relations owner. ⚠️ What belongs here is the institutional fact: **the organisation's own representative organ formulated, in 2005, a doctrine designed to answer both of the failures of the preceding decade, and did so without altering the Council's voting rule.**

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** the treatment here is too solemn about legality. The Council's authorisation requirement has been circumvented repeatedly - by the great powers in their own spheres throughout the Cold War, by an alliance over Yugoslavia in 1999, and by the invading states in 2003. A rule that the powerful ignore whenever it matters was never a rule; 2003 merely made the position honest.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection has real historical support and it fails on a distinction that this lesson exists to establish.

⚠️ Consider what happened in the earlier cases. ⚠️ In Hungary and Czechoslovakia the intervening power **vetoed** condemnation and, in 1968, claimed an **invitation** - that is, it worked inside the Charter's categories and sought to place its action within them. ⚠️ Over Yugoslavia in 1999 the action was defended as legitimate, and the account records the Secretary-General's endorsement and its own contrary judgement. ⚠️ In every case, the acting state offered a justification framed in the Charter's own terms.

⚠️ Why does that matter? ⚠️ Because a rule that is broken with an excuse is a rule that the breaker still acknowledges. ⚠️ The excuse is a tribute; the effort to fit conduct into a legal category concedes that the category governs. ⚠️ The distinctive feature of 2003 is not that force was used without authorisation but that the resolution **was not put to a vote at all** because a veto was anticipated - which treats the authorisation process as a formality to be avoided rather than a hurdle to be cleared or an obstacle to be excused.

⚠️ There is a second reply, and it is empirical. ⚠️ If the rule were worthless, the invading states would not have spent months seeking a resolution, would not have submitted to inspections, and would not have troubled to claim that earlier resolutions justified the war. ✅ They did all three. ⚠️ The effort expended on obtaining authorisation is the measure of what authorisation is worth.

⚠️ The defensible formulation: **the authorisation rule has been broken often and abandoned rarely, and the significance of 2003 lies in the shift from justification to avoidance - which is why the organisation's own officer described it as a fork rather than as another pothole.**

### 8. Examination use

⚠️ This lesson serves every question on the organisation's authority, legitimacy and contemporary relevance.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "The invasion of Iraq in 2003 exposed the limits of the United Nations more sharply than any event since 1945." Examine.
> ⚠️ Discuss the relationship between the United Nations and the sole superpower after the end of the Cold War.
> ⚠️ Examine the tension between state sovereignty and the international protection of populations in the practice of the United Nations.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first framing, the decisive analytical move is the category distinction: 2003 belongs neither with blocked cases nor with defied ones but with bypassed ones, because no resolution was put and therefore no veto was cast. ⚠️ Build the answer on the sequence - inspections finding nothing of significance, four states wanting more time, an anticipated veto, no vote, invasion, and the fork-in-the-road warning - and close on the precedent argument rather than on the outcome in Iraq. ⚠️ For the third framing, run Article 2(7) and its Chapter VII proviso through Bosnia, Rwanda, Iraq and the 2005 doctrine in that order, and conclude that the doctrine relocated the failure from principle to will.

### Traps

- ❌ Saying the invasion was vetoed. ✅ **No vote was taken**; when it became clear that France and Russia were prepared to veto, the USA and Britain went ahead **without putting the resolution to a vote**.
- ❌ Omitting the inspections finding. ✅ Inspectors had spent **months** in Iraq and **found nothing of any significance**.
- ❌ Naming only permanent members as opponents. ✅ **France, Russia, China and Germany** wanted to allow more time - and Germany was not a permanent member.
- ❌ Saying the invading states claimed no legal basis. ✅ They **claimed that Saddam's violations of earlier UN resolutions were a justification for war**.
- ❌ Misdating or mislocating the fork-in-the-road statement. ✅ **Kofi Annan, September 2003, at the opening of the annual General Assembly session.**
- ❌ Getting Article 51 wrong in this context. ✅ The stated position was that **all states needed Council authorization to use force beyond the normal right of self-defence, as prescribed by Article 51**.
- ❌ Listing only three of the four rejections of 2001. ✅ The **1972 Anti-Ballistic Missile Treaty**, the **1997 Kyoto Protocol**, the **Rome Statute**, and **Council offers of a resolution authorizing a war against terrorism**.
- ❌ Presenting the 2005 doctrine as authorising unilateral humanitarian intervention. ✅ Collective action runs **through the Security Council, in accordance with the Charter**, case by case.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **March 2003:** the USA and Britain invaded Iraq on the stated grounds of removing weapons of mass destruction and freeing the Iraqi people from Saddam Hussein's regime.
2. ✅ **Weapons inspectors had spent months in Iraq and found nothing of any significance.**
3. ✅ The attack proceeded **even though the Council had not given its authorization**; an attempt to push a resolution through failed because **France, Russia, China and Germany wanted to allow Saddam more time**.
4. ✅ When it became clear **France and Russia would veto**, the two states **went ahead unilaterally without putting the resolution to a vote**, claiming that **violations of earlier resolutions justified war**.
5. ✅ **A serious blow to the prestige of the organisation.**
6. ✅ **September 2003, Kofi Annan:** the action had brought the organisation to **"a fork in the road"**; until then all states needed Council authorization to use force **beyond the normal right of self-defence, as prescribed by Article 51**; continued unilateral, pre-emptive action against a **perceived** threat would be a **fundamental challenge to the entire principles** the organisation had striven for, **however imperfectly, for fifty-eight years**, setting precedents producing **"a proliferation of the unilateral and lawless use of force"**.
7. ⚠️ The three operative words in the warning: **unilaterally** (no Council), **pre-emptively** (no armed attack), **perceived** (no demonstration). Remove all three from the Charter and the pre-1945 position returns.
8. ⚠️ **Category:** 2003 was neither **blocked** nor **defied** but **bypassed** - no veto was cast because no vote was held.
9. ⚠️ The system contains **no mechanism** for determining the lawfulness of a use of force when the acting states are permanent members.
10. ✅ **2001 rejections:** the **1972 Anti-Ballistic Missile Treaty**, the **1997 Kyoto Protocol**, the **Rome Statute**, and **Council offers of a resolution authorizing a war against terrorism**, the last because the state **prefers to conduct its own self-defence in whatever way it chooses**.
11. ✅ The USA was **so disproportionately powerful that it could ignore the organisation and act as it pleased unless the organisation delivered the outcome it wanted**; the challenge is to **harness** that power rather than be **impeded or stampeded** by it.
12. ⚠️ The five-decade pattern: authorisation seized in 1950, obtained in 1991, declined when offered in 2001, avoided in 2003 - the Charter unchanged, the distribution of power transformed.
13. ✅ **2005 World Summit Outcome, resolution 60/1, 16 September 2005, paragraphs 138-139:** the responsibility to protect populations from **genocide, war crimes, ethnic cleansing and crimes against humanity**, with primary responsibility on the state, assistance by the international community, and collective action **through the Security Council** where national authorities manifestly fail.
14. ⚠️ The doctrine answered the Article 2(7) objection raised by Rwanda and Bosnia and denied the unilateral-humanitarian claim raised by Iraq - **converting an absence of principle into an absence of will** without altering the voting rule.
15. ⚠️ The distinction that survives the strongest objection: **a rule broken with an excuse is still acknowledged; the shift in 2003 was from justification to avoidance.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Iraq crisis of 2003 belongs to a different category of institutional failure from Hungary in 1956, and state what the 2005 responsibility-to-protect formulation did and did not resolve.

**Model answer:** In Hungary the Charter's machinery operated fully and produced nothing. The Hungarian government appealed, the Security Council considered a resolution calling for Soviet withdrawal, the Russians vetoed it, the General Assembly passed the same resolution and set up a committee of investigation, and the Russians refused to co-operate. That is a blocked case: a power the Charter confers was exercised inside the institution, on the record. In 2003 the machinery was not engaged at the decisive moment. Weapons inspectors had spent months in Iraq and found nothing of any significance; the USA and Britain tried to push a resolution through the Council; France, Russia, China and Germany wanted to allow more time; and when it became clear that France and Russia would veto, the two states went ahead without putting the resolution to a vote at all, claiming that Saddam's violations of earlier resolutions justified war. No veto was cast because no vote was held, so the case is not blocked and not defied - there was no decision to defy - but bypassed. That is why the Secretary-General called it a fork in the road in September 2003: his argument was about precedent, that continued unilateral and pre-emptive action against a perceived threat would challenge the principles the organisation had striven for, however imperfectly, for fifty-eight years, and could only produce a proliferation of the unilateral and lawless use of force. The 2005 World Summit Outcome, resolution 60/1 of 16 September 2005, addressed both sides of the problem in paragraphs 138 and 139. It resolved the question of principle: mass atrocity is not purely a domestic matter, so the Article 2(7) bar does not shield genocide, war crimes, ethnic cleansing and crimes against humanity; and it resolved the question of route, by locating collective action in the Security Council in accordance with the Charter rather than in any unilateral humanitarian right. What it did not resolve is the question of will, because the organ in which it placed the decision remains the organ that any of five states can block - so after 2005 inaction in the face of atrocity is a failure measured against a standard the membership has itself adopted, which is a real but limited advance.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating 2003 as simply the latest in a long line of great-power violations of the Charter. The earlier violations were accompanied by justifications framed in the Charter's own categories - a veto exercised, an invitation claimed, a legitimacy asserted - and an excuse concedes that the rule governs. The distinctive feature of 2003 was the decision not to seek a determination at all, which treats the authorisation requirement as avoidable rather than as a rule being broken.

## Lesson 17 - Reform: Charter amendment, practice, housekeeping - and where India's claim actually sits

Progress: 17/18 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the six recorded weaknesses and the four recorded strengths; the three categories every reform proposal belongs to; the standing-capability proposals of 2000 and the counter-terrorism and budget agenda of 2002; the 1945-versus-now mismatch as the durable structural argument; Article 108 as the reason composition reform has not moved; where India's claim sits and what a history answer may and may not say; the Pact for the Future; the one dated current linkage in this session

Book context: queried - the core account's verdict section listing the weaknesses (the lack of a permanent army, the problem of when to become involved, the increasing membership from the 1970s, waste and duplication among agencies, and the shortage of funds) and the strengths (a world assembly where representatives of around 190 nations can come together and even the smallest nation can make itself heard, wars brought to an end more quickly and further conflict prevented with human suffering and bloodshed prevented by peacekeeping forces and refugee agencies, around 85,000 peacekeepers in action at the time of writing in 2012, valuable work investigating and publicizing human rights violations under repressive regimes, and the stimulation of international co-operation on economic, social and technical matters); and its section on the future, including Kofi Annan's reputation as head of peacekeeping operations, his ordering of a thorough review of all peace operations, the report published in 2000 recommending permanent brigade-size forces of five thousand troops ready for immediate deployment and commanded by military professionals, and his Agenda for Further Change of September 2002 strengthening the role in fighting terrorism and streamlining the cumbersome budget system, with the judgement that none of the suggested reforms is beyond the bounds of possibility
