### Visual: every reform proposal sorted by what it would actually require

```text
   ===================================================================
   CATEGORY A - REQUIRES CHARTER AMENDMENT (Art 108)
   Two-thirds of the General Assembly
   PLUS ratification by two-thirds of members
   *** INCLUDING ALL FIVE PERMANENT MEMBERS ***
   -------------------------------------------------------------------
     - adding or removing PERMANENT MEMBERS
     - abolishing or restricting the VETO
     - changing the SIZE of the Council (done once: 11 -> 15, 1965)
     - abolishing an ORGAN (which is why the Trusteeship Council was
       SUSPENDED, not abolished, on 1 Nov 1994)
   RECORD: enlargements yes (Council once, ECOSOC twice);
           REDISTRIBUTION OF POWER - NEVER
   ===================================================================
   CATEGORY B - ACHIEVABLE BY PRACTICE, WITHOUT AMENDMENT
   -------------------------------------------------------------------
     - the ABSTENTION CONVENTION (already done: abstention by a
       permanent member gradually accepted as not a veto, NEVER
       written into the Charter)
     - 'UNITING FOR PEACE' (already done: res 377 A (V), 3 Nov 1950,
       NOT added to the Charter)
     - PEACEKEEPING ITSELF (already done: nowhere in the Charter)
     - STANDBY / RAPIDLY DEPLOYABLE FORCES under Art 43 machinery
       that was designed and NEVER IMPLEMENTED
     - working methods, transparency, consultation with troop
       contributors, restraint in the use of the veto
   ===================================================================
   CATEGORY C - ADMINISTRATION AND FINANCE
   -------------------------------------------------------------------
     - budget systems, assessment, arrears
     - agency overlap and duplication
     - staffing, procurement, shared services, evaluation
   RECORD: this is where almost ALL achieved reform has happened
   ===================================================================

   THE RECORDED REFORM AGENDA
   +-----------------------------------------------------------------+
   | KOFI ANNAN, Secretary-General from December 1996, had gained an |
   | excellent reputation as head of peacekeeping operations         |
   | ---- ordered A THOROUGH REVIEW OF ALL UN PEACE OPERATIONS       |
   | 2000  THE RESULTING REPORT recommended, among other things,     |
   |       that the UN should maintain PERMANENT BRIGADE-SIZE FORCES |
   |       OF 5,000 TROOPS, READY FOR IMMEDIATE DEPLOYMENT,          |
   |       COMMANDED BY MILITARY PROFESSIONALS                       |
   |       [Report of the Panel on United Nations Peace Operations,  |
   |        the Brahimi Report, 21 August 2000]                      |
   | 2002  AGENDA FOR FURTHER CHANGE (September), prompted by the    |
   |       spread of terrorism and the September 2001 attacks:       |
   |       reforms to strengthen the UN's role in FIGHTING TERRORISM |
   |       and a much-needed STREAMLINING OF THE CUMBERSOME BUDGET   |
   |       SYSTEM                                                     |
   | VERDICT: 'These things take time, but NONE OF THE SUGGESTED     |
   |          REFORMS IS BEYOND THE BOUNDS OF POSSIBILITY'           |
   +-----------------------------------------------------------------+

   NOTICE WHERE THOSE TWO AGENDAS SIT: the 2000 report is CATEGORY B,
   the 2002 agenda is CATEGORY B and C. NEITHER touches CATEGORY A.
```

*⚠️ Sort any reform proposal into those three boxes before writing a word about it, and the answer organises itself. Everything that has been achieved in eighty years sits in B or C. Everything that dominates public discussion - permanent seats, the veto - sits in A, and A requires the consent of exactly those whose position it would alter. ⚠️ This is not pessimism; it is the structure of the problem, and an answer that names the structure will be more useful than one that lists demands.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Reform is the part of this topic where candidates write most and say least, because "the UN needs reform" is a sentence with which everyone agrees and which contains no information.

⚠️ The way to make it informative is to ask a single question of every proposal: **what would actually have to happen for this to occur?**

⚠️ For a proposal to give India a permanent seat, the answer is: a two-thirds vote of the Assembly, ratification by two-thirds of the membership, and ratification by every one of the five permanent members - each of which would be consenting to the dilution of its own position, and at least one of which would be consenting to elevate a neighbour it has reason not to elevate.

⚠️ For a proposal to hold brigade-size forces ready for immediate deployment, the answer is: agreement among contributing states and money. ⚠️ No amendment at all.

⚠️ Those two answers are so different that treating both as "UN reform" obscures everything. ⚠️ Hence the three categories in the visual, and hence the rule for this lesson: **classify first, evaluate second.**

### 2. The recorded weaknesses, and what each one actually is

✅ **Fact:** the core account's verdict is measured. ✅ The organisation **has been in existence for well over half a century, but is still nowhere near achieving its basic aims**; the world is still full of economic and social problems and acts of aggression and wars continue; and its **failures were caused to some extent by weaknesses in its system**.

✅ **Fact:** the weaknesses are set out as follows.

- ✅ **The lack of a permanent army.** ✅ This makes it difficult to prevail upon powerful states to accept decisions if they choose to put self-interest first; the organisation has to rely on member nations to provide troops. ✅ The USSR ignored demands to withdraw from **Hungary in 1956** and **Afghanistan in 1980**; ✅ Somalia and Bosnia between 1992 and 1995 **showed the impossibility of stopping a war when the warring parties were not ready to stop fighting**; ✅ and **the USA and Britain were determined to attack Iraq in 2003 without authorization, and the organisation could do nothing about it**, especially with the USA as the world's only superpower.
- ✅ **When should the organisation become involved?** ✅ Sometimes it **hangs back too long**, so the problem becomes harder to solve; sometimes it hesitates so long that it scarcely becomes involved at all - as with **Vietnam** and **Angola**. ✅ This left it open to **accusations of indecision and lack of firmness**, and caused some states to **put more faith in their own regional organizations such as NATO**; many agreements were worked out without involving it, including **the end of the Vietnam War, the Camp David peace between Israel and Egypt in 1979 and the settlement of the Rhodesia/Zimbabwe problem in the same year**.
- ✅ Part of this problem was that **the Council was hampered by the veto**; although Uniting for Peace could offset this to some extent, **the veto could still cause long delays before decisive action was taken**. ✅ **If a potential aggressor knew that his forces would be met by a UN armed force, equipped and mandated to fight, this would be a powerful disincentive** - for example if a force had been deployed on the Kuwait side of the Iraqi-Kuwait frontier in 1990, or on the Croatian side of the Serbia-Croatia border in 1991, **hostilities might well have been prevented from breaking out**.
- ✅ **The increasing membership from the 1970s** and the resulting political friction, examined in Lesson 11.
- ✅ **Waste of effort and resources among the agencies**, examined in Lesson 13.
- ✅ **Shortage of funds**, examined in Lesson 13.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** sort those six into the three categories and the reform map draws itself.

| Recorded weakness | Category | Why |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ No permanent army | ⚠️ **B** | ⚠️ Article 43 machinery exists and was never implemented; standby arrangements need agreement and money, not amendment |
| ✅ Timing of involvement, and veto-induced delay | ⚠️ **A for the veto; B for the practice** | ⚠️ Abolishing the veto needs amendment; restraint in its use, earlier deployment and preventive presence do not |
| ✅ Political friction from expanded membership | ⚠️ **Neither** | ⚠️ This is not a defect to be repaired but the consequence of universality; the only "remedy" would be to unmake the organisation's representativeness |
| ✅ Agency overlap and waste | ⚠️ **C**, with a limit | ⚠️ ECOSOC can coordinate but not instruct, so consolidation needs each agency's own membership to agree |
| ✅ Shortage of funds | ⚠️ **C**, with a limit | ⚠️ Assessment and budgeting are reformable; the dependence on a few large contributors is structural |

⚠️ Two observations follow. ⚠️ First, the single most discussed reform - the veto - is the only one of the six that requires amendment, and it is therefore the only one that has never moved. ⚠️ Second, the **preventive-deployment** suggestion embedded in the second weakness is genuinely important and is almost always missed: the claim that a force positioned **before** hostilities, equipped and mandated to fight, would have deterred the invasions of 1990 and 1991. ⚠️ That is a Category B proposal with a large potential effect, and it connects directly to the standing-capability recommendation of 2000.

### 3. The recorded strengths, stated in full

✅ **Fact:** **in spite of all these criticisms, it would be wrong to write the organisation off as a failure, and there can be no doubt that the world would be a far worse place without it.** ✅ Four strengths are recorded.

- ✅ **It provides a world assembly where representatives of around 190 nations can come together and talk to each other. Even the smallest nation has a chance to make itself heard in a world forum.**
- ✅ **Although it has not prevented wars, it has been successful in bringing some wars to an end more quickly, and has prevented further conflict. A great deal of human suffering and bloodshed have been prevented by the actions of peacekeeping forces and refugee agencies.** ✅ At the time of writing, in **2012**, there were **around eighty-five thousand peacekeepers in action across the world**.
- ✅ **It has done valuable work investigating and publicizing human rights violations under repressive regimes like the military governments of Chile and Zaire**, slowly influencing governments by bringing international pressure to bear.
- ✅ **Perhaps its most important achievement has been to stimulate international co-operation on economic, social and technical matters. Millions of people, especially in poorer countries, are better off thanks to the work of the agencies.** ✅ It continues to involve itself in current problems - **UNESCO, the ILO and the WHO running a joint project to help drug addicts, and a series of fifteen conferences on AIDS** to coordinate the struggle against the disease, particularly in Africa.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the first strength is stated in the weakest possible terms - a place where people can talk - and it is in fact the most consequential of the four, for a reason worth stating.

⚠️ A permanent universal forum removes the largest transaction cost in international politics: the cost of arranging a conversation. ⚠️ Before 1945, a state wishing to raise a grievance against another had to construct an occasion for doing so, and constructing an occasion was itself an escalatory act. ⚠️ After 1945 the occasion exists permanently, annually, with an agenda and a translation service in six languages. ⚠️ **The smallest state's ability to be heard is not a courtesy; it is the removal of a structural disadvantage that had previously been absolute.**

⚠️ The fourth strength should be read with the expenditure figure from Lesson 13 behind it. ⚠️ An institution that devotes more than ninety per cent of its annual expenditure to economic and social activities and is judged to have made millions of people better off has, on that measure, done its main job - and its main job is the second of its three founding aims.

### 4. The reform agenda that was actually pursued

✅ **Fact:** **many people thought that with the end of the Cold War, most of the world's problems would disappear. In fact this did not happen; during the 1990s there seemed to be more conflicts than ever before, and the world seemed to be less and less stable.** ✅ There was **still a vitally important role to play as international peacekeeper, and many people were anxious for the organisation to reform and strengthen itself.**

✅ **Fact:** **Kofi Annan, who became Secretary-General in December 1996, had gained an excellent reputation over the previous few years as head of peacekeeping operations.** ✅ **He was well aware of the organisation's weaknesses and was determined to do something about them.** ✅ **He ordered a thorough review of all peace operations; the resulting report, published in 2000, recommended, among other things, that the organisation should maintain permanent brigade-size forces of five thousand troops, which would be ready for immediate deployment, commanded by military professionals.**

✅ **Fact:** that report is the **Report of the Panel on United Nations Peace Operations**, known as the **Brahimi Report**, issued on **21 August 2000**.

✅ **Fact:** **the spread of terrorism, especially with the September 2001 attacks on New York, prompted Annan, now in his second term, to produce his Agenda for Further Change in September 2002.** ✅ This was **a plan for reforms to strengthen the organisation's role in fighting terrorism, and it included a much-needed streamlining of the cumbersome budget system.** ✅ **These things take time, but none of the suggested reforms is beyond the bounds of possibility.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the standing-force recommendation is the single most important reform proposal in this session and it deserves to be understood as what it is - an attempt to implement, by practice, the capability the Charter designed in Article 43 and never delivered.

⚠️ Note the three specifications, because each answers a recorded failure. ⚠️ **Brigade-size forces of five thousand** answers the under-resourcing of Bosnia. ⚠️ **Ready for immediate deployment** answers the second recorded weakness - hanging back too long - and the preventive-deployment argument about 1990 and 1991. ⚠️ **Commanded by military professionals** answers the command incoherence visible whenever national contingents serve under divided authority.

⚠️ And note the verdict that accompanies it: **none of the suggested reforms is beyond the bounds of possibility**. ⚠️ That judgement is defensible precisely because every item in the 2000 and 2002 agendas sits in Category B or C. ⚠️ The reforms that *are* beyond the bounds of near-term possibility - permanent membership and the veto - are not in those agendas at all, and their absence is itself informative.

### 5. The durable structural argument, and how to state it safely

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** there is one reform argument that can be made in a history answer with complete safety, because it rests entirely on facts established earlier in this session and requires no claim about any current negotiation.

⚠️ The argument is this. ✅ The Council's permanent membership was fixed by an assessment of power made in 1944-45, by four states meeting at Dumbarton Oaks and a conference of fifty at San Francisco. ✅ The organisation's membership has since risen to 193, through decolonisation, state dissolution and late self-determination. ✅ The Assembly reflects that transformation fully, one state one vote. ✅ ECOSOC was enlarged twice to reflect it, from eighteen to twenty-seven in force in 1965 and to fifty-four in force in 1973. ✅ The Council was enlarged once, in the non-permanent seats only, from eleven to fifteen. ✅ And Article 23 states that elected seats should be filled with due regard **first** to a member's contribution to the maintenance of international peace and security and to the other purposes of the organisation, and **also** to equitable geographical distribution - a criterion the Charter applies to the ten and not to the five.

⚠️ **Stated in one sentence for an answer: the composition of authority in this organisation was fixed in 1945 and the composition of the organisation was not, and every other organ has been adjusted to the change except the one whose decisions bind.**

⚠️ That argument has three properties that make it examination-proof. ⚠️ It is entirely historical, so it cannot go out of date. ⚠️ It uses no figure that is unstable. ⚠️ And it is an argument about structure rather than about any state's claim, so it can be made without taking a position that an examiner might regard as advocacy.

✅ **The counter-argument must be given too**, and it is the one from Lesson 5. ⚠️ The permanent members are the states whose forces and finance any enforcement depends on; an arrangement that made enforcement decisions without them would produce decisions nobody executes. ⚠️ And the defensible synthesis, already stated in Lesson 5, is that **the veto is defensible as a structural necessity and indefensible as a permanent allocation.**

### 6. Where India's claim sits, and what a history answer may say

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the point at which the boundary discipline matters most, and observing it visibly will improve rather than weaken an answer.

✅ **Fact:** India is a founding member: it participated in the San Francisco conference and is among the original members. ✅ India has served repeatedly as an elected non-permanent member of the Council, most recently for the **2021-22 term**, its eighth. ✅ India is one of the four states of the **G4** grouping - **Brazil, Germany, India and Japan** - which support one another's candidacies for permanent membership, and is opposed in that objective by the **Uniting for Consensus** grouping, which resists the creation of new permanent seats. ✅ Negotiations on Council reform have been conducted since **2008** through the **Intergovernmental Negotiations** process in the General Assembly.

⚠️ **What a history answer may do with this.** ⚠️ It may state the structural argument of section 5 and observe that a state's claim to elected or permanent membership is made on the Charter's own Article 23 criterion - contribution to the maintenance of peace and security and to the other purposes of the organisation, and equitable geographical distribution. ⚠️ It may note that the organisation's peacekeeping record has depended throughout on troop-contributing states rather than on any force of its own, which is the general fact established in Lesson 5 and confirmed by every case in Lessons 6 to 15.

⚠️ **What a history answer must not do.** ❌ It must not assert any current outcome, negotiating position, vote count or timetable for Council reform; ❌ it must not state current troop-contribution or budget figures for any state; ❌ and it must not convert the structural argument into advocacy for a particular state's seat, because that is the register error identified in the session opening. ⚠️ Live Indian institutional and diplomatic material is owned by the Indian foreign-policy and international-relations owners, and the correct move in a General Studies Paper I answer is to make the structural argument and hand over.

⚠️ The formulation that satisfies both requirements: **the case for reform is that the Charter's own criterion for Council membership is applied to ten seats and not to five, and that the mismatch between the composition of authority and the composition of the organisation has widened continuously since 1945; what follows from that case is a matter of negotiation among members and is not settled by the historical record.**

### 7. The system's own later restatements

✅ **Fact:** the organisation's institutional-reform arc did not stop with the agendas of 2000 and 2002. ✅ On **22 September 2024** the General Assembly adopted the **Pact for the Future**, resolution **A/RES/79/1**, with the **Global Digital Compact** and the **Declaration on Future Generations** annexed to it. ✅ The organisation's 2025 observance of its eightieth anniversary linked that anniversary to the Pact and to the **UN80 initiative**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the significance of the Pact for a history answer is structural rather than substantive. ⚠️ It shows that the reform question in the 2020s spans peace and security, development, digital cooperation, future generations and institutional design simultaneously - which is a far wider agenda than the peacekeeping-and-budget agenda of 2000-02, and evidence that the organisation's own conception of what needs changing has broadened.

⚠️ And it shows something about method. ⚠️ The Pact is an **Assembly resolution with annexes**, which is to say a Category B and C instrument: it commits, declares and directs without amending anything. ⚠️ Twenty-four years after the standing-force recommendation, the organisation was still reforming through the only route available to it.

⚠️ **Status discipline, stated explicitly.** ❌ No claim is made here that any reform of the Council's composition or voting rule has occurred, is agreed or is imminent. ⚠️ The Pact and the anniversary initiative concern the adaptation of the existing multilateral system, not its constitutional reconstruction.

### 8. One dated current linkage, used once and limited here

📰 **Current linkage, retrieved 29 September 2026.** ✅ The organisation's **UN80 Initiative** was **launched in March 2025** and **welcomed by General Assembly resolution 79/318**. ✅ It is described by the organisation as a system-wide effort directed at **how the system is structured, managed and coordinated** - modernising outdated arrangements and reducing bureaucracy, fragmentation and duplication - rather than at redefining what the system does.

✅ It advances through **three workstreams**: efficiency proposals across the system, with initial proposals reflected in the **revised estimates for the 2026 Secretariat proposed programme budget**; the **Report of the Mandate Implementation Review**, considered by a member-state-led **Informal Ad Hoc Working Group**; and **"Shifting Paradigms: United to Deliver"**, the Secretary-General's report on potential **structural and programmatic realignments**.

✅ In **November 2025** an **Action Plan** was presented to member states at an informal meeting of the General Assembly, setting out how the proposals already on the table would be advanced: **87 actions grouped into 31 work packages across the three workstreams**, with responsibilities, timelines and the intergovernmental bodies that will consider them identified. ✅ A **Steering Committee chaired monthly by the Secretary-General** and a **Task Force meeting weekly** were established to direct and coordinate implementation, and a **public actions dashboard** was launched. ✅ The organisation stated expressly that the Initiative **is not a solution to the system's financial crisis**, which it described as continuing.

⚠️ **How this is used, and its limit.** ⚠️ It is used for exactly one purpose: to show that the reform categories established in this lesson remain the live ones. ⚠️ Look at where the three workstreams sit. ⚠️ Efficiency and budget estimates are **Category C**. ⚠️ Mandate review and structural realignment of the system's entities are **Category B and C**. ⚠️ **Not one of the three workstreams concerns the composition of the Security Council or the veto**, which remain in Category A and therefore outside what the organisation can do for itself.

❌ This linkage is **not** used as evidence about any historical case in this session, about any campaign, about any state's claim to membership, or about the outcome of any reform negotiation. ⚠️ It is taught once, here, recalled afterwards only as a revision item, and is not extended.

### 9. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** the three-category framework is a counsel of despair dressed as analysis. It concludes that the only reforms achievable are the ones that do not matter, and that the ones that matter are impossible. If that is right, eighty years of reform effort has been displacement activity, and the honest conclusion is that the organisation cannot be reformed and should be replaced.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection misreads Category B, which is where the framework's optimism actually lies, and the misreading is worth correcting because it changes the conclusion entirely.

⚠️ Consider what Category B has already produced. ✅ The **abstention convention** - a change to the Council's voting practice, adopted without amendment, that has made the Council far more able to act than a literal reading of Article 27 would permit. ✅ **Uniting for Peace** - a procedure never added to the Charter and used many times. ✅ **Peacekeeping itself** - an instrument nowhere mentioned in the Charter, invented in 1956, which has been the organisation's principal security activity ever since and which the account credits with preventing a great deal of human suffering and bloodshed.

⚠️ Those are not marginal adjustments. ⚠️ The organisation's three most important operational capabilities were all created in Category B. ⚠️ **The Charter has proved far more adaptable in practice than it is in text, and the historical record of successful reform is a record of working around Article 108 rather than through it.**

⚠️ Two concessions keep this honest. ⚠️ First, Category B cannot reach the veto, and no amount of practice will make a permanent member consent to be bound against its will. ⚠️ Second, the adaptations of Category B are reversible in a way amendments are not, because a convention can decay as quietly as it formed.

⚠️ The defensible formulation: **the organisation cannot amend its constitution and has repeatedly rewritten its own practice - so the realistic reform question is not whether the Charter will change but how much can be achieved without changing it, and the answer, on eight decades of evidence, is considerably more than the amendment record suggests.**

### 10. Examination use

⚠️ This lesson answers every reform question and supplies the qualified position that keeps such an answer from becoming advocacy.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "The United Nations reflects the world of 1945 and not the world of today." Examine the case for reform of the Security Council.
> ⚠️ Discuss the major criticisms of the United Nations and assess the prospects for reform.
> ⚠️ Examine the efforts made to strengthen United Nations peacekeeping since the end of the Cold War.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first framing, the structure is: state the mismatch as a dated historical argument; give the Article 23 criterion and note that it is applied to ten seats and not five; give the counter-argument that enforcement depends on the states the veto protects; state the synthesis - necessary as a structure, indefensible as a permanent allocation; and then close with the Article 108 requirement, which explains why the debate has lasted decades without resolution. ⚠️ Do **not** end with a demand. ⚠️ For the third framing, lead with the 2000 report's three specifications and show how each answers a named failure of the preceding decade.

### Traps

- ❌ Writing that the organisation "must be reformed" without saying what reform requires. ⚠️ Classify first: **A needs amendment under Article 108; B needs practice; C needs administration.**
- ❌ Stating that Charter amendment needs only an Assembly majority. ✅ **Article 108:** two-thirds of the Assembly **and** ratification by two-thirds of members **including all five permanent members**.
- ❌ Attributing the standing-force proposal to the wrong moment. ✅ Annan ordered a review of all peace operations; the **report published in 2000** recommended **permanent brigade-size forces of five thousand, ready for immediate deployment, commanded by military professionals**.
- ❌ Omitting the 2002 agenda. ✅ The **Agenda for Further Change, September 2002**, addressed **terrorism** and a **streamlining of the cumbersome budget system**.
- ❌ Forgetting the recorded strengths. ✅ A world assembly of **around 190 nations** where the smallest can be heard; wars ended more quickly and conflict prevented, with **around eighty-five thousand peacekeepers** recorded in action in **2012**; human-rights investigation and publicity; and the stimulation of economic, social and technical co-operation.
- ❌ Asserting a current reform outcome. ❌ No claim is made in this session about any current negotiation, vote, timetable or expansion.
- ❌ Turning the structural argument into advocacy. ⚠️ State the mismatch and the Article 23 criterion; leave the conclusion to negotiation.
- ❌ Presenting the veto as pure obstruction. ⚠️ Give the counter-argument: enforcement depends on the states the veto protects.

### Revision notes

1. ⚠️ **Three categories:** **A** needs **Article 108** amendment - permanent membership, the veto, organ size, abolition of an organ; **B** is achievable by practice; **C** is administration and finance.
2. ✅ **Article 108:** two-thirds of the Assembly **plus** ratification by two-thirds of members **including all five permanent members**. ✅ Article 109 applies the same permanent-member ratification to a review conference.
3. ⚠️ Category B has already produced the **abstention convention**, **Uniting for Peace** and **peacekeeping itself** - the organisation's three most important operational capabilities, none of them in the Charter.
4. ✅ **Six recorded weaknesses:** no permanent army; the problem of when to become involved, with veto-induced delay; friction from expanded membership; waste and duplication among agencies; shortage of funds - with Hungary 1956, Afghanistan 1980, Somalia and Bosnia 1992-95 and Iraq 2003 as the evidence for the first.
5. ✅ States put more faith in **regional organizations such as NATO**; agreements reached without the organisation included the **end of the Vietnam War**, **Camp David 1979** and the **Rhodesia/Zimbabwe settlement of 1979**.
6. ✅ **Preventive deployment argument:** a force on the Kuwait side of the frontier in 1990, or on the Croatian side in 1991, **might well have prevented hostilities**.
7. ✅ **Four recorded strengths:** a world assembly of **around 190 nations** where even the smallest is heard; wars ended more quickly and conflict prevented by peacekeeping forces and refugee agencies, with **around 85,000 peacekeepers in action in 2012**; investigation and publicity of human-rights violations under repressive regimes; and the stimulation of economic, social and technical co-operation, with UNESCO, the ILO and WHO running a joint project on drug addiction and **fifteen conferences on AIDS**.
8. ✅ **Annan from December 1996**, previously head of peacekeeping; ordered a thorough review; the **report published in 2000** - the **Brahimi Report of 21 August 2000** - recommended **permanent brigade-size forces of five thousand, ready for immediate deployment, commanded by military professionals**.
9. ✅ **Agenda for Further Change, September 2002:** strengthening the role in fighting terrorism and **streamlining the cumbersome budget system**. ✅ Verdict: **none of the suggested reforms is beyond the bounds of possibility**.
10. ⚠️ Both agendas sit in **Category B and C**; neither touches Category A - and that absence is itself evidence.
11. ⚠️ **The durable structural argument:** permanent membership fixed by a 1944-45 assessment; membership now 193; the Assembly fully adjusted; ECOSOC enlarged twice (to 27 in force 1965, to 54 in force 1973); the Council enlarged once in its non-permanent seats only; and **Article 23's criterion** - contribution to peace and security and the other purposes, plus equitable geographical distribution - applied to ten seats and not to five.
12. ⚠️ **The counter-argument:** enforcement depends on the forces and finance of the states the veto protects. **Synthesis:** necessary as a structure, indefensible as a permanent allocation.
13. ✅ **India:** founding member and San Francisco participant; elected non-permanent member most recently for **2021-22**, its eighth term; member of the **G4** with Brazil, Germany and Japan; opposed by **Uniting for Consensus**; reform discussed in the **Intergovernmental Negotiations** process since **2008**.
14. ❌ A history answer must not assert current negotiating positions, outcomes, timetables or contribution figures; it makes the structural argument and hands over.
15. ✅ **Pact for the Future**, General Assembly resolution **A/RES/79/1**, adopted **22 September 2024**, with the **Global Digital Compact** and **Declaration on Future Generations** annexed; the 2025 eightieth-anniversary observance linked it to the **UN80** initiative.
16. 📰 **One dated linkage:** the **UN80 Initiative**, launched **March 2025** and welcomed by resolution **79/318**, advancing through three workstreams - system efficiency reflected in the revised estimates for the 2026 Secretariat proposed programme budget; the **Mandate Implementation Review** before a member-state-led informal working group; and **"Shifting Paradigms: United to Deliver"** on structural and programmatic realignment - with an **Action Plan of 87 actions in 31 work packages** presented in **November 2025**, a monthly Steering Committee, a weekly Task Force and a public dashboard, and an express statement that it is **not** a solution to the system's financial crisis. ⚠️ **None of the three workstreams concerns the Council's composition or the veto.**

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate proposes three reforms: abolishing the veto, creating a rapidly deployable standing force, and consolidating overlapping agencies. Classify each by what it would actually require, and state which of the three the historical record suggests is most achievable and why.

**Model answer:** Abolishing the veto is a Charter amendment. Under Article 108 it would need adoption by a two-thirds vote of the General Assembly and ratification by two-thirds of the members including all five permanent members - that is, the consent of every state whose blocking power it would remove - and Article 109 imposes the same permanent-member ratification on any alteration recommended by a review conference. The record is that amendments have enlarged organs and adjusted vote thresholds and have never redistributed power. A rapidly deployable standing force requires no amendment at all. The Charter already contemplates it: Article 43 obliges members to make forces available under special agreements governing numbers, types and readiness, and Article 47 establishes a Military Staff Committee to direct them; no such agreement was ever concluded, so the gap is one of implementation. The review Annan ordered produced a report in 2000 recommending permanent brigade-size forces of five thousand, ready for immediate deployment and commanded by military professionals, and what that needs is agreement among contributing states and money. Consolidating agencies is largely administrative, but it has a legal limit: ECOSOC may conclude relationship agreements, consult, recommend and obtain reports, and it cannot instruct a specialized agency, because each has its own constitution and membership - so consolidation requires each agency's own members to agree. The historical record points clearly to the second as most achievable, and for a reason that generalises: the organisation's three most important operational capabilities - the convention that a permanent member's abstention is not a veto, the Uniting for Peace procedure of 1950, and peacekeeping itself, invented in 1956 - were all created by practice without amending the Charter. Reform in this organisation has historically worked around Article 108 rather than through it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that because the veto is the most discussed problem it is the most likely subject of reform. The opposite follows from the amendment rule: the reforms that attract most attention are precisely those requiring the consent of the states they would constrain, which is why the agendas the organisation has actually pursued - the peace-operations review of 2000, the budget and counter-terrorism agenda of 2002, and the workstreams of the current institutional initiative - contain no proposal touching the Council's composition or its voting rule.

## Lesson 18 - The verdict and the answer architecture: converting eight decades into marks

Progress: 18/18 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the two-question verdict and why a single verdict is always wrong; the directive and demand map; four qualified theses; mark-scaled structures for 10, 15 and 20; the verdict scaffolds; the complete factual-risk control list; the boundary handovers in one place; the seven items to rehearse

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the canonical owners' directive and demand map, their four qualified thesis templates, their mark-scaled structures for ten, fifteen and twenty marks, their verdict scaffolds for the three commonest framings, their complete factual-risk control list, and the probable-question banks recorded in both owners; together with the core account's own end-of-chapter questions on whether the social, economic and humanitarian work has been more successful and valuable than the peacekeeping role, on whether the organisation has only been successful when a superpower intervened to support it, and on whether it has been more successful in dealing with conflicts since 1990 than during the Cold War
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session was used in Lesson 17 and is not extended
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the decision procedure, from stem to spine

```text
   STEP 1   READ THE DIRECTIVE, NOT THE TOPIC
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   'How far / To what extent'  -> a DEGREE question: you must argue
                                  BOTH directions and land on a
                                  qualified position
   'Assess / Evaluate'         -> a JUDGEMENT question: state the
                                  criterion FIRST, then apply it
   'Examine / Discuss'         -> an ANATOMY question: take the
                                  thing apart and show what each
                                  part does
   'Explain'                   -> a MECHANISM question: causes and
                                  processes, not evaluation
   'Comment' on a quotation    -> engage the CLAIM the quotation
                                  makes, not the subject it names
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   STEP 2   DECIDE WHICH LAYER LEADS
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   HISTORICAL ORIGIN AND RECORD -> Paper I register: dated evidence,
                                   named cases, causal explanation
   STRUCTURE AND MANDATE        -> Paper II register: organs, powers,
                                   legal categories, status discipline
   (Prepare both; let the directive decide which leads and which
    supplies one supporting paragraph)
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   STEP 3   PICK THE THESIS
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   COMPARATIVE · PERFORMANCE · BALANCE · STRUCTURAL  (section 3)
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   STEP 4   BUILD THE SPINE TO THE MARK
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   10 marks / 150 words : thesis -> TWO items -> condition -> verdict
   15 marks / 250 words : thesis -> design lesson -> structure ->
                          mixed record -> non-military work -> verdict
   20 marks / 250 words : all of the above PLUS structural limits,
                          the post-Cold-War change, and the reform
                          question kept status-disciplined
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   STEP 5   RUN THE RISK CONTROLS (section 6) BEFORE THE LAST LINE
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   STEP 6   CLOSE ON A QUALIFIED VERDICT, NEVER ON A DEMAND
```

*⚠️ The procedure is worth rehearsing as a procedure, because the commonest failure in this topic is not ignorance but misdirection: a well-informed answer to a question that was not asked. Steps 1 and 2 take about thirty seconds and prevent it.*

### 1. Why a single verdict is always wrong

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** every evaluative question on this topic is a trap of the same shape. It invites a single judgement about an institution that performs several unrelated functions with radically different success rates.

⚠️ The resolution is to answer **two questions instead of one**, and to say so explicitly in the opening lines.

| Question | Answer | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ **Judged as a world government - a body able to enforce its decisions against any member?** | ⚠️ A failure, and by design | ✅ No permanent army; ✅ the veto; ✅ Hungary 1956; ✅ Czechoslovakia 1968; ✅ Afghanistan 1980; ✅ the Nicaragua merits judgment of 1986; ✅ Iraq 2003 |
| ⚠️ **Judged as a permanent forum, norm-setter, crisis manager and humanitarian coordinator?** | ⚠️ The most successful international institution ever built | ✅ A world assembly of around 190 nations where the smallest is heard; ✅ smallpox eradicated, with the last natural case in 1977; ✅ the Universal Declaration; ✅ Suez, West New Guinea, Cambodia, Mozambique; ✅ more than ninety per cent of expenditure on economic and social work by 1980 |

⚠️ The two answers are not in tension; they are answers to different questions, and the difference is the one built into the design in Lesson 1. ⚠️ **The organisation is weak exactly where it must coerce the powerful and strong exactly where it must coordinate the willing** - and since almost all of its work falls in the second category, a verdict drawn only from the first is a verdict on a minority of the institution.

⚠️ Note that this is not a formula for sitting on the fence. ⚠️ It is a claim with a mechanism attached, and the mechanism is testable against every case in this session.

### 2. The directive and demand map

⚠️ Use this before writing. The middle column is what is actually being tested; the right column is the commonest wasted answer.

| If the stem says | It is really testing | Do **not** write |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ *How far has the UN overcome the weaknesses of the League?* | ⚠️ Design differences **and** what stayed the same | ⚠️ A structure comparison, or a League narrative |
| ⚠️ *Assess the UN as a peacekeeping organisation* | ⚠️ A case-based record with a stated success condition | ⚠️ A list of missions |
| ⚠️ *"The greatest strength of the UN lies outside the battlefield." Discuss* | ⚠️ The non-military record as the real record, with the comparison refused | ⚠️ Only the Security Council |
| ⚠️ *Explain the structure and mandate* | ⚠️ Charter organs, actual powers and the legal categories | ⚠️ Confusing organs, agencies and funds |
| ⚠️ *Reform of the Security Council* | ⚠️ The structural logic of the veto, kept date-disciplined | ⚠️ Undated claims about live negotiations |
| ⚠️ *Global governance since 1945* | ⚠️ The historical evolution of the multilateral order | ⚠️ A current-affairs summary |
| ⚠️ *"The UN has only succeeded when a superpower supported it"* | ⚠️ The two-limbed success condition, tested against a hostile case | ⚠️ Agreement, followed by examples of failure |
| ⚠️ *More successful since 1990 than during the Cold War?* | ⚠️ Volume against quality; freedom against strength | ⚠️ A chronological narrative of the 1990s |
| ⚠️ *Sovereignty against human protection* | ⚠️ Article 2(7), its Chapter VII proviso, and the 2005 formulation | ⚠️ A moral argument without the legal route |

### 3. Four qualified theses

⚠️ Each of these can be deployed in a single opening sentence and each commits the answer to an argument rather than a description.

- ⚠️ **Comparative.** "The United Nations improved on the League in scope, membership and social work while retaining its central defect - the dependence of enforcement on the very great powers whose conduct most needs restraining."
- ⚠️ **Performance.** "The organisation's security record is not random: it succeeds where the great powers are indifferent or agreed, and fails where their interests collide directly - which is why Suez and Hungary, one month apart under identical procedures, produced opposite results."
- ⚠️ **Balance.** "Judged as a world government the organisation is a failure; judged as a permanent forum, norm-setter and humanitarian coordinator it is the most successful international institution ever built."
- ⚠️ **Structural.** "The veto is not a flaw in the design; it *is* the design - the price of getting the great powers inside the organisation rather than outside it, as the United States had been to the League."

⚠️ A fifth, useful where the question concerns reform specifically:

- ⚠️ **Reform.** "The Charter has proved unamendable where power is at stake and remarkably adaptable in practice - the abstention convention, Uniting for Peace and peacekeeping itself were all created without changing a word of it."

### 4. Mark-scaled structures

| Marks | Structure |
|---|---|
| ⚠️ **10 (150 words)** | ⚠️ Thesis -> **two** organs or functions, **or** two cases (one success, one failure) -> the success condition -> qualified verdict |
| ⚠️ **15 (250 words)** | ⚠️ Thesis -> origins and the design lesson from the League -> structure -> the mixed security record with the condition stated -> the non-military work -> verdict |
| ⚠️ **20 (250 words)** | ⚠️ All of the above **plus** structural limitations, the post-Cold-War change in the nature of conflict, and the reform question kept status-disciplined -> verdict |

⚠️ Two cautions about the twenty-mark structure. ⚠️ The word limit does not rise with the marks, so a twenty-mark answer is not a longer fifteen-mark answer; it is a **denser** one, and density comes from replacing description with named evidence. ⚠️ And the reform paragraph is where most twenty-mark answers lose marks, because it is where candidates drift into undated advocacy. ⚠️ Keep it to the structural argument of Lesson 17 section 5 and stop.

### 5. Verdict scaffolds for the three commonest framings

- ⚠️ **"More successful than the League?"** -> "Substantially, and for a structural reason: the organisation kept the great powers inside it, which is precisely why it can rarely act against them."
- ⚠️ **"As a peacekeeper?"** -> "Effective as a fire-break and ineffective as a fire brigade: it separates combatants and supervises transitions well, and it cannot compel a great power or resolve a civil war it has not been empowered to settle."
- ⚠️ **"Greatest strength outside the battlefield?"** -> "Yes - measured by lives affected, its health, refugee, food and education work exceeds anything achieved by its enforcement arm, and unlike enforcement it does not require great-power unanimity."

⚠️ A fourth, for the post-1990 framing that the core account's own end-of-chapter question poses:

- ⚠️ **"More successful since 1990?"** -> "Freer, not stronger: the Council could finally agree, which produced over thirty operations between 1990 and 2003 and two completed transitions in Cambodia and Mozambique; but the constraint had simply moved from the voting rule to the willingness of members to supply force, which is what Somalia, Bosnia and the absence from Rwanda demonstrate."

### 6. The complete factual-risk control list

⚠️ Run this before the final line of any answer on this topic. Each item is an error that has cost marks and each has a correction attached.

**Dates and origins**
- ❌ Do not date the founding to 26 June 1945. ✅ The Charter was **signed** then; it **came into force on 24 October 1945**, and the organisation officially came into existence in **October 1945**.
- ❌ Do not name France among the Dumbarton Oaks participants. ✅ **USSR, USA, China, Britain**, in **1944**.
- ❌ Do not say fifty-one states attended San Francisco. ✅ **Fifty** attended; there were **fifty-one original members**.

**Organs and categories**
- ❌ Do not call the International Criminal Court a principal organ. ✅ The **ICJ** is; the ICC is a **separate treaty-based court** - Rome Statute of **July 1998**, in force **1 July 2002**, inaugurated **March 2003** with **eighteen judges**.
- ❌ Do not call the World Trade Organization a specialized agency. ✅ It is not; **GATT became the WTO in January 1995**.
- ❌ Do not flatten the categories. ✅ **WHO, FAO, UNESCO, ILO, IMF and the World Bank** are specialized agencies; **UNICEF** is a fund; **UNRWA** is a General Assembly-created agency; **UNCTAD** is a body of the organisation.
- ❌ Do not say the Trusteeship Council was abolished. ✅ It **suspended operation on 1 November 1994** and remains a principal organ.
- ❌ Do not give ECOSOC twenty-seven members as its current size. ✅ **Fifty-four**; twenty-seven was correct between the amendments in force in **1965** and **1973**.

**Powers and procedure**
- ❌ Do not say nine votes suffice in the Council. ✅ Nine **including the concurring votes of the permanent members** on non-procedural matters.
- ❌ Do not say the Charter treats abstention as a veto. ✅ The practice that abstention is **not** a veto **has never been written into the Charter**.
- ❌ Do not say the Charter made no provision for forces. ✅ **Article 43** provided for special agreements; **none was ever concluded**.
- ❌ Do not say Assembly resolutions are binding. ✅ They are recommendations; the **budget apportionment under Article 17**, with the **Article 19** vote-loss sanction, is the exception.
- ❌ Do not say Uniting for Peace amended the Charter. ✅ **Resolution 377 A (V), 3 November 1950**, **not added to the Charter**.
- ❌ Do not say Charter amendment needs only a two-thirds Assembly vote. ✅ **Article 108** also requires ratification by two-thirds of members **including all five permanent members**.

**Cases**
- ❌ Do not call Korea 1950 or the Gulf 1991 peacekeeping. ✅ Both were **authorised enforcement** by member-state forces; the Gulf authorisation was resolution **678 of 29 November 1990** - "all necessary means".
- ❌ Do not say the USSR was outvoted over Korea. ✅ It was **absent**, boycotting since **January 1950** over Chinese membership.
- ❌ Do not say the Council authorised the Suez force. ✅ The Council resolution was **vetoed by Britain and France**; the Assembly voted **sixty-four to five** and a force of **five thousand from ten countries** followed.
- ❌ Do not say the organisation did nothing over Hungary. ✅ It received the appeal, was vetoed in the Council, passed the same resolution in the Assembly and set up an investigating committee; the Russians refused to co-operate.
- ❌ Do not say the organisation solved Palestine, Kashmir or Cyprus. ✅ It managed crises and obtained ceasefires; the **Annan Plan of 2004** was accepted by the Turks and **rejected by the Greeks**.
- ❌ Do not say Iraq 2003 was vetoed. ✅ **No vote was held**; the resolution was never put, because a veto was anticipated.
- ❌ Do not say the organisation failed in Somalia through poor execution. ✅ It had **set itself an impossible task from the beginning**.

**Numbers and status**
- ❌ Do not give current budget, arrears, mission-count, staffing or troop-contribution figures. ⚠️ Use only the dated figures recorded in this session.
- ❌ Do not assert any current outcome, vote or timetable for Council reform.
- ❌ Do not attribute responsibility for violence to nations, peoples or religions. ✅ Name governments, armed forces, named commanders and the organs that acted or failed to act.

### 7. Boundaries, collected in one place

⚠️ Each of these is a handover, and each should be made in one clause rather than a paragraph.

| Material | Owner | The one clause this session supplies |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ The League's aims, organs and collapse | ⚠️ The interwar international-relations topic | ⚠️ "designed against a League that had proved incapable of restraining aggressive dictators" |
| ⚠️ The Second World War | ⚠️ The war topic | ⚠️ "framed while the war was still being fought, by the states that were winning it" |
| ⚠️ Cold War diplomacy - Korea, Suez, Hungary, Cuba, détente as episodes | ⚠️ The Cold War topic | ⚠️ "the crises in which the institution was tested" |
| ⚠️ Independence movements and the end of empire | ⚠️ The decolonisation topic | ⚠️ "which transformed the membership and therefore the Assembly" |
| ⚠️ The post-1991 transition | ⚠️ The post-Cold-War topic | ⚠️ "which changed the kind of conflict the organisation faced" |
| ⚠️ India's current positions, campaign and alignments | ⚠️ Indian foreign policy and international relations | ⚠️ "a matter of negotiation among members, not settled by the historical record" |

### 8. Comparison: the four questions an examiner can ask, and the one paragraph each needs

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** almost every question in this topic reduces to one of four demands. ⚠️ Rehearse one paragraph for each and the preparation is complete.

| Demand | The paragraph it needs |
|---|---|
| ⚠️ **Design** - why is it built this way? | ⚠️ Four League defects repaired, two retained; the two retained are the price of repairing the first; universality bought with immunity |
| ⚠️ **Performance** - has it worked? | ⚠️ The two-limbed success condition, with Suez and Hungary as the controlled comparison and Korea and the Gulf as limb (b) |
| ⚠️ **Scope** - what does it actually do? | ⚠️ The non-military record, the expenditure figure, and the two of three founding aims that describe it |
| ⚠️ **Change** - can it be fixed? | ⚠️ The three reform categories, Article 108, and the record of adaptation by practice |

### 9. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** a lesson on answer technique is coaching, not history, and a candidate who learns four theses and a risk list is being taught to produce a formula rather than to think.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection would be right if the theses were detachable from the evidence, and they are not. ⚠️ Each of the four in section 3 is a compressed statement of an argument built over several lessons: the comparative thesis is Lesson 1's repaired-and-retained table; the performance thesis is Lesson 8's controlled comparison; the balance thesis is Lesson 12's expenditure and capacity evidence; the structural thesis is Lesson 2's construction sequence. ⚠️ A candidate who has not followed those arguments cannot defend the sentences, and an examiner who probes will find that out in one sub-question.

⚠️ What the technique adds is the ability to deploy the argument **under the constraint that actually applies** - one hundred and fifty or two hundred and fifty words, written once, without notes. ⚠️ Under that constraint, the difference between a candidate who has an organising claim and one who has a store of facts is decisive, and it is not a difference of knowledge.

⚠️ The defensible formulation: **the architecture is not a substitute for the history; it is the form in which the history becomes usable in an examination, and a candidate who has only one of the two will not be able to show the other.**

### 10. The seven items to rehearse

⚠️ If time is short, rehearse these and nothing else.

1. ⚠️ **The repaired-and-retained table** of Lesson 1 - four League defects fixed, two kept, and the trade that explains it.
2. ⚠️ **The construction sequence** of Lesson 2 - Dumbarton Oaks 1944, San Francisco 25 April to 26 June 1945, in force 24 October 1945, fifty-one original members, and the ratification condition that shows the veto predates the organisation.
3. ⚠️ **The Chapter VI / Chapter VII distinction** of Lesson 5, with Article 39 as the hinge and Article 43 as the rung never built.
4. ⚠️ **The Suez-Hungary controlled comparison** of Lessons 7 and 8, and the two-limbed success condition drawn from it.
5. ⚠️ **The three principles of peacekeeping** - consent, impartiality that is not neutrality, and non-use of force except in self-defence and defence of the mandate - used as a diagnostic on Somalia.
6. ⚠️ **The non-military record** of Lesson 12 with the expenditure figure of Lesson 13, as the balance material.
7. ⚠️ **The three reform categories** of Lesson 17 and Article 108, as the disciplined close to any reform question.

### Traps

- ⚠️ Answering the topic instead of the directive. ⚠️ Run step 1 of the procedure first.
- ⚠️ Writing a single verdict. ⚠️ Answer the two questions of section 1 and say that you are doing so.
- ⚠️ Treating a twenty-mark answer as a longer fifteen-mark answer. ⚠️ The word limit is the same; density must rise.
- ⚠️ Ending on a demand for reform. ⚠️ End on a qualified verdict; leave the demand to the negotiators.
- ⚠️ Blending the two preparation layers. ⚠️ Let the directive decide which leads.
- ⚠️ Using an undated current claim to close an answer. ⚠️ The historical argument of Lesson 17 section 5 closes better and cannot go stale.
- ⚠️ Listing missions under an *assess* directive. ⚠️ State the criterion first, then apply it to two or three cases.
- ⚠️ Conceding a hostile quotation entirely. ⚠️ Concede the empirical half and refuse the implied comparison.

### Revision notes

1. ⚠️ **Two questions, not one:** as a world government, a failure by design; as a forum, norm-setter, crisis manager and humanitarian coordinator, the most successful international institution ever built.
2. ⚠️ **The mechanism behind both answers:** weak where it must coerce the powerful, strong where it must coordinate the willing.
3. ⚠️ **Directives:** *how far / to what extent* = degree; *assess / evaluate* = judgement with a stated criterion; *examine / discuss* = anatomy; *explain* = mechanism; *comment* = engage the claim.
4. ⚠️ **Two layers:** historical origin and record for Paper I; structure and mandate for Paper II. Prepare both, let the directive choose.
5. ⚠️ **Four theses:** comparative, performance, balance, structural - plus a fifth for reform questions.
6. ⚠️ **10 marks:** thesis, two items, condition, verdict. **15:** thesis, design lesson, structure, mixed record, non-military work, verdict. **20:** add structural limits, the post-Cold-War change and a status-disciplined reform paragraph.
7. ⚠️ **Verdict scaffolds:** "kept the great powers inside, which is why it can rarely act against them"; "a fire-break, not a fire brigade"; "measured by lives affected, the non-military work exceeds the enforcement arm"; "freer, not stronger, after 1990".
8. ⚠️ **Risk controls, most-missed five:** October 1945 not 26 June; the ICC is not an organ; the WTO is not a specialized agency; Korea and the Gulf were enforcement, not peacekeeping; Iraq 2003 was bypassed, not vetoed.
9. ⚠️ **Status discipline:** no current budget, arrears, mission-count, staffing or troop figures; no current reform outcome; no collective attribution of responsibility.
10. ⚠️ **Six handovers in one clause each:** League, war, Cold War, decolonisation, post-1991 order, and India's current positions.
11. ⚠️ **Four demands:** design, performance, scope, change - one rehearsed paragraph each.
12. ⚠️ **Seven items to rehearse** if time is short: the repaired-and-retained table; the construction sequence; Chapter VI against Chapter VII; Suez against Hungary; the three peacekeeping principles as a diagnostic; the non-military record with the expenditure figure; and the three reform categories with Article 108.

### Concept check

**Question:** A twenty-mark question asks: "The United Nations has been more successful in dealing with conflicts since 1990 than it was during the Cold War." To what extent would it be true to say this? Produce the spine of the answer - thesis, sequence of moves and closing verdict - without writing the answer itself.

**Model answer:** The thesis is that the organisation became freer rather than stronger, because the constraint moved rather than disappeared. Move one establishes what changed: with the Council able to agree, well over thirty peacekeeping operations were undertaken between 1990 and 2003, with a mid-1990s peak of over eighty thousand troops from seventy-seven countries, which is a measure of release rather than of capability. Move two shows what release made possible where the underlying conditions were right - the Cambodian settlement, with Vietnamese withdrawal in September 1989 and elections in June 1993 won by Sihanouk's party, and the Mozambican transition, where the organisation demobilised and disarmed the armies after the Rome ceasefire of October 1992 failed to hold, producing successful elections in October 1994. Move three shows the new constraint: in Somalia thirty-seven thousand troops arrived in December 1992 on a mandate to disarm warlords who would not be disarmed, an impossible task set from the beginning, and were withdrawn by March 1995; in Bosnia the organisation failed to send enough troops because both the European Community and the United States were reluctant to get involved, and Serb forces took Srebrenica and Zepa in July 1995 despite their designation as safe areas; and in Rwanda in 1994 no action was taken at all. Move four supplies the Cold War comparison that the stem requires, using the Suez and Hungary pair of 1956 to show that the earlier constraint was the voting rule, and the Gulf operation of 1991 to show that great-power agreement rather than institutional change is what produced the decade's one decisive enforcement success. Move five, for the twenty-mark band, adds the structural point that the change in the nature of conflict - from inter-state aggression to internal collapse - meant the organisation was released precisely when the problems had become the kind the Charter never anticipated. The closing verdict is that the proposition is true of volume and false of capability: the organisation after 1990 was freer to act and no better equipped to act, and the constraint had simply moved from the Council's voting rule to the members' willingness to supply force.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the higher number of operations after 1990 as evidence of greater success. Volume measures permission, not performance. The same decade contains two completed transitions and three of the worst failures in the organisation's history, and the variable separating them is not the date but whether the parties had agreed to stop and whether members were willing to resource what had been mandated.
