## What a topic with no owned question requires

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a nil return over nine years in the world-history allocation should change preparation rather than reduce it, and three things follow.

1. ⚠️ **The topic is examined through the other paper.** ✅ Every question in the span that concerns this institution - UNESCO funding, the health agency, the counter-terrorism committee, the Nobel-awarded agencies, the peacekeeping chronology - is routed to the international-relations owner and appears in General Studies Paper II or the objective paper. ⚠️ The material taught here is therefore heavily examined; it is simply not examined as world history. ⚠️ A candidate who treats a world-history nil return as permission to skip the topic will meet it in another paper without the historical depth that makes an institutional answer good.
2. ⚠️ **The Paper I syllabus clause nevertheless covers it.** ✅ The syllabus names the world wars and their effects, and the organisation is the principal institutional effect of the second of them; the canonical owner records the Paper I relevance alongside the Paper II clause on international institutions, agencies and fora, their structure and mandate. ⚠️ A stem on the post-1945 order, on global governance since 1945 or on the transformation of world politics by the war would route here.
3. ⚠️ **Rehearse architectures, not answers.** ⚠️ Lesson 18 section 10 lists the seven items. ⚠️ The two that transfer fastest to an unanticipated stem are the two-limbed success condition and the three reform categories.

⚠️ **The probable-question bank.** ✅ The two canonical owners record eight probable framings between them: how far the organisation has been more successful than the League; an assessment of it as a peacekeeping organisation; whether its greatest strength lies outside the battlefield; its structure and mandate with reference to contemporary global governance; how far it has overcome the League's weaknesses; the conditions under which it has succeeded or failed in peacekeeping; its character as an institution of global governance rather than only a peacekeeping body; and its structure and mandate illustrated with historical examples. ✅ The core account's own end-of-chapter questions add three more: whether the social, economic and humanitarian work has been far more successful and valuable than the peacekeeping role; whether it has only been successful in resolving conflict when a superpower intervened to support it; and whether it has been more successful in dealing with conflicts since 1990 than during the Cold War. ⚠️ The six original questions below are constructed to cover all eleven.

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

These run across lesson blocks rather than within a single lesson. Attempt each from memory before reading the model answer; the misconception line names the error that most often costs marks in that block.

## Block A - design, law and the organs, Lessons 1 to 5

### Cumulative check A1 - the bargain, traced from 1944 to Article 108

**Question:** Trace the great-power bargain from the wartime conferences to the amendment rule, showing at each stage how the same principle is expressed in a different institutional form.

**Model answer:** The bargain is that the great powers would be inside the organisation and would not be bound against their will, and it appears five times in different forms. At Dumbarton Oaks in 1944 four powers - the USSR, the USA, China and Britain - settled the architecture, and the Council voting formula was agreed among the great powers before the fifty-state conference met at San Francisco between 25 April and 26 June 1945, so the hardest question was closed before the drafting body convened. In the Charter's own entry-into-force condition, ratification was required individually from China, France, the Soviet Union, the United Kingdom and the United States, and merely from a majority of all other signatories - the asymmetry written into the organisation's birth. In Article 23 the same five are named as permanent members of a Council to which, under Article 24, members confer primary responsibility for peace and security and which acts on their behalf. In Article 27 decisions on all non-procedural matters require nine affirmative votes including the concurring votes of the permanent members. And in Article 108 amendment requires a two-thirds Assembly vote plus ratification by two-thirds of members including all five permanent members, which makes the arrangement self-protecting. The principle is constant - universality purchased with immunity - and the forms are procedural, constitutional, operational and finally constitutive.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating these as five separate facts to be memorised. They are one arrangement expressed five times, and an answer that states the principle and then shows it recurring will be far stronger than one that lists articles.

### Cumulative check A2 - the escalation ladder and the rung that was never built

**Question:** Set out the Charter's security ladder from pacific settlement to enforcement, and identify the two provisions whose non-use has shaped the organisation's entire operational history.

**Model answer:** Chapter VI governs pacific settlement: Article 33 requires the parties first to seek negotiation, enquiry, mediation, conciliation, arbitration, judicial settlement, resort to regional agencies or other peaceful means; Article 34 permits the Council to investigate; and Articles 36 and 37 permit it to recommend procedures or terms. The output is advisory and a party to a Chapter VI dispute must abstain from voting under Article 27(3). The ladder turns on Article 39, under which the Council determines the existence of a threat to the peace, breach of the peace or act of aggression. Chapter VII then supplies provisional measures under Article 40, non-military measures under Article 41 - interruption of economic relations, of rail, sea, air, postal, telegraphic and radio communications, and severance of diplomatic relations - and, where those would be or have proved inadequate, action by air, sea or land forces under Article 42. Decisions taken under this chapter bind every member under Article 25, and Article 103 makes Charter obligations prevail over conflicting treaty obligations. The two provisions whose non-use has shaped everything are Article 43, under which members undertook to make forces available by special agreement, and Article 47's Military Staff Committee: no Article 43 agreement was ever concluded, so no standing capability exists, and the organisation has depended ever since on member-state forces - which is why enforcement has taken the form of authorisation rather than command.

**Misconception to avoid:** Saying the Charter made no provision for armed force. It made detailed provision; the provision was never implemented, and the difference matters because a failure of implementation can in principle be repaired without amendment, which is exactly what the standing-capability proposals attempt.

## Block B - the record, Lessons 6 to 16

### Cumulative check B1 - six mechanisms that are all called "UN intervention"

**Question:** Distinguish the six mechanisms conflated under the phrase "United Nations intervention", assign one case to each, and state why the distinction determines any assessment of performance.

**Model answer:** Pacific settlement is mediation, inquiry or good offices without force: U Thant's appeal that reopened negotiations over West New Guinea in 1961-62 is the example. Observation is unarmed or lightly armed monitoring of an accepted line: UNTSO from 1948 and UNMOGIP from January 1949 are the earliest. Consent-based peacekeeping interposes a force admitted by the parties: the five thousand troops from ten countries deployed after Suez in November 1956, on the condition the withdrawing states themselves demanded. Robust peacekeeping adds tactical force with Council authorisation while host consent is retained. Authorised enforcement is the Council licensing member states to use force against a party that has not consented, executed under national command: Korea from 1950, where troops of sixteen countries served under an American Commander-in-Chief; the Gulf in 1991 under resolution 678 of 29 November 1990 authorising all necessary means; and UNITAF in Somalia from December 1992 under resolution 794. Unilateral action is force used without any licence: the invasion of Iraq in March 2003, where no resolution was put to a vote. The distinction determines assessment because the six have different success conditions. Enforcement needs great-power agreement and willingness to fight; consent-based peacekeeping needs parties who want to stop; observation needs only a ceasefire. Judging a peacekeeping mission by whether it defeated anyone, or an enforcement operation by whether it was impartial, produces a verdict on a standard the operation was never attempting to meet.

**Misconception to avoid:** Placing Korea and the 1991 Gulf operation among the peacekeeping successes. Both were authorised enforcement by national forces under national command, and both are cited by the record as instances of the rule that military intervention had most chance of success where UN troops actively supported one side against the other.

### Cumulative check B2 - the success condition, tested against four hostile cases

**Question:** State the condition governing the organisation's security record and test it against Suez, the Gulf in 1991, Somalia and Iraq in 2003, including the cases that appear to contradict it.

**Model answer:** The condition has two limbs: the organisation acts effectively where great-power interests are not directly engaged, or where the great powers whose interests are engaged choose to support or accept the action; it fails where a great power's interests are engaged and that power decides to ignore or defy it. Suez appears to contradict the condition, because Britain and France were great powers whose interests were directly engaged and who nevertheless complied after the Assembly condemned the invasions sixty-four to five. The refinement it forces is that the operative variable is sensitivity to isolation rather than raw power: the organisation restrains a state to the extent that the state values what it can withdraw, and these were powers that still required international legitimacy. The Gulf in 1991 confirms limb (b) directly, since great-power interests were intensely engaged and the operation succeeded because those powers wanted it. Somalia appears to contradict the condition in the opposite direction, because no veto was cast and the operation still collapsed; it is explained by limb (b) read as political backing rather than legal permission, since the American withdrawal in March 1994 preceded the general one in March 1995. Iraq in 2003 confirms the negative limb in its purest form: a great power's interests were engaged, it declined to seek a determination at all, and nothing could be done. The condition therefore survives all four.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the condition as a restatement of the veto rule. It explains Suez, where a veto was cast and failed, and Somalia and Bosnia, where none was cast at all - so it is a claim about political backing, of which the veto is only the most visible legal instrument.

### Cumulative check B3 - three kinds of atrocity failure, and what each demands

**Question:** Distinguish what went wrong in Somalia, Bosnia and Rwanda, and state the institutional lesson each yields.

**Model answer:** Somalia was a mandate failure. Thirty-seven thousand troops, mainly American, arrived in December 1992 as UNITAF, a US-led coalition authorised by Security Council resolution 794 rather than a UN-commanded force, to safeguard aid and restore order by disarming warlords who were not prepared to be disarmed; forcible disarmament is enforcement and destroys the access that relief requires, so the two halves of the mandate were incompatible, and the account records that the organisation had set itself an impossible task from the beginning. The lesson is that consent is not a formality attached to peacekeeping but the condition that makes the instrument function. Bosnia was a resourcing failure. The organisation did not send enough troops to impose law and order, partly because both the European Community and the United States were reluctant to get involved, and in July 1995 Serb forces captured Srebrenica and Zepa, which the Council had designated safe areas for Muslims, and then murdered around eight thousand Muslim men. The lesson is harder than simple inaction: a commitment the organisation cannot honour is worse than none, because the designation concentrated a vulnerable population in a place publicly declared safe. Rwanda was an absence. In 1994 the organisation took no action in the civil war and genocide, in the same year as the American withdrawal from Somalia - a sequence that explains the non-decision without discharging it. The lesson is that where political will is absent no institutional design produces action, which is why the 2005 responsibility-to-protect formulation located collective action in the Council and thereby converted an absence of principle into an absence of will.

**Misconception to avoid:** Merging the three into a single verdict about incompetence. They are a mandate failure, a resourcing failure and a non-decision, and they require three different remedies - realistic mandates, matched resources and political will - only two of which are within the organisation's own gift.

## Block C - system, money and reform, Lessons 11 to 18

### Cumulative check C1 - representation, money and the two rules the system runs on

**Question:** Show how the same institution operates under two opposite rules of decision, using the membership transformation and the funding crisis, and state what this implies for reform.

**Model answer:** In the Assembly the rule is equality: every member has one vote, decisions are taken by simple or two-thirds majorities, and the abandonment of the League's unanimity requirement made obstruction a recorded minority position rather than a silent block. Decolonisation turned that rule into a transfer of control - forty-three new members joined between 1963 and 1968, Third World members were in a clear majority by 1970, and only that bloc could be certain of carrying its resolutions, with both western and communist blocs finding it increasingly difficult to carry theirs. In the financial bodies and in practice over the budget, the rule is contribution. The Fund's Board is dominated by the states supplying most of the cash and attached conditions that Jamaica and Tanzania experienced as interference; the Bank's decisions are controlled by its largest contributor, and Poland and Czechoslovakia resigned in 1950 and 1954 after refusals; and in 1986 ninety-eight members owed money while the United States withheld over one hundred million dollars, demanding that the largest givers have more say. The Sri Lankan reply was that at home the wealthy do not have more votes than the poor. In 1987 changes gave the main contributors more control and the situation improved. The implication for reform is that the organisation resolves the conflict between equality and contribution differently in each arena, so a proposal must specify which rule it is invoking - and proposals that invoke equality against the Council collide with Article 108, which requires the consent of the contributors.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming the two rules can simply be harmonised. Each expresses a genuine principle - representation and accountability - and every reform proposal is a trade between them, which is why the debate has outlasted every particular scheme.

### Cumulative check C2 - what has actually been reformed, and by what route

**Question:** Identify the reforms the organisation has actually achieved, classify them by the route they took, and explain what this predicts about the Council.

**Model answer:** Almost everything achieved has taken the route of practice or administration rather than amendment. By practice: the convention that abstention by a permanent member does not count as a veto, gradually accepted and never written into the Charter; the Uniting for Peace procedure, resolution 377 A (V) of 3 November 1950, permitting the Assembly to meet within twenty-four hours after a veto and decide on action by a two-thirds majority, also never added to the Charter; and peacekeeping itself, which appears nowhere in the Charter and was improvised at Suez in 1956. By administration: the budgeting changes of 1987 that gave the main contributors more control and improved the financial position, and the streamlining of the cumbersome budget system proposed in the Agenda for Further Change of September 2002. By amendment, the record is short and uniform: the Council was enlarged from eleven to fifteen with the vote threshold raised from seven to nine, and ECOSOC was enlarged from eighteen to twenty-seven and then to fifty-four - enlargements and thresholds only, never a redistribution of power. This predicts that the Council's composition and voting rule will not change, because they fall in the amendment category and Article 108 requires ratification by all five permanent members. It also predicts, more usefully, that operational capability can improve without amendment - which is what the 2000 recommendation of permanent brigade-size forces of five thousand, ready for immediate deployment and commanded by military professionals, was designed to achieve under Article 43 machinery that had never been implemented.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that because the Council cannot be reformed, reform is futile. The organisation's three most important operational capabilities were all created without amending a word of the Charter, so the realistic question is how much can be achieved outside Article 108 rather than whether Article 108 will ever be used.

### Cumulative check C3 - the whole institution in one balanced judgement

**Question:** Produce a single balanced judgement on the organisation that is defensible against both its strongest critics and its strongest admirers, and name the evidence each side would use.

**Model answer:** The judgement is that the organisation is weak exactly where it must coerce the powerful and strong exactly where it must coordinate the willing, and that this is a consequence of its design rather than of its performance. The critic's evidence is real: no permanent army, because no Article 43 agreement was ever concluded; a veto that blocked action over Hungary in 1956 and Czechoslovakia in 1968; Soviet forces remaining in Afghanistan after 1980 despite demands to withdraw; a merits judgment for Nicaragua in 1986 that the losing state simply refused to accept, with the Council never using its Article 94 power; Somalia, Bosnia and the absence from Rwanda; and an invasion in March 2003 conducted without any resolution being put to a vote. The admirer's evidence is equally real: a permanent assembly of around 190 nations where the smallest can be heard; the Universal Declaration of 1948 and a human-rights apparatus that made enemies of Russia, China and the United States alike; the eradication of smallpox, its last natural case falling in 1977; the Trusteeship Council, which completed its mandate and suspended operation on 1 November 1994; Suez, West New Guinea, Cambodia and Mozambique; and more than ninety per cent of annual expenditure devoted to economic and social work by 1980. Both sets are true because they describe different functions, and the founding aims are three rather than one - so the organisation must be judged against all three, and the honest verdict is uneven by design.

**Misconception to avoid:** Resolving the two sets of evidence by averaging them into a verdict of "partial success". That formulation explains nothing. The two records differ because the tasks differ in whether they require coercing a state that does not consent, and naming that variable is what converts a balanced verdict into an argument.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

⚠️ All six questions are original. ✅ This topic owns no verified previous-year question in the 2018-2026 span, so no stem below reproduces or adapts an examined question. ⚠️ Each model answer is written to the stated ceiling and follows the pattern claim, named evidence, analysis, qualification. ⚠️ The scoring note under each answer is specific to that question.

## Question 1 - 10 marks, 150 words

> ⚠️ **Explain how far the United Nations overcame the weaknesses of the League of Nations.**

**Model answer.** The founders repaired four League defects and deliberately retained two. Both superpowers became founder-members, where the United States had never joined the League; the Charter replaced the unanimity rule with simple and two-thirds majorities; the organisation acquired a far wider social and economic remit, with every specialized agency except the International Labour Organization of 1919 created from 1945; and it committed itself to individual human rights. What survived was the absence of a permanent army and the power of any one permanent member to veto a decision. These retentions were not oversights. Universality was purchased with immunity: a body able to coerce its strongest members would not have attracted them. The result is a precise pattern - the organisation is stronger than the League in scope, legitimacy and procedure, and no stronger in enforcing order against an unwilling great power, which is the defect that destroyed its predecessor.

**Scoring note.** ⚠️ The mark separator here is the word *deliberately*. Candidates who list improvements and then list surviving weaknesses produce a comparison; candidates who explain the two retentions as the price of the first repair produce an argument. ⚠️ The 1919 date for the labour agency is the single cheapest precision marker in this answer.

## Question 2 - 10 marks, 150 words

> ⚠️ **Examine the distinction between Chapter VI and Chapter VII of the United Nations Charter, with one illustration of each.**

**Model answer.** Chapter VI governs pacific settlement. Article 33 requires parties to seek negotiation, mediation or arbitration first; Article 34 allows the Council to investigate; Articles 36 and 37 allow it to recommend procedures or terms. Its output is advisory, and under Article 27(3) a party to such a dispute must abstain. Chapter VII begins only when the Council determines under Article 39 that a threat to the peace, breach of the peace or act of aggression exists. It then supplies provisional measures, non-military measures under Article 41 including interruption of economic relations and communications, and force under Article 42. These decisions bind every member under Article 25. The mediation that reopened negotiations over West New Guinea in 1962 illustrates the first; the authorisation of all necessary means against the occupation of Kuwait in resolution 678 of November 1990 illustrates the second. The hinge between them is the Article 39 determination.

**Scoring note.** ⚠️ Reward the Article 39 hinge above everything else; a script that names the chapters without identifying the determination has described two lists rather than a threshold. ⚠️ Penalise any answer that offers a peacekeeping mission as a Chapter VII illustration.

## Question 3 - 15 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ **Assess the United Nations as a peacekeeping organisation, stating clearly the condition under which it has succeeded.**

**Model answer.** The organisation succeeds where great-power interests are not directly engaged, or where the great powers whose interests are engaged choose to support or accept the action; it fails where such a power is engaged and decides to ignore or defy it. Two simultaneous crises in 1956 establish the condition under controlled comparison. At Suez a Council resolution was vetoed by Britain and France, the Assembly condemned the invasions sixty-four to five, and the aggressors withdrew in view of the weight of opinion against them, with a force of five thousand from ten countries deployed on the condition they demanded. Over Hungary the identical machinery ran - appeal, veto, an Assembly resolution and an investigating committee - and the Russians simply refused to co-operate. The law, the procedures and the month were constant; only the identity of the state using force varied.

The condition explains the wider record. Where parties were ready to stop and needed execution machinery, it delivered: the supervised transfer of West New Guinea in 1962, and elections in Cambodia in June 1993 and Mozambique in October 1994 after demobilisation. Where parties refused to stop, it could not manufacture agreement: Cyprus has needed three thousand troops since 1964 and the Annan Plan of 2004 was rejected by the Greek community.

The qualification is that this is an assessment of enforcement and interposition only. It says nothing about ceasefire supervision, supervised transitions or the humanitarian record, where no great-power consent is required and the performance is correspondingly different.

**Scoring note.** ⚠️ The controlled comparison of 1956 is worth more than any number of additional cases, because it isolates a single variable. ⚠️ Award the top band only where the final qualification restricts the verdict to the functions actually assessed; an unqualified verdict on "the UN" from peacekeeping evidence alone is the characteristic upper-middle-band error.

## Question 4 - 15 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ **"The greatest strength of the United Nations lies outside the battlefield." Discuss.**

**Model answer.** The empirical claim is correct and the implied comparison should be refused. It is correct because the organisation's own accounting supports it: by 1980 more than ninety per cent of annual expenditure was devoted to economic and social activities, and two of the three founding aims concern economic, social, educational, scientific and cultural progress and the rights of individuals and peoples rather than peace directly.

The record sustains the claim. The health agency contained the Egyptian cholera outbreak of 1947 within weeks, maintains a permanent vaccine bank, and eradicated smallpox, whose last natural case fell in 1977 - an achievement requiring simultaneous action across hostile states that no single government could have organised. The food agency introduced higher-yielding, disease-resistant maize and rice; the children's fund, founded in 1946, launched its child health revolution campaign in 1983; the relief agency of 1950 built schools, hospitals and training centres as refugee camps proved permanent; and the Universal Declaration of 1948 supplied a standard for naming and shaming.

The comparison should nonetheless be refused, because the two arms face different problems. Non-military work coordinates willing actors; enforcement must coerce unwilling great powers, which no international institution has ever achieved. Comparing performance across those tasks measures the difficulty of the task rather than the competence of the body.

The qualification is that the preventive theory behind this work - that educating minds and raising living standards removes the causes of conflict - is a hypothesis, and no count of wars prevented is available.

**Scoring note.** ⚠️ The discriminator is the refusal of the comparison; agreement with the quotation, however well evidenced, belongs in the middle band. ⚠️ Look specifically for the unfalsifiability qualification at the close, which is the mark of a candidate who has noticed that the preventive claim cannot be tested.

## Question 5 - 20 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ **"The composition of authority in the United Nations was fixed in 1945; the composition of the organisation was not." Critically examine the case for reform of the Security Council in the light of this statement.**

**Model answer.** The statement is accurate and it establishes the strongest case for reform without requiring any claim about current negotiations. Permanent membership was settled by an assessment of power made by four states at Dumbarton Oaks in 1944 and by fifty at San Francisco in 1945. The organisation has since grown to 193 members: forty-three joined between 1963 and 1968, membership reached 159 by 1985 and 183 by 1993, and South Sudan became the 193rd in July 2011. Every organ was adjusted to that growth except the one whose decisions bind. The Assembly reflects it exactly; ECOSOC was enlarged from eighteen to twenty-seven and then to fifty-four; the Council was enlarged once, from eleven to fifteen, in its elected seats alone. Article 23 directs that elected seats be filled with regard first to a member's contribution to peace and security and to the other purposes, and also to equitable geographical distribution - a criterion applied to ten seats and not to five.

The counter-argument is structural rather than complacent. Enforcement depends on the forces and finance of the states the veto protects; a Council able to decide against them would issue decisions nobody executed.

The defensible position is that the veto is necessary as a structure and indefensible as a permanent allocation. Article 108 explains the impasse: amendment requires ratification by all five permanent members, so the reform must be granted by those it would constrain.

**Scoring note.** ⚠️ This question rewards restraint. ⚠️ Any script that asserts a current negotiating position, a vote count, a timetable or a particular state's entitlement has converted analysis into advocacy and cannot reach the top band, however well informed. ⚠️ The Article 23 criterion and the Article 108 requirement are the two textual anchors that distinguish a first-class answer.

## Question 6 - 20 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ **Examine how far the end of the Cold War changed the capacity of the United Nations to deal with conflict.**

**Model answer.** It made the organisation freer without making it stronger, because the binding constraint moved rather than disappeared. Freedom is measurable: well over thirty peacekeeping operations were undertaken between 1990 and 2003, with a mid-1990s peak of over eighty thousand troops from seventy-seven countries, and disputes frozen by superpower alignment became soluble - Vietnamese forces left Cambodia in September 1989 and elections followed in June 1993, while in Mozambique the organisation demobilised and disarmed the armies after the Rome ceasefire of October 1992 failed to hold, producing successful elections in October 1994.

The new constraint was the willingness of members to supply force. In Somalia a US-led coalition of thirty-seven thousand arrived in December 1992 to disarm warlords who refused to be disarmed - an impossible task - and left by March 1995. In Bosnia too few troops were sent, partly because both the European Community and the United States were reluctant to get involved, and Srebrenica and Zepa fell in July 1995 despite their safe-area designation. In Rwanda in 1994 no action was taken.

Two deeper changes explain this. The conflicts requiring attention were now internal collapses rather than inter-state aggression, which the Charter never anticipated; and the sole remaining superpower increasingly valued authorisation less, declining an offered counter-terrorism resolution in 2001 and acting without one in March 2003.

The qualification is that the single decisive enforcement success of the period, the reversal of the occupation of Kuwait in 1991, depended on great-power agreement exactly as Korea had.

**Scoring note.** ⚠️ Reward the separation of volume from capability in the opening sentence; scripts that treat the number of operations as evidence of success have answered a different question. ⚠️ The Kuwait qualification at the close is the test of whether the candidate has seen that the success condition survived the Cold War unchanged.

# REMEDIATION

⚠️ Five error families account for almost every avoidable loss of marks on this topic. Each entry states the error, the correction and the place in this session where the correction is taught.

## Error family A - dates, origins and numbers

| Error | Correction | Taught in |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ Dating the founding to 26 June 1945 | ✅ Signed 26 June 1945; **in force 24 October 1945**; officially in existence from **October 1945** | ⚠️ Lesson 2 |
| ⚠️ Naming France at Dumbarton Oaks | ✅ **USSR, USA, China, Britain**, 1944 | ⚠️ Lesson 2 |
| ⚠️ Giving fifty-one as the number at San Francisco | ✅ **Fifty** attended; **fifty-one original members** | ⚠️ Lesson 2 |
| ⚠️ Saying the Council always had fifteen members | ✅ **Eleven** until the non-permanent seats rose to **ten in 1965** | ⚠️ Lesson 5 |
| ⚠️ Giving ECOSOC twenty-seven members today | ✅ **Fifty-four**; eighteen to twenty-seven in force 1965, to fifty-four in force 1973 | ⚠️ Lesson 13 |
| ⚠️ Citing a current budget, arrears, mission-count or troop figure | ❌ None is asserted in this session; use only the dated figures recorded here | ⚠️ Session opening; Lesson 18 |

## Error family B - categories and legal status

| Error | Correction | Taught in |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ Calling the International Criminal Court a principal organ | ✅ The **ICJ** is; the ICC rests on the **Rome Statute adopted 17 July 1998** (120-7-21), with **139 signatures** by 31 December 2000, in force **1 July 2002**, inaugurated **March 2003** with eighteen judges | ⚠️ Lesson 14 |
| ⚠️ Saying the Rome Statute was signed by 120 states | ✅ 120 is the **adoption vote**; the **signature** total by the 31 December 2000 deadline was **139** | ⚠️ Lesson 14 |
| ⚠️ Dating the Nicaragua judgment to 1984 | ✅ 1984 gave provisional measures (10 May) and the jurisdiction ruling (26 November); the USA withdrew from the proceedings in **January 1985**; the **merits judgment is 27 June 1986** | ⚠️ Lesson 14 |
| ⚠️ Calling the July 2001 hearing Milosevic's trial | ✅ **3 July 2001** was his **initial appearance**; the **trial commenced 12 February 2002** and he died on **11 March 2006** | ⚠️ Lesson 14 |
| ⚠️ Giving four ECOSOC regional commissions | ✅ There are **five**: ECE, ECLAC, ECA, **ESCAP** (ECAFE renamed **1974**) and **ESCWA** (**1973**) | ⚠️ Lesson 13 |
| ⚠️ Placing Hammarskjold's death in the Congo | ✅ He died in an air crash on **18 September 1961 near Ndola, Northern Rhodesia - now Zambia** | ⚠️ Lesson 10 |
| ⚠️ Dating smallpox eradication to the 1980s | ✅ **Last natural case 1977**; **certified December 1979**; **declared by the World Health Assembly on 8 May 1980** (WHA33.3) | ⚠️ Lesson 12 |
| ⚠️ Ending the Lebanese civil war in 1987 | ✅ It ran **1975-1990**, closed by the Taif Agreement of 1989 | ⚠️ Lesson 9 |
| ⚠️ Dating the American return to UNESCO to 2002 | ✅ **Announced September 2002**, **rejoined 2003** | ⚠️ Lesson 11 |
| ⚠️ Calling Cyprus a British colony from 1878 | ✅ **Administration 1878**, **annexation 1914**, **Crown colony 1925**, independence **1960** | ⚠️ Lesson 9 |
| ⚠️ Calling the World Trade Organization a specialized agency | ✅ It is not; **GATT became the WTO in January 1995** | ⚠️ Lesson 13 |
| ⚠️ Treating UNICEF, UNRWA and WHO as the same kind of body | ✅ **Fund**, **General Assembly-created agency**, **specialized agency** respectively | ⚠️ Lessons 12 and 13 |
| ⚠️ Saying the Trusteeship Council was abolished | ✅ **Suspended operation on 1 November 1994**; still a principal organ | ⚠️ Lesson 10 |
| ⚠️ Saying Assembly resolutions bind members | ✅ Recommendations; the **Article 17** budget apportionment with the **Article 19** vote-loss sanction is the exception | ⚠️ Lesson 4 |

## Error family C - mechanisms and procedure

| Error | Correction | Taught in |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ Calling Korea 1950 or the Gulf 1991 peacekeeping | ✅ **Authorised enforcement** by member-state forces; the Gulf authorisation is **resolution 678 of 29 November 1990** | ⚠️ Lessons 6 and 15 |
| ⚠️ Saying the USSR was outvoted over Korea | ✅ **Absent**, boycotting since **January 1950** over the exclusion of communist China | ⚠️ Lesson 6 |
| ⚠️ Saying the Council authorised the Suez force | ✅ The Council resolution was **vetoed by Britain and France**; the Assembly voted **sixty-four to five** | ⚠️ Lesson 7 |
| ⚠️ Saying Uniting for Peace amended the Charter | ✅ **Resolution 377 A (V), 3 November 1950**, never added to the Charter, and its output remains a recommendation | ⚠️ Lesson 6 |
| ⚠️ Saying peacekeepers must be neutral | ✅ **Impartial but not neutral** in executing the mandate | ⚠️ Lesson 7 |
| ⚠️ Saying the Charter made no provision for forces | ✅ **Article 43** provided for special agreements; **none was ever concluded** | ⚠️ Lesson 5 |
| ⚠️ Saying Iraq 2003 was vetoed | ✅ **No vote was held**; the resolution was never put because a veto was anticipated | ⚠️ Lesson 16 |

## Error family D - judgement and attribution

| Error | Correction | Taught in |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ Writing "the United Nations failed" where a state blocked | ✅ On enforcement, **name the state**; on norms and service delivery, name the institution | ⚠️ Lesson 1 |
| ⚠️ Saying the organisation did nothing over Hungary | ✅ Appeal received, Council vetoed, **Assembly passed the same resolution and appointed a committee**; the Russians refused to co-operate | ⚠️ Lesson 8 |
| ⚠️ Blaming poor execution for Somalia | ✅ It had **set itself an impossible task from the beginning** | ⚠️ Lesson 15 |
| ⚠️ Attributing atrocity to nations or peoples | ✅ Name governments, armed forces, named commanders and the organs that acted or failed | ⚠️ Session opening; Lesson 15 |
| ⚠️ Delivering a single verdict on the whole institution | ✅ Answer **two questions**: as a world government, and as a forum, norm-setter and coordinator | ⚠️ Lesson 18 |
| ⚠️ Using "failure" as one undifferentiated category | ✅ **Blocked**, **defied**, **overmatched**, **bypassed** - four types, four remedies | ⚠️ Lesson 8 |

## Error family E - reform, status and boundaries

| Error | Correction | Taught in |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ Saying amendment needs only a two-thirds Assembly vote | ✅ **Article 108** also requires ratification by two-thirds of members **including all five permanent members** | ⚠️ Lessons 2 and 17 |
| ⚠️ Asserting a current reform outcome or negotiating position | ❌ Not asserted anywhere in this session; make the structural argument and stop | ⚠️ Lesson 17 |
| ⚠️ Turning the structural argument into advocacy for a seat | ✅ State the **Article 23** criterion and the mismatch; leave the conclusion to negotiation | ⚠️ Lesson 17 |
| ⚠️ Treating the veto as pure obstruction | ✅ Give the counter-argument: enforcement depends on the states it protects. **Necessary as structure, indefensible as permanent allocation** | ⚠️ Lessons 5 and 17 |
| ⚠️ Re-narrating the League's collapse, the war or the Cold War | ✅ Take only the defect list, the wartime design moment and the institutional behaviour inside the crises | ⚠️ Lesson 1; Lesson 18 |
| ⚠️ Importing independence-movement narrative into a decolonisation answer here | ✅ Use **Article 1(2)**, **Article 73**, the trusteeship record and the change in Assembly membership | ⚠️ Lesson 11 |

## Map 2 - The causal chain from the design bargain to the reform impasse

```text
   1945  THE BARGAIN
   Universality and great-power presence purchased with great-power
   immunity
        |
        +--> EXPRESSED AS: ratification condition · Art 23 permanent
        |    members · Art 27 concurring votes · Art 108 amendment
        |
        v
   TWO DEFECTS RETAINED
   (1) NO PERMANENT ARMY -- because Art 43 agreements were never
       concluded -- a CAPABILITY gap
   (2) THE VETO -- a PERMISSION gap
        |
        +-- capability gaps can be narrowed by money and practice
        |   -> standby forces · Brahimi 2000 · preventive deployment
        |
        +-- permission gaps need amendment
            -> Art 108 requires the consent of the permission-holders
        |
        v
   OPERATIONAL CONSEQUENCE
   Enforcement happens only by AUTHORISATION of member-state forces
   Korea 1950 (Soviet absence) · Gulf 1991 (great-power agreement)
        |
        v
   WHERE AUTHORISATION IS BLOCKED -> the Assembly route
   Uniting for Peace 1950 -> Suez 1956 -> a RECOMMENDATION, which
   works on states sensitive to isolation and fails on states that
   are not (Hungary 1956 · Czechoslovakia 1968 · Afghanistan 1980)
        |
        v
   MEANWHILE THE MEMBERSHIP TRANSFORMS
   43 new members 1963-68 -> Third World majority by 1970 ->
   UNCTAD and G77 1964 -> NIEO 1974 -> agency direction contested ->
   UNESCO crisis 1983 -> withdrawals 1985-86 -> returns 1997, 2002
        |
        v
   AND THE MONEY BECOMES A SECOND VETO
   Congo 1960-64: USSR, France and Belgium withhold -> near bankruptcy
   1986: 98 members in arrears; USA withholds $100m+
   1987: contributors gain more control
   1993: Boutros-Ghali warns all peacekeeping is in jeopardy
        |
        v
   POST-1990: FREER, NOT STRONGER
   30+ operations 1990-2003 -> Cambodia and Mozambique succeed ->
   Somalia, Bosnia and Rwanda fail -> constraint has moved from the
   VOTING RULE to the SUPPLY OF FORCE
        |
        v
   2003: BYPASSED ENTIRELY -- no vote put -- 'fork in the road'
        |
        v
   REFORM IMPASSE
   Category A (composition, veto) blocked by Art 108
   Category B (practice) is where everything has been achieved
   Category C (administration) is where current effort is concentrated
```

   The veto is DEFENSIBLE AS A STRUCTURAL NECESSITY and
