# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## 1. The design brief and the trade of 1945

- ✅ Officially in existence **October 1945**, after the Second World War, to replace a League that had **proved incapable of restraining aggressive dictators**. ✅ The great powers tried to eliminate **some** of the weaknesses that had handicapped it.
- ✅ **Three aims:** preserve peace and eliminate war; remove the causes of conflict by encouraging economic, social, educational, scientific and cultural progress throughout the world, **especially in under-developed countries**; safeguard the rights of all individual human beings and the rights of peoples and nations.
- ✅ **Four League defects repaired:** American absence (both superpowers were founder-members); Versailles linkage (own Charter aims); unanimity (simple and two-thirds majorities); narrow social remit (a far wider economic and social scope, plus a human-rights commitment). ✅ Every specialized agency except the **ILO of 1919** was created from 1945.
- ✅ **Two retained:** **no permanent army of its own**, and the **veto** of any one permanent member.
- ⚠️ The trade: universality and great-power presence purchased with great-power immunity. ⚠️ **Capability gap** (no force) against **permission gap** (the veto) - only the first can be narrowed without amendment.
- ⚠️ Two preparation layers: historical origin and record; structure and mandate. ⚠️ Agency rule: on enforcement name the state, on norms and service delivery name the institution.

## 2. Construction of the Charter, 1944-45

- ✅ **1944 Dumbarton Oaks**, United States: **USSR, USA, China, Britain** produce the proposals on which the Charter was based. ⚠️ The Council voting formula was settled among the great powers before the drafting conference.
- ✅ **San Francisco, 25 April - 26 June 1945**: **fifty countries** draft and sign; **signed 26 June 1945**; **in force 24 October 1945** after ratification by **China, France, the Soviet Union, the United Kingdom and the United States** plus a **majority of other signatories**. ✅ **Fifty-one original members.**
- ✅ The Charter is an **international treaty**; members are bound; it codifies **sovereign equality** and the **prohibition of the use of force**.
- ✅ **Amendment record:** Arts 23, 27, 61 adopted 1963, in force **31 August 1965**; Art 109 adopted 1965, in force **12 June 1968**; Art 61 again adopted 1971, in force **24 September 1973**. ⚠️ Summary statements of "three amendments in 1963, 1965 and 1973" count adoption years; the divergence is recorded, not reconciled. ✅ Every amendment enlarged an organ or raised a threshold; **none redistributed power**.
- ✅ **Article 108:** amendment requires a two-thirds Assembly vote **and** ratification by two-thirds of members **including all five permanent members**. ✅ Article 109 applies the same condition to a review conference.
- ✅ The **Statute of the ICJ is annexed** to the Charter and forms an integral part of it - the technical reason the Court is a principal organ and the criminal court is not.

## 3. Purposes, principles and the membership gate

- ✅ **Article 1 - four purposes:** peace and security by collective measures **and** peaceful adjustment; friendly relations based on **equal rights and self-determination of peoples**; international co-operation on economic, social, cultural and humanitarian problems and on **human rights without distinction as to race, sex, language or religion**; a centre for harmonizing the actions of nations.
- ✅ **Article 2 - seven principles:** sovereign equality; good faith; peaceful settlement; **2(4)** no threat or use of force; assistance to UN action and none to its target; **2(6)** effect on non-members; **2(7)** non-intervention in domestic jurisdiction, **without prejudice to Chapter VII enforcement**.
- ⚠️ 2(4) binds **members**; 2(7) binds **the organisation**. ⚠️ Purpose 3 commits outward and Principle 7 bars inward; the Chapter VII proviso is the only general doorway, and it is controlled by the Council.
- ✅ **Article 3:** original members. ✅ **Article 4:** admission of "peace-loving states" by **Assembly decision on Council recommendation**, with a two-thirds Assembly majority. ✅ **Articles 5 and 6:** suspension and expulsion, both on **Council recommendation** - which is why neither functions as a sanction.
- ✅ **1950:** the USA vetoed the **People's Republic of China's** application and repeated the veto **every year for twenty years**; **1971**, it refrained, and the Assembly voted that the PRC should **take over** Taiwan's membership and permanent seat - a transfer requiring no amendment of Article 23.
- ✅ **Membership series:** 51 original; **43 joined 1963-68**; **159 by 1985**; **183 by 1993**; **East Timor 191st in 2002**; **Montenegro 2006**; **South Sudan 193rd in July 2011**. ✅ The League never exceeded about fifty.

## 4. The General Assembly

- ✅ All members; **up to five representatives, one vote each**; regular annual session from **September**, about **three months**; special sessions callable by the members themselves or the Council and convoked by the Secretary-General. ✅ **Six official languages:** English, French, Russian, Chinese, Spanish, Arabic.
- ✅ **Four functions:** discuss and decide on international problems; consider the budget and each member's contribution; elect Council members; supervise other bodies.
- ✅ **Art 10** general power of discussion and recommendation; **Art 11** peace-and-security principles including disarmament; **Art 13** progressive development and **codification of international law** plus economic, social and human-rights co-operation; **Art 15** receives Council and other reports; **Art 12** no recommendation on a dispute the Council is handling unless it requests.
- ✅ **No unanimity rule.** Simple majority generally; **two-thirds for important questions** - peace-and-security recommendations, elections to the Council, ECOSOC and elective Trusteeship seats, admission, suspension, expulsion, trusteeship questions and **budgetary questions**.
- ✅ **Art 17:** approves the budget; expenses borne by members **as apportioned by the Assembly**; examines specialized-agency administrative budgets. ✅ **Art 19:** a member two full years in arrears **loses its Assembly vote**, subject to a beyond-control exception - the only automatic sanction in the Charter.
- ⚠️ Majority voting converts obstruction into a recorded minority - the mechanism behind the **sixty-four to five** Suez vote. ⚠️ Assembly output is **costly rather than binding**. ⚠️ Assembly legitimacy rests on **representation**; Council legitimacy on **effectiveness**.

## 5. The Security Council and the security ladder

- ✅ Permanent session; deals with crises by whatever action seems appropriate, including calling on members for economic or military action against an aggressor; approves membership applications.
- ✅ **1945: eleven members** - five permanent (China, France, USA, USSR, Britain) and six elected for two-year terms. ✅ **1965:** non-permanent raised to **ten**; total **fifteen**. ✅ **Art 23:** elected seats with due regard **first** to contribution to peace and security and the other purposes, **also** to equitable geographical distribution; no immediate re-election.
- ✅ **Art 24:** primary responsibility conferred; the Council **acts on their behalf**. ✅ **Art 25:** members agree to **accept and carry out** decisions. ✅ **Art 103:** Charter obligations **prevail** over other treaty obligations.
- ✅ **Art 27:** nine votes for procedural matters; nine **including the concurring votes of the permanent members** for all others; a **party to a Chapter VI dispute shall abstain**. ✅ **Practice, never written into the Charter:** abstention by a permanent member is **not** a veto. ✅ The **USSR used the veto more often than any other member** in the period covered.
- ✅ **Chapter VI:** Art 33 means of settlement; Art 34 investigation; Arts 36-37 recommendations - **advisory**.
- ✅ **Chapter VII:** **Art 39** determination is the hinge; Art 40 provisional measures; **Art 41** non-military measures (economic relations; rail, sea, air, postal, telegraphic and radio communications; diplomatic severance); **Art 42** air, sea or land action including demonstrations and blockade - **binding**.
- ✅ **Art 43:** special agreements to make forces available - **never concluded**. ✅ **Art 45:** air contingents held available. ✅ **Art 47:** Military Staff Committee of the permanent members' Chiefs of Staff. ⚠️ Result: no standing capability; enforcement by **authorisation** of national forces.
- ✅ **Art 51:** inherent right of individual or collective **self-defence if an armed attack occurs**, **until the Council has acted**, with **immediate reporting**. ✅ **Chapter VIII:** regional arrangements may settle local disputes; **no enforcement action without Council authorization**.
- ⚠️ **Verdict formula:** the veto is defensible as a structural necessity and indefensible as a permanent allocation.

## 6. Collective security: Korea and Uniting for Peace

- ✅ Korea was **the only occasion** on which decisive action was taken in a crisis **directly involving a superpower's interests**.
- ✅ **June 1950:** South Korea invaded by communist North Korea; the Council **immediately** condemned the North and called on members to send help - possible only because the **Russian delegates were absent**, boycotting since **January 1950** over the exclusion of communist China; they returned too late.
- ✅ **Sixteen countries** sent troops; the frontier along the **38th parallel** was preserved. ✅ Very much an **American operation**: vast majority of troops, **General MacArthur** as Commander-in-Chief, and a US decision to intervene taken **a day before** the Council acted. ✅ Recognised as **not likely to be repeated**.
- ✅ **Uniting for Peace - GA resolution 377 A (V), 3 November 1950:** after a veto the Assembly may meet **within twenty-four hours** and decide what action to take, **even military intervention**, by a **two-thirds majority**. ✅ **Not added to the Charter**; the USSR always maintained a Council veto should take precedence; the Assembly used it **many times** regardless.
- ⚠️ The Soviet objection had a textual basis in **Arts 12 and 24**; the counter is that a vetoed Council has failed to exercise its function. ⚠️ The resolution transfers **the agenda, not the power**.
- ✅ Second result: a bitter Russian attack on **Trygve Lie**, whose position became impossible; he retired early and was replaced by **Dag Hammarskjold**.

## 7. The invention of peacekeeping: Suez 1956

- ✅ Nasser nationalized the canal; Britain and France protested and sent troops; **Israel invaded from the east**; the real aim of all three was to **bring down Nasser**.
- ✅ Council resolution condemning force **vetoed by Britain and France**; the Assembly condemned the invasions and called for withdrawal **sixty-four to five**; the aggressors withdrew **in view of the weight of opinion against them**, **provided** the organisation ensured a canal settlement and kept the parties apart; a force of **five thousand from ten different countries** moved in.
- ✅ Prestige of the organisation and of **Hammarskjold** greatly enhanced; ✅ **American and Russian pressure was also important** in bringing about a ceasefire.
- ✅ **Earlier missions:** **UNTSO 1948**; **UNMOGIP from January 1949** - observation, not interposition. ✅ **UNEF, November 1956** - the **first armed peacekeeping force**, created by the Assembly in emergency special session.
- ✅ **Three principles:** **consent of the parties**; **impartiality but not neutrality** in executing the mandate; **non-use of force except in self-defence and defence of the mandate**. ✅ Main-party consent does not guarantee local consent where parties are divided, command is weak or spoilers are present. ✅ **Robust peacekeeping** = tactical force with Council authorisation **and host consent**; **enforcement** = no consent, strategic level, otherwise barred by **Art 2(4)**.
- ⚠️ Peacekeeping is nowhere in the Charter; "Chapter Six and a Half" is a description of practice usually attributed to Hammarskjold. ⚠️ It is not a way of defeating an aggressor but **a way of making withdrawal tolerable**.

## 8. The great-power limit: Hungary, Czechoslovakia, and the success condition

- ✅ **Hungary 1956**, simultaneous with Suez, showed the organisation **at its most ineffective**: Soviet troops crushed the rising, the government appealed, the **Russians vetoed** the Council resolution, the **Assembly passed the same resolution and appointed an investigating committee**, and the Russians **refused to co-operate**.
- ✅ The recorded contrast: **Britain and France were willing to bow to international pressure; the Russians simply ignored the organisation.**
- ✅ **Czechoslovakia 1968** - almost a repeat: **Russian and other Warsaw Pact troops**; Council motion **vetoed**; a Russian claim of an **invitation**, **denied by the Czechs**. ⚠️ The invitation device places an intervention **outside Art 2(4)**, and the Charter supplies no impartial mechanism for settling the contested fact.
- ⚠️ **The success condition, two limbs:** effective where great-power interests are **not directly engaged**, **or** where engaged great powers **choose to support or accept** the action; ineffective where a great power's interests are engaged and it decides to **ignore or defy**.
- ⚠️ **Refinement from Suez:** the operative variable is **sensitivity to isolation**, not raw power.
- ✅ Later confirmation: the USSR ignored withdrawal demands over **Hungary 1956** and **Afghanistan 1980**.
- ⚠️ **Four types of failure:** **blocked** (Hungary, Czechoslovakia); **defied** (Afghanistan, Namibia before 1990, the Nicaragua merits judgment of 1986); **overmatched** (Somalia, Bosnia); **bypassed** (Iraq 2003) - four types, four remedies.

## 9. Settlement once, containment thereafter

- ✅ **West New Guinea:** 1946 independence for the Dutch East Indies as Indonesia, West Irian unresolved; **1961** fighting; **U Thant** appeals; **1962** agreement that it become part of Indonesia; **transfer organized and policed by a UN force**. ✅ Verdict: a vital role in getting negotiations off the ground, **though it did not itself make the decision**.
- ✅ **Palestine 1947:** after investigation the organisation **decided to divide Palestine** and set up Israel - one of its **most controversial decisions**, rejected by the majority of Arabs; it could **not prevent** the wars of **1948-9, 1967 and 1973**; it **did** arrange ceasefires and provide supervisory forces, with **UNRWA** caring for the Arab refugees.
- ✅ **Kashmir:** claimed by both states after 1947; **1948 ceasefire negotiated**; the line policed for **sixteen years**; a short war in **1965** ended by successful intervention; **1999** violent clashes; **little prospect of the organisation or any other agency finding a permanent solution**.
- ✅ **Cyprus:** under **British administration from 1878**, annexed **1914**, **Crown colony 1925**; independence **1960**; civil war **1963** (Greeks about **eighty per cent**); force from **March 1964**; **three thousand** troops permanently required; **1974** Greek Cypriot attempt at union with Greece, Turkish Cypriots with invading Turkish troops seized the north and expelled Greeks living there; the organisation condemned but could not remove them, achieved a ceasefire and still polices the frontier; **Annan Plan 2004** accepted by the Turks and **rejected by the Greeks**.
- ✅ **Lebanon:** civil war **1975-1990**, closed by the Taif Agreement of 1989; Israeli invasion **March 1978**; **June 1978** Israel agreed to withdraw **provided** the organisation policed the frontier - **UNIFIL, about seven thousand**; Hezbollah from the early 1990s; Israeli attack **April 1996** and occupation until **1999**; force raised to about **eight thousand**; reduced to some **three thousand** in **2002**; UNIFIL trained and equipped the **Lebanese army**; **July 2006** ambush, blockade, bombing and rockets at over a hundred a day; ceasefire **mid-August**; force raised to **twelve thousand**; renewed incidents in **2011** around **Ghajar**.
- ✅ **Iran-Iraq 1980-8:** ceasefire negotiated after years of attempted mediation, **helped by both sides being close to exhaustion**.
- ⚠️ **Crisis management** (strong) against **conflict resolution** (thin). ⚠️ A force can hold a line both sides accept and cannot prevent, reverse or punish a determined military action. ⚠️ Force levels track the failure of the political process; **capacity-building, not interposition, is the exit**. ⚠️ Mediation cannot change objectives and can supply a route once objectives have changed.

## 10. Complexity, cost and defied law: the Congo and Namibia

- ✅ **Congo 1960-64:** the **most complex operation to date except Korea**; the country **dissolved into chaos immediately after independence**; a force of **over twenty thousand at its largest** restored **some sort of precarious order**; a special **Congo Fund** supported recovery and development.
- ✅ The **cost brought the organisation close to bankruptcy**, especially when the **USSR, France and Belgium refused to pay** because they **disapproved of the way the situation had been handled**. ✅ **Hammarskjold died in an air crash on 18 September 1961 near Ndola, Northern Rhodesia - now Zambia - not in the Congo.**
- ⚠️ **Withholding is a veto exercised after the vote** - open to any substantial contributor, needing no Council seat, unanswerable by procedure, and attacking the operation rather than the decision. ⚠️ **Art 19** is a weak answer: a two-year threshold, loss of vote rather than recovery of money, and a discretionary exception.
- ⚠️ The Congo is the **prototype of the post-Cold-War mission**.
- ✅ **Trusteeship Council:** replaced the League's **Mandates Commission of 1919**; **did its job well**, and by **1970 most mandates had gained independence**; supervised **eleven trust territories** under **seven** administering states; all attained self-government or independence by 1994; **suspended operation on 1 November 1994** and remains a principal organ.
- ✅ **Namibia:** South Africa's white-minority government refused independence to a territory on its frontier that would have a black African majority government; **1971** the **ICJ ruled the occupation a breach of international law requiring immediate withdrawal**; **South Africa ignored the organisation**; **1990** black African nationalism and world opinion forced release. ⚠️ **Stored-asset principle:** an unenforceable determination becomes effective when political conditions change.

## 11. The Assembly transformed: decolonisation and the Global South

- ✅ **Art 1(2)** self-determination of peoples; ✅ **Art 73** - administering powers accept **as a sacred trust** the obligation to promote self-government in non-self-governing territories; ✅ **GA res 1514 (XV), 14 December 1960** - Declaration on the Granting of Independence to Colonial Countries and Peoples.
- ⚠️ Chapter XI covered the whole colonial world with a principle; trusteeship covered eleven territories with a mechanism - and the principle proved the stronger once the votes existed.
- ✅ **43 new members 1963-68**; **clear Third World majority by 1970**; only that bloc could be certain of carrying resolutions, and **both western and communist blocs** found it increasingly difficult to carry theirs.
- ✅ **1974** UNESCO resolutions condemning **colonialism** and **imperialism**; **1979** a western motion condemning **terrorism** defeated by the Arab states and their supporters.
- ✅ **1983 UNESCO Congress crisis:** charges of **inefficiency, waste and unacceptable political aims**; the trigger was a proposal by **some communist states** for the **internal licensing of foreign journalists**, which the USA said would let states censor one another's media. ✅ **USA withdrew from 1 January 1985**; **Britain and Singapore in 1986**; **Britain rejoined 1997**; the **USA announced its return in September 2002 and rejoined in 2003**.
- ✅ **UNCTAD** first met **1964** and soon became permanent - encouraging Third World industry and **pressurising rich countries into buying Third World products**; ✅ **Group of 77** founded **15 June 1964** at its first session; ✅ **NIEO declaration, GA res 3201 (S-VI), 1974**.
- ✅ The recorded gloss on "too political": the western nations **meant acting in a way the West disapproved of**.
- ⚠️ The transformation was real in norms, programmes and money and **nil in security**, because Art 108 puts the Charter beyond a majority.

## 12. The non-military record

- ✅ **The majority of the organisation's work** concerns human rights and economic, social, educational and cultural progress.
- ✅ **Human Rights Commission** under ECOSOC; **thirty-point Universal Declaration, 1948** - res **217 A (III), 10 December 1948** - covering an adequate standard of living; freedom from slavery, racial discrimination, arbitrary arrest and torture; fair public trial and presumption of innocence; freedom of movement and to leave one's country; marriage, family, work, property and the vote; and freedom of opinion and expression. ✅ **Declaration of the Rights of the Child, 1959** - food and medical care, free education, relaxation and play against excessive child labour, and protection from discrimination.
- ✅ Reports expected **every three years**; many states do not report and ignore the terms; the remaining instrument is **publicity and world opinion**. ✅ Campaigns against **apartheid** in South Africa and **Pinochet's** treatment of political prisoners in Chile. ✅ **Mary Robinson**, Commissioner **1997-2002**, made enemies of **Russia, China and the USA**; replaced by **Sergio Vieira de Mello** despite the Secretary-General's wish, the block **widely reported** to be American.
- ✅ **ILO**, Geneva, **founded 1919**: a job for everyone; equal opportunity irrespective of race, sex or religion; minimum standards of decent working conditions; the right to organise and bargain collectively; full social security. ✅ **Nobel Peace Prize 1969**; **Turin** training centre; obstructed where governments forbid trade unions, including communist countries and Chile, Argentina and Mexico.
- ✅ **WHO:** Egyptian cholera **1947** contained in weeks; permanent **cholera vaccine bank**; malaria, tuberculosis and leprosy; training of doctors and nurses; **smallpox eradicated - last natural case 1977, certified December 1979, declared by the World Health Assembly on 8 May 1980 in resolution WHA33.3**; a **drug-resistant malaria strain in the 1970s**; tuberculosis reported worsening in **March 2000**; AIDS work through evidence, reports and pressure on drug prices; the **global AIDS fund of June 2001** aiming at **$10 billion a year**.
- ✅ **FAO:** higher-yielding, disease-resistant **maize and rice**; fertilizers, techniques, machinery and project funding; emergency supply its main problem.
- ✅ **UNESCO**, Paris: literacy; scientific and scholarly co-operation; schools and teacher-training colleges; **International Hydrological Decade 1965-75**; restoration after the **Florence floods of 1968**; the premise that the best way to avoid war is by educating minds in the pursuit of peace.
- ✅ **UNICEF:** founded **1946**; "emergency" dropped **1953**; works with WHO; **1983 child health revolution** - breastfeeding and immunization against measles, diphtheria, polio and tetanus.
- ✅ **UNRWA:** **1950**; food, clothing, shelter and medical supplies, then **schools, hospitals, houses and training centres** as camps proved permanent.
- ✅ **OCHA:** Department of Humanitarian Affairs **1991**; expanded and renamed **1998**; staff of **some 860**; **2003** aid to some **200 million** disaster victims and **45 million** victims of complex emergencies; criticised for lacking power and resources. ✅ **Boxing Day 2004** Indian Ocean tsunami - **no effective warning system**, at least **150,000 killed**, **eleven states** sending troops, ships and aircraft and **over 400** agencies involved within days, under Emergency Relief Coordinator **Jan Egeland**.
- ⚠️ Three patterns: the **emergency that becomes permanent**; **technical transfer** as the dominant method; the agencies as the **preventive half of the Charter's theory of peace**.

## 13. Coordination, the financial bodies and the money

- ✅ **ECOSOC:** elected by the Assembly, one-third retiring each year; health, education and other social and economic projects; **five regional commissions** - ECE Europe (1947), ECLAC Latin America and the Caribbean (1948), ECA Africa (1958), ESCAP Asia and the Pacific (ECAFE 1947, renamed 1974) and ESCWA Western Asia (1973); commissions on **population, drugs, human rights and the status of women**; coordinates **around thirty** commissions and agencies. ✅ **By 1980 more than ninety per cent** of annual expenditure went to ECOSOC activities.
- ⚠️ **Membership:** the core account records **twenty-seven**; the Charter as amended and current practice record **fifty-four**. Art 61 amended eighteen to twenty-seven in force **1965** and to fifty-four in force **1973**. ⚠️ The discrepancy is recorded, not silently resolved.
- ⚠️ **Arts 62-64:** relationship agreements, consultation, recommendation and reporting - **influence, not authority** - which is why overlap persists.
- ✅ **Overlap:** WHO and FAO criticised for duplication; FAO criticised in **1984** for administrative spending. ✅ **Contradiction:** GATT eliminates trade restrictions while UNCTAD seeks preference for Third World products - **they even seem to be working against each other**.
- ✅ **IMF:** short-term loans **provided policies meet its approval**; **1977** emergency fund; conditions on **Jamaica and Tanzania** seen as unacceptable interference; Board dominated by rich western countries, especially the USA. ✅ **World Bank:** project loans for dams, agricultural techniques and family planning; the USA provides the largest share and **controls its decisions**; **Poland resigned 1950, Czechoslovakia 1954**. ✅ **GATT** signed **1947**, met under ECOSOC supervision, **became the WTO in January 1995**.
- ✅ **Category discipline:** specialized agencies - ILO, WHO, FAO, UNESCO, IMF, World Bank; **fund** - UNICEF; **General Assembly-created agency** - UNRWA; **a body of the organisation** - UNCTAD; **principal organ** - ICJ; **not an organ** - ICC; **not a specialized agency** - WTO.
- ✅ **Funding:** an annual contribution **based on general wealth and ability to pay**, plus a share of **each peacekeeping operation**, plus contributions to the special agencies. ✅ **1986:** **ninety-eight** members owing; the USA withholding **over $100 million** until budgeting was reformed and extravagance curbed; the American demand that the largest givers have more say, rejected by smaller members as **undemocratic**, with the Sri Lankan delegate's reply that at home the wealthy do not have more votes than the poor. ✅ **1987:** changes gave the main contributors more control and the position improved. ✅ **Early 1990s:** expenses soared over the Gulf, Yugoslavia and Somalia. ✅ **August 1993:** Boutros-Ghali warned that without an immediate injection of cash **all peacekeeping operations would be in jeopardy**. ✅ **USA about 30%, European Community about 35%, Japan 11% = three-quarters of the expenses.**
- ⚠️ **Formal equality in the Assembly, effective inequality wherever resources are decisive.**
- ✅ **Development mandate restated:** founding aim 1945 -> Universal Declaration entitlement 1948 -> UNCTAD, G77 and NIEO 1964-74 -> **Millennium Declaration, res 55/2, September 2000** -> **2030 Agenda and the SDGs, res 70/1, September 2015**.

## 14. The judicial organ and the international civil service

- ✅ **ICJ:** The Hague; **fifteen judges of different nationalities**; **nine-year terms, five retiring every third year**; elected by the **Assembly and Council jointly**; the only principal organ outside New York. ✅ **Art 92** annexed Statute; **Art 93** all members ipso facto parties; **Art 96** advisory opinions.
- ✅ Successes include a **frontier dispute between Holland and Belgium** and a **fishing-limits disagreement between Britain and Norway**. ✅ **Nicaragua, phased:** provisional measures **10 May 1984**; **jurisdiction upheld 26 November 1984**; **US withdrawal from the proceedings January 1985**; **merits judgment 27 June 1986** for Nicaragua over the **mining of its harbours**, with reparation due; **the USA refused to accept it and no further action was taken**. ✅ **Art 94** permits recourse to the Council, which **may** act to give effect to a judgment - **it has never done so**.
- ⚠️ **The Art 94 loop:** a losing permanent member can veto enforcement against itself. ✅ The Court **can only operate successfully when both parties agree to accept the verdict**.
- ✅ **ICC:** idea first discussed at a League convention **1937**; revived **1989**; Council-created tribunals for **Rwanda 1994** and **Bosnia 1995**; **Milosevic** transferred by Belgrade **29 June 2001**, **initial appearance 3 July 2001**, **trial commenced 12 February 2002**, first former head of state before an international criminal tribunal, **died 11 March 2006** before a verdict. ✅ **Rome Statute adopted 17 July 1998** by **120 votes to 7 with 21 abstentions**; **139 signatures** by **31 December 2000**; in force **1 July 2002**; **eighteen judges**; inaugurated **March 2003**; Clinton signed, **Bush withdrew the signature May 2002**; by **June 2003** the USA had agreements with **thirty-seven states** against surrender of its personnel.
- ✅ **Secretariat:** the office staff, **over 50,000 employees** - minutes, translations, information, administration. ✅ **Art 97:** the Secretary-General is **appointed by the Assembly on the Council's recommendation**, **five-year term**, **not from a major power**, chief administrative officer. ✅ **Art 98:** annual report. ✅ **Art 99:** may bring to the Council any matter which in his opinion may threaten peace and security. ✅ **Art 100:** shall not seek or receive instructions; members undertake not to seek to influence. ✅ **Art 101:** efficiency, competence and integrity paramount, with wide geographical recruitment.
- ✅ **Holders:** Lie (Norway, 1946-52); Hammarskjold (Sweden, 1952-61); U Thant (Burma, 1961-71); Waldheim (Austria, 1971-81); Perez de Cuellar (Peru, 1981-91); Boutros-Ghali (Egypt, 1991-6); Annan (Ghana, 1996-2006); Ban Ki-moon (South Korea, from 2006); **Guterres (Portugal, from 1 January 2017)**.
- ✅ **1996:** Boutros-Ghali had displeased Washington by refusing a Somalia mission and delaying the NATO bombing of Serbia; renewal looked likely; **Clinton administration pressure secured Annan instead**. ✅ **1999:** Annan called the NATO bombing of Yugoslavia legitimate - the account judges it a blatant Charter violation, reported here as the account's position. ✅ **2003:** Annan **was eventually forced to admit the invasion of Iraq had been illegal**.
- ⚠️ Three appointment episodes - Lie, Boutros-Ghali, Robinson - running in different political directions: the finding is **structural, not partisan**.

## 15. The post-Cold-War decade

- ✅ **The end of the Cold War did not mean the end of potential conflict.** ✅ **Well over thirty operations 1990-2003**; mid-1990s peak of **over eighty thousand troops from seventy-seven countries**.
- ✅ **Gulf 1991:** Kuwait invaded **August 1990**; Council warning; a large force to **Saudi Arabia**; short decisive campaign; Kuwait liberated; **resolution 678, 29 November 1990** - **all necessary means**. ✅ The **oil objection** and the **East Timor 1975** comparison.
- ✅ **Cambodia:** Khmer Rouge under **Pol Pot** from **1975**; **about a third of the population** slaughtered in three years; **Vietnamese invasion 1978**, condemned at American prompting because Vietnam was a Soviet ally; withdrawal **September 1989**; **elections June 1993** won by Sihanouk's party and widely accepted.
- ✅ **Mozambique:** independent **1975**; in ruins by 1990 with **both sides exhausted**; **Rome ceasefire October 1992** organised by the Roman Catholic Church and the Italian government, **not holding**; demobilisation, disarmament, relief and preparation produced **October 1994** elections; **Joachim Chissano** elected, re-elected **1999**.
- ✅ **Somalia:** **Siad Barre** overthrown **1991**; Generals **Aidid** and **Ali Mohammed**; **OAU** request; **thirty-seven thousand troops, mainly American, December 1992 - UNITAF, US-led under resolution 794 of 3 December 1992, not under UN command; UNOSOM II took UN command in May 1993**; the warlords refused disarmament; casualties; **American withdrawal March 1994**, general withdrawal **March 1995**; a **humiliating backdown** on an **impossible task set from the beginning**.
- ✅ **Bosnia:** not enough troops sent, **partly because both the European Community and the USA were reluctant to get involved**; **July 1995** Serb forces captured **Srebrenica and Zepa**, designated **safe areas**, and murdered **around eight thousand Muslim men** in Srebrenica.
- ✅ **Rwanda 1994:** the organisation **took no action** in the civil war and genocide.
- ✅ **The recorded rule:** military interventions had **most chance of success when UN troops actively supported one side against the other**, as in **Korea 1950-3** and the **1991 Gulf War**.
- ⚠️ A commitment the organisation cannot honour is **worse than no commitment**. ⚠️ The standing dilemma: accept only certain mandates and decline every hard case, or accept hard cases and sometimes fail them catastrophically.

## 16. The legitimacy crisis of 2003

- ✅ **March 2003:** the USA and Britain invaded Iraq to remove weapons of mass destruction and free the Iraqi people. ✅ Inspectors had spent **months** in Iraq and **found nothing of any significance**.
- ✅ The Council **had not given its authorization**; **France, Russia, China and Germany** wanted to allow more time; when it became clear **France and Russia would veto**, the two states went ahead **without putting the resolution to a vote**, claiming **violations of earlier resolutions** justified war. ✅ **A serious blow to the prestige of the organisation.**
- ✅ **September 2003, Annan:** a **"fork in the road"**; until then all states needed Council authorization to use force **beyond the normal right of self-defence, as prescribed by Article 51**; continued **unilateral and pre-emptive** action against a **perceived** threat would be a **fundamental challenge to the entire principles** pursued **however imperfectly for fifty-eight years**, setting precedents producing **"a proliferation of the unilateral and lawless use of force"**.
- ⚠️ **Category:** **bypassed** - no veto cast because no vote held. ⚠️ The system has **no mechanism** to determine the lawfulness of a use of force by permanent members.
- ✅ **2001 rejections:** the **1972 ABM Treaty**, the **1997 Kyoto Protocol**, the **Rome Statute**, and **Council offers of a resolution authorizing a war against terrorism**, the last because the state **prefers to conduct its own self-defence in whatever way it chooses**. ✅ The USA was **so disproportionately powerful that it could ignore the organisation unless the organisation delivered the outcome it wanted**; the challenge is to **harness** that power rather than be **impeded or stampeded** by it.
- ✅ **2005 World Summit Outcome, res 60/1, 16 September 2005, paras 138-139:** responsibility to protect populations from **genocide, war crimes, ethnic cleansing and crimes against humanity**, with primary responsibility on the state, assistance by the international community and collective action **through the Security Council**. ⚠️ It converted an **absence of principle into an absence of will** without altering the voting rule.
- ⚠️ A rule broken with an excuse is still acknowledged; the shift in 2003 was **from justification to avoidance**.

## 17. Reform: the three categories

- ✅ **Recorded weaknesses:** no permanent army; the problem of when to become involved, with veto-induced delay; friction from expanded membership; agency waste and duplication; shortage of funds. ✅ Evidence: Hungary 1956, Afghanistan 1980, Somalia and Bosnia 1992-95, Iraq 2003; and states putting more faith in **regional organizations such as NATO**, with the end of the Vietnam War, **Camp David 1979** and the **Rhodesia/Zimbabwe settlement of 1979** reached without the organisation.
- ✅ **Preventive-deployment argument:** a force on the Kuwait side of the frontier in **1990**, or the Croatian side in **1991**, **might well have prevented hostilities**.
- ✅ **Recorded strengths:** a world assembly of **around 190 nations** where even the smallest is heard; wars ended more quickly and conflict prevented, with **around 85,000 peacekeepers in action in 2012**; investigation and publicity of human-rights violations under repressive regimes; and the stimulation of economic, social and technical co-operation, with UNESCO, the ILO and WHO running a joint project on drug addiction and **fifteen conferences on AIDS**.
- ✅ **Annan from December 1996**, previously head of peacekeeping; ordered a thorough review; the **report published in 2000** - the **Brahimi Report of 21 August 2000** - recommended **permanent brigade-size forces of five thousand, ready for immediate deployment, commanded by military professionals**. ✅ **Agenda for Further Change, September 2002:** counter-terrorism and **streamlining the cumbersome budget system**. ✅ **None of the suggested reforms is beyond the bounds of possibility.**
- ⚠️ **Three categories:** **A** needs **Art 108** amendment; **B** is achievable by practice; **C** is administration and finance. ⚠️ Category B already produced the **abstention convention**, **Uniting for Peace** and **peacekeeping itself**.
- ⚠️ **The durable structural argument:** authority fixed in 1945; membership now 193; the Assembly fully adjusted; ECOSOC enlarged twice; the Council enlarged once in elected seats only; and **Art 23's criterion applied to ten seats and not five**. ⚠️ **Counter:** enforcement depends on the states the veto protects. ⚠️ **Synthesis:** necessary as a structure, indefensible as a permanent allocation.
- ✅ **India:** founding member and San Francisco participant; elected non-permanent member most recently for **2021-22**, its eighth term; member of the **G4** with Brazil, Germany and Japan; opposed by **Uniting for Consensus**; reform discussed through the **Intergovernmental Negotiations** process since **2008**. ❌ No current negotiating position, outcome, timetable or contribution figure is asserted.
- ✅ **Pact for the Future**, res **A/RES/79/1**, **22 September 2024**, with the **Global Digital Compact** and **Declaration on Future Generations** annexed; the 2025 eightieth-anniversary observance linked it to the **UN80** initiative.
- 📰 **One dated linkage (29 September 2026):** the **UN80 Initiative**, launched **March 2025**, welcomed by res **79/318**, advancing through three workstreams - system efficiency reflected in the revised estimates for the 2026 Secretariat proposed programme budget; the **Mandate Implementation Review**; and **"Shifting Paradigms: United to Deliver"** on structural and programmatic realignment - with an **Action Plan of 87 actions in 31 work packages** presented in **November 2025**, a monthly Steering Committee, a weekly Task Force and a public dashboard, and an express statement that it is **not** a solution to the system's financial crisis. ⚠️ **No workstream concerns the Council's composition or the veto.**

## 18. Answer architecture

- ⚠️ **Two questions, not one:** as a world government, a failure by design; as a forum, norm-setter, crisis manager and humanitarian coordinator, the most successful international institution ever built. ⚠️ **Mechanism:** weak where it must coerce the powerful, strong where it must coordinate the willing.
- ⚠️ **Directives:** *how far / to what extent* = degree; *assess / evaluate* = judgement with a stated criterion; *examine / discuss* = anatomy; *explain* = mechanism; *comment* = engage the claim.
- ⚠️ **Four theses:** comparative, performance, balance, structural - with a fifth for reform.
- ⚠️ **10 (150 words):** thesis, two items, condition, verdict. **15 (250):** thesis, design lesson, structure, mixed record, non-military work, verdict. **20 (250):** add structural limits, the post-Cold-War change and a status-disciplined reform paragraph.
- ⚠️ **Verdict scaffolds:** "kept the great powers inside, which is why it can rarely act against them"; "a fire-break, not a fire brigade"; "measured by lives affected, the non-military work exceeds the enforcement arm"; "freer, not stronger, after 1990".
- ⚠️ **Six boundary handovers, one clause each:** the League, the war, the Cold War, decolonisation, the post-1991 order, and India's current positions.
- ⚠️ **Seven items to rehearse:** the repaired-and-retained table; the construction sequence; Chapter VI against Chapter VII; Suez against Hungary; the three peacekeeping principles as a diagnostic; the non-military record with the expenditure figure; the three reform categories with Article 108.

## Construction record for this edition

- ⚠️ The lesson count of eighteen is derived from the dependency audit rather than from any source's section order, and the grouping accounts for every lesson without overlap or omission: **Lessons 1-3 (three)** build the legal and institutional foundation; **Lessons 4-5 (two)** anatomise the two political organs, the General Assembly and the Security Council; **Lessons 6-10 (five)** follow the Cold War security record from Korea to the Congo; **Lessons 11-13 (three)** cover the transformed Assembly, the non-military system and the money; **Lesson 14 (one)** treats the judicial and administrative organs; **Lesson 15 (one)** covers the post-Cold-War decade; and **Lessons 16-18 (three)** move from the legitimacy crisis to reform and then to marks. ⚠️ The totals close: 3 + 2 + 5 + 3 + 1 + 1 + 3 = 18.
- ⚠️ Structural variation is content-led. Lesson 1 is a design brief read as a repaired-and-retained table; Lesson 2 is a construction sequence; Lesson 3 is a promise set against a restraint with a membership gate; Lesson 4 is a functions table with one binding exception; Lesson 5 is an escalation ladder with a missing rung; Lesson 6 is a chain of accidents; Lesson 7 is a negotiation that produced an instrument; Lesson 8 is a controlled comparison; Lesson 9 is six cases sorted by product; Lesson 10 is one operation with four simultaneous problems; Lesson 11 is a constitutional lever and a political consequence; Lesson 12 is a system described by function; Lesson 13 is coordination, contradiction and money; Lesson 14 is two organs with two kinds of powerlessness; Lesson 15 is one decade with four outcomes; Lesson 16 is a sequence defined by an absence; Lesson 17 is a three-category sorting device; Lesson 18 is a decision procedure.
- ⚠️ **Quantities** appear only where a permitted source records them, with the measure that source states, and none is totalled, averaged, projected or updated. The principal permitted set is enumerated in the session opening, with a catch-all rule for the smaller counts used inside individual evidence sentences. ❌ No current budget, arrears, mission-count, staffing or troop-contribution figure is asserted anywhere in this file.
- ⚠️ **Sensitive material.** Three episodes involving mass killing - Cambodia under the Khmer Rouge, Bosnia in 1995 and Rwanda in 1994 - are taught through dates, mandates, decisions and named responsibility. Where the core account states a figure, it is reported as that account's statement without adjustment. Responsibility is attributed to governments, armed forces, named commanders, party formations and the organs that acted or failed to act, never to nations, peoples or religions.
- ⚠️ **One current linkage** is used, in Lesson 17 section 8, where it is taught and explicitly limited; it is recalled as a revision item twice - once in that lesson's own revision notes and once in register note 17 - and is not repeated or extended anywhere else. Every other lesson's checklist records truthfully that no current linkage was sought or carried.
- ⚠️ **No compiled objective-question corpus**, key, rotation or option-elimination material appears anywhere in this file.

## Discrepancies found in the evidence base and how they were handled

| Discrepancy | ⚠️ Handling |
|---|---|
| **The number of principal organs.** The core account states that there are now seven main organs and lists the International Criminal Court among them; the organisation's own description of its main bodies lists six, all established under the Charter in 1945 | ⚠️ The Charter position is taught, and the divergence is made explicit rather than concealed. The canonical owner independently records that the seventh-organ presentation is not Charter-accurate. Lesson 14 supplies the technical reason - the Court's Statute is annexed to the Charter, while the criminal court rests on a separate treaty |
| **ECOSOC membership.** The core account gives twenty-seven members; the Charter as amended and the organisation's current description give fifty-four | ⚠️ Both are stated in Lesson 13 section 2 with their periods, using the Charter's own amendment note - eighteen to twenty-seven in force 31 August 1965, twenty-seven to fifty-four in force 24 September 1973. The figure of twenty-seven is identified as out of date rather than erroneous, and the current figure is used for structure answers |
| **The Charter's amendment record.** The organisation's summary description says the Charter has been amended three times, in 1963, 1965 and 1973; the explanatory note attached to the Charter text records four adopted amendments with entry-into-force dates in 1965, 1968 and 1973 | ⚠️ The detailed note is reproduced in Lesson 2 section 5 and the divergence is stated rather than reconciled. The safe formulation offered for answers avoids a count and states the substance - enlargements and threshold changes only, never a redistribution of power |
| **The legality of the 1999 bombing of Yugoslavia.** The core account states that the action was a blatant violation of the Charter which it most certainly was, and criticises the Secretary-General for calling it legitimate | ⚠️ Reported in Lesson 14 section 5 as the account's own recorded judgement and expressly not adopted as an established legal determination. The relevant Charter provisions - Article 2(4) and the Chapter VIII bar on enforcement action by regional agencies without Council authorization - are taught in Lesson 5, and no verdict is issued by this session |
| **The Suez force and the Uniting for Peace procedure.** The core account describes the Assembly vote and the deployment of a force of five thousand from ten countries without naming the force or the procedural instrument | ⚠️ The account's description is used for the substance. The force's name and the emergency special session are supplied from the single verification pass and recorded below, because the connection between the 1950 procedure and the 1956 deployment is a genuine syllabus point that the account leaves implicit |
| **The Nicaragua case compressed into one year.** The core account states that "in 1984 Nicaragua sued the USA for mining its harbours; the Court judged in favour of Nicaragua and ordered the USA to pay compensation" | ⚠️ Corrected in Lesson 14 section 2 and taught as a phased case: provisional measures **10 May 1984**, jurisdiction and admissibility upheld **26 November 1984**, American withdrawal from the proceedings **January 1985**, and the **merits judgment on 27 June 1986**. ⚠️ The divergence is stated rather than absorbed, because the sequence - jurisdiction lost, then forum denied, then judgment refused - is the analytically important part and is invisible in the compressed version |
| **The number and names of the regional commissions.** The core account records **four** regional commissions and calls one of them "Asia and the Far East" | ⚠️ Corrected in Lesson 13 section 2 to **five**: ECE, ECLAC, ECA, **ESCAP** and **ESCWA**. ⚠️ Neither point is a simple error: **ESCWA was established in 1973** and **ECAFE was renamed ESCAP in 1974**, so the account describes the position before those changes. ⚠️ The earlier trap entry that treated "four" as the corrective has been removed, and the corrective now runs the other way |
| **The place of Hammarskjold's death.** The core account says he was "killed in a plane crash in the Congo" | ⚠️ Corrected in Lesson 10 section 2. ✅ The crash occurred on **18 September 1961 near Ndola, in Northern Rhodesia - now Zambia** - outside Congolese territory, while he was travelling to negotiate. ⚠️ The divergence is recorded rather than smoothed because the cross-border journey is part of why the circumstances have been repeatedly investigated |
| **The Rome Statute signature count.** The core account says the Statute was "signed by 120 member states" in July 1998 | ⚠️ Corrected in Lesson 14 section 3. ✅ **120** is the number of states voting to **adopt** the text on **17 July 1998** (120 in favour, 7 against, 21 abstentions); the **signature** total by the closing date of **31 December 2000** was **139**. ⚠️ Adoption, signature and ratification are distinguished explicitly in the teaching |
| **The Milosevic proceedings.** The core account says the trial "opened in July 2001" and "dragged on for almost five years" | ⚠️ Corrected in Lesson 14 section 3. ✅ He was transferred on **29 June 2001**; **3 July 2001** was his **initial appearance**; the **trial commenced on 12 February 2002**; he died on **11 March 2006**. ⚠️ An initial appearance is not a trial, and the proceedings ran a little over four years from commencement |
| **The eradication of smallpox.** The core account says the agency "eliminated smallpox in the 1980s" | ⚠️ Corrected in Lesson 12 section 4. ✅ The **last known naturally occurring case was in 1977**; a **Global Commission certified the world free of smallpox in December 1979**; and the **Thirty-third World Health Assembly declared on 8 May 1980 that the world and its peoples had won freedom from smallpox**, in resolution **WHA33.3**. ⚠️ Only the certification and declaration fall after 1977 |
| **The span of the Lebanese civil war.** The core account gives it as 1975-87 | ⚠️ Corrected in Lesson 9 section 6 to **1975-1990**, closed by the Taif Agreement of 1989. ⚠️ The corrected span is used in the visual, teaching, traps, revision notes and register notes, and the divergence is recorded here |
| **The American return to UNESCO.** The core account says "the USA followed in 2002" | ⚠️ Corrected in Lesson 11 section 4. ✅ The return was **announced in September 2002** and **took effect in 2003**. ⚠️ The two-stage sequence is taught, because the account's single year conflates an announcement with a re-entry |
| **The constitutional status of Cyprus.** The core account calls it "a British colony since 1878" | ⚠️ Corrected in Lesson 9 section 5. ✅ **Administration from 1878** under the Cyprus Convention, **annexation in 1914**, **Crown colony in 1925**, independence in **1960**. ⚠️ The distinction between administration and colonial status is the examinable one |
| **The command of the December 1992 Somalia deployment.** The core account calls the thirty-seven thousand troops who arrived "UN troops" | ⚠️ Corrected in Lesson 15 section 4. ✅ The December 1992 force was **UNITAF**, a **United States-led coalition authorised by Security Council resolution 794 of 3 December 1992**, operating under **national** and not United Nations command; the **United Nations assumed command in May 1993 with UNOSOM II**. ⚠️ The correction also changes the mechanism classification, placing the first phase with Korea and the Gulf rather than with consent-based peacekeeping |
| **The rendering of a place name in the extraction.** The OCR renders the Blue Line village as "Gharjah" | ⚠️ The standard form **Ghajar** is used in teaching, the visual, the chronology and the register notes, and the variant rendering is recorded here. ⚠️ No factual claim depends on the difference |

## Open gaps recorded rather than filled

1. ⚠️ **Veto-use data.** The core account records only that the USSR used the veto more often than any other member during the period it covers. No count, period breakdown or comparison by state is asserted anywhere in this session, and the qualitative statement is used instead.
2. ⚠️ **Peacekeeping personnel and mission series.** The session uses only the dated figures the sources record - over twenty thousand in the Congo, five thousand at Suez, three thousand in Cyprus, the UNIFIL series, thirty-seven thousand in Somalia, over eighty thousand in the mid-1990s and around eighty-five thousand in 2012. No current or continuous series exists in the held evidence and none is constructed.
3. ⚠️ **Assessed-contribution scales.** Only the shares recorded for the early 1990s - about thirty per cent, about thirty-five per cent and eleven per cent, together three-quarters of expenses - are used. No current scale is asserted.
4. ⚠️ **The legality of the 2003 invasion.** No source held here adjudicates the competing claims, and this session records the Secretary-General's statement and the acting states' claim without issuing a determination. This is stated openly in Lesson 16 section 3.
5. ⚠️ **India's peacekeeping contribution.** The advanced owner records that official material continues to document major troop, police, medical and women-peacekeeper contributions, and expressly declines to freeze an unstable count. This session therefore makes no quantitative claim, and the qualitative point is routed to the Indian foreign-policy and international-relations owners.
6. ⚠️ **Post-2005 operational practice of the responsibility-to-protect doctrine.** Recorded as owned by the international-relations owner and not developed here.

## Live source consulted for the current linkage, 29 September 2026

| Purpose | Result |
|---|---|
| One bounded current linkage for a topic whose institutional reform question remains live, restricted by the canonical owner to the institutional-reform arc and forbidden from asserting any reform outcome | ✅ The organisation's UN80 Initiative was launched in March 2025 and welcomed by General Assembly resolution 79/318. ✅ It is described by the organisation as directed at how the system is structured, managed and coordinated rather than at redefining what it does. ✅ It advances through three workstreams: efficiency proposals reflected in the revised estimates for the 2026 Secretariat proposed programme budget; the Report of the Mandate Implementation Review, before a member-state-led Informal Ad Hoc Working Group; and "Shifting Paradigms: United to Deliver", the Secretary-General's report on potential structural and programmatic realignments. ✅ An Action Plan presented to member states at an informal General Assembly meeting in November 2025 sets out 87 actions grouped into 31 work packages across the three workstreams, with responsibilities, timelines and the intergovernmental bodies that will consider them; a Steering Committee chaired monthly by the Secretary-General and a Task Force meeting weekly were established, and a public actions dashboard was launched. ✅ The organisation stated expressly that the Initiative is not a solution to the system's financial crisis |
| Use and limitation | ⚠️ Used once, in Lesson 17 section 8, solely to show that the reform categories established in that lesson remain the live ones and that none of the three workstreams concerns the Council's composition or the veto. ⚠️ Recalled as a revision item twice - once in that lesson's revision notes and once in register note 17. ❌ Explicitly **not** used as evidence about any historical case in this session, about any state's claim to membership, about any campaign, or about the outcome of any reform negotiation. ⚠️ The canonical owner's own framing is followed: the linkage concerns the institutional-reform arc, and no completed reform outcome is claimed |

## Evidence and limitation controls applied throughout

1. ⚠️ Four registers are kept distinct in the teaching: Charter law, recorded practice, analytical inference and reform advocacy. Where the Charter text and the recorded practice diverge - most importantly on the abstention convention - both are stated and the divergence is taught.
2. ⚠️ Quantities appear only where a permitted source records them, with the measure that source states, and are never totalled, averaged, projected or updated. The principal permitted set is enumerated in the session opening, together with the catch-all rule covering the smaller counts used inside individual evidence sentences.
3. ❌ No current budget, arrears, mission-count, staffing, membership-of-organ or troop-contribution figure is asserted anywhere in this file.
4. ❌ No current Security Council reform outcome, negotiating position, vote count or timetable is asserted.
5. ⚠️ Responsibility for violence, occupation and atrocity is attributed to governments, armed forces, named commanders, party formations and the organs that acted or failed to act, never to nations, peoples or religions.
6. ⚠️ Where the core account states a figure for a mass killing, it is reported as that account's statement, without adjustment or supplementation, and the surrounding teaching uses dates, mandates and decisions rather than description.
7. ⚠️ Contested questions - the legality of the 2003 invasion, the legality of the 1999 bombing, the selectivity objection to the 1991 Gulf operation, and the conditionality dispute in the financial bodies - are reported as contested with the positions named, and are resolved only where the evidence base itself resolves them.
8. ⚠️ Sixteen divergences between the core account or a summary description and the verified position are taught explicitly rather than silently corrected. Each has its own row in the discrepancy table above, and the count there and the count stated here are the same sixteen.
9. ⚠️ Six open gaps are recorded rather than filled, and are listed above.
10. ⚠️ Boundary material belonging to the interwar-diplomacy, Second World War, Cold War, decolonisation, post-1991 and Indian foreign-policy owners is excluded, with each handover stated in ordinary teaching language.
11. ❌ No compiled objective-question corpus, key, rotation or option-elimination material appears anywhere in this file.
12. ⚠️ The single current linkage is used once, recalled as a revision item in Lesson 17's revision notes and in register note 17, and is not repeated or extended; every other lesson's pre-teach record states truthfully that none was sought or carried.
13. ⚠️ Source-ownership, verification-pass and retrieval machinery is confined to the coverage matrix and this ledger and does not appear as learner-facing teaching labelling.
